=== os-01 | What an operating system does: kernel mode, system calls and the process
--- teach
In Systems Programming you called `malloc`, `fopen` and `printf`, and things happened: memory appeared, a file opened, text reached the screen. None of those functions can do that alone. Your program is not allowed to touch the disk, the screen or another program's memory. Something else does it on your behalf, and checks that you are allowed to ask. That something is the **operating system**. This course opens it up. You will build working models of its main parts in Python: the process table, the scheduler, locks, memory, paging and a file system.

This first lesson answers three questions. What is the operating system for? How does the hardware stop an ordinary program from doing whatever it likes? And how does a program ask the operating system for help?

### What an operating system is

An **operating system** (OS) is the software that manages a computer's hardware and gives programs a simpler, safer machine to run on. In everyday words: it is the building manager. Tenants (programs) never wire the electricity themselves. They ask the manager, the manager checks the request, and the manager keeps one tenant from flooding the flat below.

It has three jobs:

1. **Referee.** Many programs share one machine. The OS decides who runs when, and stops one program from reading or wrecking another's memory. This is **protection** (or **isolation**).
2. **Illusionist.** It turns awkward hardware into clean ideas. A disk is a pile of numbered blocks; the OS shows you named files. There is one CPU; the OS lets every program act as if it had its own. Each clean idea is an **abstraction**: a simple picture that hides messy details.
3. **Glue.** It offers common services, so every program does not rewrite them: reading files, sending network packets, starting other programs.

The part of the OS that is always in memory and runs with full control of the hardware is the **kernel**. Programs you run (your editor, a shell, a telemetry logger) are **user programs**. The rest of what people call "the OS" (the shell, the windowing system, `libc`) are ordinary user programs and libraries that happen to ship with the kernel.

### Two modes: how the hardware keeps control

Protection cannot be done by software alone. If any program could run any instruction, a buggy program could switch off the clock, rewrite the memory map, or talk to the disk directly. So the CPU itself has a **mode bit**: a one-bit setting inside the CPU that says who is running.

- In **kernel mode** (also called supervisor mode) every instruction is allowed.
- In **user mode** a set of **[[privileged instructions|privileged]]** is forbidden: changing the memory map, talking to devices, turning interrupts off, setting the timer, and changing the mode bit itself.

If a program in user mode tries a privileged instruction, the CPU does not run it. Instead it raises an **exception**: it stops the program, switches to kernel mode, and jumps to the kernel's handler for that kind of event. The kernel then usually kills the program. On Linux you see this as a crash with `SIGILL` (illegal instruction) or `SIGSEGV` (bad memory access).

The RISC-V machine from Computer Organization has three levels: machine mode, supervisor mode (the kernel) and user mode. An x86 chip has four "rings", and ordinary systems use two of them: ring 0 for the kernel and ring 3 for programs.

### The three ways into the kernel

There are exactly three ways the CPU goes from user mode to kernel mode, and all three jump to code the kernel chose in advance:

- An **interrupt**: a device needs attention. The disk finished a read; a packet arrived; the **timer** went off.
- An **exception** (also called a fault): the program did something wrong or unusual, such as dividing by zero, touching a bad address, or running a privileged instruction.
- A **system call**: the program asks on purpose, with a special instruction (`ecall` on RISC-V, `syscall` on x86-64).

Each of these is called a **trap**. At boot, while the CPU is still in kernel mode, the kernel writes the address of its trap handler into a special register, the **[[trap vector|trap-vector]]**. On every trap the hardware does four things in one step: it saves the program counter, records the cause, switches the mode bit to kernel, and jumps to the trap vector. Going back is one privileged instruction (`sret` on RISC-V) that restores the saved program counter and switches back to user mode.

### Why this keeps the kernel in charge

Here is the argument, the same shape as a loop invariant. The claim: **user code never runs in kernel mode, and never runs forever without the kernel getting the CPU back.**

- **At boot** the CPU starts in kernel mode, running the kernel. The kernel sets the trap vector and the timer before it starts any user program.
- **While a user program runs,** the mode bit says user. The only ways to reach kernel mode are the three traps, and each one jumps to the trap vector, which is kernel code. A program cannot point the trap vector at its own code, because writing that register is privileged. It cannot turn the timer off either, for the same reason.
- **So the timer keeps firing.** Even a program stuck in `while True: pass` is interrupted when the timer goes off, and the kernel runs again. The kernel then decides who runs next (lessons 3 and 4 are about that choice).

Every step depends on the one before: the kernel sets things up first, and user code can never undo them. Take away any one piece and it breaks. If the trap vector were writable in user mode, a program could make every trap run its own code with kernel powers. If the timer were writable, a program could keep the CPU forever.

### System calls: asking the kernel for help

A **system call** (syscall) is a request from a user program to the kernel, made through a trap. Each kind of request has a number. On RISC-V Linux, `write` is 64, `read` is 63, `close` is 57 and `getpid` ("what is my process id?") is 172. The calling convention says where everything goes:

| register | holds |
|---|---|
| `a7` | the system call number |
| `a0`, `a1`, `a2`, … | the arguments, in order |
| `a0` (afterwards) | the result |

So `write(1, buf, 5)`, "write 5 bytes from address `buf` to file descriptor 1", is this in RISC-V assembly:

```text
li   a7, 64        # system call number: write
li   a0, 1         # argument 1: file descriptor 1 (standard output)
la   a1, buf       # argument 2: the address of the bytes
li   a2, 5         # argument 3: how many bytes
ecall              # trap into the kernel
# now a0 holds the number of bytes written, or a negative error code
```

The number `1` here is a **[[file descriptor|fd]]**: a small whole number that names one of the files this program has open. Every program starts with three: 0 is standard input, 1 standard output and 2 standard error.

Nobody writes this by hand. The C library (`libc`) has a small **wrapper** function for each system call, and `printf` calls the `write` wrapper when it has text ready. In C++, the wrapper is declared in `<unistd.h>`:

```cpp fragment
#include <unistd.h>

ssize_t n = write(1, "hello\n", 6);   // one system call
if (n < 0) {
    // failed: errno says why
}
```

### What the kernel does with a system call

When `ecall` traps, the kernel's handler does the same steps for every request:

1. **Save** the program's registers, so it can carry on later exactly where it stopped.
2. **Read the number** from `a7` and look it up in the **system call table**: an array of function pointers, one per number, like the dispatch tables from Systems Programming. An unknown number fails with `ENOSYS` ("no such system call").
3. **Check every argument.** The program might be buggy or hostile. A file descriptor must be one the program has open, or the call fails with `EBADF` ("bad file descriptor"). A pointer must point into the program's own memory, all the way to its last byte, or the call fails with `EFAULT` ("bad address").
4. **Do the work**, copying bytes between the program's memory and the kernel's.
5. **Put the result in `a0`.** On Linux, success is a number of zero or more; failure is a small negative number, minus the error code: −9 for `EBADF`, −14 for `EFAULT`.
6. **Return past the `ecall`.** The saved program counter points at the `ecall` itself. The handler adds 4 (the size of one RISC-V instruction) before `sret`. Forget that, and the program runs the same `ecall` again, forever.

Here is step 2 on its own, in Python, with a table of three made-up telemetry commands instead of system calls. A dict maps each number to a function:

```python
def ping():
    return "pong"

def uptime():
    return 3600

def reboot():
    return "rebooting"

commands = {1: ping, 2: uptime, 9: reboot}

for number in [2, 1, 5]:
    handler = commands.get(number)
    print(number, handler() if handler else "unknown command")
```

It prints `2 3600`, then `1 pong`, then `5 unknown command`. The dict lookup is O(1), and the kernel's array lookup is too. So the cost of finding the handler does not grow with the number of system calls (Linux has more than 300).

### Checking a buffer, all the way to the end

Step 3 deserves a closer look, because it is where real kernels have had real security holes. A program asks the kernel to read `count` bytes starting at address `addr`. Its memory holds addresses `0` up to `size - 1`. The buffer covers `addr`, `addr + 1`, …, `addr + count - 1`. It is safe only if **all** of those are inside: `addr >= 0`, `count >= 0`, and the last byte `addr + count - 1` is at most `size - 1`, which is the same as `addr + count <= size`.

Checking only the start address is the classic mistake:

```python
size = 16
for addr, count in [(4, 8), (12, 8), (16, 0), (-2, 4)]:
    starts_ok = 0 <= addr < size
    all_ok = addr >= 0 and count >= 0 and addr + count <= size
    print(addr, count, starts_ok, all_ok)
```

The buffer at 12 with 8 bytes starts inside but runs 4 bytes past the end. A kernel that checked only the start would copy 4 bytes of whatever lies beyond: another program's data, or the kernel's own. The buffer at 16 with 0 bytes is fine: it is empty.

In Python, a slice past the end of a `bytearray` quietly gives you fewer bytes. In C, reading past the end reads whatever memory is there. Either way the check has to happen first, before any byte is copied, so that a failing call changes nothing.

### What a system call costs

A function call costs about a nanosecond. A system call costs roughly 100 nanoseconds to a microsecond, a hundred times more or worse: the trap, saving and restoring registers, the checks, and the return. Since 2018, on many Intel processors, kernels also [[swap memory maps on every entry|kpti]] to defend against the Meltdown attack, which adds more.

So a program that makes many small system calls is slow for no good reason. Writing 1,000,000 characters one `write` at a time makes 1,000,000 system calls. `printf` avoids this by collecting output in a **buffer** in your own memory and calling `write` only when the buffer is full (or at a newline, when the output is a terminal). With a 4,096-byte buffer, the same million characters take ⌈1,000,000 / 4,096⌉ = 245 system calls. You met this buffering in Systems Programming; now you know why it exists.

### The process: the OS's central abstraction

A **process** is a running program: the program's code, plus everything the OS keeps for one run of it. In everyday words: a program is a recipe; a process is someone cooking it in a kitchen of their own. Ten people can cook the same recipe at once, each with their own pots.

Each process has:

- its own **address space**: the memory it can see, which starts at address 0 as far as it knows, and holds its code, globals, heap and stack. Another process's memory is simply not visible;
- one or more **threads** of execution, each with its own program counter, registers and stack (lesson 5);
- its **open resources**, such as its table of file descriptors;
- an **identity**: a process id (**pid**), the user who owns it, and its parent.

The process is the unit the referee protects and the illusion the illusionist builds. Each process behaves as if it had a CPU and a memory of its own. Next lesson builds the kernel's record of a process, and the calls that create one.

### How kernels are built

- A **monolithic kernel** (Linux, the BSDs) puts the scheduler, memory manager, file systems, network stack and device drivers into one big program running in kernel mode. It is fast, because parts call each other directly, but a bug in any driver can crash the whole machine.
- A **[[microkernel|microkernel]]** (seL4, QNX) keeps only the essentials in kernel mode: address spaces, threads and messages. File systems and drivers run as user processes. A crashed driver can be restarted.
- Many spacecraft run a **real-time operating system** (RTOS) such as VxWorks or RTEMS. Its main promise is timing: a task that must run every 10 milliseconds does run every 10 milliseconds.

### Where it is used

- **Every program you have written** made system calls. `print("hi")` in Python ends in one `write(1, ...)` call. You can watch the calls a program makes with [[strace|strace]].
- **Containers** (lesson 16) are ordinary processes that the kernel shows a restricted view of the system.
- **Flight software** relies on the same protection: a crashed payload process must not take down attitude control.

**Watch out:** three mistakes, one per idea in this lesson.

- **Checking only where a buffer starts.** Check that the whole range `addr` to `addr + count` fits, reject a negative `count`, and do it before copying anything.
- **Treating system calls as free.** A loop that writes one byte per call is hundreds of times slower than one that buffers.
- **Forgetting to step past the trap.** A handler that returns to the saved program counter without adding 4 runs the same `ecall` again, forever.

::: context privileged Instructions only the kernel may run
A privileged instruction is one the CPU refuses to run in user mode. On RISC-V they include writing the control registers: `satp` holds the memory map, `stvec` holds the trap vector, and `sstatus` holds the interrupt switch and the mode to return to. Returning from a trap (`sret`) and waiting for an interrupt (`wfi`) are privileged too. On x86 the classic examples are `cli` (turn interrupts off), `hlt`, and writing the `cr3` register (the memory map). A user program that tries any of them gets an illegal-instruction exception, not the effect.
:::

::: context trap-vector One door into the kernel
The trap vector is a register (on RISC-V it is called `stvec`) that holds the address of the kernel's trap handler. Think of a building with one staffed door: whatever brings you in (a delivery, a fire alarm, a visitor), you arrive at the same desk. The handler reads another register (`scause`) to learn why it was called: 8 means a system call from user mode, 2 an illegal instruction, and a set top bit means an interrupt, such as the timer. Because only kernel mode can write `stvec`, a program cannot swap in its own handler.
:::

::: context fd File descriptors are small numbers
A file descriptor is an index into a table the kernel keeps for each process. Entry 3 might point to an open log file, entry 4 to a network connection. Your program only ever holds the number; the real object lives in the kernel, where your code cannot damage it. This is the same trick as a cloakroom ticket: the number is useless to anyone else, and the coat stays behind the counter. When the kernel opens a new file for you, it hands out the lowest number not in use.
:::

::: context kpti Why system calls got slower in 2018
Meltdown was a hardware flaw found in 2018 in many Intel processors. It let user code read kernel memory by timing the CPU's speculative execution, even though the mode bit forbade it. The fix, called kernel page-table isolation (KPTI), gives user mode a memory map with almost nothing of the kernel in it and switches maps on every system call and interrupt. That switch has a cost, so system-call-heavy programs got several percent to tens of percent slower. You will meet memory maps (page tables) in lesson 11.
:::

::: context microkernel Small kernels you can prove correct
seL4 is a microkernel of about 10,000 lines of C. In 2009 a team in Australia proved, with a machine-checked mathematical proof, that its code does exactly what its specification says: no crashes, no buffer overflows, no missing checks. That is only possible because the kernel is so small; Linux has tens of millions of lines. seL4 has flown in experimental military helicopters and drones, and runs in other systems where one bug is too many. QNX, another microkernel, runs in cars and medical devices.
:::

::: context strace Watching system calls
On Linux, `strace` runs a program and prints every system call it makes, with the arguments and the result. For example, `strace -e trace=write python3 -c 'print("hi")'` shows a line like `write(1, "hi\n", 3) = 3`: file descriptor 1, the bytes, the count, and after the equals sign what the kernel returned. A failing call shows `-1` and the error name, such as `openat(AT_FDCWD, "missing.txt", O_RDONLY) = -1 ENOENT (No such file or directory)`. It is one of the most useful tools for finding out why a program misbehaves.
:::
--- task
Write the kernel side of a system call, for a model of a RISC-V machine. The starter gives you the model: a `CPU` with a `mode` (`"user"` or `"kernel"`), a `pc` and registers `a0`, `a1`, `a2`, `a7`; a `Proc` with a `pid`, its user memory `mem` (a `bytearray`, addresses `0` to `len(mem) - 1`) and `fds`, a dict from file descriptor number to a `Stream`; and a `Stream`, whose bytes are in `data` (a `bytearray`). Leave the given code as it is.

Write `handle_ecall(cpu, proc)`. The user program has just run `ecall` at address `cpu.pc`, in user mode. Your function must:

1. Switch `cpu.mode` to `"kernel"`.
2. Read the call number from `a7` and the arguments from `a0`, `a1`, `a2`, then do the call:
   - `SYS_WRITE` (64), arguments `fd, addr, count`: append the `count` bytes of `mem` starting at `addr` to the end of that stream's `data`. The result is `count`.
   - `SYS_READ` (63), arguments `fd, addr, count`: take up to `count` bytes from the **front** of that stream's `data` (fewer if it has fewer), remove them from the stream, and copy them into `mem` starting at `addr`. The result is how many bytes were copied (0 when the stream is empty).
   - `SYS_CLOSE` (57), argument `fd`: remove that descriptor from `fds`. The result is 0.
   - `SYS_GETPID` (172): the result is the process's pid.
   - Any other number: the result is `-ENOSYS`.
3. Check arguments before doing anything, in this order. If `fd` is not in `fds`, the result is `-EBADF`. Then, for read and write, if `count < 0`, `addr < 0` or `addr + count > len(mem)`, the result is `-EFAULT`. A failed call changes no memory and no stream.
4. Put the result in `a0`, add 4 to `pc`, and switch `mode` back to `"user"`.

The starter's `do(cpu, proc, number, a0, a1, a2)` sets the registers, calls your function and returns `a0`, and `boot(mem_size, pid)` makes a CPU and a process with streams 0, 1 and 2 open. The checks use both.
--- starter
EBADF = 9
EFAULT = 14
ENOSYS = 38

SYS_CLOSE = 57
SYS_READ = 63
SYS_WRITE = 64
SYS_GETPID = 172


class CPU:
    def __init__(self):
        self.mode = "user"
        self.pc = 0
        self.regs = {"a0": 0, "a1": 0, "a2": 0, "a7": 0}


class Stream:
    def __init__(self, data=b""):
        self.data = bytearray(data)


class Proc:
    def __init__(self, pid, mem_size):
        self.pid = pid
        self.mem = bytearray(mem_size)
        self.fds = {}


def boot(mem_size, pid):
    cpu = CPU()
    cpu.pc = 0x1000
    proc = Proc(pid, mem_size)
    proc.fds = {0: Stream(), 1: Stream(), 2: Stream()}
    return cpu, proc


def do(cpu, proc, number, a0=0, a1=0, a2=0):
    cpu.regs.update({"a7": number, "a0": a0, "a1": a1, "a2": a2})
    handle_ecall(cpu, proc)
    return cpu.regs["a0"]


def handle_ecall(cpu, proc):
    raise NotImplementedError("write the trap handler")
--- solution
EBADF = 9
EFAULT = 14
ENOSYS = 38

SYS_CLOSE = 57
SYS_READ = 63
SYS_WRITE = 64
SYS_GETPID = 172


class CPU:
    def __init__(self):
        self.mode = "user"
        self.pc = 0
        self.regs = {"a0": 0, "a1": 0, "a2": 0, "a7": 0}


class Stream:
    def __init__(self, data=b""):
        self.data = bytearray(data)


class Proc:
    def __init__(self, pid, mem_size):
        self.pid = pid
        self.mem = bytearray(mem_size)
        self.fds = {}


def boot(mem_size, pid):
    cpu = CPU()
    cpu.pc = 0x1000
    proc = Proc(pid, mem_size)
    proc.fds = {0: Stream(), 1: Stream(), 2: Stream()}
    return cpu, proc


def do(cpu, proc, number, a0=0, a1=0, a2=0):
    cpu.regs.update({"a7": number, "a0": a0, "a1": a1, "a2": a2})
    handle_ecall(cpu, proc)
    return cpu.regs["a0"]


def buffer_ok(proc, addr, count):
    return count >= 0 and addr >= 0 and addr + count <= len(proc.mem)


def sys_write(proc, fd, addr, count):
    if fd not in proc.fds:
        return -EBADF
    if not buffer_ok(proc, addr, count):
        return -EFAULT
    proc.fds[fd].data.extend(proc.mem[addr:addr + count])
    return count


def sys_read(proc, fd, addr, count):
    if fd not in proc.fds:
        return -EBADF
    if not buffer_ok(proc, addr, count):
        return -EFAULT
    stream = proc.fds[fd]
    n = min(count, len(stream.data))
    proc.mem[addr:addr + n] = stream.data[:n]
    del stream.data[:n]
    return n


def sys_close(proc, fd, addr, count):
    if fd not in proc.fds:
        return -EBADF
    del proc.fds[fd]
    return 0


def sys_getpid(proc, fd, addr, count):
    return proc.pid


TABLE = {
    SYS_WRITE: sys_write,
    SYS_READ: sys_read,
    SYS_CLOSE: sys_close,
    SYS_GETPID: sys_getpid,
}


def handle_ecall(cpu, proc):
    cpu.mode = "kernel"
    regs = cpu.regs
    handler = TABLE.get(regs["a7"])
    if handler is None:
        result = -ENOSYS
    else:
        result = handler(proc, regs["a0"], regs["a1"], regs["a2"])
    regs["a0"] = result
    cpu.pc += 4
    cpu.mode = "user"
--- hint
Keep a dict from call number to a small function for each call, as the telemetry command table does. Every handler can take the same four things (`proc`, then the three argument registers) even if it ignores some of them.
--- hint
In read and write, test the file descriptor first, then the buffer: `count >= 0 and addr >= 0 and addr + count <= len(proc.mem)`. Return the negative error code before touching memory or the stream.
--- hint
For read, `n = min(count, len(stream.data))`; copy with `proc.mem[addr:addr + n] = stream.data[:n]`, then `del stream.data[:n]`. At the end of `handle_ecall`, store the result in `cpu.regs["a0"]`, do `cpu.pc += 4` and set the mode back to `"user"`.
--- check test | write copies bytes from memory to standard output and returns the count
(lambda c, p: (p.mem.__setitem__(slice(2, 7), b"hello"), do(c, p, SYS_WRITE, 1, 2, 5), bytes(p.fds[1].data))[1:] == (5, b"hello"))(*boot(32, 7))
--- check test | Two writes append in order, to the right stream
(lambda c, p: (p.mem.__setitem__(slice(0, 6), b"GO\nERR"), do(c, p, SYS_WRITE, 1, 0, 3), do(c, p, SYS_WRITE, 2, 3, 3), do(c, p, SYS_WRITE, 1, 0, 2), bytes(p.fds[1].data), bytes(p.fds[2].data))[1:] == (3, 3, 2, b"GO\nGO", b"ERR"))(*boot(16, 3))
--- check test | After the call: back in user mode, pc moved past the ecall
(lambda c, p: (do(c, p, SYS_GETPID), c.mode, c.pc) == (42, "user", 0x1004))(*boot(8, 42))
--- check test | Each call moves pc on by exactly 4, even a failing one
(lambda c, p: (do(c, p, SYS_GETPID), do(c, p, 999), do(c, p, SYS_WRITE, 8, 0, 1), c.pc) == (5, -38, -9, 0x100C))(*boot(8, 5))
?? Add 4 to cpu.pc on every return, success or failure.
--- check test | The handler runs in kernel mode
(lambda c, p: (c.__setattr__("seen", []), p.fds.__setitem__(1, type("Spy", (), {"data": property(lambda s: (c.seen.append(c.mode), bytearray())[1])})()), do(c, p, SYS_WRITE, 1, 0, 1), c.seen[:1], c.mode)[3:] == (["kernel"], "user"))(*boot(8, 1))
--- check case | An unknown call number gives -ENOSYS
(lambda c, p: (do(c, p, 0), do(c, p, 1000), do(c, p, 93)))(*boot(8, 1))
=> (-38, -38, -38)
--- check test | A file descriptor that is not open gives -EBADF for read, write and close
(lambda c, p: (do(c, p, SYS_WRITE, 3, 0, 1), do(c, p, SYS_READ, -1, 0, 1), do(c, p, SYS_CLOSE, 7)) == (-9, -9, -9))(*boot(8, 1))
--- check test | A buffer that runs past the end gives -EFAULT and writes nothing
(lambda c, p: (p.mem.__setitem__(slice(12, 16), b"abcd"), do(c, p, SYS_WRITE, 1, 12, 8), bytes(p.fds[1].data))[1:] == (-14, b""))(*boot(16, 1))
?? Check the last byte, not just the first: addr + count must be at most len(proc.mem).
--- check test | Negative address or negative count gives -EFAULT
(lambda c, p: (do(c, p, SYS_WRITE, 1, -2, 4), do(c, p, SYS_WRITE, 1, 4, -1), do(c, p, SYS_READ, 0, -1, 1)) == (-14, -14, -14))(*boot(16, 1))
--- check test | A buffer that ends exactly at the end of memory is fine
(lambda c, p: (p.mem.__setitem__(slice(12, 16), b"TAIL"), do(c, p, SYS_WRITE, 1, 12, 4), bytes(p.fds[1].data))[1:] == (4, b"TAIL"))(*boot(16, 1))
--- check test | Writing 0 bytes, even at address len(mem), returns 0
(lambda c, p: (do(c, p, SYS_WRITE, 1, 16, 0), bytes(p.fds[1].data)) == (0, b""))(*boot(16, 1))
--- check test | A bad fd is reported before a bad buffer
(lambda c, p: do(c, p, SYS_READ, 5, -3, 100) == -9)(*boot(16, 1))
--- check test | read takes bytes from the front of the stream into memory
(lambda c, p: (p.fds[0].data.extend(b"burn 30s\n"), do(c, p, SYS_READ, 0, 4, 4), bytes(p.mem[4:8]), bytes(p.fds[0].data))[1:] == (4, b"burn", b" 30s\n"))(*boot(16, 1))
--- check test | read returns fewer bytes when the stream runs short, then 0
(lambda c, p: (p.fds[0].data.extend(b"ok"), do(c, p, SYS_READ, 0, 0, 10), do(c, p, SYS_READ, 0, 0, 10), bytes(p.mem[:3]))[1:] == (2, 0, b"ok\x00"))(*boot(16, 1))
--- check test | A failing read leaves both memory and the stream alone
(lambda c, p: (p.fds[0].data.extend(b"data"), do(c, p, SYS_READ, 0, 14, 4), bytes(p.mem), bytes(p.fds[0].data))[1:] == (-14, bytes(16), b"data"))(*boot(16, 1))
--- check test | close removes the descriptor; using it afterwards gives -EBADF
(lambda c, p: (do(c, p, SYS_CLOSE, 1), 1 in p.fds, do(c, p, SYS_WRITE, 1, 0, 1), do(c, p, SYS_CLOSE, 1)) == (0, False, -9, -9))(*boot(8, 1))
--- check test | An echo program: read then write the same bytes
(lambda c, p: (p.fds[0].data.extend(b"T+00:05 nominal"), [do(c, p, SYS_WRITE, 1, 0, do(c, p, SYS_READ, 0, 0, 6)) for _ in range(4)], bytes(p.fds[1].data), c.pc)[2:] == (b"T+00:05 nominal", 0x1000 + 32))(*boot(6, 1))

+++ question | What the timer protects
--- ask
A user program runs `while True: pass` and never makes a system call. Why does the kernel still get the CPU back?
--- choice
The Python interpreter checks in with the kernel every few instructions.
--- choice correct
The kernel set a hardware timer before starting the program, and only kernel mode can change it. When it fires, the interrupt traps into the kernel.
--- choice
The CPU switches to kernel mode by itself after every loop iteration.
--- choice
The kernel cannot get the CPU back; the machine has to be rebooted.
--- why
A timer interrupt is one of the three ways into the kernel, and it needs no help from the program. Because setting or disabling the timer is privileged, the program cannot stop it, so the kernel is guaranteed to run again. This is what makes preemptive scheduling (lessons 3 and 4) possible.

+++ question | The step past the trap
--- ask
A student's trap handler does everything right except one thing: it returns with `sret` to the saved program counter without adding 4. What happens when a program makes a `getpid` call?
--- choice
The call works normally, because `sret` adds the 4 by itself.
--- choice correct
The program runs the same `ecall` again as soon as it returns, and keeps calling `getpid` forever.
--- choice
The program skips the instruction after the `ecall`.
--- why
For a system call, the saved program counter points at the `ecall` instruction itself, so the handler must move it on to the next instruction (4 bytes later on RISC-V). For some faults the opposite is right: after the kernel fixes a missing page (lesson 11), it returns to the same instruction on purpose, so it runs again and succeeds.

+++ question | Counting system calls
--- ask
A program prints 100,000 lines of 40 bytes each to a file, through `printf` with a 4,096-byte buffer. About how many `write` system calls does it make? Type a whole number (round up).
--- answer
977
--- why
100,000 × 40 = 4,000,000 bytes. A full buffer is flushed with one `write` of 4,096 bytes, so it takes ⌈4,000,000 / 4,096⌉ = 977 calls (976 full buffers and one partial flush at exit). Without the buffer, one `write` per line would be 100,000 calls. Writing to a terminal is different: there `printf` flushes at every newline.

+++ practice | User mode, traps and the timer
--- task
Model a CPU running one user program, to see the three ways into the kernel.

Write `run(program, timer_every)`. `program` is a list of instruction names (strings). The CPU starts in user mode at instruction 0 and runs them in order. Record what happens as a list of events (tuples):

- An instruction in the set `PRIVILEGED` (given in the starter) cannot run in user mode. Record `("illegal", i)`, where `i` is its position, then `("killed", i)`, and stop. It does not count as executed.
- `"ecall"` is a system call. Record `("syscall", i)`. The kernel handles it and returns to the next instruction. It counts as executed.
- Any other instruction just runs and counts as executed.
- After every `timer_every` executed instructions, the timer interrupts: record `("timer", i)`, where `i` is the position of the instruction that just finished. It comes after that instruction's own event. If `timer_every` is 0, there is no timer.
- If the program runs off its end, record `("exit", n)`, where `n` is how many instructions were executed.

For example, `run(["add", "ecall", "load", "sret", "add"], 2)` gives `[("syscall", 1), ("timer", 1), ("illegal", 3), ("killed", 3)]`.
--- starter
PRIVILEGED = {"csrw stvec", "csrw satp", "csrw sstatus", "sret", "wfi"}


def run(program, timer_every):
    return []
--- solution
PRIVILEGED = {"csrw stvec", "csrw satp", "csrw sstatus", "sret", "wfi"}


def run(program, timer_every):
    events = []
    executed = 0
    for i, instruction in enumerate(program):
        if instruction in PRIVILEGED:
            events.append(("illegal", i))
            events.append(("killed", i))
            return events
        if instruction == "ecall":
            events.append(("syscall", i))
        executed += 1
        if timer_every and executed % timer_every == 0:
            events.append(("timer", i))
    events.append(("exit", executed))
    return events
--- hint
Walk the program with `enumerate`, keeping a count of executed instructions. A privileged instruction ends the run straight away, before the count goes up.
--- hint
After counting an instruction, the timer fires when the count is a multiple of `timer_every`: `executed % timer_every == 0`. Guard it with `timer_every` being non-zero, or `% 0` raises an error.
--- check case | The example from the task
run(["add", "ecall", "load", "sret", "add"], 2)
=> [("syscall", 1), ("timer", 1), ("illegal", 3), ("killed", 3)]
--- check case | A program that finishes, with no timer
run(["load", "ecall", "add", "ecall"], 0)
=> [("syscall", 1), ("syscall", 3), ("exit", 4)]
--- check case | The timer fires every 3 instructions
run(["add"] * 7, 3)
=> [("timer", 2), ("timer", 5), ("exit", 7)]
--- check case | An empty program exits at once
run([], 5)
=> [("exit", 0)]
--- check case | A privileged instruction first: nothing is executed
run(["csrw satp", "add"], 1)
=> [("illegal", 0), ("killed", 0)]
--- check case | A timer every instruction, and the event order on an ecall
run(["ecall", "add"], 1)
=> [("syscall", 0), ("timer", 0), ("timer", 1), ("exit", 2)]
--- check case | wfi is privileged too; the timer count stops at the fault
run(["add", "add", "wfi"], 2)
=> [("timer", 1), ("illegal", 2), ("killed", 2)]

+++ practice | Why did we trap? Decoding scause
--- task
On RISC-V, a trap handler reads the register `scause` to learn why it was called. It is a 64-bit number. Its top bit (bit 63) is 1 for an interrupt and 0 for an exception. The other 63 bits are a code.

Write `decode(scause)` that returns a pair `(kind, name)`. `kind` is `"interrupt"` or `"exception"`. `name` comes from these tables, or is `"unknown"` for any other code:

- interrupts: 1 `"software"`, 5 `"timer"`, 9 `"external"`
- exceptions: 2 `"illegal instruction"`, 3 `"breakpoint"`, 5 `"load fault"`, 7 `"store fault"`, 8 `"ecall from user"`, 12 `"instruction page fault"`, 13 `"load page fault"`, 15 `"store page fault"`

Then write `next_pc(scause, sepc)`. `sepc` is the saved program counter: for an exception, the address of the instruction that trapped; for an interrupt, the address of the next instruction that has not run yet. Return where the program should carry on, or `None` if it must be killed:

- a system call from user mode: the instruction after it, `sepc + 4`;
- an interrupt, or any page fault (the kernel fixes the page and runs the instruction again): `sepc`;
- anything else (including unknown codes): `None`.

For example, `decode(8)` is `("exception", "ecall from user")`, and `next_pc(8, 0x400)` is `0x404`.
--- starter
def decode(scause):
    return ("exception", "unknown")


def next_pc(scause, sepc):
    return sepc
--- solution
INTERRUPT_BIT = 1 << 63

INTERRUPTS = {1: "software", 5: "timer", 9: "external"}
EXCEPTIONS = {
    2: "illegal instruction",
    3: "breakpoint",
    5: "load fault",
    7: "store fault",
    8: "ecall from user",
    12: "instruction page fault",
    13: "load page fault",
    15: "store page fault",
}


def decode(scause):
    code = scause & (INTERRUPT_BIT - 1)
    if scause & INTERRUPT_BIT:
        return ("interrupt", INTERRUPTS.get(code, "unknown"))
    return ("exception", EXCEPTIONS.get(code, "unknown"))


def next_pc(scause, sepc):
    kind, name = decode(scause)
    if kind == "interrupt":
        return sepc
    if name == "ecall from user":
        return sepc + 4
    if name.endswith("page fault"):
        return sepc
    return None
--- hint
The top bit is `1 << 63`. Test it with `scause & (1 << 63)`, and get the code by masking it off: `scause & ((1 << 63) - 1)`.
--- hint
Two dicts, one per kind, with `.get(code, "unknown")`. In `next_pc`, decide from the pair `decode` returns: interrupts resume at `sepc`, the user ecall at `sepc + 4`, page faults at `sepc`, and everything else is `None`.
--- check case | The system call from the task
(decode(8), next_pc(8, 0x400))
=> (("exception", "ecall from user"), 0x404)
--- check case | The timer interrupt resumes at the same pc
(decode((1 << 63) | 5), next_pc((1 << 63) | 5, 0x2000))
=> (("interrupt", "timer"), 0x2000)
--- check case | Code 5 means different things for the two kinds
(decode(5), decode((1 << 63) + 5))
=> (("exception", "load fault"), ("interrupt", "timer"))
--- check case | An illegal instruction is fatal
(decode(2), next_pc(2, 0x10))
=> (("exception", "illegal instruction"), None)
--- check case | Page faults run the same instruction again
[next_pc(c, 0x88) for c in (12, 13, 15)]
=> [0x88, 0x88, 0x88]
--- check case | Unknown codes of both kinds
(decode(4), decode((1 << 63) | 3), next_pc(4, 0), next_pc((1 << 63) | 3, 0x50))
=> (("exception", "unknown"), ("interrupt", "unknown"), None, 0x50)
--- check case | The external interrupt and a breakpoint
(decode((1 << 63) | 9), decode(3), next_pc(3, 0x40))
=> (("interrupt", "external"), ("exception", "breakpoint"), None)

+++ practice | A stdio buffer, counted in system calls
--- task
Model the buffer inside `printf`. Write a class `BufferedWriter`:

- `BufferedWriter(sink, size)`: `sink` is a function that takes one `bytes` value; each call to it stands for one `write` system call. `size` is the buffer's capacity in bytes.
- `write(data)`: add the bytes `data` to the end of the buffer. Then, while the buffer holds `size` bytes or more, call `sink` with exactly its first `size` bytes and remove them.
- `flush()`: if the buffer is not empty, call `sink` once with everything in it, and empty it. An empty buffer makes no call.

The sink must receive every byte exactly once, in order, and `write` must not call `sink` when the buffer is still short of `size` bytes.

For example, with `size` 4, writing `b"ab"`, then `b"cdefghij"`, then flushing calls the sink with `b"abcd"`, `b"efgh"`, then `b"ij"`.
--- starter
class BufferedWriter:
    def __init__(self, sink, size):
        self.sink = sink

    def write(self, data):
        self.sink(bytes(data))

    def flush(self):
        pass
--- solution
class BufferedWriter:
    def __init__(self, sink, size):
        self.sink = sink
        self.size = size
        self.buffer = bytearray()

    def write(self, data):
        self.buffer.extend(data)
        while len(self.buffer) >= self.size:
            self.sink(bytes(self.buffer[:self.size]))
            del self.buffer[:self.size]

    def flush(self):
        if self.buffer:
            self.sink(bytes(self.buffer))
            self.buffer.clear()
--- hint
Keep a `bytearray` as the buffer. `extend` adds bytes to its end, and `del buf[:k]` removes the first `k`.
--- hint
In `write`, use a `while` loop, not an `if`: one big write can fill the buffer several times over. `flush` calls the sink only when the buffer is non-empty.
--- check test | The example from the task
(lambda out: (lambda w: (w.write(b"ab"), w.write(b"cdefghij"), w.flush(), out)[3])(BufferedWriter(out.append, 4)))([]) == [b"abcd", b"efgh", b"ij"]
--- check test | Short writes make no call until flush
(lambda out: (lambda w: (w.write(b"T+1 "), w.write(b"ok"), len(out), w.flush(), out)[2::2])(BufferedWriter(out.append, 16)))([]) == (0, [b"T+1 ok"])
--- check test | Flushing an empty buffer, or twice, makes no extra call
(lambda out: (lambda w: (w.flush(), w.write(b"x"), w.flush(), w.flush(), out)[4])(BufferedWriter(out.append, 8)))([]) == [b"x"]
--- check test | A write that exactly fills the buffer is sent at once
(lambda out: (lambda w: (w.write(b"abcd"), list(out))[1])(BufferedWriter(out.append, 4)))([]) == [b"abcd"]
--- check test | 1,000,000 one-byte writes with a 4,096-byte buffer take 245 calls
(lambda out: (lambda w: ([w.write(b"z") for _ in range(1000000)], w.flush(), len(out), sum(map(len, out)), set(map(len, out[:-1])))[2:])(BufferedWriter(out.append, 4096)))([]) == (245, 1000000, {4096})
--- check test | Every byte arrives once, in order, over mixed write sizes
(lambda out: (lambda w: ([w.write(bytes([i % 256]) * (i % 11)) for i in range(300)], w.flush(), b"".join(out) == b"".join(bytes([i % 256]) * (i % 11) for i in range(300)) and all(len(c) == 7 for c in out[:-1]))[2])(BufferedWriter(out.append, 7)))([])

+++ practice | Writing everything, whatever write returns
--- task
A real `write` system call may write **fewer** bytes than you asked for (a full pipe, a slow network), or fail with a negative error code. One code is special: `-EINTR` means "a signal interrupted the call before anything was written; just try again".

Write `write_all(sys_write, fd, data)`. `sys_write(fd, chunk)` stands for the raw system call: it returns how many bytes of `chunk` it wrote (from the front), or a negative error code. Your function must:

- keep calling `sys_write` with the part of `data` not written yet, until all of it is written, then return `len(data)`;
- on `-EINTR`, call again with the same remaining bytes;
- on any other negative result, stop and return that result;
- if `sys_write` returns 0 while bytes remain, stop and return `-EIO` (it would otherwise loop forever);
- for empty `data`, return 0 without calling `sys_write` at all.

`EINTR` is 4 and `EIO` is 5; the starter defines both, and `EAGAIN` (11).
--- starter
EINTR = 4
EIO = 5
EAGAIN = 11


def write_all(sys_write, fd, data):
    return sys_write(fd, data)
--- solution
EINTR = 4
EIO = 5
EAGAIN = 11


def write_all(sys_write, fd, data):
    written = 0
    while written < len(data):
        result = sys_write(fd, data[written:])
        if result == -EINTR:
            continue
        if result < 0:
            return result
        if result == 0:
            return -EIO
        written += result
    return written
--- hint
Keep a count `written` of the bytes already out. Each call passes `data[written:]`, and a positive result is added to the count.
--- hint
Check the result in this order: `-EINTR` means go round again; any other negative result is returned as it is; 0 is turned into `-EIO`. The loop condition `written < len(data)` makes empty data return 0 with no call.
--- check test | Partial writes are continued until everything is out
(lambda log: (write_all(lambda fd, c: (log.append((fd, bytes(c))), min(3, len(c)))[1], 1, b"telemetry"), log) == (9, [(1, b"telemetry"), (1, b"emetry"), (1, b"try")]))([])
--- check test | A full write takes one call
(lambda log: (write_all(lambda fd, c: (log.append(bytes(c)), len(c))[1], 2, b"ok\n"), log) == (3, [b"ok\n"]))([])
--- check test | EINTR is retried with the same bytes
(lambda rs, log: (write_all(lambda fd, c: (log.append(bytes(c)), rs.pop(0))[1], 1, b"abcdef"), log) == (6, [b"abcdef", b"abcdef", b"cdef", b"cdef", b"cdef"]))([-4, 2, -4, -4, 4], [])
--- check test | Another error stops at once and is returned
(lambda rs, log: (write_all(lambda fd, c: (log.append(bytes(c)), rs.pop(0))[1], 1, b"abcdef"), log) == (-11, [b"abcdef", b"def"]))([3, -11, 3], [])
--- check test | Empty data makes no call
(lambda log: (write_all(lambda fd, c: log.append(1) or 0, 1, b""), log) == (0, []))([])
--- check test | A write that returns 0 gives -EIO instead of looping forever
(lambda log: (write_all(lambda fd, c: (log.append(1), 0)[1], 1, b"xyz"), len(log)) == (-5, 1))([])
--- check test | One byte at a time still gets there
write_all(lambda fd, c: 1 if c else 0, 1, bytes(500)) == 500

+++ practice | Debug: the kernel copies past the end
--- task
`sys_write(mem, fds, fd, addr, count)` is a kernel's `write` call. It should check the whole buffer before copying: if `count < 0`, `addr < 0` or `addr + count > len(mem)`, it must return `-EFAULT` and copy nothing. Otherwise it appends `mem[addr:addr + count]` to `fds[fd]` (a `bytearray`) and returns `count`. A descriptor that is not open gives `-EBADF`, checked first.

It has a bug. Asked to write 8 bytes starting 4 bytes before the end of a 16-byte memory, it copies the last 4 bytes and returns 8, as if it had written them all. In C this would copy 4 bytes of someone else's memory. Find the bug and fix it.
--- starter
EBADF = 9
EFAULT = 14


def copy_from_user(mem, addr, count):
    if addr < 0 or addr >= len(mem):
        return None
    return bytes(mem[addr:addr + count])


def sys_write(mem, fds, fd, addr, count):
    if fd not in fds:
        return -EBADF
    data = copy_from_user(mem, addr, count)
    if data is None:
        return -EFAULT
    fds[fd].extend(data)
    return count
--- solution
EBADF = 9
EFAULT = 14


def copy_from_user(mem, addr, count):
    if count < 0 or addr < 0 or addr + count > len(mem):
        return None
    return bytes(mem[addr:addr + count])


def sys_write(mem, fds, fd, addr, count):
    if fd not in fds:
        return -EBADF
    data = copy_from_user(mem, addr, count)
    if data is None:
        return -EFAULT
    fds[fd].extend(data)
    return count
--- hint
Which bytes does the buffer cover? The first is at `addr`, the last at `addr + count - 1`. Which of those does the check look at?
--- hint
Replace the test in `copy_from_user` with one that checks the count and the end: `count < 0 or addr < 0 or addr + count > len(mem)`.
--- check test | The bug from the task: running off the end is refused
(lambda fds: (sys_write(bytearray(range(16)), fds, 1, 12, 8), fds[1]) == (-14, bytearray()))({1: bytearray()})
--- check test | A buffer that ends exactly at the end is copied
(lambda fds: (sys_write(bytearray(range(16)), fds, 1, 12, 4), fds[1]) == (4, bytearray([12, 13, 14, 15])))({1: bytearray()})
--- check test | A zero-byte write at the very end is fine
(lambda fds: sys_write(bytearray(16), fds, 1, 16, 0) == 0 and fds[1] == bytearray())({1: bytearray()})
--- check test | Negative address or count is refused
(lambda fds: (sys_write(bytearray(16), fds, 1, -1, 4), sys_write(bytearray(16), fds, 1, 3, -2), fds[1]) == (-14, -14, bytearray()))({1: bytearray()})
--- check test | A bad descriptor is still reported first
sys_write(bytearray(4), {}, 3, 100, 1) == -9
--- check test | A buffer that starts past the end is refused
(lambda fds: sys_write(bytearray(8), fds, 1, 9, 1) == -14)({1: bytearray()})

+++ practice | Descriptor tables and shell redirection
--- task
Each process has a table from file descriptor numbers to open files. Model it, then use it the way a shell does.

Write a class `FdTable`. A new table has 0, 1 and 2 all pointing at `"tty"`. Targets are strings (file names).

- `open(name)`: point the **lowest** free descriptor number at `name`, and return that number.
- `close(fd)`: remove it and return 0, or return `-EBADF` if it is not open.
- `dup(fd)`: point the lowest free number at the same target as `fd` and return it, or return `-EBADF`.
- `dup2(old, new)`: if `old` is not open, return `-EBADF`. If `old == new`, return `new` and change nothing. Otherwise close `new` if it is open, point `new` at `old`'s target, and return `new`.
- `targets()`: a dict from each open descriptor to its target.

Then write `redirect(tokens)`, which starts a fresh table, applies the tokens **from left to right** as a shell does between fork and exec, and returns `targets()`. Each token is one of:

- `"N>name"`: open `name`, `dup2` the new descriptor onto N, then close the new descriptor (unless it already was N). Without a number, `">name"` means N = 1.
- `"N<name"`: the same with N = 0 when there is no number.
- `"N>&M"`: `dup2(M, N)`. If M is not open, stop and return `None`.

So `redirect([">out.txt", "2>&1"])` sends both 1 and 2 to `"out.txt"`, but `redirect(["2>&1", ">out.txt"])` gives `{0: "tty", 1: "out.txt", 2: "tty"}`, because 2 copied where 1 pointed **at that moment**. `EBADF` is 9.
--- starter
EBADF = 9


class FdTable:
    def __init__(self):
        self.table = {0: "tty", 1: "tty", 2: "tty"}

    def open(self, name):
        fd = len(self.table)
        self.table[fd] = name
        return fd

    def close(self, fd):
        return 0

    def dup(self, fd):
        return -EBADF

    def dup2(self, old, new):
        return -EBADF

    def targets(self):
        return dict(self.table)


def redirect(tokens):
    return FdTable().targets()
--- solution
EBADF = 9


class FdTable:
    def __init__(self):
        self.table = {0: "tty", 1: "tty", 2: "tty"}

    def lowest_free(self):
        fd = 0
        while fd in self.table:
            fd += 1
        return fd

    def open(self, name):
        fd = self.lowest_free()
        self.table[fd] = name
        return fd

    def close(self, fd):
        if fd not in self.table:
            return -EBADF
        del self.table[fd]
        return 0

    def dup(self, fd):
        if fd not in self.table:
            return -EBADF
        new = self.lowest_free()
        self.table[new] = self.table[fd]
        return new

    def dup2(self, old, new):
        if old not in self.table:
            return -EBADF
        if old != new:
            self.table[new] = self.table[old]
        return new

    def targets(self):
        return dict(self.table)


def redirect(tokens):
    fds = FdTable()
    for token in tokens:
        if ">&" in token:
            left, right = token.split(">&")
            target = int(left) if left else 1
            if fds.dup2(int(right), target) < 0:
                return None
            continue
        arrow = ">" if ">" in token else "<"
        left, name = token.split(arrow, 1)
        target = int(left) if left else (1 if arrow == ">" else 0)
        fd = fds.open(name)
        if fd != target:
            fds.dup2(fd, target)
            fds.close(fd)
    return fds.targets()
--- hint
The lowest free number is found by counting up from 0 until a number is not in the table. After closing 0, the next `open` returns 0: that is how old shells redirected standard input.
--- hint
`dup2` onto an open descriptor simply replaces its target: closing it first and then setting it has the same effect in this model. Remember the `old == new` case.
--- hint
In `redirect`, split each token at `">&"`, `">"` or `"<"`; the part before is the number (empty means 1 for output, 0 for input). For a file, `fd = open(name)`, then `dup2(fd, N)` and `close(fd)` when `fd != N`.
--- check test | The lowest free number is reused after close
(lambda t: (t.open("a"), t.open("b"), t.close(0), t.open("c"), t.targets()) == (3, 4, 0, 0, {0: "c", 1: "tty", 2: "tty", 3: "a", 4: "b"}))(FdTable())
--- check test | close and dup of a descriptor that is not open give -EBADF
(lambda t: (t.close(7), t.dup(5), t.close(2), t.close(2)) == (-9, -9, 0, -9))(FdTable())
--- check test | dup shares the target at the lowest free number
(lambda t: (t.close(1), t.open("log"), t.dup(0), t.targets()) == (0, 1, 3, {0: "tty", 1: "log", 2: "tty", 3: "tty"}))(FdTable())
--- check test | dup2 replaces an open target, handles old == new and a bad old
(lambda t: (t.open("x"), t.dup2(3, 1), t.dup2(2, 2), t.dup2(9, 1), t.targets()) == (3, 1, 2, -9, {0: "tty", 1: "x", 2: "tty", 3: "x"}))(FdTable())
--- check test | dup2 onto a closed descriptor opens it
(lambda t: (t.open("x"), t.dup2(3, 6), t.targets()) == (3, 6, {0: "tty", 1: "tty", 2: "tty", 3: "x", 6: "x"}))(FdTable())
--- check case | > file then 2>&1: both go to the file
redirect([">out.txt", "2>&1"])
=> {0: "tty", 1: "out.txt", 2: "out.txt"}
--- check case | 2>&1 then > file: only standard output goes to the file
redirect(["2>&1", ">out.txt"])
=> {0: "tty", 1: "out.txt", 2: "tty"}
--- check case | Input, output and error each to their own file
redirect(["<cmds.txt", "2>err.log", "1>run.log"])
=> {0: "cmds.txt", 1: "run.log", 2: "err.log"}
--- check case | No tokens, and a copy from a descriptor that is not open
(redirect([]), redirect(["2>&5"]))
=> ({0: "tty", 1: "tty", 2: "tty"}, None)
--- check case | Swapping stdout and stderr through a spare descriptor
redirect([">a.log", "2>b.log", "3>&1", "1>&2", "2>&3"])
=> {0: "tty", 1: "b.log", 2: "a.log", 3: "a.log"}

=== os-02 | Processes: states, the PCB, fork, exec and wait
--- teach
Last lesson ended with the process: a running program with its own memory, its own open files and an id. This lesson looks inside the kernel's record of a process, follows a process through its life, and builds the three calls that create and end processes on Unix: `fork`, `exec` and `wait`. You will build a model of the kernel's process table that behaves like the real one.

### The process control block

For every process, the kernel keeps one record, the **process control block** (PCB). In everyday words: the process's file in the manager's cabinet. When the process is not running, the PCB is all there is of it, so it must hold everything needed to carry on later:

- the **pid**, and the pid of its **parent** (the process that created it);
- its **state** (below);
- its **saved registers**: the program counter, the stack pointer and the rest, as they were when it last stopped;
- where its **memory** is: a pointer to its memory map (lesson 11);
- its **file descriptor table** (last lesson);
- **scheduling** facts: its priority and how much CPU time it has used (lessons 3 and 4);
- its **exit status**, once it has finished.

Linux calls its PCB `struct task_struct`; it has hundreds of fields. The kernel keeps all of them in a **process table**, which is searchable by pid.

### The states of a process

A CPU core runs one process at a time. The others wait, and they wait for different reasons. The kernel tracks this with a **state** in each PCB:

- **new**: being created, not yet allowed to run;
- **ready**: could run right now, and is waiting for a CPU;
- **running**: on a CPU right now;
- **blocked** (or waiting): cannot run until something happens, such as a disk read finishing or a key being pressed;
- **terminated**: finished, but its record is still kept (more on this below).

Only certain moves between states make sense. Each has a cause:

| from | to | cause |
|---|---|---|
| new | ready | the kernel admits it |
| ready | running | the scheduler picks it (**dispatch**) |
| running | ready | the timer interrupt takes the CPU away (**preemption**) |
| running | blocked | it asks for something slow, like a read |
| blocked | ready | the thing it waited for happened |
| running | terminated | it calls `exit` (or is killed) |

Notice what is missing. A blocked process never goes straight to running: when its disk read finishes it becomes ready, and waits its turn. And only a running process can block or exit, because only a running process executes instructions. The [[state diagram|state-diagram]] draws the same table.

The invariant the scheduler keeps: **on each CPU core, exactly one process is running** (or the core's idle loop, when nothing is ready). Every ready process is in a queue, the **ready queue**; every blocked process is in the queue of the event it waits for.

### The context switch

When the kernel takes the CPU from process A and gives it to process B, it does a **context switch**:

1. A trap (a timer interrupt, or A's own system call) enters the kernel.
2. The kernel saves A's registers into A's PCB, and sets A's state to ready or blocked.
3. It picks B from the ready queue (the scheduler's job).
4. It loads B's registers from B's PCB, switches to B's memory map, and sets B's state to running.
5. It returns to user mode, and B carries on exactly where it stopped.

A switch takes a few microseconds of direct work. The larger cost is indirect: B's data is not in the CPU's caches yet, so B runs slowly for a while after it starts. That is why schedulers do not switch more often than they need to (lesson 3).

### fork: one process becomes two

On Unix, every process except the very first is made by copying an existing one. The system call **`fork()`** creates a new process, the **child**, which is a copy of the caller, the **parent**:

- the child's memory holds the same values as the parent's, but it is a **separate copy**: a later change in one is not seen by the other;
- the child's registers are the same, so it carries on from the same point: returning from `fork`;
- the child's file descriptor table is a copy, so it has the same files open;
- the child gets a new pid, and its parent's pid is the caller's.

Then comes the one difference. `fork` returns **twice**, once in each process, with different values: in the parent it returns the child's pid, and in the child it returns 0. (It returns −1 in the parent, and makes no child, if it fails.) The kernel does this simply by putting a different number in each copy's return register.

```cpp fragment
#include <cstdio>
#include <sys/wait.h>
#include <unistd.h>

int main() {
    pid_t pid = fork();
    if (pid == 0) {
        std::printf("child: my pid is %d\n", getpid());
    } else {
        std::printf("parent: I made %d\n", pid);
    }
}
```

Both lines are printed, in an order nobody can predict: after `fork`, the two processes are scheduled independently. (This lesson's C++ is a fragment: ORBIT runs C++ as WebAssembly, which has no processes, so you will model them in Python instead.)

Python offers the same calls on Linux and macOS as `os.fork()`, `os.execvp()` and `os.waitpid()`. They are not available in ORBIT's browser Python.

### Why forking is cheap: copy-on-write

Copying all of a big process's memory on every `fork` would be slow, and usually wasted, since the child often replaces its memory at once (next section). So real kernels use **[[copy-on-write|cow]]**: after `fork`, parent and child share the same physical memory, marked read-only. Only when one of them writes to a page does the kernel copy that one page. With copy-on-write, `fork` costs time proportional to the size of the memory map (a few microseconds to a millisecond), not to the memory itself. Your model will simply copy.

### Counting processes: a proof by induction

What does this print?

```cpp fragment
fork();
fork();
std::printf("hi\n");
```

The first `fork` makes two processes. **Both** carry on to the second `fork`, so each makes a child: four processes, and each prints `hi`. The claim in general: **if every process runs a straight line of k calls to `fork`, then 2ᵏ processes finish the line.**

- **Base case:** with k = 0 there is 1 = 2⁰ process.
- **Step:** suppose 2ᵏ processes reach the (k + 1)th `fork`. Each one creates exactly one child, and both parent and child carry on after it. So 2 · 2ᵏ = 2ᵏ⁺¹ processes continue.

This is why an accidental `fork` in a loop, a **[[fork bomb|fork-bomb]]**, can fill the process table in seconds. It is also why forking code is written with `if (pid == 0)` to send the child down a different path.

### exec: replace the program

`fork` gives you a copy of the **same** program. To run a **different** one, the process calls **`exec`** (in C, one of `execve`, `execvp` and friends). `exec` throws away the process's memory, loads a new program into it, and starts it from the top.

- The pid stays the same. So do the parent, and the open file descriptors.
- `exec` **does not return** when it works: the code that called it no longer exists. If `exec` returns at all, it failed (for example, the program was not found).

A shell runs the command `ls -l` with both calls:

```cpp fragment
pid_t pid = fork();
if (pid == 0) {
    char* argv[] = {(char*)"ls", (char*)"-l", nullptr};
    execvp("ls", argv);
    _exit(127);                        // only reached if exec failed
}
int status;
waitpid(pid, &status, 0);              // wait for ls to finish
```

Why two calls, not one "run this program" call? Because the child can change things **between** `fork` and `exec`: it can `dup2` a file onto descriptor 1 to redirect output, change directory, or drop privileges. The new program inherits all of it. That is exactly how `ls > out.txt` works.

### wait, zombies and orphans

When a process calls `exit(status)`, the kernel frees its memory and closes its files straight away. But it cannot delete the PCB yet, because the parent may want to know how the child ended. So the child becomes a **zombie**: terminated, holding only its pid and exit status, until the parent collects it.

The parent collects a child with **`wait`** or **`waitpid`**. It returns the pid of a child that has exited, and its status, and then the kernel deletes that child's PCB. This is called **reaping** the child. If no child has exited yet, `wait` blocks until one does. With the option `WNOHANG` ("no hang") it returns 0 at once instead of blocking. If the caller has no children at all, it fails at once with `ECHILD`.

What if the parent exits first? Its children become **orphans**. The kernel gives them a new parent: process 1, called **init** (on most Linux systems today it is `systemd`). Init calls `wait` in a loop forever, so orphans are always reaped in the end.

A parent that never calls `wait` fills the process table with zombies. Each takes only a PCB, but pids run out: Linux's classic default limit is 32,768 (many systems raise it).

### What it costs

- **Finding a process by pid:** the process table is a hash table or tree keyed by pid, so O(1) or O(log n).
- **`fork`:** with copy-on-write, proportional to the size of the memory map. Your model copies memory, so it is O(size of memory).
- **`exec`:** proportional to what is loaded now. Real kernels load pages lazily, only as the program touches them.
- **Exit and reparenting:** the exiting process's children must be handed to init, so exit is O(number of children).

### Where it is used

- **Every shell** runs each command with fork, then exec, then wait.
- **Servers** such as the Apache web server (in its classic mode) and the PostgreSQL database fork a process per client, so one crashed handler cannot damage the others.
- **Android** starts every app by forking one warm process, called zygote, that already has the common libraries loaded. Copy-on-write makes that fast.
- **Chrome** runs each site in its own process, so a crashed or hacked tab cannot read another tab's memory.

**Watch out:**

- **Forgetting that fork copies unflushed output.** `printf("hello")` with no newline leaves the text in the stdio buffer, which is in memory. `fork` copies it, so both processes print `hello` when they exit. Flush with `fflush(stdout)` before forking.
- **Forgetting to wait.** Every child needs reaping, or the zombies pile up.
- **Code after `exec`.** It runs only if `exec` failed. Always end the child's branch there with `_exit`, or the child carries on running the parent's code.

The arrows are the only moves the kernel allows. Every arrow into "running" comes from "ready", and every arrow out of "blocked" goes to "ready".
