:::

::: context cow Copy only what changes
Copy-on-write is lazy copying. Picture two students handed one shared set of notes, with a rule: the moment either wants to scribble on a page, they photocopy that page first and scribble on their copy. Pages nobody changes are never copied. After `fork`, the kernel marks every page of both processes read-only. A write traps into the kernel (a page fault, lesson 11), which copies that one page, gives the writer the copy with write permission, and returns to the same instruction, which now succeeds.
:::

::: context fork-bomb When a loop forks without end
A fork bomb is a program that forks in an endless loop: every child forks too, so the count doubles again and again. The famous one-line version for the bash shell is `:(){ :|:& };:`, a function that calls itself twice in the background. Within seconds the process table is full and nothing new can start, not even the command to stop it. Systems defend with limits on processes per user (`ulimit -u`) and, in containers, with cgroup limits (lesson 16).
:::
--- task
Build a model of the kernel's process table. The starter gives you the `PCB` class: `pid`, `ppid` (parent's pid), `program`, `memory` (a dict), `regs` (a dict of registers), `fds` (a list), `state` (`"alive"` or `"zombie"`) and `status` (exit status, or `None`). Leave it as it is.

Write the class `ProcessTable`. It keeps `procs`, a dict from pid to `PCB`.

- `ProcessTable()` holds one process: pid 1, parent 0, program `"init"`, empty memory, registers `{"a0": 0}`, fds `["tty", "tty", "tty"]`, alive.
- `fork(pid)`: if `pid` is not an alive process, return −1. Otherwise make a child with the next unused pid (2, then 3, …; pids are never reused), parent `pid`, the same program, and **copies** of the parent's memory, registers and fds, so that later changes to one never show in the other. Set the child's `regs["a0"]` to 0 and the parent's `regs["a0"]` to the child's pid. Return the child's pid.
- `exec(pid, program)`: if `pid` is not alive, return −1. Otherwise set the program, empty the memory, set the registers to `{"a0": 0}`, and return 0. The pid, parent and fds stay.
- `exit(pid, status)`: if `pid` is not alive, or is 1, return −1. Otherwise it becomes a zombie, its `status` is `status % 256` (so `exit(256)` looks like 0 and `exit(-1)` like 255), its memory and fds are emptied, and every child it has (alive or zombie) gets parent 1. Return 0.
- `wait(pid)`: works like `waitpid(-1, &status, WNOHANG)`. If `pid` is not alive or has no children, return `(-1, None)`. If some of its children are zombies, reap the one that **exited earliest**: delete it from `procs` and return `(child_pid, status)`. Otherwise return `(0, None)`.
- `ps()`: a list of `(pid, ppid, state, program)` for every process, sorted by pid.
--- starter
class PCB:
    def __init__(self, pid, ppid, program, memory, regs, fds):
        self.pid = pid
        self.ppid = ppid
        self.program = program
        self.memory = memory
        self.regs = regs
        self.fds = fds
        self.state = "alive"
        self.status = None


class ProcessTable:
    def __init__(self):
        self.procs = {1: PCB(1, 0, "init", {}, {"a0": 0}, ["tty", "tty", "tty"])}

    def fork(self, pid):
        return -1

    def exec(self, pid, program):
        return -1

    def exit(self, pid, status):
        return -1

    def wait(self, pid):
        return (-1, None)

    def ps(self):
        return []
--- solution
class PCB:
    def __init__(self, pid, ppid, program, memory, regs, fds):
        self.pid = pid
        self.ppid = ppid
        self.program = program
        self.memory = memory
        self.regs = regs
        self.fds = fds
        self.state = "alive"
        self.status = None


class ProcessTable:
    def __init__(self):
        self.procs = {1: PCB(1, 0, "init", {}, {"a0": 0}, ["tty", "tty", "tty"])}
        self.next_pid = 2
        self.exits = 0
        self.exit_order = {}

    def alive(self, pid):
        return pid in self.procs and self.procs[pid].state == "alive"

    def children(self, pid):
        return [p for p in self.procs.values() if p.ppid == pid]

    def fork(self, pid):
        if not self.alive(pid):
            return -1
        parent = self.procs[pid]
        child_pid = self.next_pid
        self.next_pid += 1
        child = PCB(child_pid, pid, parent.program, dict(parent.memory), dict(parent.regs), list(parent.fds))
        child.regs["a0"] = 0
        parent.regs["a0"] = child_pid
        self.procs[child_pid] = child
        return child_pid

    def exec(self, pid, program):
        if not self.alive(pid):
            return -1
        proc = self.procs[pid]
        proc.program = program
        proc.memory = {}
        proc.regs = {"a0": 0}
        return 0

    def exit(self, pid, status):
        if pid == 1 or not self.alive(pid):
            return -1
        proc = self.procs[pid]
        proc.state = "zombie"
        proc.status = status % 256
        proc.memory = {}
        proc.fds = []
        self.exits += 1
        self.exit_order[pid] = self.exits
        for child in self.children(pid):
            child.ppid = 1
        return 0

    def wait(self, pid):
        if not self.alive(pid):
            return (-1, None)
        kids = self.children(pid)
        if not kids:
            return (-1, None)
        zombies = [k for k in kids if k.state == "zombie"]
        if not zombies:
            return (0, None)
        first = min(zombies, key=lambda k: self.exit_order[k.pid])
        del self.procs[first.pid]
        return (first.pid, first.status)

    def ps(self):
        return [(p.pid, p.ppid, p.state, p.program) for p in sorted(self.procs.values(), key=lambda p: p.pid)]
--- hint
Keep a counter for the next pid, starting at 2. For the copies use `dict(...)` and `list(...)`: plain assignment would make parent and child share one object.
--- hint
To reap "the zombie that exited earliest", remember the order of exits: for example a counter that goes up on every `exit`, stored per pid. Reparenting is a loop over the processes whose `ppid` is the one exiting.
--- hint
In `wait`, handle the cases in order: not alive, then no children at all (`(-1, None)`), then no zombie children (`(0, None)`), then reap. `ps` sorts the PCBs by pid and builds the tuples.
--- check case | A new table holds only init
ProcessTable().ps()
=> [(1, 0, "alive", "init")]
--- check test | fork returns the new pid, and the child is a copy
(lambda t: (t.fork(1), t.fork(1), t.ps()) == (2, 3, [(1, 0, "alive", "init"), (2, 1, "alive", "init"), (3, 1, "alive", "init")]))(ProcessTable())
--- check test | fork returns 0 in the child and the child's pid in the parent
(lambda t: (t.fork(1), t.procs[1].regs["a0"], t.procs[2].regs["a0"]) == (2, 2, 0))(ProcessTable())
--- check test | Memory is copied: later writes do not leak either way
(lambda t: (t.procs[1].memory.update(x=1), t.fork(1), t.procs[2].memory.update(x=99), t.procs[1].memory.update(y=5), t.procs[1].memory, t.procs[2].memory)[4:] == ({"x": 1, "y": 5}, {"x": 99}))(ProcessTable())
?? Give the child dict(parent.memory), a new dict, not the parent's own dict.
--- check test | Registers and fds are copies too
(lambda t: (t.procs[1].regs.update(sp=400), t.fork(1), t.procs[2].fds.append("log.txt"), t.procs[2].regs.update(sp=8), t.procs[1].fds, t.procs[1].regs["sp"], t.procs[2].regs["sp"])[4:] == (["tty", "tty", "tty"], 400, 8))(ProcessTable())
--- check test | A child forks a grandchild; forking a missing pid fails
(lambda t: (t.fork(1), t.fork(2), t.fork(9), t.ps()) == (2, 3, -1, [(1, 0, "alive", "init"), (2, 1, "alive", "init"), (3, 2, "alive", "init")]))(ProcessTable())
--- check test | exec replaces program, memory and registers, keeps pid, parent and fds
(lambda t: (t.fork(1), t.procs[2].memory.update(k=1), t.procs[2].fds.append("out.txt"), t.exec(2, "ls"), t.procs[2].program, t.procs[2].memory, t.procs[2].regs, t.procs[2].ppid, t.procs[2].fds)[3:] == (0, "ls", {}, {"a0": 0}, 1, ["tty", "tty", "tty", "out.txt"]))(ProcessTable())
--- check test | exit makes a zombie with its status; wait reaps it
(lambda t: (t.fork(1), t.exit(2, 3), t.ps(), t.wait(1), t.ps()) == (2, 0, [(1, 0, "alive", "init"), (2, 1, "zombie", "init")], (2, 3), [(1, 0, "alive", "init")]))(ProcessTable())
--- check test | wait with live children only returns (0, None); with none, (-1, None)
(lambda t: (t.wait(1), t.fork(1), t.wait(1), t.wait(2)) == ((-1, None), 2, (0, None), (-1, None)))(ProcessTable())
--- check test | The zombie that exited first is reaped first, whatever its pid
(lambda t: (t.fork(1), t.fork(1), t.fork(1), t.exit(4, 40), t.exit(2, 20), t.wait(1), t.wait(1), t.wait(1)) == (2, 3, 4, 0, 0, (4, 40), (2, 20), (0, None)))(ProcessTable())
--- check test | Exit statuses are taken mod 256
(lambda t: ([t.fork(1) for _ in range(3)], t.exit(2, 256), t.exit(3, -1), t.exit(4, 300), [t.wait(1) for _ in range(3)])[4] == [(2, 0), (3, 255), (4, 44)])(ProcessTable())
--- check test | Orphans, alive or zombie, are adopted by init and can be reaped by it
(lambda t: (t.fork(1), t.fork(2), t.fork(2), t.exit(3, 7), t.exit(2, 1), t.ps(), t.wait(1), t.wait(1), t.wait(1), t.ps())[5:] == ([(1, 0, "alive", "init"), (2, 1, "zombie", "init"), (3, 1, "zombie", "init"), (4, 1, "alive", "init")], (3, 7), (2, 1), (0, None), [(1, 0, "alive", "init"), (4, 1, "alive", "init")]))(ProcessTable())
--- check test | init cannot exit; a zombie cannot exit, fork, exec or wait
(lambda t: (t.exit(1, 0), t.fork(1), t.exit(2, 0), t.exit(2, 0), t.fork(2), t.exec(2, "sh"), t.wait(2)) == (-1, 2, 0, -1, -1, -1, (-1, None)))(ProcessTable())
--- check test | Exit empties memory and fds; pids are never reused after reaping
(lambda t: (t.fork(1), t.procs[2].memory.update(a=1), t.exit(2, 0), t.procs[2].memory, t.procs[2].fds, t.wait(1), t.fork(1))[3:] == ({}, [], (2, 0), 3))(ProcessTable())
--- check test | A shell pattern: fork, exec in the child, exit, wait
(lambda t: (t.procs[1].memory.update(cmd="ls"), t.fork(1), t.exec(2, t.procs[2].memory["cmd"]), t.ps()[1], t.exit(2, 0), t.wait(1))[1:] == (2, 0, (2, 1, "alive", "ls"), 0, (2, 0)))(ProcessTable())

+++ question | How many lines?
--- ask
How many lines of `x` does this program print, if its output goes to a terminal and nothing fails?

```cpp fragment
for (int i = 0; i < 3; i++) {
    fork();
    std::printf("x\n");
}
```
--- answer
14
--- why
Before the loop there is 1 process. In each pass every process forks, then every process prints. So pass 1 has 2 processes printing, pass 2 has 4, and pass 3 has 8: 2 + 4 + 8 = 14 lines. At the end there are 2³ = 8 processes. The newline matters: a terminal is line-buffered, so each `x` is written out before the next `fork` copies the buffer.

+++ question | Hello, twice
--- ask
This program's output goes to a terminal, and it prints `hello` twice. Why?

```cpp fragment
std::printf("hello");
fork();
return 0;
```
--- choice
`fork` runs the program again from the top in the child.
--- choice correct
The text was still in `printf`'s buffer, in the process's memory, when `fork` copied that memory. Each process flushes its own copy when it exits.
--- choice
The terminal prints every line once per running process.
--- choice
`printf` is called again when the child returns from `fork`.
--- why
With no newline, a line-buffered stream keeps `hello` in memory. The child is an exact copy of the parent, buffer included, so both processes write `hello` when they exit. The child does not rerun anything before the `fork`. The fix is `fflush(stdout)` before forking.

+++ question | After exec
--- ask
In a child process, the line right after `execvp("sensorctl", argv);` runs. What does that tell you?
--- choice
The new program `sensorctl` has finished running.
--- choice
The new program is running alongside the old code.
--- choice correct
`execvp` failed (for example, `sensorctl` was not found), because a successful `exec` never returns.
--- why
A successful `exec` replaces the process's whole memory with the new program, so the calling code no longer exists and there is nothing to return to. Returning at all means failure, with `errno` saying why. The child should then report the error and call `_exit`, rather than carry on as a copy of its parent.

+++ practice | Checking a process's life story
--- task
A process moves through the five states `"new"`, `"ready"`, `"running"`, `"blocked"` and `"terminated"`. These events are the only legal moves:

- `"admit"`: new to ready
- `"dispatch"`: ready to running
- `"preempt"`: running to ready
- `"block"`: running to blocked
- `"wake"`: blocked to ready
- `"exit"`: running to terminated

Write `replay(events)`. The process starts in `"new"`. Apply the events in order and return the final state. If an event is not legal in the current state (including any event after `"terminated"`, and any unknown event name), stop and return `("illegal", i, state)`: the event's position and the state it was tried in.

For example, `replay(["admit", "dispatch", "block", "wake"])` is `"ready"`, and `replay(["admit", "dispatch", "block", "dispatch"])` is `("illegal", 3, "blocked")`.
--- starter
def replay(events):
    return "new"
--- solution
MOVES = {
    ("new", "admit"): "ready",
    ("ready", "dispatch"): "running",
    ("running", "preempt"): "ready",
    ("running", "block"): "blocked",
    ("blocked", "wake"): "ready",
    ("running", "exit"): "terminated",
}


def replay(events):
    state = "new"
    for i, event in enumerate(events):
        nxt = MOVES.get((state, event))
        if nxt is None:
            return ("illegal", i, state)
        state = nxt
    return state
--- hint
Write the six moves as a dict keyed by the pair `(state, event)`. A pair that is not in the dict is an illegal move.
--- hint
Walk the events with `enumerate`, so you have the position ready for the error tuple.
--- check case | The examples from the task
(replay(["admit", "dispatch", "block", "wake"]), replay(["admit", "dispatch", "block", "dispatch"]))
=> ("ready", ("illegal", 3, "blocked"))
--- check case | No events: still new
replay([])
=> "new"
--- check case | A full life with preemptions
replay(["admit", "dispatch", "preempt", "dispatch", "block", "wake", "dispatch", "exit"])
=> "terminated"
--- check case | Nothing happens after terminated
replay(["admit", "dispatch", "exit", "admit"])
=> ("illegal", 3, "terminated")
--- check case | A ready process cannot block or exit
(replay(["admit", "block"]), replay(["admit", "exit"]))
=> (("illegal", 1, "ready"), ("illegal", 1, "ready"))
--- check case | Dispatch before admit, and an unknown event
(replay(["dispatch"]), replay(["admit", "sleep"]))
=> (("illegal", 0, "new"), ("illegal", 1, "ready"))

+++ practice | How many processes, how many prints?
--- task
Model a program that forks. A program is a list of operations, and every process runs the operations after its own position, one by one:

- `"fork"`: the process makes a child. Both carry on with the next operation. In the child, the fork "returned 0"; in the parent it "returned a pid".
- `"print TEXT"`: the process prints `TEXT` (everything after the first space).
- `"exit"`: the process stops.
- `"child OP"`: do the operation `OP` only if this process's most recent `fork` returned 0 (it is the child of that fork).
- `"parent OP"`: do `OP` only if this process's most recent `fork` returned a pid.

The first process has not forked, so neither `child` nor `parent` operations run in it until it forks. A new child's most recent fork is the one that created it, so it starts as a child; if it forks later, it is the parent of that fork.

Write `run_forks(program)` that returns `(processes, printed)`: the total number of processes that ever existed (including the first), and a dict from each printed text to how many times it was printed. (The order of printing is not defined, which is why you return counts.)

For example, `run_forks(["fork", "fork", "print hi"])` is `(4, {"hi": 4})`, and `run_forks(["fork", "child exit", "fork", "print x"])` is `(3, {"x": 2})`.
--- starter
def run_forks(program):
    return (1, {})
--- solution
def run_forks(program):
    printed = {}
    processes = 0

    def run(pc, last):
        nonlocal processes
        processes += 1
        while pc < len(program):
            op = program[pc]
            pc += 1
            if op.startswith("child "):
                if last != "child":
                    continue
                op = op[len("child "):]
            elif op.startswith("parent "):
                if last != "parent":
                    continue
                op = op[len("parent "):]
            if op == "fork":
                run(pc, "child")
                last = "parent"
            elif op == "exit":
                return
            elif op.startswith("print "):
                text = op[len("print "):]
                printed[text] = printed.get(text, 0) + 1

    run(0, None)
    return (processes, printed)
--- hint
Write a helper `run(pc, last)` that plays one process from position `pc`, where `last` records what its most recent fork returned (`None` at the start). Each call counts one process.
--- hint
On `"fork"`, call `run(pc, "child")` for the child (with `pc` already moved past the fork), then carry on in the parent with `last` set to `"parent"`.
--- hint
For `"child OP"` and `"parent OP"`, either skip the operation or strip the prefix and handle `OP` like any other operation.
--- check case | The examples from the task
(run_forks(["fork", "fork", "print hi"]), run_forks(["fork", "child exit", "fork", "print x"]))
=> ((4, {"hi": 4}), (3, {"x": 2}))
--- check case | Printing between forks
run_forks(["print a", "fork", "print b", "fork", "print c"])
=> (4, {"a": 1, "b": 2, "c": 4})
--- check case | No forks, and an empty program
(run_forks(["print only"]), run_forks([]))
=> ((1, {"only": 1}), (1, {}))
--- check case | Only the parent keeps forking: a chain, not a tree
run_forks(["fork", "child exit", "fork", "child exit", "fork", "child exit", "print left"])
=> (4, {"left": 1})
--- check case | Only children keep forking
run_forks(["fork", "parent exit", "fork", "parent exit", "print leaf"])
=> (3, {"leaf": 1})
--- check case | child and parent have no effect before any fork
run_forks(["child print c", "parent print p", "fork", "child print c", "parent print p"])
=> (2, {"c": 1, "p": 1})
--- check case | Ten forks in a row make 1,024 processes
run_forks(["fork"] * 10 + ["print z"])
=> (1024, {"z": 1024})
--- check case | Each process follows its own most recent fork
run_forks(["fork", "fork", "parent print P", "child print C"])
=> (4, {"P": 2, "C": 2})

+++ practice | Taking down a process tree
--- task
A process table is given as a list of `(pid, ppid)` rows.

Write two functions:

- `kill_order(rows, root)`: the pids of `root` and all its descendants, in the order to kill them so that no process is killed before its children (a child's whole subtree comes before its parent). Visit children in increasing pid order. If `root` is not in the table, return `[]`.
- `tree_lines(rows, root)`: the subtree drawn as lines, parent before children, children in increasing pid order, each line the pid indented by two spaces per level below `root`. If `root` is not in the table, return `[]`.

For example, with rows `[(1, 0), (2, 1), (5, 1), (3, 2), (4, 2)]`, `kill_order(rows, 1)` is `[3, 4, 2, 5, 1]` and `tree_lines(rows, 2)` is `["2", "  3", "  4"]`.
--- starter
def kill_order(rows, root):
    return [pid for pid, ppid in rows]


def tree_lines(rows, root):
    return []
--- solution
def children_of(rows):
    kids = {}
    for pid, ppid in rows:
        kids.setdefault(ppid, []).append(pid)
    for pids in kids.values():
        pids.sort()
    return kids


def kill_order(rows, root):
    if root not in {pid for pid, ppid in rows}:
        return []
    kids = children_of(rows)
    order = []

    def visit(pid):
        for child in kids.get(pid, []):
            visit(child)
        order.append(pid)

    visit(root)
    return order


def tree_lines(rows, root):
    if root not in {pid for pid, ppid in rows}:
        return []
    kids = children_of(rows)
    lines = []

    def visit(pid, depth):
        lines.append("  " * depth + str(pid))
        for child in kids.get(pid, []):
            visit(child, depth + 1)

    visit(root, 0)
    return lines
--- hint
First build a dict from each pid to the sorted list of its children. Both functions are then depth-first searches from `root`.
--- hint
`kill_order` records a pid after visiting all its children (post-order). `tree_lines` records a pid before its children (pre-order), with the depth passed down the recursion.
--- check case | The examples from the task
(kill_order([(1, 0), (2, 1), (5, 1), (3, 2), (4, 2)], 1), tree_lines([(1, 0), (2, 1), (5, 1), (3, 2), (4, 2)], 2))
=> ([3, 4, 2, 5, 1], ["2", "  3", "  4"])
--- check case | The whole tree drawn
tree_lines([(1, 0), (7, 1), (3, 1), (9, 3), (4, 7)], 1)
=> ["1", "  3", "    9", "  7", "    4"]
--- check case | A leaf alone
(kill_order([(1, 0), (2, 1)], 2), tree_lines([(1, 0), (2, 1)], 2))
=> ([2], ["2"])
--- check case | A root that is not in the table
(kill_order([(1, 0)], 42), tree_lines([(1, 0)], 42))
=> ([], [])
--- check case | Rows in any order, children sorted by pid
kill_order([(12, 10), (11, 10), (10, 1), (1, 0), (13, 11)], 10)
=> [13, 11, 12, 10]
--- check test | A chain 500 deep
(lambda rows: kill_order(rows, 1)[:3] == [500, 499, 498] and tree_lines(rows, 1)[-1] == "  " * 499 + "500")([(1, 0)] + [(i, i - 1) for i in range(2, 501)])

+++ practice | What the parent sees when a child ends
--- task
`waitpid` gives the parent one integer, the **wait status**, that says how a child ended. Linux packs it like this:

- if the child called `exit(n)`, the status is `(n % 256) * 256`: the exit code sits in the second-lowest byte, and the lowest 7 bits are 0;
- if a signal killed it, the lowest 7 bits hold the signal number (1 to 64), and bit 7 (the value 128) is set if a core dump was written.

Write three functions:

- `encode(kind, value, core=False)`: `kind` is `"exited"` (then `value` is the argument given to `exit`, any integer) or `"signaled"` (then `value` is the signal number). Return the status.
- `decode(status)`: return `("exited", code)` when the lowest 7 bits are 0, otherwise `("signaled", signal)`. The core-dump bit never changes the signal number.
- `shell_code(status)`: the number the shell shows as `$?`: the exit code, or 128 plus the signal number.

For example, `encode("exited", 3)` is `768`, and `shell_code(encode("signaled", 9))` is `137`.
--- starter
def encode(kind, value, core=False):
    return value


def decode(status):
    return ("exited", status)


def shell_code(status):
    return status
--- solution
def encode(kind, value, core=False):
    if kind == "exited":
        return (value % 256) << 8
    return value | (0x80 if core else 0)


def decode(status):
    if status & 0x7F == 0:
        return ("exited", (status >> 8) & 0xFF)
    return ("signaled", status & 0x7F)


def shell_code(status):
    kind, value = decode(status)
    if kind == "exited":
        return value
    return 128 + value
--- hint
Multiplying by 256 is shifting left by 8, and `n % 256` keeps the lowest byte (in Python it is never negative, so `exit(-1)` becomes 255). Decode with `(status >> 8) & 0xFF`.
--- hint
The lowest 7 bits are `status & 0x7F`; the core-dump bit is `0x80`, which that mask leaves out.
--- check case | The examples from the task
(encode("exited", 3), shell_code(encode("signaled", 9)))
=> (768, 137)
--- check case | exit(0) is the status 0
(encode("exited", 0), decode(0), shell_code(0))
=> (0, ("exited", 0), 0)
--- check case | exit(256) looks like success, exit(-1) like 255
(decode(encode("exited", 256)), decode(encode("exited", -1)), decode(encode("exited", 300)))
=> (("exited", 0), ("exited", 255), ("exited", 44))
--- check case | A segfault with a core dump
(encode("signaled", 11, core=True), decode(encode("signaled", 11, core=True)), shell_code(139))
=> (139, ("signaled", 11), 139)
--- check case | Signals without a core dump
[decode(encode("signaled", s)) for s in (2, 15, 64)]
=> [("signaled", 2), ("signaled", 15), ("signaled", 64)]
--- check case | Every exit code survives the round trip
all(decode(encode("exited", n)) == ("exited", n) and shell_code(encode("exited", n)) == n for n in range(256))
=> True

+++ practice | Debug: the child that writes into its parent
--- task
`fork(procs, pid, new_pid)` should add a child process to `procs` (a dict from pid to a dict with keys `"ppid"`, `"memory"` and `"fds"`), and return `new_pid`. The child's memory (a dict) and fds (a list) must be **copies** of the parent's: after the fork, a change made by one process must never be seen by the other.

It has a bug. When the child stores a value in its memory, the parent's memory changes too. Find it and fix it.
--- starter
def fork(procs, pid, new_pid):
    parent = procs[pid]
    procs[new_pid] = {
        "ppid": pid,
        "memory": parent["memory"],
        "fds": list(parent["fds"]),
    }
    return new_pid
--- solution
def fork(procs, pid, new_pid):
    parent = procs[pid]
    procs[new_pid] = {
        "ppid": pid,
        "memory": dict(parent["memory"]),
        "fds": list(parent["fds"]),
    }
    return new_pid
--- hint
Assigning a dict to a new name, or a new key, does not copy it: both names then refer to the one dict. Which of the two values is copied, and which is shared?
--- hint
Make the child's memory `dict(parent["memory"])`, a new dict with the same items.
--- check test | The child's writes stay in the child
(lambda procs: (fork(procs, 1, 2), procs[2]["memory"].update(v=9), procs[1]["memory"])[2] == {"v": 1})({1: {"ppid": 0, "memory": {"v": 1}, "fds": ["tty"]}})
--- check test | The parent's writes stay in the parent
(lambda procs: (fork(procs, 1, 2), procs[1]["memory"].update(w=3), procs[2]["memory"])[2] == {"v": 1})({1: {"ppid": 0, "memory": {"v": 1}, "fds": ["tty"]}})
--- check test | The child starts with the same values and the right parent
(lambda procs: (fork(procs, 1, 5), procs[5])[1] == {"ppid": 1, "memory": {"a": 1, "b": 2}, "fds": ["tty", "log"]})({1: {"ppid": 0, "memory": {"a": 1, "b": 2}, "fds": ["tty", "log"]}})
--- check test | fds stay separate too
(lambda procs: (fork(procs, 1, 2), procs[2]["fds"].append("x"), procs[1]["fds"])[2] == ["tty"])({1: {"ppid": 0, "memory": {}, "fds": ["tty"]}})
--- check test | A grandchild is independent of both
(lambda procs: (fork(procs, 1, 2), fork(procs, 2, 3), procs[3]["memory"].update(n=3), procs[2]["memory"].update(n=2), [procs[p]["memory"]["n"] for p in (1, 2, 3)])[4] == [1, 2, 3])({1: {"ppid": 0, "memory": {"n": 1}, "fds": []}})

+++ practice | Counting zombie time from a kernel log
--- task
A kernel log records process events. Each event is a tuple:

- `(t, "fork", parent, child)`: at time `t`, `parent` made `child`.
- `(t, "exit", pid)`: at time `t`, `pid` exited and became a zombie.
- `(t, "wait", parent, child)`: at time `t`, `parent` reaped its zombie child `child`.

Process 1 (init) exists from the start and never exits. The events are in time order and always valid. When a process exits, every child it has (alive or zombie) is adopted by init. Init reaps at once: when a process whose parent is init exits, it is reaped at that same time, and when init adopts a child that is already a zombie, it reaps it at that same time.

Write `zombie_report(events)` that returns `(times, leaked)`: `times` is a dict from every reaped pid to how long it was a zombie (reap time minus exit time), and `leaked` is the sorted list of pids that are still zombies at the end.

For example, for `[(0, "fork", 1, 2), (1, "fork", 2, 3), (4, "exit", 3), (9, "wait", 2, 3), (10, "exit", 2)]` the answer is `({3: 5, 2: 0}, [])`: 3 waited 5 time units for its parent, and 2's parent is init.
--- starter
def zombie_report(events):
    return ({}, [])
--- solution
def zombie_report(events):
    parent = {1: 0}
    exited_at = {}
    times = {}

    def reap(pid, t):
        times[pid] = t - exited_at.pop(pid)

    for event in events:
        t, kind = event[0], event[1]
        if kind == "fork":
            parent[event[3]] = event[2]
        elif kind == "exit":
            pid = event[2]
            exited_at[pid] = t
            for child in [c for c, p in parent.items() if p == pid]:
                parent[child] = 1
                if child in exited_at:
                    reap(child, t)
            if parent[pid] == 1:
                reap(pid, t)
        elif kind == "wait":
            reap(event[3], t)
    return (times, sorted(exited_at))
--- hint
Keep a dict from pid to parent, and a dict from each current zombie to the time it exited. Reaping a pid removes it from the zombie dict and records the time difference.
--- hint
On an exit: record the exit time, then hand every child to init, reaping at once the ones that are already zombies. Finally, if the exiting process's own parent is init, reap it at once too.
--- check case | The example from the task
zombie_report([(0, "fork", 1, 2), (1, "fork", 2, 3), (4, "exit", 3), (9, "wait", 2, 3), (10, "exit", 2)])
=> ({3: 5, 2: 0}, [])
--- check case | A parent that never waits leaks a zombie
zombie_report([(0, "fork", 1, 2), (1, "fork", 2, 3), (2, "exit", 3)])
=> ({}, [3])
--- check case | The parent exits: init adopts and reaps the zombie child
zombie_report([(0, "fork", 1, 2), (1, "fork", 2, 3), (2, "exit", 3), (7, "exit", 2)])
=> ({3: 5, 2: 0}, [])
--- check case | An orphan that is still alive is reaped at once when it exits later
zombie_report([(0, "fork", 1, 2), (1, "fork", 2, 3), (3, "exit", 2), (8, "exit", 3)])
=> ({2: 0, 3: 0}, [])
--- check case | No events
zombie_report([])
=> ({}, [])
--- check case | Several leaks, sorted, and one reaped late
zombie_report([(0, "fork", 1, 2), (1, "fork", 2, 9), (1, "fork", 2, 4), (1, "fork", 2, 6), (2, "exit", 9), (3, "exit", 4), (4, "exit", 6), (20, "wait", 2, 4)])
=> ({4: 17}, [6, 9])
--- check case | Grandchildren are adopted by init, not by the grandparent
zombie_report([(0, "fork", 1, 2), (1, "fork", 2, 3), (2, "fork", 3, 4), (3, "exit", 4), (5, "exit", 3)])
=> ({4: 2}, [3])

=== os-03 | Scheduling I: FCFS, SJF and round robin
--- teach
Last lesson, processes waited in the ready queue while one of them ran, and the timer let the kernel take the CPU back. Someone has to decide which ready process runs next. That part of the kernel is the **scheduler**, and the rule it follows is its **scheduling policy**. This lesson builds the three classic policies as simulators, and measures them the way operating-systems research does.

### Jobs, bursts and the Gantt chart

A process alternates between using the CPU and waiting for I/O. One stretch of CPU use is a **CPU burst**. To study policies, we simplify: each **job** is one burst, with a name, an **arrival time** (when it becomes ready) and a **burst length** (how much CPU time it needs). Times are whole numbers of milliseconds.

A schedule is drawn as a **Gantt chart**: a timeline of which job had the CPU when. As data, it is a list of segments `(name, start, end)`:

```text
jobs: A arrives 0, needs 5    B arrives 1, needs 3

 A   A   A   A   A   B   B   B
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
0                   5           8        gantt = [("A", 0, 5), ("B", 5, 8)]
```

### Measuring a schedule

For each job, with arrival A and burst B:

- **completion time** C: when its last segment ends;
- **turnaround time** = C − A: how long from arriving to finishing;
- **waiting time** = turnaround − B: how long it sat in the ready queue, ready but not running;
- **response time**: the start of its first segment, minus A. How long until it first got the CPU. For an interactive program this is what a person feels.

Above, B has completion 8, turnaround 8 − 1 = 7, waiting 7 − 3 = 4 and response 5 − 1 = 4. For a job that runs in one piece, waiting and response are equal. Once a job can be interrupted and resumed, they differ. The system-wide numbers are the averages over all jobs, plus **throughput**, the jobs finished per second, and **utilisation**, the fraction of time the CPU is busy.

Computing a job's completion and first start from a Gantt chart is one pass over it. Later segments overwrite the completion, and only the first one sets the start:

```python
gantt = [("X", 0, 2), ("Y", 2, 6), ("X", 6, 7)]
first_start = {}
completion = {}
for name, start, end in gantt:
    first_start.setdefault(name, start)
    completion[name] = end
print(first_start, completion)    # {'X': 0, 'Y': 2} {'X': 7, 'Y': 6}
```

### First come, first served

**First come, first served** (FCFS) runs jobs in order of arrival, each to the end. It is **non-preemptive**: once a job has the CPU, it keeps it until it finishes. If the CPU is free and nothing has arrived, it sits **idle** until the next arrival.

Take three jobs that all arrive at time 0: a long one, L, needing 24 ms, and two short ones, S1 and S2, needing 3 each.

| order | waiting times | average waiting |
|---|---|---|
| L, S1, S2 | 0, 24, 27 | 17 |
| S1, S2, L | 0, 3, 6 | 3 |

Same jobs, same total work, and the average wait differs by a factor of almost six. When a long job arrives first, every short job queues behind it. This is the **convoy effect**, like a whole road of cars stuck behind one tractor.

### Shortest job first, and why it is optimal

**Shortest job first** (SJF): whenever the CPU becomes free, run the **arrived** job with the shortest burst. It is non-preemptive too.

The claim: **when all jobs arrive at time 0, SJF gives the smallest possible average waiting time.** Here is the proof, by an **exchange argument**: show that any other order can be improved by swapping, so it cannot be the best.

1. Run jobs with bursts b₁, b₂, …, bₙ in that order. Job k waits for all the jobs before it: b₁ + … + bₖ₋₁.
2. Now swap two neighbours, job i and job i + 1. Jobs before them wait exactly as long as before. Jobs after them also wait as long as before: the same set of jobs still runs ahead of them. Only the two swapped jobs change.
3. Before the swap, job i + 1 waited bᵢ longer than job i did (it waited for job i). After the swap, job i waits bᵢ₊₁ longer than job i + 1. So the total waiting time changes by bᵢ₊₁ − bᵢ.
4. If bᵢ > bᵢ₊₁, a longer job before a shorter one, that change is negative: the swap strictly lowers the total.
5. So any order with a longer job directly before a shorter one is not optimal. The only orders with no such pair are the sorted ones, shortest first, and every sorted order has the same total. An optimal order exists, since there are finitely many, so the sorted order is optimal.

With jobs arriving at different times, non-preemptive SJF is no longer optimal: a long job may start just before a short one arrives. The preemptive version, **shortest remaining time first** (SRTF), fixes that: when a job arrives whose burst is shorter than what the running job still needs, it takes the CPU. SRTF minimises the average waiting time for any arrival times, by an exchange argument on slices of time.

### Knowing the future

SJF has a catch: the OS does not know how long a burst will be. It **predicts** it from the past, using the fact that a process's bursts tend to be similar. The standard predictor is the **exponential average**: if tₙ is the length of the n-th burst and τₙ was the guess for it, the next guess is

τₙ₊₁ = α · tₙ + (1 − α) · τₙ,

where α (alpha) is a number between 0 and 1. With α = 1/2, each new guess is halfway between the last guess and the last real burst:

```python
guess = 10
for real in [6, 4, 6]:
    guess = 0.5 * real + 0.5 * guess
    print(guess)                  # 8.0, then 6.0, then 6.0
```

Unrolled, τₙ₊₁ = α·tₙ + α(1 − α)·tₙ₋₁ + α(1 − α)²·tₙ₋₂ + …: every past burst counts, and older ones count exponentially less. That is where the name comes from.

### Round robin

SJF is unfair to long jobs and needs predictions. FCFS makes short jobs wait. **Round robin** (RR) is the policy behind every time-sharing system. It gives each job a fixed slice of time, the **quantum** q (often 1 to 100 ms), in turn:

1. The ready queue is a first-in, first-out queue.
2. Take the job at the front. Run it for q, or until it finishes if it needs less.
3. If it is not finished, the timer [[preempts|preempt]] it and it goes to the **back** of the queue.

One rule decides ties, and simulators must state it: **a job that arrives during a slice, or at the very moment it ends, joins the queue before the job that was just preempted.** Textbooks differ on this; this course always uses this rule.

The three jobs from before, with q = 4:

```text
 L        S1       S2       L
|--------|--------|--------|----------------------------------|
0        4        7       10                                   30
```

S1 and S2 now wait only 4 and 7, and L waits 6. The average waiting is about 5.67: much better than FCFS in the bad order, worse than SJF. But no job needs a prediction, and no job waits long for its first turn.

The rotation itself is two `deque` operations from `collections`. Here is a queue of three names turning twice:

```python
from collections import deque

turns = deque(["red", "green", "blue"])
for _ in range(2):
    who = turns.popleft()
    turns.append(who)
print(list(turns))                # ['blue', 'red', 'green']
```

### Why round robin responds quickly

The claim: **with n jobs in the queue, a newly arrived job waits at most (n − 1) · q before it first runs** (plus switching time). The argument: at worst it joins behind n − 1 others, and each of them runs for at most q before being sent to the back, behind it. So interactive jobs stay responsive however long the other jobs are. This is the guarantee FCFS and SJF cannot give.

The price is **context switches**. If a switch costs s and the quantum is q, at most a fraction q / (q + s) of the time does useful work. With s = 0.01 ms, q = 10 ms is 99.9% useful; q = 0.1 ms is only about 91%. With a huge q, round robin turns back into FCFS. Choosing q is next lesson.

### What it costs to simulate

- **FCFS:** sort by arrival, O(n log n), then one pass.
- **SJF:** each time the CPU frees up, find the shortest arrived job. With a heap (from DSA I) that is O(log n) a choice, O(n log n) in all.
- **RR:** one queue operation per slice. Job i needs ⌈bᵢ / q⌉ slices, so the simulation takes O(n log n + Σ ⌈bᵢ / q⌉) time.

A simulator should jump time forward from event to event (an arrival, the end of a slice), not tick one millisecond at a time: the work then depends on the number of events, not on how long the jobs are.

### Where it is used

- **Batch systems** on supercomputers run jobs with [[user-given time limits|sjf-hpc]], and use those limits much as SJF uses burst predictions.
- **Round robin** is the base of time-sharing: every desktop and server scheduler slices time, with refinements (next lesson).
- **A spacecraft's onboard computer** often runs a fixed, repeating timetable of tasks, a round robin whose slices are planned before launch.
- The **[[convoy effect|convoy]]** shows up far from CPUs too: in disk queues, network routers and supermarket checkouts.

**Watch out:**

- **Mixing up waiting and response.** In RR a job may get its first slice quickly (low response) and still wait a long time in total.
- **Forgetting idle time.** If nothing has arrived, the CPU is idle: jump the clock to the next arrival rather than running a job that is not there yet.
- **The tie at the end of a slice.** Put new arrivals in the queue before the preempted job, or your schedule will not match anyone else's.
- **Choosing from jobs that have not arrived.** SJF picks the shortest job **that has arrived**, not the shortest in the whole list.

::: context preempt Taking the CPU away
To preempt means to take the CPU from a running process before it is finished, without asking it. It relies on the timer interrupt from lesson 1: when the quantum runs out, the timer traps into the kernel, which saves the process's registers in its PCB, puts it back in the ready queue and runs another. A non-preemptive policy only switches when the running process blocks or exits. Early Windows and Mac OS were non-preemptive, so one program stuck in a loop froze the whole computer.
:::

::: context sjf-hpc Why real systems still use estimates
Supercomputer schedulers such as Slurm ask each user for a time limit when they submit a job. The scheduler then fills gaps in its plan with short jobs that are sure to finish in time, a technique called backfilling. It is SJF-like thinking with the user's estimate as the prediction. Users who set generous limits wait longer, so the estimate has a price.
:::

::: context convoy Stuck behind a tractor
The convoy effect happens whenever a queue serves one customer at a time, in arrival order, and the jobs vary a lot in size. One big job at the front delays everyone behind it by its full length. A supermarket fixes it with an express lane for short jobs (a crude SJF), and a CPU scheduler fixes it with preemption. The name was made popular by a 1979 IBM paper, "The Convoy Phenomenon", about database transactions piling up behind one slow lock holder. Operating-systems textbooks use it for short, I/O-heavy processes stuck behind one CPU-heavy process.
:::
--- task
Write a simulator for two policies, and the measurements.

A job is a tuple `(name, arrival, burst)`: names are unique, arrivals are whole numbers ≥ 0, bursts whole numbers ≥ 1. The list may be in any order. A Gantt chart is a list of `(name, start, end)` segments in time order; idle time does not appear, and two back-to-back segments of the same job with no gap between them are **merged into one**.

- `sjf(jobs)`: non-preemptive shortest job first. Whenever the CPU is free, it starts the arrived job with the smallest burst; ties go to the earlier arrival, then to the job that comes first in `jobs`. If no job has arrived, the CPU is idle until the next arrival. Return the Gantt chart.
- `round_robin(jobs, quantum)`: round robin as in the lesson. Jobs join the back of the queue when they arrive (jobs arriving at the same time join in the order they appear in `jobs`). The front job runs for `quantum`, or less if it finishes. A job that arrives during that slice, or at its end, joins the queue **before** the preempted job goes to the back. Return the Gantt chart.
- `averages(jobs, gantt)`: return a tuple of the average turnaround time, average waiting time and average response time over all jobs, each rounded with `round(x, 2)`. For no jobs, return `(0.0, 0.0, 0.0)`.

For example, with `jobs = [("A", 0, 5), ("B", 1, 3)]`, `round_robin(jobs, 2)` is `[("A", 0, 2), ("B", 2, 4), ("A", 4, 6), ("B", 6, 7), ("A", 7, 8)]`.
--- starter
def sjf(jobs):
    return []


def round_robin(jobs, quantum):
    return []


def averages(jobs, gantt):
    return (0.0, 0.0, 0.0)
--- solution
from collections import deque


def add_segment(gantt, name, start, end):
    if gantt and gantt[-1][0] == name and gantt[-1][2] == start:
        gantt[-1] = (name, gantt[-1][1], end)
    else:
        gantt.append((name, start, end))


def by_arrival(jobs):
    return sorted(range(len(jobs)), key=lambda i: (jobs[i][1], i))


def sjf(jobs):
    order = by_arrival(jobs)
    gantt = []
    ready = []
    t = 0
    k = 0
    while k < len(order) or ready:
        while k < len(order) and jobs[order[k]][1] <= t:
            ready.append(order[k])
            k += 1
        if not ready:
            t = jobs[order[k]][1]
            continue
        pick = min(ready, key=lambda i: (jobs[i][2], jobs[i][1], i))
        ready.remove(pick)
        name, arrival, burst = jobs[pick]
        add_segment(gantt, name, t, t + burst)
        t += burst
    return gantt


def round_robin(jobs, quantum):
    order = by_arrival(jobs)
    remaining = {name: burst for name, arrival, burst in jobs}
    queue = deque()
    gantt = []
    t = 0
    k = 0

    def admit(now):
        nonlocal k
        while k < len(order) and jobs[order[k]][1] <= now:
            queue.append(jobs[order[k]][0])
            k += 1

    admit(t)
    while queue or k < len(order):
        if not queue:
            t = jobs[order[k]][1]
            admit(t)
            continue
        name = queue.popleft()
        run = min(quantum, remaining[name])
        add_segment(gantt, name, t, t + run)
        t += run
        remaining[name] -= run
        admit(t)
        if remaining[name] > 0:
            queue.append(name)
    return gantt


def averages(jobs, gantt):
    if not jobs:
        return (0.0, 0.0, 0.0)
    first_start = {}
    completion = {}
    for name, start, end in gantt:
        first_start.setdefault(name, start)
        completion[name] = end
    turnaround = waiting = response = 0
    for name, arrival, burst in jobs:
        turnaround += completion[name] - arrival
        waiting += completion[name] - arrival - burst
        response += first_start[name] - arrival
    n = len(jobs)
    return (round(turnaround / n, 2), round(waiting / n, 2), round(response / n, 2))
--- hint
Sort the job positions by `(arrival, position)` once, and keep an index to the next job that has not arrived. Before every choice, move every job with `arrival <= t` into the ready set or queue. If nothing is ready, jump `t` to the next arrival.
--- hint
For SJF, pick with `min` over the ready jobs using the key `(burst, arrival, position)`. For round robin, use a `deque` of names and a dict of remaining times. After running a slice, admit the new arrivals first, then put the preempted job at the back.
--- hint
Write one helper that appends a segment, or extends the last one when it is the same job and it ended exactly at this start. In `averages`, one pass over the Gantt chart gives each job's first start and completion; then turnaround is `completion - arrival`, waiting is `turnaround - burst`, response is `first start - arrival`.

=> [("A", 0, 2), ("B", 2, 4), ("A", 4, 6), ("B", 6, 7), ("A", 7, 8)]

=> [("A", 0, 3), ("B", 3, 5), ("A", 5, 8)]
?? B arrives at 3, the moment A's slice ends. B joins the queue first, then A goes behind it.

=> [("x", 0, 1), ("y", 1, 2), ("z", 2, 3), ("x", 3, 4), ("y", 4, 6)]
--- check case | No jobs

=> ([], [])
--- check case | SJF on the convoy: shortest first, tie by list order
sjf([("L", 0, 24), ("S1", 0, 3), ("S2", 0, 3)])
=> [("S1", 0, 3), ("S2", 3, 6), ("L", 6, 30)]

=> [("long", 0, 8), ("short", 8, 9), ("mid", 9, 13)]
--- check case | SJF ties: equal bursts go to the earlier arrival
sjf([("P", 0, 4), ("Q", 3, 2), ("R", 1, 2)])
=> [("P", 0, 4), ("R", 4, 6), ("Q", 6, 8)]
--- check case | SJF with an idle gap, jobs listed out of order
sjf([("b", 9, 2), ("a", 1, 3)])
=> [("a", 1, 4), ("b", 9, 11)]
--- check case | averages for the round robin convoy
averages([("L", 0, 24), ("S1", 0, 3), ("S2", 0, 3)], [("L", 0, 4), ("S1", 4, 7), ("S2", 7, 10), ("L", 10, 30)])
=> (15.67, 5.67, 3.67)
--- check case | averages with late arrivals
averages([("A", 0, 5), ("B", 1, 3)], [("A", 0, 2), ("B", 2, 4), ("A", 4, 6), ("B", 6, 7), ("A", 7, 8)])
=> (7.0, 3.0, 0.5)
--- check case | averages of no jobs
averages([], [])
=> (0.0, 0.0, 0.0)
--- check test | SJF's average waiting is the smallest of all orders, for 6 jobs arriving at 0
