--- check test | Busy time is exactly the total burst, for both policies
(lambda jobs: all(sum(e - s for _, s, e in g) == sum(b for _, _, b in jobs) for g in (sjf(jobs), round_robin(jobs, 3))))([(f"j{i}", (i * 37) % 50, 1 + (i * 13) % 9) for i in range(40)])
--- check test | 2,000 jobs with bursts up to 20,000 ms: the simulator jumps between events
(lambda jobs: (lambda g: len(g) > 2000 and sum(e - s for _, s, e in g) == sum(b for _, _, b in jobs) and all(g[i][2] <= g[i + 1][1] for i in range(len(g) - 1)))(round_robin(jobs, 200)))([(f"j{i}", i * 5, 1 + (i * 7919) % 20000) for i in range(2000)])
?? Do not advance the clock one millisecond at a time: jump straight to the end of each slice, or to the next arrival when the CPU is idle.

+++ question | The step in the exchange argument
--- ask
In the proof that SJF minimises total waiting when all jobs arrive at time 0, we swap two neighbouring jobs i and i + 1 with bursts bᵢ > bᵢ₊₁. Why does the waiting time of every job **after** the pair stay the same?
--- choice
Because those jobs have shorter bursts than the pair.
--- choice correct
Because the same set of jobs still runs before each of them, so the sum of the bursts ahead of them does not change.
--- choice
Because a swap only ever changes one job's waiting time.
--- choice
Because the average stays the same even when individual waiting times change.
--- why
A job's waiting time is the sum of the bursts of the jobs ahead of it. Swapping two neighbours changes the order within the pair, not which jobs are ahead of a later job. Only the two swapped jobs' waits change, and the total changes by bᵢ₊₁ − bᵢ, which is negative when the longer job came first.

+++ question | Response time in round robin
--- ask
Round robin runs with a quantum of 20 ms. Nine jobs are in the ready queue when a tenth arrives and joins the back. Ignoring the time context switches take, at most how many milliseconds pass before the tenth job starts running? Type a whole number.
--- answer
180
--- why
At worst each of the nine jobs ahead runs a full 20 ms slice before the new job reaches the front: 9 × 20 = 180 ms. Any job that is preempted goes to the back, behind the new one, so nobody gets a second turn first. The bound is (n − 1) · q, or (n − 1)(q + s) counting switches of s each, and it does not depend on how long the jobs are.

+++ practice | A first-come, first-served table
--- task
Write `fcfs_table(jobs)` for first come, first served. A job is a tuple `(name, arrival, burst)`; the list may be in any order. Jobs run to the end in order of arrival; jobs that arrive at the same time run in the order they appear in `jobs`. If nothing has arrived when the CPU is free, it is idle until the next arrival.

Return one tuple per job, in the order they run: `(name, start, end, waiting)`, where `waiting` is how long the job was ready before it started.

For example, `fcfs_table([("A", 0, 4), ("B", 1, 3)])` is `[("A", 0, 4, 0), ("B", 4, 7, 3)]`. With no jobs, return `[]`.
--- starter
def fcfs_table(jobs):
    return [(name, 0, burst, 0) for name, arrival, burst in jobs]
--- solution
def fcfs_table(jobs):
    order = sorted(range(len(jobs)), key=lambda i: (jobs[i][1], i))
    table = []
    t = 0
    for i in order:
        name, arrival, burst = jobs[i]
        start = max(t, arrival)
        t = start + burst
        table.append((name, start, t, start - arrival))
    return table
--- hint
Sort the positions of the jobs by `(arrival, position)` so that ties keep the list order.
--- hint
Keep the time `t` at which the CPU becomes free. A job starts at `max(t, arrival)`: that `max` is what makes the CPU sit idle when the next job has not arrived yet.
--- check case | The example from the task
fcfs_table([("A", 0, 4), ("B", 1, 3)])
=> [("A", 0, 4, 0), ("B", 4, 7, 3)]
--- check case | Out of order, with a tie and an idle gap
fcfs_table([("A", 0, 4), ("B", 1, 3), ("C", 9, 2), ("D", 1, 1)])
=> [("A", 0, 4, 0), ("B", 4, 7, 3), ("D", 7, 8, 6), ("C", 9, 11, 0)]
--- check case | The convoy: a long job first
fcfs_table([("L", 0, 24), ("S1", 0, 3), ("S2", 0, 3)])
=> [("L", 0, 24, 0), ("S1", 24, 27, 24), ("S2", 27, 30, 27)]
--- check case | No jobs, and one late job
(fcfs_table([]), fcfs_table([("late", 50, 5)]))
=> ([], [("late", 50, 55, 0)])
--- check case | Jobs that arrive exactly when the CPU frees up do not wait
fcfs_table([("a", 0, 2), ("b", 2, 2), ("c", 4, 1)])
=> [("a", 0, 2, 0), ("b", 2, 4, 0), ("c", 4, 5, 0)]

+++ practice | Predicting the next burst
--- task
SJF needs each process's next CPU burst before it happens. Use the exponential average from the lesson: after a burst of length `t`, the new guess is `alpha * t + (1 - alpha) * guess`.

Write two functions:

- `predict(bursts, alpha, first_guess)`: `bursts` are one process's past burst lengths, oldest first. Return the list of all guesses: `first_guess` (the guess before any burst), then the guess after each burst. So the list is one longer than `bursts`. Compute with full precision, and round each value with `round(x, 3)` only when building the list you return.
- `next_order(histories, alpha, first_guess)`: `histories` is a dict from process name to its list of past bursts. Return the names sorted by their latest (unrounded) guess, smallest first, as SJF would run them. Ties go in alphabetical order.

For example, `predict([6, 4, 6], 0.5, 10)` is `[10, 8.0, 6.0, 6.0]`.
--- starter
def predict(bursts, alpha, first_guess):
    return [first_guess] + list(bursts)


def next_order(histories, alpha, first_guess):
    return sorted(histories)
--- solution
def predict(bursts, alpha, first_guess):
    guesses = [first_guess]
    for real in bursts:
        guesses.append(alpha * real + (1 - alpha) * guesses[-1])
    return [round(g, 3) for g in guesses]


def next_order(histories, alpha, first_guess):
    def unrounded(bursts):
        guess = first_guess
        for real in bursts:
            guess = alpha * real + (1 - alpha) * guess
        return guess
    return sorted(histories, key=lambda name: (unrounded(histories[name]), name))
--- hint
Keep the running guess in a variable and append after each burst. Round only in the final list, so rounding errors do not pile up.
--- hint
For `next_order`, compute each process's final guess and sort with the key `(guess, name)`. A process with no history keeps `first_guess`.
--- check case | The example from the task
predict([6, 4, 6], 0.5, 10)
=> [10, 8.0, 6.0, 6.0]
--- check case | A longer history, where the guess lags behind a change
predict([6, 4, 6, 4, 13, 13, 13], 0.5, 10)
=> [10, 8.0, 6.0, 6.0, 5.0, 9.0, 11.0, 12.0]
--- check case | alpha = 0.8 trusts the latest burst more
predict([8, 8, 8], 0.8, 2)
=> [2, 6.8, 7.76, 7.952]
--- check case | No history: just the first guess
predict([], 0.3, 5)
=> [5]
--- check case | alpha = 1 forgets the past; alpha = 0 never learns
(predict([5, 7], 1, 9), predict([5, 7], 0, 9))
=> ([9, 5, 7], [9, 9, 9])
--- check case | Ordering processes by their predicted next burst
next_order({"a": [10, 2, 2], "b": [3, 3, 9], "c": []}, 0.5, 6)
=> ["a", "c", "b"]
--- check case | Ties are broken by name
next_order({"zeta": [4], "alpha": [4], "mid": [1]}, 0.5, 4)
=> ["mid", "alpha", "zeta"]

+++ practice | Shortest remaining time first, with a heap
--- task
Write `srtf(jobs)`, the preemptive version of SJF: at every moment the CPU runs the arrived job with the least **remaining** time. When a job arrives with less remaining time than the running job has left, it takes over at once. Ties go to the earlier arrival, then to the job that comes first in `jobs`, so an arriving job with the **same** remaining time does not preempt. A job is a tuple `(name, arrival, burst)`.

Return the Gantt chart as a list of `(name, start, end)`, merging back-to-back segments of the same job and leaving idle time out. Use `heapq` so each decision is O(log n): a check runs 20,000 jobs.

For example, `srtf([("A", 0, 5), ("B", 1, 2)])` is `[("A", 0, 1), ("B", 1, 3), ("A", 3, 7)]`.
--- starter
def srtf(jobs):
    return [(name, arrival, arrival + burst) for name, arrival, burst in jobs]
--- solution
import heapq


def srtf(jobs):
    order = sorted(range(len(jobs)), key=lambda i: (jobs[i][1], i))
    heap = []
    gantt = []
    t = 0
    k = 0
    while k < len(order) or heap:
        while k < len(order) and jobs[order[k]][1] <= t:
            i = order[k]
            heapq.heappush(heap, (jobs[i][2], jobs[i][1], i))
            k += 1
        if not heap:
            t = jobs[order[k]][1]
            continue
        remaining, arrival, i = heapq.heappop(heap)
        stop = t + remaining
        if k < len(order):
            stop = min(stop, jobs[order[k]][1])
        name = jobs[i][0]
        if gantt and gantt[-1][0] == name and gantt[-1][2] == t:
            gantt[-1] = (name, gantt[-1][1], stop)
        else:
            gantt.append((name, t, stop))
        remaining -= stop - t
        t = stop
        if remaining > 0:
            heapq.heappush(heap, (remaining, arrival, i))
    return gantt
--- hint
Decisions only change at arrivals. So run the best job until it finishes **or** the next job arrives, whichever comes first, then push it back with its new remaining time and decide again.
--- hint
Push tuples `(remaining, arrival, position)` onto the heap: the heap's order is then exactly the tie rule. Merge a segment into the last one when it is the same job and starts where that one ended.
--- check case | The example from the task
srtf([("A", 0, 5), ("B", 1, 2)])
=> [("A", 0, 1), ("B", 1, 3), ("A", 3, 7)]
--- check case | The classic four-job example
srtf([("P1", 0, 8), ("P2", 1, 4), ("P3", 2, 9), ("P4", 3, 5)])
=> [("P1", 0, 1), ("P2", 1, 5), ("P4", 5, 10), ("P1", 10, 17), ("P3", 17, 26)]
--- check case | Equal remaining time does not preempt
(srtf([("A", 0, 5), ("B", 2, 3)]), srtf([("A", 0, 4), ("B", 2, 2)]))
=> ([("A", 0, 5), ("B", 5, 8)], [("A", 0, 4), ("B", 4, 6)])
--- check case | Idle gaps and no jobs
(srtf([("x", 3, 2), ("y", 10, 1)]), srtf([]))
=> ([("x", 3, 5), ("y", 10, 11)], [])
--- check case | An arrival that is longer does not interrupt, so the segment stays whole
srtf([("short", 0, 3), ("long", 1, 10), ("tiny", 2, 2)])
=> [("short", 0, 3), ("tiny", 3, 5), ("long", 5, 15)]
--- check test | 20,000 jobs: every job finishes and the CPU never runs two at once
(lambda jobs: (lambda g: sum(e - s for _, s, e in g) == sum(b for _, _, b in jobs) and all(g[i][2] <= g[i + 1][1] for i in range(len(g) - 1)) and len({n for n, _, _ in g}) == 20000)(srtf(jobs)))([(f"j{i}", (i * 7) // 3, 1 + (i * 7919) % 97) for i in range(20000)])
?? Scanning every ready job for the shortest is O(n) a decision. A heap gives the shortest in O(log n).

+++ practice | Round robin that pays for its switches
--- task
Every context switch costs time. Write `rr_with_switches(jobs, quantum, switch)`, a round robin simulator (same rules as the lesson's task: arrivals in order, new arrivals join before the preempted job) that also charges for switches:

- Before a slice starts, if the CPU's last slice was a **different** job and the CPU has not been idle since, the switch costs `switch` time units, and the slice starts after it.
- Continuing the same job costs nothing. The very first slice, and the first slice after the CPU was idle, cost nothing.
- Jobs that arrive during a switch join the queue with the other arrivals when the slice ends.

Return `(completion, switches)`: a dict from each job's name to its completion time, and the number of switches paid for.

For example, `rr_with_switches([("A", 0, 5), ("B", 1, 3)], 2, 1)` is `({"B": 10, "A": 12}, 4)`.
--- starter
def rr_with_switches(jobs, quantum, switch):
    return ({name: arrival + burst for name, arrival, burst in jobs}, 0)
--- solution
from collections import deque


def rr_with_switches(jobs, quantum, switch):
    order = sorted(range(len(jobs)), key=lambda i: (jobs[i][1], i))
    remaining = {name: burst for name, arrival, burst in jobs}
    completion = {}
    queue = deque()
    t = 0
    k = 0
    last = None
    switches = 0

    def admit(now):
        nonlocal k
        while k < len(order) and jobs[order[k]][1] <= now:
            queue.append(jobs[order[k]][0])
            k += 1

    admit(t)
    while queue or k < len(order):
        if not queue:
            t = jobs[order[k]][1]
            last = None
            admit(t)
            continue
        name = queue.popleft()
        if last is not None and last != name:
            t += switch
            switches += 1
        run = min(quantum, remaining[name])
        t += run
        remaining[name] -= run
        last = name
        admit(t)
        if remaining[name] > 0:
            queue.append(name)
        else:
            completion[name] = t
    return completion, switches
--- hint
Start from a round robin simulator, and keep one more variable: the name of the job whose slice ran last, or `None` when there is none to switch from (at the start, and after the CPU has been idle).
--- hint
When you take a job from the queue, charge `switch` and count it only if the last job is not `None` and is a different job. When the queue is empty and you jump the clock to the next arrival, set the last job back to `None`.
--- check case | The example from the task
rr_with_switches([("A", 0, 5), ("B", 1, 3)], 2, 1)
=> ({"B": 10, "A": 12}, 4)
--- check case | One job alone never pays for a switch
rr_with_switches([("solo", 0, 10)], 3, 5)
=> ({"solo": 10}, 0)
--- check case | No jobs
rr_with_switches([], 3, 5)
=> ({}, 0)
--- check case | After an idle gap there is nothing to switch from
rr_with_switches([("A", 0, 2), ("B", 5, 2)], 4, 3)
=> ({"A": 2, "B": 7}, 0)
--- check case | The convoy with q = 4 and 1 ms switches
rr_with_switches([("L", 0, 24), ("S1", 0, 3), ("S2", 0, 3)], 4, 1)
=> ({"S1": 8, "S2": 12, "L": 33}, 3)
--- check case | A zero-cost switch is still counted
rr_with_switches([("a", 0, 2), ("b", 0, 2)], 1, 0)
=> ({"a": 3, "b": 4}, 3)
--- check case | A job that arrives during a switch
rr_with_switches([("A", 0, 4), ("B", 0, 4), ("C", 3, 1)], 2, 2)
=> ({"A": 10, "C": 13, "B": 17}, 4)

+++ practice | Debug: the preempted job cuts in line
--- task
`round_robin(jobs, quantum)` should follow the lesson's rule: a job that arrives during a slice, or exactly when it ends, joins the queue **before** the job that was just preempted goes to the back. A job is `(name, arrival, burst)`, and the function returns the Gantt chart with back-to-back segments of the same job merged.

With `jobs = [("A", 0, 6), ("B", 3, 2)]` and quantum 3, it should give `[("A", 0, 3), ("B", 3, 5), ("A", 5, 8)]`, but it gives `[("A", 0, 6), ("B", 6, 8)]`: A goes back into the queue ahead of B. Find the bug and fix it.
--- starter
from collections import deque


def round_robin(jobs, quantum):
    order = sorted(range(len(jobs)), key=lambda i: (jobs[i][1], i))
    remaining = {name: burst for name, arrival, burst in jobs}
    queue = deque()
    gantt = []
    t = 0
    k = 0
    while queue or k < len(order):
        while k < len(order) and jobs[order[k]][1] <= t:
            queue.append(jobs[order[k]][0])
            k += 1
        if not queue:
            t = jobs[order[k]][1]
            continue
        name = queue.popleft()
        run = min(quantum, remaining[name])
        if gantt and gantt[-1][0] == name and gantt[-1][2] == t:
            gantt[-1] = (name, gantt[-1][1], t + run)
        else:
            gantt.append((name, t, t + run))
        t += run
        remaining[name] -= run
        if remaining[name] > 0:
            queue.append(name)
    return gantt
--- solution
from collections import deque


def round_robin(jobs, quantum):
    order = sorted(range(len(jobs)), key=lambda i: (jobs[i][1], i))
    remaining = {name: burst for name, arrival, burst in jobs}
    queue = deque()
    gantt = []
    t = 0
    k = 0
    while queue or k < len(order):
        while k < len(order) and jobs[order[k]][1] <= t:
            queue.append(jobs[order[k]][0])
            k += 1
        if not queue:
            t = jobs[order[k]][1]
            continue
        name = queue.popleft()
        run = min(quantum, remaining[name])
        if gantt and gantt[-1][0] == name and gantt[-1][2] == t:
            gantt[-1] = (name, gantt[-1][1], t + run)
        else:
            gantt.append((name, t, t + run))
        t += run
        remaining[name] -= run
        while k < len(order) and jobs[order[k]][1] <= t:
            queue.append(jobs[order[k]][0])
            k += 1
        if remaining[name] > 0:
            queue.append(name)
    return gantt
--- hint
Follow the example by hand. At time 3, A's slice ends and B arrives. Which one does the code put into the queue first?
--- hint
Arrivals are only admitted at the top of the loop, after A is already back in the queue. Admit the arrivals up to the new `t` right after the slice, before appending the preempted job.
--- check case | The example from the task
round_robin([("A", 0, 6), ("B", 3, 2)], 3)
=> [("A", 0, 3), ("B", 3, 5), ("A", 5, 8)]
--- check case | An arrival in the middle of a slice also goes first
round_robin([("A", 0, 5), ("B", 1, 3)], 2)
=> [("A", 0, 2), ("B", 2, 4), ("A", 4, 6), ("B", 6, 7), ("A", 7, 8)]
--- check case | The convoy with q = 4 is unchanged
round_robin([("L", 0, 24), ("S1", 0, 3), ("S2", 0, 3)], 4)
=> [("L", 0, 4), ("S1", 4, 7), ("S2", 7, 10), ("L", 10, 30)]
--- check case | Idle gaps and no jobs
(round_robin([("x", 5, 2)], 3), round_robin([], 3))
=> ([("x", 5, 7)], [])
--- check case | Three arrivals during one long slice
round_robin([("A", 0, 8), ("B", 1, 1), ("C", 2, 1), ("D", 4, 1)], 4)
=> [("A", 0, 4), ("B", 4, 5), ("C", 5, 6), ("D", 6, 7), ("A", 7, 11)]

+++ practice | Two CPUs, one queue
--- task
A flight computer has several identical CPU cores and one shared ready queue. Write `multi_sjf(jobs, cpus)`, a non-preemptive shortest-job-first scheduler for `cpus` cores numbered from 0:

- Whenever at least one core is free and at least one arrived job is waiting, the waiting job with the smallest burst starts on the **lowest-numbered** free core. Ties between jobs go to the earlier arrival, then to the job that comes first in `jobs`.
- A core that finishes a job at time `t` is free at time `t`, and a job that arrives at `t` can start at `t`.
- Jobs are `(name, arrival, burst)`.

Return a list of `(name, core, start, end)`, one per job, sorted by start time and then by core.

For example, `multi_sjf([("a", 0, 5), ("b", 0, 3), ("c", 1, 1), ("d", 2, 2)], 2)` is `[("b", 0, 0, 3), ("a", 1, 0, 5), ("c", 0, 3, 4), ("d", 0, 4, 6)]`.
--- starter
def multi_sjf(jobs, cpus):
    return [(name, 0, arrival, arrival + burst) for name, arrival, burst in jobs]
--- solution
import heapq


def multi_sjf(jobs, cpus):
    order = sorted(range(len(jobs)), key=lambda i: (jobs[i][1], i))
    free = list(range(cpus))
    running = []
    ready = []
    result = []
    t = 0
    k = 0
    while k < len(order) or ready or running:
        while running and running[0][0] <= t:
            end, cpu = heapq.heappop(running)
            heapq.heappush(free, cpu)
        while k < len(order) and jobs[order[k]][1] <= t:
            i = order[k]
            heapq.heappush(ready, (jobs[i][2], jobs[i][1], i))
            k += 1
        while free and ready:
            burst, arrival, i = heapq.heappop(ready)
            cpu = heapq.heappop(free)
            result.append((jobs[i][0], cpu, t, t + burst))
            heapq.heappush(running, (t + burst, cpu))
        times = []
        if running:
            times.append(running[0][0])
        if k < len(order):
            times.append(jobs[order[k]][1])
        if not times:
            break
        t = max(t, min(times))
    return sorted(result, key=lambda r: (r[2], r[1]))
--- hint
Simulate from event to event. The events are arrivals and job ends. At each event time, first free the cores whose jobs ended, then add the new arrivals to the ready set, then start jobs while there is both a free core and a waiting job.
--- hint
Three heaps do all the choosing: free cores (smallest number first), waiting jobs keyed by `(burst, arrival, position)`, and running jobs keyed by `(end, core)`. The next event time is the smaller of the earliest end and the next arrival.
--- check case | The example from the task
multi_sjf([("a", 0, 5), ("b", 0, 3), ("c", 1, 1), ("d", 2, 2)], 2)
=> [("b", 0, 0, 3), ("a", 1, 0, 5), ("c", 0, 3, 4), ("d", 0, 4, 6)]
--- check case | More cores than jobs: the lowest cores are used
multi_sjf([("a", 0, 5), ("b", 1, 2)], 3)
=> [("a", 0, 0, 5), ("b", 1, 1, 3)]
--- check case | No jobs
multi_sjf([], 2)
=> []
--- check case | Cores free at the same moment, and a later arrival
multi_sjf([("p", 0, 4), ("q", 0, 4), ("r", 0, 4), ("s", 6, 1)], 2)
=> [("p", 0, 0, 4), ("q", 1, 0, 4), ("r", 0, 4, 8), ("s", 1, 6, 7)]
--- check case | One core is plain SJF
multi_sjf([("long", 0, 8), ("short", 1, 1), ("mid", 2, 4)], 1)
=> [("long", 0, 0, 8), ("short", 0, 8, 9), ("mid", 0, 9, 13)]
--- check case | An idle gap on every core
multi_sjf([("x", 10, 2), ("y", 10, 1), ("z", 10, 3)], 2)
=> [("y", 0, 10, 11), ("x", 1, 10, 12), ("z", 0, 11, 14)]
--- check test | 5,000 jobs on 4 cores: no core ever runs two jobs at once
(lambda r: len(r) == 5000 and all(all(a[3] <= b[2] for a, b in zip(rs, rs[1:])) for rs in [sorted([x for x in r if x[1] == c], key=lambda x: x[2]) for c in range(4)]))(multi_sjf([(f"j{i}", i // 2, 1 + (i * 31) % 17) for i in range(5000)], 4))

=== os-04 | Scheduling II: priorities, MLFQ, starvation and aging
--- teach
Last lesson's policies each had a flaw. FCFS lets a long job hold up short ones. SJF needs to know burst lengths in advance. Round robin treats every job the same, so a text editor waiting for a keystroke gets no better service than a ten-hour simulation. Real schedulers want three things at once: quick **response** for interactive jobs, good **throughput** for long ones, and a way to say that some jobs **matter more**. This lesson builds the tools that do it: priorities, aging, and the multi-level feedback queue, the design behind the schedulers in Windows, macOS and the BSDs. It ends with how to choose a quantum.

### Priority scheduling

In **priority scheduling**, every job has a **priority** number, and the scheduler always runs the ready job with the best priority. On Unix a **smaller number means more important**: the `nice` value runs from −20 (most important) to 19 (least), so a "nice" process is one that lets others go first. Like SJF, priority scheduling can be **non-preemptive** (a better job waits for the running one to finish) or **preemptive** (a better job that arrives takes the CPU at once). SJF is itself priority scheduling, with the burst length as the priority.

### Starvation

Priorities have a danger. Suppose a job with priority 10 is ready, and jobs with priority 1 keep arriving, one every few milliseconds. The priority-10 job never runs. It is not blocked; it is ready the whole time; it just never wins. This is **starvation**: a ready job waits forever because others are always preferred. Folklore says that when an old IBM mainframe at MIT was shut down in 1973, a low-priority job from 1967 was found still waiting.

### Aging, and why it ends starvation

The fix is **aging**: the longer a job waits, the better its priority gets. A common rule: a job's **effective priority** is its base priority minus one for every A time units it has waited since it last ran, never going below 0 (the best value). When it runs, its waiting count resets.

The claim: **with aging, no ready job waits forever.** The argument, in steps:

1. Take a waiting job with base priority p. Each A time units that it waits, its effective priority drops by 1. So within p · A time units, it reaches 0, the best value there is.
2. From then on, no job can be strictly better than it. Only jobs also at 0 can tie with it.
3. If ties go to the job that has waited longest (or arrived first), the jobs that can still go ahead of it are the ones already tied with it at that moment. There are finitely many, each with a finite burst.
4. So after at most p · A time units plus the work of those jobs, it runs. Its wait is bounded.

The price: aging blurs priorities. With a small A, low-priority jobs catch up fast and priorities matter less; with a large A, starving jobs wait a long time before they catch up.

### Priority inversion

One more trap waits in priorities, and it happened on Mars. In 1997, the Pathfinder lander kept resetting itself. A low-priority weather task held a lock (lesson 6) on a shared data bus. A high-priority bus task needed that lock, so it waited. Then a medium-priority communications task became ready and, being more important than the weather task, took the CPU, for a long time. The high-priority task was stuck behind the low one, and the low one was stuck behind the medium one. A watchdog timer noticed that the bus task had missed its deadline and reset the computer.

This is **priority inversion**: a high-priority job waits for a lower one, while jobs in between run. The standard fix is **priority inheritance**: while a job holds a lock that a better job is waiting for, it runs at that better job's priority. The weather task would have run at high priority, finished with the bus quickly and released the lock. Engineers on Earth turned on priority inheritance in the lander's [[real-time operating system|rtos]] with a software update, and the resets stopped.

### The multi-level feedback queue

Now the central design. The **multi-level feedback queue** (MLFQ) wants SJF's good behaviour without knowing burst lengths. It **learns** from how a job behaves: a job that uses its whole slice probably has a long burst, and a job that gives up the CPU early (waiting for a keystroke) is probably interactive.

It keeps several ready queues, one per priority **level**. Level 0 is the most important. Each level has its own quantum, short at the top and longer lower down. The rules, as set out in the well-known [[OSTEP|ostep]] textbook:

1. **Higher level first.** If a job at a higher level is ready, it runs, preempting any job from a lower level.
2. **Round robin within a level.** Jobs at the same level take turns.
3. **New jobs start at the top.** The scheduler assumes a new job might be short, and gives it the best chance to finish quickly.
4. **Use up your allotment, move down.** Each level gives a job an **allotment** of CPU time. Once a job has used it up, counted across all its turns at that level, it moves down one level. (In this course a level's allotment is its quantum.)
5. **Boost every S time units.** Periodically, every job moves back to the top level, with a fresh allotment.

Each rule has a reason:

- Rules 1 to 3 make MLFQ act like SJF. A short job starts at the top and finishes before it uses up its allotment. A long job sinks, level by level, until it runs only when nothing more urgent is ready.
- Rule 4 counts time **across turns** so that a job cannot **[[game the scheduler|gaming]]**. If a job's count reset every time it gave up the CPU, a program could give up the CPU just before its slice ended and stay at the top forever.
- Rule 5 stops starvation. Without it, a steady stream of short jobs at the top could keep a long job at the bottom from ever running. It also rescues a job that was CPU-heavy for a while and then becomes interactive.

Here is an MLFQ with three levels and quanta 1, 3 and 6, and no boost. `batch` needs 12 ms and arrives at 0; `edit` needs 2 ms and arrives at 4:

| time | what runs | why |
|---|---|---|
| 0 | batch, level 0 | the only job; after 1 ms its level-0 allotment is used up, down to level 1 |
| 1 to 3 | batch, level 1 | 3 ms at level 1, allotment used up, down to level 2 |
| 4 | edit, level 0 | a new job starts at the top and beats batch; after 1 ms, down to level 1 |
| 5 | edit, level 1 | level 1 beats level 2; edit finishes at 6 |
| 6 to 13 | batch, level 2 | nothing else is ready |

The editor started the moment it arrived and was done 2 ms later, even though a long job was running. With round robin and a big quantum, it would have waited behind `batch`.

### Picking the quantum

How long should a slice be? Two numbers pull in opposite directions. With a context switch cost of s and a quantum of q:

- **Overhead:** at most q / (q + s) of the time is useful work, so s / (q + s) is wasted. A longer quantum wastes less.
- **Response:** with n jobs ready, a new job may wait up to (n − 1)(q + s) before its first slice. A shorter quantum responds faster.

The classic rule of thumb: choose q a little longer than most CPU bursts, so that about 80% of bursts finish within one quantum. An interactive job then usually finishes its burst in one slice and blocks, and it rarely pays for a preemption. Real systems use about 1 to 100 ms. MLFQ uses both ends: short quanta at the top, where interactive jobs live and response matters, and long quanta at the bottom, where CPU-heavy jobs live and overhead matters.

The best way to pick is to measure: simulate the workload with each candidate quantum and look at the numbers you care about. One of the practice problems does exactly that.

### Proportional sharing: what Linux does

Linux does not use MLFQ. From 2007 to 2023 its scheduler was the **Completely Fair Scheduler** (CFS). Each job has a **virtual runtime**: the CPU time it has used, scaled by its weight. The job with the smallest virtual runtime runs next. Weights come from the `nice` value: each step of `nice` changes the weight by a factor of about 1.25, so nice 0 has weight 1024 and nice 5 about 335. A job with three times the weight gains virtual runtime three times more slowly, and so gets about three times the CPU. CFS keeps the ready jobs in a red-black tree ordered by virtual runtime (a structure from DSA I), so picking the next job is O(log n). In 2023, Linux 6.6 replaced CFS with **EEVDF** ("earliest eligible virtual deadline first"), which keeps the same idea of fair shares and adds deadlines for better latency.

### What it costs

- **Priority scheduling:** keep the ready jobs in a heap keyed by priority. Picking is O(log n), and so is adding a job.
- **Aging:** effective priorities change as time passes, so a plain heap goes stale. Either recompute over all ready jobs at each decision, O(n), or store for each job the time at which its effective priority will next change.
- **MLFQ:** one FIFO queue per level. Picking means finding the first non-empty level, O(L) for L levels (a handful), then O(1). A boost moves every job, O(n), but only once every S time units.

### Where it is used

- **Windows, macOS and FreeBSD** use multi-level feedback designs, with priority boosts for jobs that just finished waiting for I/O.
- **Linux** uses weighted fair sharing (CFS, then EEVDF) for normal jobs, and strict priorities (`SCHED_FIFO`, `SCHED_RR`) for real-time ones.
- **Flight software** runs on fixed-priority preemptive real-time systems, with priority inheritance switched on, since Pathfinder showed why.

**Watch out:**

- **Which way priorities run.** On Unix, a smaller number is more important. Check the convention every time you read a scheduler's code.
- **Resetting the allotment on every turn.** A job must not get a fresh allotment each time it gives up the CPU, or it can game the scheduler.
- **Forgetting the boost.** An MLFQ without Rule 5 can starve CPU-heavy jobs forever.
- **Bounded is not the same as short.** Aging and boosts guarantee that a job runs eventually. They do not make it fast.

::: context rtos Operating systems that keep time
A real-time operating system promises when things happen, not just that they happen. Tasks have fixed priorities, and the highest-priority ready task always runs, within microseconds. VxWorks, the system on Pathfinder, also flies on the Mars rovers; RTEMS and FreeRTOS fly on many satellites. Designers prove that every task meets its deadline. A classic result from 1973, by Liu and Layland, says that if each task's priority follows its rate (more frequent tasks first), all deadlines are met whenever the total CPU use stays below about 69%.
:::

::: context ostep The textbook behind these rules
"Operating Systems: Three Easy Pieces" (OSTEP), by Remzi and Andrea Arpaci-Dusseau of the University of Wisconsin, is a free online textbook used in many university courses. Its MLFQ chapter builds the five rules one at a time, showing the problem each one fixes. MLFQ itself goes back to Fernando Corbató's Compatible Time-Sharing System at MIT in 1962, work that was part of his 1990 Turing Award.
:::

::: context gaming Cheating a scheduler
Gaming the scheduler means shaping a program's behaviour to get more CPU than the policy intends. Under an MLFQ that forgot a job's used time whenever it gave up the CPU, a program could compute for 99% of its quantum, then do a tiny I/O, get a fresh allotment, and stay at the top level for ever, starving everyone else. It is a real concern on shared machines, where users compete for CPU time. Counting all the time used at a level, across every turn, closes the loophole.
:::
--- task
Write `mlfq(jobs, quanta, boost)`, an MLFQ simulator that runs one millisecond at a time. A job is `(name, arrival, burst)`. `quanta` lists each level's quantum, from level 0 (the top) down; a level's allotment is its quantum. `boost` is the boost period S, or 0 for no boost.

At each whole time `t`, starting from 0, do these steps in order:

1. Jobs that arrive at `t` join the back of level 0, in the order they appear in `jobs`, each with 0 time used.
2. If `boost` is not 0, `t > 0` and `t` is a multiple of `boost`: move every job from level 1 to the back of level 0 (in queue order), then every job from level 2, and so on. Every job's used time becomes 0, including jobs already at level 0.
3. Choose the job at the **front** of the highest non-empty level. If every level is empty, the CPU is idle for this millisecond.
4. Run it from `t` to `t + 1`. It now has 1 ms less to go, and 1 ms more used at its level.
5. If it is finished, remove it. Otherwise, if its used time equals its level's quantum, take it off the front and put it at the back of the next level down (the bottom level: the back of the same level), with 0 used. Otherwise it **stays at the front** of its level, so it carries on next millisecond unless a higher-level job appears.

Return the Gantt chart: a list of `(name, start, end)`, with back-to-back segments of the same job merged and idle time left out.

For example, `mlfq([("A", 0, 3), ("B", 0, 3)], [2, 4], 0)` is `[("A", 0, 2), ("B", 2, 4), ("A", 4, 5), ("B", 5, 6)]`.
--- starter
def mlfq(jobs, quanta, boost):
    return []
--- solution
from collections import deque


def mlfq(jobs, quanta, boost):
    order = sorted(range(len(jobs)), key=lambda i: (jobs[i][1], i))
    remaining = {name: burst for name, arrival, burst in jobs}
    levels = [deque() for _ in quanta]
    level_of = {}
    used = {}
    gantt = []
    t = 0
    k = 0
    while k < len(order) or any(levels):
        while k < len(order) and jobs[order[k]][1] == t:
            name = jobs[order[k]][0]
            levels[0].append(name)
            level_of[name] = 0
            used[name] = 0
            k += 1
        if boost and t > 0 and t % boost == 0:
            for lower in levels[1:]:
                while lower:
                    levels[0].append(lower.popleft())
            for name in used:
                level_of[name] = 0
                used[name] = 0
        top = next((lv for lv in range(len(levels)) if levels[lv]), None)
        if top is None:
            t += 1
            continue
        name = levels[top][0]
        if gantt and gantt[-1][0] == name and gantt[-1][2] == t:
            gantt[-1] = (name, gantt[-1][1], t + 1)
        else:
            gantt.append((name, t, t + 1))
        t += 1
        remaining[name] -= 1
        used[name] += 1
        if remaining[name] == 0:
            levels[top].popleft()
            del used[name]
        elif used[name] == quanta[top]:
            levels[top].popleft()
            down = min(top + 1, len(levels) - 1)
            levels[down].append(name)
            level_of[name] = down
            used[name] = 0
    return gantt
--- hint
Keep one `deque` per level, a dict of remaining times, and a dict of time used at the current level. Step the clock one millisecond at a time, so arrivals and boosts can be tested with `==` and `%`.
--- hint
The job at the front of the highest non-empty level runs, but it is **not** removed from the queue just because it ran: it only leaves the front when it finishes or uses up its allotment. That is what makes a preempted job carry on where it left off.
--- hint
For a boost, pop every job from levels 1, 2, … in order and append each to level 0, then set every unfinished job's used time to 0. A demotion goes to level `min(top + 1, len(quanta) - 1)`.
--- check case | The example from the task
mlfq([("A", 0, 3), ("B", 0, 3)], [2, 4], 0)
=> [("A", 0, 2), ("B", 2, 4), ("A", 4, 5), ("B", 5, 6)]
--- check case | One job sinks through the levels in one unbroken segment
mlfq([("A", 0, 10)], [2, 4, 8], 0)
=> [("A", 0, 10)]
--- check case | A new job preempts a lower level at once
mlfq([("A", 0, 10), ("B", 3, 3)], [2, 4, 8], 0)
=> [("A", 0, 3), ("B", 3, 5), ("A", 5, 8), ("B", 8, 9), ("A", 9, 13)]
?? At time 3, B arrives at level 0 while A is at level 1, so B runs. A stays at the front of level 1 with the time it has already used there.
--- check case | Short interactive jobs get the CPU as soon as they arrive
mlfq([("long", 0, 30), ("i1", 5, 1), ("i2", 12, 2)], [1, 2, 4], 0)
=> [("long", 0, 5), ("i1", 5, 6), ("long", 6, 12), ("i2", 12, 14), ("long", 14, 33)]
--- check case | Three equal jobs take turns level by level
mlfq([("A", 0, 6), ("B", 0, 6), ("C", 0, 6)], [1, 2, 4], 0)
=> [("A", 0, 1), ("B", 1, 2), ("C", 2, 3), ("A", 3, 5), ("B", 5, 7), ("C", 7, 9), ("A", 9, 12), ("B", 12, 15), ("C", 15, 18)]
--- check case | The bottom level is round robin
mlfq([("A", 0, 20), ("B", 0, 20)], [2, 4], 0)
=> [("A", 0, 2), ("B", 2, 4), ("A", 4, 8), ("B", 8, 12), ("A", 12, 16), ("B", 16, 20), ("A", 20, 24), ("B", 24, 28), ("A", 28, 32), ("B", 32, 36), ("A", 36, 38), ("B", 38, 40)]
--- check case | A boost every 10 ms lifts both jobs back to the top
mlfq([("A", 0, 20), ("B", 0, 20)], [2, 4], 10)
=> [("A", 0, 2), ("B", 2, 4), ("A", 4, 8), ("B", 8, 12), ("A", 12, 14), ("B", 14, 18), ("A", 18, 22), ("B", 22, 24), ("A", 24, 28), ("B", 28, 32), ("A", 32, 34), ("B", 34, 38), ("A", 38, 40)]

+++ practice | Non-preemptive priority scheduling
--- task
Write `priority_np(jobs)`. A job is `(name, arrival, burst, priority)`, and a **smaller** priority number is more important. Whenever the CPU is free, it starts the arrived job with the best priority and runs it to the end. Ties go to the earlier arrival, then to the job that comes first in `jobs`. If nothing has arrived, the CPU is idle until the next arrival.

Return the Gantt chart as a list of `(name, start, end)`.

For example, `priority_np([("A", 0, 4, 3), ("B", 1, 2, 1), ("C", 2, 1, 2)])` is `[("A", 0, 4), ("B", 4, 6), ("C", 6, 7)]`: A runs first because it is alone, and it is not preempted.
--- starter
def priority_np(jobs):
    return [(name, arrival, arrival + burst) for name, arrival, burst, priority in jobs]
--- solution
def priority_np(jobs):
    order = sorted(range(len(jobs)), key=lambda i: (jobs[i][1], i))
    ready = []
    gantt = []
    t = 0
    k = 0
    while k < len(order) or ready:
        while k < len(order) and jobs[order[k]][1] <= t:
            ready.append(order[k])
            k += 1
        if not ready:
            t = jobs[order[k]][1]
            continue
        pick = min(ready, key=lambda i: (jobs[i][3], jobs[i][1], i))
        ready.remove(pick)
        name, arrival, burst, priority = jobs[pick]
        gantt.append((name, t, t + burst))
        t += burst
    return gantt
--- hint
The shape is the same as non-preemptive SJF: admit every job that has arrived by `t`, and jump `t` forward when nothing is ready.
--- hint
Choose with `min` and the key `(priority, arrival, position)`, where position is the job's index in `jobs`.
--- check case | The example from the task
priority_np([("A", 0, 4, 3), ("B", 1, 2, 1), ("C", 2, 1, 2)])
=> [("A", 0, 4), ("B", 4, 6), ("C", 6, 7)]
--- check case | Equal priorities go to the earlier arrival
priority_np([("A", 0, 4, 3), ("B", 1, 2, 1), ("C", 2, 1, 2), ("D", 2, 3, 1)])
=> [("A", 0, 4), ("B", 4, 6), ("D", 6, 9), ("C", 9, 10)]
--- check case | Same arrival and priority: list order
priority_np([("a", 0, 3, 2), ("b", 0, 3, 2)])
=> [("a", 0, 3), ("b", 3, 6)]
--- check case | No jobs, and one late job
(priority_np([]), priority_np([("x", 5, 2, 0)]))
=> ([], [("x", 5, 7)])
--- check case | Priority beats burst length
priority_np([("first", 0, 1, 5), ("long", 1, 9, 0), ("short", 1, 1, 7)])
=> [("first", 0, 1), ("long", 1, 10), ("short", 10, 11)]

+++ practice | Aging stops starvation
--- task
Write `aging_schedule(jobs, every)`, a preemptive priority scheduler with aging that runs one millisecond at a time. A job is `(name, arrival, burst, priority)`, and a smaller priority number is more important.

- A ready job is one that has arrived and is not finished. Each keeps a count of how many milliseconds it has waited since it last ran (0 when it arrives).
- Its **effective priority** is `max(0, priority - waited // every)`. If `every` is 0 there is no aging, and the effective priority is just `priority`.
- At each millisecond, run the ready job with the smallest effective priority. Ties go to the job that ran in the previous millisecond (if it is one of the tied jobs), then to the earlier arrival, then to the job that comes first in `jobs`. If no job is ready, the CPU is idle.
- The job that runs has its count reset to 0; every other ready job's count goes up by 1.

Return a dict from each job's name to its completion time.

For example, `aging_schedule([("A", 0, 3, 2), ("B", 0, 3, 2)], 1)` is `{"A": 5, "B": 6}`: with aging, two equal jobs take turns.
--- starter
def aging_schedule(jobs, every):
    return {name: arrival + burst for name, arrival, burst, priority in jobs}
--- solution
def aging_schedule(jobs, every):
    remaining = {name: burst for name, arrival, burst, priority in jobs}
    waited = {}
    completion = {}
    position = {job[0]: i for i, job in enumerate(jobs)}
    info = {job[0]: job for job in jobs}
    last = None
    t = 0
    while len(completion) < len(jobs):
        for name, arrival, burst, priority in jobs:
            if arrival == t:
                waited[name] = 0
        if not waited:
            t += 1
            last = None
            continue

        def effective(name):
            base = info[name][3]
            if every > 0:
                return max(0, base - waited[name] // every)
            return base

        pick = min(waited, key=lambda n: (effective(n), n != last, info[n][1], position[n]))
        for name in waited:
            waited[name] += 1
        waited[pick] = 0
        remaining[pick] -= 1
        t += 1
        last = pick
        if remaining[pick] == 0:
            del waited[pick]
            completion[pick] = t
    return completion
--- hint
Keep a dict from each ready job's name to its waiting count. Add a job to it at the millisecond it arrives, and remove it when it finishes.
--- hint
Pick with `min` over the ready names, with the key `(effective priority, name != last, arrival, position)`: `False` sorts before `True`, so the job that ran last wins a tie. Then add 1 to every count and set the chosen job's count back to 0.
--- check case | The example from the task
aging_schedule([("A", 0, 3, 2), ("B", 0, 3, 2)], 1)
=> {"A": 5, "B": 6}
--- check case | Without aging, equal jobs run one after the other
aging_schedule([("A", 0, 3, 2), ("B", 0, 3, 2)], 0)
=> {"A": 3, "B": 6}
--- check test | A stream of important jobs starves a low one; aging lets it through sooner
(lambda jobs: (aging_schedule(jobs, 0)["low"], aging_schedule(jobs, 1)["low"]) == (23, 17))([("low", 0, 3, 5)] + [(f"h{i}", i, 1, 1) for i in range(20)])
--- check case | A more important arrival preempts
aging_schedule([("A", 0, 2, 5), ("B", 1, 2, 0)], 1)
=> {"B": 3, "A": 4}
--- check case | Priority 0 cannot get any better
aging_schedule([("p", 0, 2, 0), ("q", 0, 2, 3)], 1)
=> {"p": 2, "q": 4}
--- check case | No jobs, and a job that arrives late
(aging_schedule([], 3), aging_schedule([("solo", 3, 2, 4)], 2))
=> ({}, {"solo": 5})

+++ practice | Choosing a quantum by measuring
--- task
Pick a round robin quantum the way engineers do: by simulating the workload.

Write `choose_quantum(jobs, candidates, switch, max_response)`. A job is `(name, arrival, burst)`. For each quantum in `candidates`, simulate round robin with context switches, using the rules from last lesson: arrivals join the queue in order, a job that arrives during a slice or at its end joins before the preempted job, and a slice costs `switch` extra time units first when the CPU's last slice was a different job and the CPU has not been idle since. For each job, record its response time (when its first slice actually starts, after any switch, minus its arrival) and its turnaround time (completion minus arrival).

A quantum is acceptable when the **largest** response time is at most `max_response`. Return the acceptable quantum with the smallest **average** turnaround; on a tie, the smaller quantum. If no quantum is acceptable, return `None`. `jobs` is never empty.

For example, for `[("A", 0, 40), ("B", 0, 40), ("C", 5, 2), ("D", 30, 3)]` with `switch` 1, quantum 10 gives an average turnaround of 54.0 and a largest response time of 17, and quantum 5 gives 54.75 and 9. So with `max_response` 20 the answer is 10, and with `max_response` 15 it is 5.
--- starter
def choose_quantum(jobs, candidates, switch, max_response):
    return max(candidates)
--- solution
from collections import deque


def simulate(jobs, quantum, switch):
    order = sorted(range(len(jobs)), key=lambda i: (jobs[i][1], i))
    remaining = {name: burst for name, arrival, burst in jobs}
    first = {}
    completion = {}
    queue = deque()
    t = 0
    k = 0
    last = None

    def admit(now):
        nonlocal k
        while k < len(order) and jobs[order[k]][1] <= now:
            queue.append(jobs[order[k]][0])
            k += 1

    admit(t)
    while queue or k < len(order):
        if not queue:
            t = jobs[order[k]][1]
            last = None
            admit(t)
            continue
        name = queue.popleft()
        if last is not None and last != name:
            t += switch
        first.setdefault(name, t)
        run = min(quantum, remaining[name])
        t += run
        remaining[name] -= run
        last = name
        admit(t)
        if remaining[name] > 0:
            queue.append(name)
        else:
            completion[name] = t
    return first, completion


def choose_quantum(jobs, candidates, switch, max_response):
    best = None
    for q in sorted(candidates):
        first, completion = simulate(jobs, q, switch)
        worst = max(first[name] - arrival for name, arrival, burst in jobs)
        if worst > max_response:
            continue
        average = sum(completion[name] - arrival for name, arrival, burst in jobs) / len(jobs)
        if best is None or average < best[0]:
            best = (average, q)
    return None if best is None else best[1]
--- hint
Write a helper that simulates one quantum and returns two dicts: each job's first start and each job's completion. Record the first start with `setdefault` just after charging the switch.
--- hint
Loop over the candidates in increasing order and keep the best `(average, quantum)` seen among the acceptable ones. Replace it only when the new average is strictly smaller, so a tie keeps the smaller quantum.
--- check case | The examples from the task
(choose_quantum([("A", 0, 40), ("B", 0, 40), ("C", 5, 2), ("D", 30, 3)], [1, 2, 5, 10, 20, 50], 1, 20), choose_quantum([("A", 0, 40), ("B", 0, 40), ("C", 5, 2), ("D", 30, 3)], [1, 2, 5, 10, 20, 50], 1, 15))
=> (10, 5)
--- check case | A tight response limit leaves only the smallest quantum
choose_quantum([("A", 0, 40), ("B", 0, 40), ("C", 5, 2), ("D", 30, 3)], [1, 2, 5, 10, 20, 50], 1, 4)
=> 1
--- check case | No quantum is fast enough
choose_quantum([("A", 0, 40), ("B", 0, 40), ("C", 5, 2), ("D", 30, 3)], [20, 50], 1, 2)
=> None
--- check case | Candidates in any order; the best overall wins when the limit is loose
choose_quantum([("A", 0, 40), ("B", 0, 40), ("C", 5, 2), ("D", 30, 3)], [50, 1, 10], 1, 100)
=> 10
--- check case | One job: every quantum gives the same turnaround, so the smallest wins
choose_quantum([("only", 0, 9)], [4, 2, 8], 3, 0)
=> 2
--- check case | Expensive switches favour a long quantum
choose_quantum([("a", 0, 12), ("b", 0, 12), ("c", 0, 12)], [1, 3, 12], 5, 100)
=> 12

+++ practice | Fair shares with virtual runtime
--- task
Model the idea behind Linux's CFS. Write `cfs(jobs, slice_ms)`. A job is `(name, arrival, burst, nice)`, and `WEIGHTS` (in the starter) gives the weight for each `nice` value.

- Each ready job has a **virtual runtime**, a whole number. When a job runs for `ran` milliseconds, its virtual runtime grows by `ran * 1024000 // weight`, so a heavier job's grows more slowly.
- When a job arrives, its virtual runtime starts at the **smallest** virtual runtime among the jobs already ready at that moment, or 0 if there are none. (Starting at 0 would let a newcomer hog the CPU until it caught up.) Jobs arriving at the same time are added one by one, in the order they appear in `jobs`.
- Whenever the CPU is free, admit every job that has arrived by now, then run the ready job with the smallest virtual runtime for `slice_ms` milliseconds, or less if it finishes sooner. It is not interrupted during that slice. Ties go to the earlier arrival, then to the job that comes first in `jobs`. If nothing is ready, the CPU is idle until the next arrival.

Return the Gantt chart as `(name, start, end)` segments, with back-to-back segments of the same job merged.

For example, `cfs([("a", 0, 6, 0), ("b", 0, 6, 0)], 2)` alternates: `[("a", 0, 2), ("b", 2, 4), ("a", 4, 6), ("b", 6, 8), ("a", 8, 10), ("b", 10, 12)]`.
--- starter
WEIGHTS = {-10: 9548, -5: 3121, 0: 1024, 5: 335, 10: 110}


def cfs(jobs, slice_ms):
    return []
--- solution
WEIGHTS = {-10: 9548, -5: 3121, 0: 1024, 5: 335, 10: 110}


def cfs(jobs, slice_ms):
    order = sorted(range(len(jobs)), key=lambda i: (jobs[i][1], i))
    remaining = {name: burst for name, arrival, burst, nice in jobs}
    vruntime = {}
    ready = []
    gantt = []
    t = 0
    k = 0
    while k < len(order) or ready:
        while k < len(order) and jobs[order[k]][1] <= t:
            i = order[k]
            vruntime[jobs[i][0]] = min((vruntime[jobs[j][0]] for j in ready), default=0)
            ready.append(i)
            k += 1
        if not ready:
            t = jobs[order[k]][1]
            continue
        pick = min(ready, key=lambda i: (vruntime[jobs[i][0]], jobs[i][1], i))
        name, arrival, burst, nice = jobs[pick]
        ran = min(slice_ms, remaining[name])
        if gantt and gantt[-1][0] == name and gantt[-1][2] == t:
            gantt[-1] = (name, gantt[-1][1], t + ran)
        else:
            gantt.append((name, t, t + ran))
        t += ran
        remaining[name] -= ran
        vruntime[name] += ran * 1024000 // WEIGHTS[nice]
        if remaining[name] == 0:
            ready.remove(pick)
    return gantt
--- hint
Keep the ready jobs in a list and each job's virtual runtime in a dict. When admitting a job, compute the `min` over the ready jobs' virtual runtimes before adding it, using `default=0` for an empty list.
--- hint
Pick with the key `(virtual runtime, arrival, position)`. After the slice, add `ran * 1024000 // WEIGHTS[nice]`, and remove the job from the ready list once it has no time left.
--- check case | The example from the task
cfs([("a", 0, 6, 0), ("b", 0, 6, 0)], 2)
=> [("a", 0, 2), ("b", 2, 4), ("a", 4, 6), ("b", 6, 8), ("a", 8, 10), ("b", 10, 12)]
--- check case | A heavier job gets most of the CPU
cfs([("nice", 0, 8, 5), ("mean", 0, 8, -5)], 2)
=> [("nice", 0, 2), ("mean", 2, 10), ("nice", 10, 16)]
--- check case | A newcomer starts at the smallest virtual runtime, not at 0
cfs([("a", 0, 10, 0), ("late", 5, 3, 0)], 2)
=> [("a", 0, 8), ("late", 8, 10), ("a", 10, 12), ("late", 12, 13)]
?? late arrives while a has 6000 of virtual runtime, so late starts at 6000, not 0.
--- check case | No jobs, and one job after an idle gap
(cfs([], 3), cfs([("x", 3, 2, 0)], 4))
=> ([], [("x", 3, 5)])
--- check test | Over a long run, CPU time follows the weights
(lambda g: (lambda share: 2.8 < share["w3121"] / share["w1024"] < 3.3)({n: sum(e - s for m, s, e in g if m == n and s < 3000) for n in ("w3121", "w1024")}))(cfs([("w1024", 0, 5000, 0), ("w3121", 0, 5000, -5)], 3))
--- check case | Three jobs arriving together are admitted in list order
cfs([("c", 0, 2, 0), ("a", 0, 2, 0), ("b", 0, 2, 0)], 1)
=> [("c", 0, 1), ("a", 1, 2), ("b", 2, 3), ("c", 3, 4), ("a", 4, 5), ("b", 5, 6)]

+++ practice | Debug: the scheduler that can be gamed
--- task
`mlfq_yield(jobs, quanta)` is an MLFQ that runs one millisecond at a time, like the lesson's task but with no boost, and with one addition: a job can **yield**. A job is `(name, arrival, burst, yield_every)`. After every `yield_every` milliseconds of CPU since it last yielded or moved down (0 means never), a job that is not finished gives up the CPU and goes to the back of its **current** level. If a job uses up its allotment on the same millisecond, it moves down instead.

Rule 4 says the time a job has used at a level counts across all its turns there, including turns that ended in a yield. The code breaks that rule. As a result, a job that yields every millisecond is never moved down, and keeps a CPU-heavy job waiting until it finishes. For example, `mlfq_yield([("hog", 0, 6, 0), ("game", 0, 6, 1)], [2, 4, 8])` should be `[("hog", 0, 2), ("game", 2, 4), ("hog", 4, 8), ("game", 8, 12)]`, but the game runs from 2 to 8 in one go. Find the bug and fix it.
--- starter
from collections import deque


def mlfq_yield(jobs, quanta):
    order = sorted(range(len(jobs)), key=lambda i: (jobs[i][1], i))
    remaining = {job[0]: job[2] for job in jobs}
    yield_every = {job[0]: job[3] for job in jobs}
    levels = [deque() for _ in quanta]
    used = {}
    streak = {}
    gantt = []
    t = 0
    k = 0
    while k < len(order) or any(levels):
        while k < len(order) and jobs[order[k]][1] == t:
            name = jobs[order[k]][0]
            levels[0].append(name)
            used[name] = 0
            streak[name] = 0
            k += 1
        top = next((lv for lv in range(len(levels)) if levels[lv]), None)
        if top is None:
            t += 1
            continue
        name = levels[top][0]
        if gantt and gantt[-1][0] == name and gantt[-1][2] == t:
            gantt[-1] = (name, gantt[-1][1], t + 1)
        else:
            gantt.append((name, t, t + 1))
        t += 1
        remaining[name] -= 1
        used[name] += 1
        streak[name] += 1
        if remaining[name] == 0:
            levels[top].popleft()
        elif used[name] == quanta[top]:
            levels[top].popleft()
            down = min(top + 1, len(levels) - 1)
            levels[down].append(name)
            used[name] = 0
            streak[name] = 0
        elif yield_every[name] and streak[name] == yield_every[name]:
            levels[top].popleft()
            levels[top].append(name)
            streak[name] = 0
            used[name] = 0
    return gantt
--- solution
from collections import deque


def mlfq_yield(jobs, quanta):
    order = sorted(range(len(jobs)), key=lambda i: (jobs[i][1], i))
    remaining = {job[0]: job[2] for job in jobs}
    yield_every = {job[0]: job[3] for job in jobs}
    levels = [deque() for _ in quanta]
    used = {}
    streak = {}
    gantt = []
    t = 0
    k = 0
    while k < len(order) or any(levels):
        while k < len(order) and jobs[order[k]][1] == t:
            name = jobs[order[k]][0]
            levels[0].append(name)
            used[name] = 0
            streak[name] = 0
            k += 1
        top = next((lv for lv in range(len(levels)) if levels[lv]), None)
        if top is None:
            t += 1
            continue
        name = levels[top][0]
        if gantt and gantt[-1][0] == name and gantt[-1][2] == t:
            gantt[-1] = (name, gantt[-1][1], t + 1)
        else:
            gantt.append((name, t, t + 1))
        t += 1
        remaining[name] -= 1
        used[name] += 1
        streak[name] += 1
        if remaining[name] == 0:
            levels[top].popleft()
        elif used[name] == quanta[top]:
            levels[top].popleft()
            down = min(top + 1, len(levels) - 1)
            levels[down].append(name)
            used[name] = 0
            streak[name] = 0
        elif yield_every[name] and streak[name] == yield_every[name]:
            levels[top].popleft()
            levels[top].append(name)
            streak[name] = 0
    return gantt
--- hint
Where does the code change a job's used time? Only two events should set it back to 0: arriving, and moving down a level.
--- hint
Remove the line that resets `used[name]` when a job yields. The streak counter, which decides when the next yield happens, is the only thing a yield resets.
--- check case | The example from the task
mlfq_yield([("hog", 0, 6, 0), ("game", 0, 6, 1)], [2, 4, 8])
=> [("hog", 0, 2), ("game", 2, 4), ("hog", 4, 8), ("game", 8, 12)]
--- check case | A game that arrives later still moves down
mlfq_yield([("hog", 0, 20, 0), ("game", 1, 10, 1)], [2, 4])
=> [("hog", 0, 2), ("game", 2, 4), ("hog", 4, 8), ("game", 8, 9), ("hog", 9, 13), ("game", 13, 14), ("hog", 14, 18), ("game", 18, 19), ("hog", 19, 23), ("game", 23, 24), ("hog", 24, 26), ("game", 26, 30)]
--- check case | Two yielding jobs take turns and still move down
mlfq_yield([("a", 0, 6, 2), ("b", 0, 6, 2)], [3, 6])
=> [("a", 0, 2), ("b", 2, 4), ("a", 4, 5), ("b", 5, 6), ("a", 6, 8), ("b", 8, 10), ("a", 10, 11), ("b", 11, 12)]
--- check case | Jobs that never yield, and no jobs
(mlfq_yield([("a", 0, 5, 0)], [2, 4]), mlfq_yield([], [2]))
=> ([("a", 0, 5)], [])
--- check case | A lone yielding job runs straight through
mlfq_yield([("x", 2, 3, 1)], [2, 3])
=> [("x", 2, 5)]

+++ practice | Priority inversion on Mars
--- task
Replay the Pathfinder bug. Write `run_tasks(tasks, inherit)`, a preemptive fixed-priority scheduler with one lock, running one millisecond at a time. A task is `(name, priority, arrival, script)`; a **smaller** priority number is more important. A script is a list of steps: `("run", n)` needs `n` milliseconds of CPU, `("lock",)` takes the lock, and `("unlock",)` releases it.

At each millisecond `t`:

- Among the tasks that have arrived, are not finished and are not waiting for the lock, pick the one with the best **effective** priority. Ties go to the earlier arrival, then to the task that comes first in `tasks`.
- Lock and unlock steps take no time. Handle them one after another until the chosen task reaches a `run` step, then run it for this millisecond (one unit off that step).
  - `("lock",)`: if the lock is free, the task takes it and goes on. Otherwise the task starts waiting, and you pick again among the others, in this same millisecond.
  - `("unlock",)`: the lock is released. If tasks are waiting, the one with the best **own** priority (ties: earlier arrival, then list order) gets the lock at once, stops waiting and moves past its lock step.
- If no task can run, the CPU is idle this millisecond.
- A task finishes as soon as its last step is done: at `t + 1` if that step was its last `run` millisecond (run from `t` to `t + 1`), and at `t` if it was a lock or unlock handled at `t`. A task with an empty script finishes when it arrives.
- Effective priority: without inheritance it is the task's own priority. With `inherit` true, the task holding the lock runs at the best of its own priority and the priorities of the tasks waiting for the lock.

Return a dict from each task's name to its completion time.

For example, with L (priority 3, arrives 0: lock, run 4, unlock, run 1), H (priority 1, arrives 1: lock, run 1, unlock) and M (priority 2, arrives 2: run 10), H finishes at 15 without inheritance, stuck behind M, and at 5 with it.
--- starter
def run_tasks(tasks, inherit):
    return {name: arrival for name, priority, arrival, script in tasks}
--- solution
def run_tasks(tasks, inherit):
    steps = {name: list(script) for name, priority, arrival, script in tasks}
    info = {task[0]: task for task in tasks}
    position = {task[0]: i for i, task in enumerate(tasks)}
    owner = None
    waiters = []
    completion = {}
    t = 0

    def effective(name):
        best = info[name][1]
        if inherit and name == owner:
            for w in waiters:
                best = min(best, info[w][1])
        return best

    def finish_if_done(name, when):
        if not steps[name]:
            completion[name] = when

    while len(completion) < len(tasks):
        for name in steps:
            if info[name][2] == t:
                finish_if_done(name, t)
        while True:
            ready = [n for n in steps if info[n][2] <= t and n not in completion and n not in waiters]
            if not ready:
                break
            name = min(ready, key=lambda n: (effective(n), info[n][2], position[n]))
            script = steps[name]
            kind = script[0][0]
            if kind == "lock":
                if owner is None:
                    owner = name
                    script.pop(0)
                    finish_if_done(name, t)
                else:
                    waiters.append(name)
                continue
            if kind == "unlock":
                script.pop(0)
                owner = None
                if waiters:
                    nxt = min(waiters, key=lambda n: (info[n][1], info[n][2], position[n]))
                    waiters.remove(nxt)
                    owner = nxt
                    steps[nxt].pop(0)
                    finish_if_done(nxt, t)
                finish_if_done(name, t)
                continue
            left = script[0][1] - 1
            if left:
                script[0] = ("run", left)
            else:
                script.pop(0)
            finish_if_done(name, t + 1)
            break
        t += 1
    return completion
--- hint
Copy each script into a list you can change, and keep the lock's owner (or `None`) and the list of waiting tasks. Inside each millisecond, use a loop: pick a task, and if it only did a lock, unlock or finish step, go round and pick again.
--- hint
For a `run` step, subtract one from its count (removing the step when it reaches 0) and end this millisecond's loop. With inheritance, the owner's effective priority is the `min` of its own and every waiter's priority.
--- check case | The example from the task, without and with inheritance
(lambda T: (run_tasks(T, False), run_tasks(T, True)))([("L", 3, 0, [("lock",), ("run", 4), ("unlock",), ("run", 1)]), ("H", 1, 1, [("lock",), ("run", 1), ("unlock",)]), ("M", 2, 2, [("run", 10)])])
=> ({"M": 12, "H": 15, "L": 16}, {"H": 5, "M": 15, "L": 16})
--- check case | No lock in use: plain preemptive priorities
run_tasks([("low", 5, 0, [("run", 4)]), ("high", 1, 2, [("run", 1)])], False)
=> {"high": 3, "low": 5}
--- check case | Without a medium task there is no inversion to fix
(lambda T: (run_tasks(T, False), run_tasks(T, True)))([("L", 3, 0, [("lock",), ("run", 3), ("unlock",)]), ("H", 1, 1, [("lock",), ("run", 2), ("unlock",)])])
=> ({"L": 3, "H": 5}, {"L": 3, "H": 5})
--- check case | Two waiters: the more important one gets the lock first
run_tasks([("L", 5, 0, [("lock",), ("run", 2), ("unlock",)]), ("A", 3, 1, [("lock",), ("run", 1), ("unlock",)]), ("B", 1, 1, [("lock",), ("run", 1), ("unlock",)])], True)
=> {"L": 2, "B": 3, "A": 4}
--- check case | An empty script finishes at its arrival; the CPU idles until tasks arrive
run_tasks([("idle", 1, 3, []), ("later", 2, 5, [("run", 2)])], False)
=> {"idle": 3, "later": 7}
--- check case | Work after the lock keeps the task's own priority
run_tasks([("L", 3, 0, [("lock",), ("run", 2), ("unlock",), ("run", 5)]), ("H", 1, 1, [("lock",), ("run", 1), ("unlock",)]), ("M", 2, 1, [("run", 3)])], True)
=> {"H": 3, "M": 6, "L": 11}

+++ question | Which MLFQ rule stops gaming
--- ask
A program computes for 9 ms of its 10 ms quantum, then does a tiny read that returns at once, again and again. Under which version of the MLFQ rules does it stay at the top level for ever?
--- choice
When a job's used time counts across all its turns at a level, and it moves down once the total reaches the allotment.
--- choice correct
When a job's used time is reset every time it gives up the CPU, so it only moves down if it uses a whole quantum in one turn.
--- choice
When every job is boosted to the top level every S milliseconds.
--- why
If giving up the CPU resets the count, the program never uses a full quantum in a single turn, so it is never moved down. Counting the time used at a level across every turn (Rule 4) moves it down after about one allotment, whatever it does in between. A boost lifts every job equally, so it does not help one job cheat.

+++ question | The bound in the aging argument
--- ask
A scheduler uses aging: a job's effective priority improves by 1 for every 20 ms it waits, down to 0, the best value. A job with base priority 7 is ready and waiting. At most how many milliseconds of waiting pass before its effective priority reaches 0? Type a whole number.
--- answer
140
--- why
Each 20 ms of waiting improves it by 1, and it needs 7 steps to get from 7 to 0: 7 × 20 = 140 ms. After that no job can be strictly better than it, so only the finitely many jobs already tied with it at 0 can still go first. That is why its total wait is bounded.

+++ question | What priority inheritance changes
--- ask
With priority inheritance on, a low-priority task L holds a lock, and a high-priority task H is waiting for it. A medium-priority task M becomes ready. What happens?
--- choice
M runs, because it is more important than L's own priority.
--- choice correct
L keeps the CPU, running at H's priority until it releases the lock. Then H takes the lock and runs, and M runs after H.
--- choice
H takes the lock away from L, because H is more important.
--- choice
All three share the CPU in round robin until the lock is free.
--- why
While H waits for a lock that L holds, L inherits H's priority, so M cannot preempt it. L finishes its critical section quickly and releases the lock, and H runs next. Locks are never taken away from their holder: that could leave the shared data half-updated.

=== os-05 | Threads and race conditions
--- teach
So far every process has done one thing at a time. But a ground-station program has several jobs at once: receive packets from the radio, decode them, write them to disk and redraw the screen. Separate processes could do it, but processes do not share memory, so every packet would have to be copied from one to the next through the kernel. This lesson introduces **threads**: several flows of execution inside **one** process, sharing its memory. Sharing is what makes them fast, and sharing is what makes them dangerous. By the end you will have a simulator that runs threads step by step and finds every result a group of threads can produce.

### What a thread is

A **thread** is one sequence of instructions being executed inside a process. In everyday words: a process is a kitchen; threads are the cooks in it. Each cook follows their own recipe at their own pace, but they share the fridge, the counters and the pots.

Each thread has its **own**:

- program counter (where it is in the code) and registers;
- **stack**, holding its function calls and local variables.

All threads of a process **share**:

- the code, the global variables and the heap;
- the open files, and everything else in the PCB.

```text
one process, two threads
+------------------+
| code             |  shared
| globals          |  shared
| heap             |  shared
|        ...       |
| stack, thread 2  |  private to thread 2
| stack, thread 1  |  private to thread 1
+------------------+
```

The kernel keeps a small record per thread, a **thread control block** (TCB), with its saved registers, its stack pointer and its state (ready, running or blocked, as in lesson 2). The scheduler of lessons 3 and 4 really schedules threads: a process with four threads can be running on four cores at once.

### Why use threads

- **Overlapping waits.** While one thread is blocked waiting for the disk, another can compute. A web server can serve one client while another's request is still arriving.
- **Parallelism.** On a machine with several cores, threads of one process run at the same time, truly in parallel.
- **Cheap sharing.** Threads exchange data by writing it to memory they all see. No copying, no system calls.
- **Cheap to make and switch.** Creating a thread takes microseconds, much less than a process, because there is no memory to copy. Switching between two threads of the same process leaves the memory map alone, so the CPU's cached address translations stay valid.

### Threads in real code

In C++, `std::thread` starts a function on a new thread, and `join` waits for it to finish:

```cpp fragment
#include <thread>

int hits = 0;                         // shared by both threads

void count_hits() {
    for (int i = 0; i < 1000000; i++) hits++;
}

int main() {
    std::thread a(count_hits);
    std::thread b(count_hits);
    a.join();
    b.join();
    // hits is usually NOT 2000000
}
```

Python's `threading` module does the same:

```python fragment
import threading

t = threading.Thread(target=count_hits)
t.start()          # runs count_hits on a new thread
t.join()           # wait for it to finish
```

Python threads are real operating-system threads, but by default the [[global interpreter lock|gil]] lets only one of them run Python code at a time. ORBIT's Python runs in your browser, where threads cannot start at all, so in this course you will simulate them. The C++ above is a fragment for the same reason: ORBIT runs C++ as WebAssembly, on a single thread.

### A race condition

Why is `hits` usually not 2,000,000? Because `hits++` is not one step. The CPU does it as three:

1. **load** the value of `hits` from memory into a register;
2. **add** 1 to the register;
3. **store** the register back into `hits`.

Python is no different. Its bytecode for `count += 1`, shown by the `dis` module, is a load, an add and a store:

```text
LOAD_GLOBAL   count
LOAD_CONST    1
BINARY_OP     +=
STORE_GLOBAL  count
```

Each thread has its own registers. So here is one way two threads, each adding 1 once, can run, starting from `hits = 5`:

| step | thread A | thread B | hits |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | load: register = 5 | | 5 |
| 2 | | load: register = 5 | 5 |
| 3 | add: register = 6 | | 5 |
| 4 | | add: register = 6 | 5 |
| 5 | store 6 | | 6 |
| 6 | | store 6 | 6 |

Two additions, and `hits` went up by one. B's store wiped out A's. This is a **lost update**.

A **race condition** is a bug where the result depends on the timing of threads: on which one gets to a point first. The special case here, two threads touching the same memory at the same time, at least one of them writing, with nothing forcing an order between them, is called a **data race**. In C++ a data race is undefined behaviour (from Systems Programming): the compiler may assume it never happens.

### Interleavings

A thread runs as a sequence of **atomic steps**: steps that happen all at once, which no other thread can see half-done. A single load or store of a machine word is atomic; `hits++` is three steps. When threads run, their steps are merged into one sequence, an **interleaving** (or **schedule**). Each thread's own steps stay in order, but the threads' steps can mix in any way. Which interleaving happens depends on the scheduler, interrupts and other cores: things the program does not control.

How many interleavings are there? The claim: **two threads with m and n steps have C(m + n, m) = (m + n)! / (m! · n!) interleavings.**

- An interleaving has m + n positions. Once you know **which** positions belong to the first thread, the interleaving is fixed, because each thread's steps must fill its positions in order.
- Every choice of m positions out of m + n gives a different interleaving, and every interleaving comes from one such choice. So the count is the number of ways to choose m positions out of m + n: C(m + n, m).
- For k threads the same argument gives (n₁ + … + nₖ)! / (n₁! ⋯ nₖ!).

The numbers explode. Two threads of three steps have C(6, 3) = 20 interleavings. Two threads of 20 steps have C(40, 20), about 138 billion. That is why testing cannot show that threaded code is correct: a bad interleaving might happen once in a billion runs, and then only on a customer's machine. Such bugs, which vanish when you look for them, are called [[heisenbugs|heisenbug]].

### Simulating threads with generators

To study interleavings we need threads we can control step by step. Python's generators from the advanced Python course are exactly that. Write each thread as a generator function, and let `yield` mark the points where the scheduler may switch to another thread. **The code between two `yield`s is one atomic step.** Calling `next` on the generator runs its next step.

Here are two "talker" threads sharing a list:

```python
def talker(log, name, times):
    for i in range(times):
        log.append(f"{name}{i}")
        yield

log = []
threads = [talker(log, "A", 2), talker(log, "B", 2)]
for i in [0, 1, 1, 0]:
    next(threads[i])
print(log)        # ['A0', 'B0', 'B1', 'A1']
```

The list `[0, 1, 1, 0]` is a schedule: "thread 0 takes a step, then thread 1, then thread 1, then thread 0". A different schedule gives a different log.

A thread with k `yield`s takes k + 1 steps: the last `next` runs the code after the final `yield` and then raises `StopIteration`, which means "this thread has finished". That last step can do real work. Above, one more `next(threads[0])` would find no more loop to run and raise `StopIteration`.

To split the `+= 1` race into its real steps, put a `yield` between the load and the store, and keep the loaded value in a local variable, which plays the part of a register:

```python fragment
value = shared["level"]      # load
yield                        # another thread may run here
shared["level"] = value + 1  # store
```

### Exploring every interleaving

To find **every** result a group of threads can produce, try every interleaving. At each point, any unfinished thread may take the next step. That is a tree: the root is "nothing has run", each branch is one thread's step, and each leaf is a complete run. Walking it depth-first finds every leaf.

There is one snag. A generator cannot be copied, so after exploring one branch you cannot go back to the state before it. The fix is **replay**: to reach any point of the tree, build fresh threads and fresh memory from scratch and run the schedule that leads there. That is why the explorer takes a `setup` function rather than threads: each call hands back brand-new memory and brand-new generators. Replaying costs one run of the prefix per node, so the whole search costs about (number of interleavings) × (length of a run). That is fine for the tiny programs where races live.

### Where it is used

- **Servers** such as databases and web servers run a pool of threads, one per request in progress.
- **Flight software** splits work into threads (an RTOS calls them tasks): attitude control, telemetry, payload.
- **Every graphical app** keeps one thread for the screen and moves slow work to others, so the window never freezes.
- **Race-finding tools** such as ThreadSanitizer and [[model checkers|model-checking]] do what your explorer does, far more cleverly.

**Watch out:**

- **`x += 1` is not atomic,** in C++, in Java, or in Python, GIL or not. Any read-then-write of shared data is a race unless something forces the threads to take turns (next lesson).
- **A passing test proves little.** A race that loses one update in a million runs will pass almost every test.
- **Every thread's final step counts.** The step that ends in `StopIteration` runs the code after the last `yield`. A simulator that ignores it loses that thread's last action.

::: context gil One lock for the whole interpreter
The global interpreter lock (GIL) is a lock inside CPython, the standard Python interpreter. A thread must hold it to run Python bytecode, so only one thread runs Python code at a time, even on many cores. The GIL is released while a thread waits for I/O, so threads still help programs that mostly wait. It does not stop races: the GIL can be passed to another thread between the load and the store of `count += 1`. Python 3.13 added an optional build without the GIL, and 3.14 supports it officially.
:::

::: context heisenbug Bugs that hide when watched
A heisenbug is a bug that disappears or changes when you try to study it, named after the physicist Werner Heisenberg. Adding a print statement or running under a debugger changes the timing of threads, and the bad interleaving stops happening. In 1985 to 1987, races in the software of the Therac-25 radiation therapy machine caused massive overdoses to six patients; the fault showed up only when an operator typed quickly enough to hit a narrow timing window.
:::

::: context model-checking Proving there is no bad interleaving
A model checker explores every state a system can reach, looking for one that breaks a rule, such as "two threads are never both in the critical section". Tools like TLA+ and SPIN do this for designs of concurrent and distributed systems. Engineers at Amazon Web Services have used TLA+ to find subtle bugs in the designs of storage systems before writing the code. Your explorer is a small model checker: it tries every interleaving and collects every result.
:::
--- task
Write the two parts of a thread simulator. A **thread** is a generator: each `next` on it runs one atomic step, and a step that raises `StopIteration` means the thread has finished (that step still counts, because it ran the code after the last `yield`).

- `run(threads, schedule)`: `threads` is a list of generator objects, and `schedule` is a list of thread indexes. For each index in the schedule, if that thread has finished, skip it; otherwise run one step of it. When the schedule runs out, run every unfinished thread to the end: thread 0 until it finishes, then thread 1, and so on. Return the **trace**: the list of indexes of the steps that actually ran, in order, including the finishing steps.
- `outcomes(setup, key)`: `setup()` returns a pair `(mem, threads)`, fresh every time it is called: a dict of shared memory and a list of new generators that use it. Try **every** interleaving: at every point, each unfinished thread may take the next step, until all have finished. Return the sorted list of the distinct values `mem[key]` has at the end of a complete run.

The starter gives four thread functions that the checks use: `incrementer(mem, times)` adds 1 to `mem["count"]` `times` times, as a load step and a store step; `atomic_incrementer(mem, times)` does each addition in one step; `writer(mem, key, values)` stores each value in turn; `announcer(mem, name)` appends `name + " start"` to `mem["log"]` in its first step and `name + " end"` in its second, finishing step. Leave them as they are.
