        sems = {f"f{i}": 1 for i in range(n)}
        if seats is not None:
            sems["seats"] = seats
        return make_kernel(mem, sems, [philosopher(mem, i, n, meals) for i in range(n)])
    return setup


def eat(mem, me, n):
    if mem["eating"][(me + 1) % n] or mem["eating"][me - 1]:
        mem["clash"] = True
    mem["eating"][me] = True
    mem["meals"][me] += 1


def naive(mem, me, n, meals):
    for _ in range(meals):
        yield ("P", f"f{me}")
        yield ("P", f"f{(me + 1) % n}")
        eat(mem, me, n)
        yield
        mem["eating"][me] = False
        yield ("V", f"f{me}")
        yield ("V", f"f{(me + 1) % n}")


def seated(mem, me, n, meals):
    for _ in range(meals):
        yield ("P", "seats")
        yield ("P", f"f{me}")
        yield ("P", f"f{(me + 1) % n}")
        eat(mem, me, n)
        yield
        mem["eating"][me] = False
        yield ("V", f"f{me}")
        yield ("V", f"f{(me + 1) % n}")
        yield ("V", "seats")


def waiter_setup(n, meals):
    return table_setup(seated, n, meals, n - 1)
--- hint
The philosopher is `naive` with a P on `"seats"` before the forks and a V on it after them.
--- hint
With n seats, all n philosophers can sit down and each pick up their left fork: the deadlock is back. One seat fewer is enough to break it.
--- check case | Three philosophers, two meals each
explore(waiter_setup(3, 2), 400, lambda m: m["clash"], lambda m: m["meals"] == [2, 2, 2])
=> None
--- check case | Four philosophers, one meal each
explore(waiter_setup(4, 1), 400, lambda m: m["clash"], lambda m: m["meals"] == [1, 1, 1, 1])
=> None
--- check test | With a seat for everyone, the deadlock comes back
(lambda r: r is not None and r[0] == "deadlock")(explore(table_setup(seated, 3, 1, 3), 200, lambda m: m["clash"], lambda m: m["meals"] == [1, 1, 1]))
--- check test | The waiter holds back the last philosopher
(lambda k: ([step(k, i) for i in [0, 1, 2]], k["status"], k["waiting"]["seats"])[1:])(waiter_setup(3, 1)()) == (["ready", "ready", "blocked"], [2])
--- check test | Two philosophers get one seat between them
(lambda k: ([step(k, 0), step(k, 1)], k["sem"]["seats"], k["status"])[1:])(waiter_setup(2, 1)()) == (0, ["ready", "blocked"])
--- check test | 200 random schedules with five philosophers, the seats all given back
all(random_safe(waiter_setup(5, 3), s, lambda m: m["clash"]) for s in range(200)) and (lambda k: (run(k, []), k["sem"]["seats"])[1])(waiter_setup(5, 2)()) == 4

+++ practice | The sleeping barber
--- task
Write the sleeping barber for the simulated kernel. The semaphores are `"customers"` and `"barber"` (both starting at 0) and `"mutex"` (starting at 1). The shared memory has `"chairs"` (waiting chairs), `"waiting"` (how many are taken), `"cuts"`, and the lists `"served"` and `"left"`.

- `barber(mem)` loops forever: `yield ("P", "customers")`; `yield ("P", "mutex")`; take 1 from `mem["waiting"]` and `yield ("V", "barber")`; `yield ("V", "mutex")`; add 1 to `mem["cuts"]` and `yield`.
- `customer(mem, me)`: `yield ("P", "mutex")`. Then, if `mem["waiting"]` is less than `mem["chairs"]`: add 1 to it and `yield ("V", "customers")`; `yield ("V", "mutex")`; `yield ("P", "barber")`; append `me` to `mem["served"]`. Otherwise: append `me` to `mem["left"]` and `yield ("V", "mutex")`.

The barber never finishes, so a run ends with him asleep on `"customers"`. The starter's `explore` takes `idle=("customers",)` to accept that ending, and still reports any other thread left blocked as a deadlock. `shop_setup(chairs, customers)` builds the shop with the barber as thread 0.
--- starter
import random


def make_kernel(mem, sems, threads):
    return {
        "mem": mem,
        "sem": dict(sems),
        "waiting": {name: [] for name in sems},
        "threads": list(threads),
        "status": ["ready"] * len(threads),
    }


def step(k, i):
    try:
        request = next(k["threads"][i])
    except StopIteration:
        k["status"][i] = "done"
        return
    if request is None:
        return
    op, name = request
    if op == "P":
        if k["sem"][name] > 0:
            k["sem"][name] -= 1
        else:
            k["status"][i] = "blocked"
            k["waiting"][name].append(i)
    elif op == "V":
        if k["waiting"][name]:
            j = k["waiting"][name].pop(0)
            k["status"][j] = "ready"
        else:
            k["sem"][name] += 1


def run(k, schedule):
    trace = []
    for i in schedule:
        if k["status"][i] == "ready":
            step(k, i)
            trace.append(i)
    while "ready" in k["status"]:
        i = k["status"].index("ready")
        step(k, i)
        trace.append(i)
    return trace


def where(k, counts):
    spots = []
    for g, c in zip(k["threads"], counts):
        f = getattr(g, "gi_frame", None)
        spots.append((c, None) if f is None else (c, f.f_lasti, repr(sorted(f.f_locals.items()))))
    return repr((k["mem"], k["sem"], k["waiting"], k["status"], spots))


def explore(setup, max_steps, bad=None, final=None):
    seen = set()

    def search(prefix):
        k = setup()
        counts = [0] * len(k["threads"])
        for i in prefix:
            step(k, i)
            counts[i] += 1
        key = where(k, counts)
        if key in seen:
            return None
        seen.add(key)
        if bad is not None and bad(k["mem"]):
            return ("bad", prefix)
        ready = [i for i, s in enumerate(k["status"]) if s == "ready"]
        if not ready:
            if "blocked" in k["status"]:
                return ("deadlock", prefix)
            if final is not None and not final(k["mem"]):
                return ("wrong end", prefix)
            return None
        if len(prefix) == max_steps:
            return None
        for i in ready:
            found = search(prefix + [i])
            if found is not None:
                return found
        return None

    return search([])


def explore_idle(setup, max_steps, bad=None, final=None, idle=()):
    seen = set()

    def search(prefix):
        k = setup()
        counts = [0] * len(k["threads"])
        for i in prefix:
            step(k, i)
            counts[i] += 1
        key = where(k, counts)
        if key in seen:
            return None
        seen.add(key)
        if bad is not None and bad(k["mem"]):
            return ("bad", prefix)
        ready = [i for i, s in enumerate(k["status"]) if s == "ready"]
        if not ready:
            sleeping = [i for name in idle for i in k["waiting"][name]]
            if k["status"].count("blocked") > len(sleeping):
                return ("deadlock", prefix)
            if final is not None and not final(k["mem"]):
                return ("wrong end", prefix)
            return None
        if len(prefix) == max_steps:
            return None
        for i in ready:
            found = search(prefix + [i])
            if found is not None:
                return found
        return None

    return search([])


def shop_setup(chairs, customers):
    def setup():
        mem = {"chairs": chairs, "waiting": 0, "cuts": 0, "served": [], "left": []}
        threads = [barber(mem)] + [customer(mem, c) for c in range(customers)]
        return make_kernel(mem, {"customers": 0, "barber": 0, "mutex": 1}, threads)
    return setup


def barber(mem):
    yield


def customer(mem, me):
    yield
--- solution
import random


def make_kernel(mem, sems, threads):
    return {
        "mem": mem,
        "sem": dict(sems),
        "waiting": {name: [] for name in sems},
        "threads": list(threads),
        "status": ["ready"] * len(threads),
    }


def step(k, i):
    try:
        request = next(k["threads"][i])
    except StopIteration:
        k["status"][i] = "done"
        return
    if request is None:
        return
    op, name = request
    if op == "P":
        if k["sem"][name] > 0:
            k["sem"][name] -= 1
        else:
            k["status"][i] = "blocked"
            k["waiting"][name].append(i)
    elif op == "V":
        if k["waiting"][name]:
            j = k["waiting"][name].pop(0)
            k["status"][j] = "ready"
        else:
            k["sem"][name] += 1


def run(k, schedule):
    trace = []
    for i in schedule:
        if k["status"][i] == "ready":
            step(k, i)
            trace.append(i)
    while "ready" in k["status"]:
        i = k["status"].index("ready")
        step(k, i)
        trace.append(i)
    return trace


def where(k, counts):
    spots = []
    for g, c in zip(k["threads"], counts):
        f = getattr(g, "gi_frame", None)
        spots.append((c, None) if f is None else (c, f.f_lasti, repr(sorted(f.f_locals.items()))))
    return repr((k["mem"], k["sem"], k["waiting"], k["status"], spots))


def explore(setup, max_steps, bad=None, final=None):
    seen = set()

    def search(prefix):
        k = setup()
        counts = [0] * len(k["threads"])
        for i in prefix:
            step(k, i)
            counts[i] += 1
        key = where(k, counts)
        if key in seen:
            return None
        seen.add(key)
        if bad is not None and bad(k["mem"]):
            return ("bad", prefix)
        ready = [i for i, s in enumerate(k["status"]) if s == "ready"]
        if not ready:
            if "blocked" in k["status"]:
                return ("deadlock", prefix)
            if final is not None and not final(k["mem"]):
                return ("wrong end", prefix)
            return None
        if len(prefix) == max_steps:
            return None
        for i in ready:
            found = search(prefix + [i])
            if found is not None:
                return found
        return None

    return search([])


def explore_idle(setup, max_steps, bad=None, final=None, idle=()):
    seen = set()

    def search(prefix):
        k = setup()
        counts = [0] * len(k["threads"])
        for i in prefix:
            step(k, i)
            counts[i] += 1
        key = where(k, counts)
        if key in seen:
            return None
        seen.add(key)
        if bad is not None and bad(k["mem"]):
            return ("bad", prefix)
        ready = [i for i, s in enumerate(k["status"]) if s == "ready"]
        if not ready:
            sleeping = [i for name in idle for i in k["waiting"][name]]
            if k["status"].count("blocked") > len(sleeping):
                return ("deadlock", prefix)
            if final is not None and not final(k["mem"]):
                return ("wrong end", prefix)
            return None
        if len(prefix) == max_steps:
            return None
        for i in ready:
            found = search(prefix + [i])
            if found is not None:
                return found
        return None

    return search([])


def shop_setup(chairs, customers):
    def setup():
        mem = {"chairs": chairs, "waiting": 0, "cuts": 0, "served": [], "left": []}
        threads = [barber(mem)] + [customer(mem, c) for c in range(customers)]
        return make_kernel(mem, {"customers": 0, "barber": 0, "mutex": 1}, threads)
    return setup


def barber(mem):
    while True:
        yield ("P", "customers")
        yield ("P", "mutex")
        mem["waiting"] -= 1
        yield ("V", "barber")
        yield ("V", "mutex")
        mem["cuts"] += 1
        yield


def customer(mem, me):
    yield ("P", "mutex")
    if mem["waiting"] < mem["chairs"]:
        mem["waiting"] += 1
        yield ("V", "customers")
        yield ("V", "mutex")
        yield ("P", "barber")
        mem["served"].append(me)
    else:
        mem["left"].append(me)
        yield ("V", "mutex")
--- hint
The barber is a `while True` loop of five steps. The customer has one step, then an `if` with two branches: the branch that sits down has three more steps, and the branch that leaves has one.
--- hint
Both branches of the customer must end by releasing `"mutex"`. The customer who sits down does V on `"customers"` before V on `"mutex"`, and only then waits on `"barber"`.
--- check case | One chair, three customers: every schedule is safe and ends properly
explore_idle(shop_setup(1, 3), 400, lambda m: m["waiting"] > m["chairs"], lambda m: sorted(m["served"] + m["left"]) == [0, 1, 2] and m["cuts"] == len(m["served"]) and m["waiting"] == 0, idle=("customers",))
=> None
--- check case | Two chairs, three customers
explore_idle(shop_setup(2, 3), 400, lambda m: m["waiting"] > m["chairs"], lambda m: sorted(m["served"] + m["left"]) == [0, 1, 2] and m["cuts"] == len(m["served"]), idle=("customers",))
=> None
--- check test | With one chair, some schedule turns a customer away
(lambda r: r is not None and r[0] == "bad")(explore_idle(shop_setup(1, 3), 400, lambda m: len(m["left"]) > 0, None, idle=("customers",)))
--- check test | With three chairs for three customers, nobody ever leaves
explore_idle(shop_setup(3, 3), 400, lambda m: len(m["left"]) > 0, lambda m: sorted(m["served"]) == [0, 1, 2], idle=("customers",)) is None
--- check test | The barber sleeps until the first customer arrives
(lambda k: (step(k, 0), k["status"], k["waiting"]["customers"])[1:])(shop_setup(2, 1)()) == (["blocked", "ready"], [0])
--- check test | A customer who arrives first is remembered: no lost wakeup
(lambda k: (run(k, [1, 1, 1, 0, 0, 0, 0, 1, 0]), k["mem"]["served"], k["mem"]["cuts"], k["status"], k["sem"])[1:])(shop_setup(1, 1)()) == ([0], 1, ["blocked", "done"], {"customers": 0, "barber": 0, "mutex": 1})
--- check test | No chairs: everyone leaves, and mutex is always given back
(lambda k: (run(k, []), k["mem"]["left"], k["mem"]["served"], k["sem"]["mutex"], k["status"])[1:])(shop_setup(0, 3)()) == ([0, 1, 2], [], 1, ["blocked", "done", "done", "done"])

+++ practice | A readers-writer lock that says no
--- task
Some programs cannot block: a flight computer's control loop must never sleep waiting for a lock. They use **try-locks**, which answer at once. Write a class `RWLock` with these methods, each returning `True` or `False`:

- `try_read(t)`: thread `t` becomes a reader if no thread is writing and `t` does not already hold the lock in any mode. Otherwise `False`.
- `try_write(t)`: thread `t` becomes the writer if nobody holds the lock. As a special case, if `t` is the **only** reader, it is **upgraded**: it stops being a reader and becomes the writer, and the answer is `True`. Otherwise (other readers inside, another writer, or `t` already the writer) `False`.
- `release(t)`: `t` gives up whatever it holds and the answer is `True`. If `t` holds nothing, nothing changes and the answer is `False`.
- `holders()`: returns `("write", [t])` when `t` is writing, `("read", readers)` with the readers sorted when some are reading, and `("free", [])` otherwise.

The starter's `play(ops)` makes a lock, runs a list of operations such as `("r", "a")` (try_read), `("w", "b")` (try_write) and `("x", "a")` (release), and returns the list of answers followed by the final `holders()`.
--- starter
class RWLock:
    def __init__(self):
        self.readers = set()
        self.writer = None

    def try_read(self, t):
        return False

    def try_write(self, t):
        return False

    def release(self, t):
        return False

    def holders(self):
        return ("free", [])


def play(ops):
    lock = RWLock()
    action = {"r": lock.try_read, "w": lock.try_write, "x": lock.release}
    return [action[op](t) for op, t in ops] + [lock.holders()]
--- solution
class RWLock:
    def __init__(self):
        self.readers = set()
        self.writer = None

    def try_read(self, t):
        if self.writer is not None or t in self.readers:
            return False
        self.readers.add(t)
        return True

    def try_write(self, t):
        if self.writer is not None:
            return False
        if self.readers and self.readers != {t}:
            return False
        self.readers.discard(t)
        self.writer = t
        return True

    def release(self, t):
        if self.writer == t:
            self.writer = None
            return True
        if t in self.readers:
            self.readers.remove(t)
            return True
        return False

    def holders(self):
        if self.writer is not None:
            return ("write", [self.writer])
        if self.readers:
            return ("read", sorted(self.readers))
        return ("free", [])


def play(ops):
    lock = RWLock()
    action = {"r": lock.try_read, "w": lock.try_write, "x": lock.release}
    return [action[op](t) for op, t in ops] + [lock.holders()]
--- hint
Keep a set of readers and one writer (or `None`). Each method checks its refusals first, then changes the state.
--- hint
For `try_write`, a writer present means no. Readers present means no, unless the set of readers is exactly `{t}`: then remove `t` from the readers and make it the writer.
--- check case | Readers share, a writer waits its turn
play([("r", "a"), ("r", "b"), ("w", "c"), ("x", "a"), ("x", "b"), ("w", "c")])
=> [True, True, False, True, True, True, ("write", ["c"])]
--- check case | A writer shuts out readers and other writers
play([("w", "a"), ("r", "b"), ("w", "b"), ("w", "a"), ("r", "a")])
=> [True, False, False, False, False, ("write", ["a"])]
?? The writer asking again, or asking to read, is refused too: it already holds the lock.
--- check case | The only reader can upgrade
play([("r", "a"), ("w", "a"), ("r", "b")])
=> [True, True, False, ("write", ["a"])]
--- check case | No upgrade while another reader is inside
play([("r", "a"), ("r", "b"), ("w", "a"), ("x", "b"), ("w", "a")])
=> [True, True, False, True, True, ("write", ["a"])]
--- check case | Releasing what you do not hold changes nothing
play([("x", "a"), ("r", "b"), ("x", "a"), ("x", "b"), ("x", "b")])
=> [False, True, False, True, False, ("free", [])]
--- check case | A reader cannot read twice
play([("r", "a"), ("r", "a"), ("x", "a")])
=> [True, False, True, ("free", [])]
--- check case | Nothing at all, and readers listed in order
(play([]), play([("r", "z"), ("r", "m"), ("r", "b")]))
=> ([("free", [])], [True, True, True, ("read", ["b", "m", "z"])])

+++ practice | Debug: the turnstile that stops all reading
--- task
This is the fair readers-writer solution with a turnstile semaphore `"order"`, in the simulated kernel. It is safe, and nobody starves. But the readers never read at the same time, not even when no writer exists at all: they go in one by one, which throws away the whole point of a readers-writer lock.

Find the bug and fix it. The fixed version must still keep writers alone, must let two readers be inside together, and must keep the turnstile's fairness: a reader that arrives while a writer waits may not go in before that writer. The starter gives the kernel, `explore`, `random_safe` and `fair_setup(readers, writers)`.
--- starter
import random


def make_kernel(mem, sems, threads):
    return {
        "mem": mem,
        "sem": dict(sems),
        "waiting": {name: [] for name in sems},
        "threads": list(threads),
        "status": ["ready"] * len(threads),
    }


def step(k, i):
    try:
        request = next(k["threads"][i])
    except StopIteration:
        k["status"][i] = "done"
        return
    if request is None:
        return
    op, name = request
    if op == "P":
        if k["sem"][name] > 0:
            k["sem"][name] -= 1
        else:
            k["status"][i] = "blocked"
            k["waiting"][name].append(i)
    elif op == "V":
        if k["waiting"][name]:
            j = k["waiting"][name].pop(0)
            k["status"][j] = "ready"
        else:
            k["sem"][name] += 1


def run(k, schedule):
    trace = []
    for i in schedule:
        if k["status"][i] == "ready":
            step(k, i)
            trace.append(i)
    while "ready" in k["status"]:
        i = k["status"].index("ready")
        step(k, i)
        trace.append(i)
    return trace


def where(k, counts):
    spots = []
    for g, c in zip(k["threads"], counts):
        f = getattr(g, "gi_frame", None)
        spots.append((c, None) if f is None else (c, f.f_lasti, repr(sorted(f.f_locals.items()))))
    return repr((k["mem"], k["sem"], k["waiting"], k["status"], spots))


def explore(setup, max_steps, bad=None, final=None):
    seen = set()

    def search(prefix):
        k = setup()
        counts = [0] * len(k["threads"])
        for i in prefix:
            step(k, i)
            counts[i] += 1
        key = where(k, counts)
        if key in seen:
            return None
        seen.add(key)
        if bad is not None and bad(k["mem"]):
            return ("bad", prefix)
        ready = [i for i, s in enumerate(k["status"]) if s == "ready"]
        if not ready:
            if "blocked" in k["status"]:
                return ("deadlock", prefix)
            if final is not None and not final(k["mem"]):
                return ("wrong end", prefix)
            return None
        if len(prefix) == max_steps:
            return None
        for i in ready:
            found = search(prefix + [i])
            if found is not None:
                return found
        return None

    return search([])


def random_safe(setup, seed, bad):
    rng = random.Random(seed)
    k = setup()
    while "ready" in k["status"]:
        step(k, rng.choice([i for i, s in enumerate(k["status"]) if s == "ready"]))
        if bad(k["mem"]):
            return False
    return k["status"] == ["done"] * len(k["status"])


def fair_setup(readers, writers):
    def setup():
        mem = {"readers": 0, "reading": 0, "writing": 0, "log": []}
        threads = [reader(mem, r) for r in range(readers)] + [writer(mem, readers + w) for w in range(writers)]
        return make_kernel(mem, {"order": 1, "mutex": 1, "room": 1}, threads)
    return setup


def reader(mem, me):
    yield ("P", "order")
    yield ("P", "mutex")
    mem["readers"] += 1
    if mem["readers"] == 1:
        yield ("P", "room")
    yield ("V", "mutex")
    mem["reading"] += 1
    mem["log"].append(("read", me))
    yield
    mem["reading"] -= 1
    yield ("V", "order")
    yield ("P", "mutex")
    mem["readers"] -= 1
    if mem["readers"] == 0:
        yield ("V", "room")
    yield ("V", "mutex")


def writer(mem, me):
    yield ("P", "order")
    yield ("P", "room")
    yield ("V", "order")
    mem["writing"] += 1
    mem["log"].append(("write", me))
    yield
    mem["writing"] -= 1
    yield ("V", "room")
--- solution
import random


def make_kernel(mem, sems, threads):
    return {
        "mem": mem,
        "sem": dict(sems),
        "waiting": {name: [] for name in sems},
        "threads": list(threads),
        "status": ["ready"] * len(threads),
    }


def step(k, i):
    try:
        request = next(k["threads"][i])
    except StopIteration:
        k["status"][i] = "done"
        return
    if request is None:
        return
    op, name = request
    if op == "P":
        if k["sem"][name] > 0:
            k["sem"][name] -= 1
        else:
            k["status"][i] = "blocked"
            k["waiting"][name].append(i)
    elif op == "V":
        if k["waiting"][name]:
            j = k["waiting"][name].pop(0)
            k["status"][j] = "ready"
        else:
            k["sem"][name] += 1


def run(k, schedule):
    trace = []
    for i in schedule:
        if k["status"][i] == "ready":
            step(k, i)
            trace.append(i)
    while "ready" in k["status"]:
        i = k["status"].index("ready")
        step(k, i)
        trace.append(i)
    return trace


def where(k, counts):
    spots = []
    for g, c in zip(k["threads"], counts):
        f = getattr(g, "gi_frame", None)
        spots.append((c, None) if f is None else (c, f.f_lasti, repr(sorted(f.f_locals.items()))))
    return repr((k["mem"], k["sem"], k["waiting"], k["status"], spots))


def explore(setup, max_steps, bad=None, final=None):
    seen = set()

    def search(prefix):
        k = setup()
        counts = [0] * len(k["threads"])
        for i in prefix:
            step(k, i)
            counts[i] += 1
        key = where(k, counts)
        if key in seen:
            return None
        seen.add(key)
        if bad is not None and bad(k["mem"]):
            return ("bad", prefix)
        ready = [i for i, s in enumerate(k["status"]) if s == "ready"]
        if not ready:
            if "blocked" in k["status"]:
                return ("deadlock", prefix)
            if final is not None and not final(k["mem"]):
                return ("wrong end", prefix)
            return None
        if len(prefix) == max_steps:
            return None
        for i in ready:
            found = search(prefix + [i])
            if found is not None:
                return found
        return None

    return search([])


def random_safe(setup, seed, bad):
    rng = random.Random(seed)
    k = setup()
    while "ready" in k["status"]:
        step(k, rng.choice([i for i, s in enumerate(k["status"]) if s == "ready"]))
        if bad(k["mem"]):
            return False
    return k["status"] == ["done"] * len(k["status"])


def fair_setup(readers, writers):
    def setup():
        mem = {"readers": 0, "reading": 0, "writing": 0, "log": []}
        threads = [reader(mem, r) for r in range(readers)] + [writer(mem, readers + w) for w in range(writers)]
        return make_kernel(mem, {"order": 1, "mutex": 1, "room": 1}, threads)
    return setup


def reader(mem, me):
    yield ("P", "order")
    yield ("V", "order")
    yield ("P", "mutex")
    mem["readers"] += 1
    if mem["readers"] == 1:
        yield ("P", "room")
    yield ("V", "mutex")
    mem["reading"] += 1
    mem["log"].append(("read", me))
    yield
    mem["reading"] -= 1
    yield ("P", "mutex")
    mem["readers"] -= 1
    if mem["readers"] == 0:
        yield ("V", "room")
    yield ("V", "mutex")


def writer(mem, me):
    yield ("P", "order")
    yield ("P", "room")
    yield ("V", "order")
    mem["writing"] += 1
    mem["log"].append(("write", me))
    yield
    mem["writing"] -= 1
    yield ("V", "room")
--- hint
What does a reader hold while it reads? Could a second reader get past the turnstile while the first one is reading?
--- hint
A reader should only walk **through** the turnstile: P on `"order"` and then V on it straight away. Only the writer holds it while it waits.
--- check test | Two readers can now be inside together
(lambda r: r is not None and r[0] == "bad")(explore(fair_setup(2, 0), 100, lambda m: m["reading"] >= 2))
--- check case | Still safe: two readers and a writer, every schedule
explore(fair_setup(2, 1), 300, lambda m: m["writing"] > 1 or (m["writing"] > 0 and m["reading"] > 0), lambda m: len(m["log"]) == 3)
=> None
--- check case | Still safe: one reader and two writers
explore(fair_setup(1, 2), 300, lambda m: m["writing"] > 1 or (m["writing"] > 0 and m["reading"] > 0), lambda m: len(m["log"]) == 3)
=> None
--- check test | Still fair: a reader that comes after a waiting writer blocks behind it
(lambda k: ([step(k, i) for i in [0, 0, 0, 0, 0, 2, 2, 1]], k["status"], k["waiting"]["order"])[1:])(fair_setup(2, 1)()) == (["ready", "blocked", "blocked"], [1])
?? The writer holds order while it waits for room, so reader 1 blocks on order behind it.
--- check test | Still fair: the same schedule, run to the end, writes before the late read
(lambda k: ([step(k, i) for i in [0, 0, 0, 0, 0, 2, 2, 1]], run(k, []), k["mem"]["log"], k["status"])[2:])(fair_setup(2, 1)()) == ([("read", 0), ("write", 2), ("read", 1)], ["done", "done", "done"])
--- check test | 200 random schedules, three readers and two writers
all(random_safe(fair_setup(3, 2), s, lambda m: m["writing"] > 1 or (m["writing"] > 0 and m["reading"] > 0)) for s in range(200))

+++ practice | Writers first, with a monitor
--- task
Build a **writers-preference** readers-writer lock with the monitor kernel from last lesson's practice (a lock `"m"` and condition variables `"can_read"` and `"can_write"`, Mesa style). The shared memory has `"readers"` (how many are reading), `"writing"` (how many are writing, 0 or 1), `"waiting_writers"` and `"log"`.

- `reader(mem, me)`: acquire `"m"`; while someone is writing **or any writer is waiting**, wait on `"can_read"`; then add 1 to `mem["readers"]` and append `("read", me)` to the log, in the same step; release `"m"`. That is the reading. Then acquire `"m"`, take 1 from `mem["readers"]`, and if no readers are left, signal `"can_write"`; release `"m"`.
- `writer(mem, me)`: acquire `"m"`; add 1 to `mem["waiting_writers"]` and append `("arrive", me)` to the log, in the same step; while someone is writing or reading, wait on `"can_write"`; then take 1 from `mem["waiting_writers"]`, add 1 to `mem["writing"]` and append `("write", me)`; release `"m"`. That is the writing. Then acquire `"m"`, take 1 from `mem["writing"]`, and if writers are still waiting, signal `"can_write"`, otherwise broadcast `"can_read"`; release `"m"`.

Use `while`, never `if`, around each wait. The starter gives the monitor kernel, `explore`, `run`, `wp_setup(readers, writers)` and `prefers_writers(log)`, which is `False` if a read ever starts while a writer has arrived and not yet written.
--- starter
def make_monitor_kernel(mem, locks, cvs, threads):
    return {
        "mem": mem,
        "lock": {name: None for name in locks},
        "lock_q": {name: [] for name in locks},
        "cv_q": {name: [] for name in cvs},
        "threads": list(threads),
        "status": ["ready"] * len(threads),
    }


def where(k, counts):
    spots = []
    for g, c in zip(k["threads"], counts):
        f = getattr(g, "gi_frame", None)
        spots.append((c, None) if f is None else (c, f.f_lasti, repr(sorted(f.f_locals.items()))))
    return repr((k["mem"], k["lock"], k["lock_q"], k["cv_q"], k["status"], spots))


def explore(setup, max_steps, bad=None, final=None):
    seen = set()

    def search(prefix):
        k = setup()
        counts = [0] * len(k["threads"])
        for i in prefix:
            step(k, i)
            counts[i] += 1
        key = where(k, counts)
        if key in seen:
            return None
        seen.add(key)
        if bad is not None and bad(k["mem"]):
            return ("bad", prefix)
        ready = [i for i, s in enumerate(k["status"]) if s == "ready"]
        if not ready:
            if "blocked" in k["status"]:
                return ("deadlock", prefix)
            if final is not None and not final(k["mem"]):
                return ("wrong end", prefix)
            return None
        if len(prefix) == max_steps:
            return None
        for i in ready:
            found = search(prefix + [i])
            if found is not None:
                return found
        return None

    return search([])


def release(k, lock):
    if k["lock_q"][lock]:
        j = k["lock_q"][lock].pop(0)
        k["lock"][lock] = j
        k["status"][j] = "ready"
    else:
        k["lock"][lock] = None


def wake(k, cv):
    j, lock = k["cv_q"][cv].pop(0)
    if k["lock"][lock] is None:
        k["lock"][lock] = j
        k["status"][j] = "ready"
    else:
        k["lock_q"][lock].append(j)


def step(k, i):
    try:
        request = next(k["threads"][i])
    except StopIteration:
        k["status"][i] = "done"
        return
    if request is None:
        return
    op = request[0]
    if op == "acquire":
        lock = request[1]
        if k["lock"][lock] is None:
            k["lock"][lock] = i
        else:
            k["status"][i] = "blocked"
            k["lock_q"][lock].append(i)
    elif op == "release":
        release(k, request[1])
    elif op == "wait":
        cv, lock = request[1], request[2]
        release(k, lock)
        k["status"][i] = "blocked"
        k["cv_q"][cv].append((i, lock))
    elif op == "signal":
        if k["cv_q"][request[1]]:
            wake(k, request[1])
    elif op == "broadcast":
        while k["cv_q"][request[1]]:
            wake(k, request[1])


def run(k, schedule):
    trace = []
    for i in schedule:
        if k["status"][i] == "ready":
            step(k, i)
            trace.append(i)
    while "ready" in k["status"]:
        i = k["status"].index("ready")
        step(k, i)
        trace.append(i)
    return trace


def prefers_writers(log):
    pending = 0
    for kind, who in log:
        if kind == "arrive":
            pending += 1
        elif kind == "write":
            pending -= 1
        elif kind == "read" and pending > 0:
            return False
    return True


def wp_setup(readers, writers):
    def setup():
        mem = {"readers": 0, "writing": 0, "waiting_writers": 0, "log": []}
        threads = [reader(mem, r) for r in range(readers)] + [writer(mem, readers + w) for w in range(writers)]
        return make_monitor_kernel(mem, ["m"], ["can_read", "can_write"], threads)
    return setup


def reader(mem, me):
    yield


def writer(mem, me):
    yield
--- solution
def make_monitor_kernel(mem, locks, cvs, threads):
    return {
        "mem": mem,
        "lock": {name: None for name in locks},
        "lock_q": {name: [] for name in locks},
        "cv_q": {name: [] for name in cvs},
        "threads": list(threads),
        "status": ["ready"] * len(threads),
    }


def where(k, counts):
    spots = []
    for g, c in zip(k["threads"], counts):
        f = getattr(g, "gi_frame", None)
        spots.append((c, None) if f is None else (c, f.f_lasti, repr(sorted(f.f_locals.items()))))
    return repr((k["mem"], k["lock"], k["lock_q"], k["cv_q"], k["status"], spots))


def explore(setup, max_steps, bad=None, final=None):
    seen = set()

    def search(prefix):
        k = setup()
        counts = [0] * len(k["threads"])
        for i in prefix:
            step(k, i)
            counts[i] += 1
        key = where(k, counts)
        if key in seen:
            return None
        seen.add(key)
        if bad is not None and bad(k["mem"]):
            return ("bad", prefix)
        ready = [i for i, s in enumerate(k["status"]) if s == "ready"]
        if not ready:
            if "blocked" in k["status"]:
                return ("deadlock", prefix)
            if final is not None and not final(k["mem"]):
                return ("wrong end", prefix)
            return None
        if len(prefix) == max_steps:
            return None
        for i in ready:
            found = search(prefix + [i])
            if found is not None:
                return found
        return None

    return search([])


def release(k, lock):
    if k["lock_q"][lock]:
        j = k["lock_q"][lock].pop(0)
        k["lock"][lock] = j
        k["status"][j] = "ready"
    else:
        k["lock"][lock] = None


def wake(k, cv):
    j, lock = k["cv_q"][cv].pop(0)
    if k["lock"][lock] is None:
        k["lock"][lock] = j
        k["status"][j] = "ready"
    else:
        k["lock_q"][lock].append(j)


def step(k, i):
    try:
        request = next(k["threads"][i])
    except StopIteration:
        k["status"][i] = "done"
        return
    if request is None:
        return
    op = request[0]
    if op == "acquire":
        lock = request[1]
        if k["lock"][lock] is None:
            k["lock"][lock] = i
        else:
            k["status"][i] = "blocked"
            k["lock_q"][lock].append(i)
    elif op == "release":
        release(k, request[1])
    elif op == "wait":
        cv, lock = request[1], request[2]
        release(k, lock)
        k["status"][i] = "blocked"
        k["cv_q"][cv].append((i, lock))
    elif op == "signal":
        if k["cv_q"][request[1]]:
            wake(k, request[1])
    elif op == "broadcast":
        while k["cv_q"][request[1]]:
            wake(k, request[1])


def run(k, schedule):
    trace = []
    for i in schedule:
        if k["status"][i] == "ready":
            step(k, i)
            trace.append(i)
    while "ready" in k["status"]:
        i = k["status"].index("ready")
        step(k, i)
        trace.append(i)
    return trace


def prefers_writers(log):
    pending = 0
    for kind, who in log:
        if kind == "arrive":
            pending += 1
        elif kind == "write":
            pending -= 1
        elif kind == "read" and pending > 0:
            return False
    return True


def wp_setup(readers, writers):
    def setup():
        mem = {"readers": 0, "writing": 0, "waiting_writers": 0, "log": []}
        threads = [reader(mem, r) for r in range(readers)] + [writer(mem, readers + w) for w in range(writers)]
        return make_monitor_kernel(mem, ["m"], ["can_read", "can_write"], threads)
    return setup


def reader(mem, me):
    yield ("acquire", "m")
    while mem["writing"] or mem["waiting_writers"] > 0:
        yield ("wait", "can_read", "m")
    mem["readers"] += 1
    mem["log"].append(("read", me))
    yield ("release", "m")
    yield ("acquire", "m")
    mem["readers"] -= 1
    if mem["readers"] == 0:
        yield ("signal", "can_write")
    yield ("release", "m")


def writer(mem, me):
    yield ("acquire", "m")
    mem["waiting_writers"] += 1
    mem["log"].append(("arrive", me))
    while mem["writing"] or mem["readers"] > 0:
        yield ("wait", "can_write", "m")
    mem["waiting_writers"] -= 1
    mem["writing"] += 1
    mem["log"].append(("write", me))
    yield ("release", "m")
    yield ("acquire", "m")
    mem["writing"] -= 1
    if mem["waiting_writers"] > 0:
        yield ("signal", "can_write")
    else:
        yield ("broadcast", "can_read")
    yield ("release", "m")
--- hint
Each thread is two short visits to the monitor: one to get in and one to get out. The work between them (reading or writing) is the step that ends with `yield ("release", "m")`.
--- hint
The reader's waiting test is `while mem["writing"] or mem["waiting_writers"] > 0: yield ("wait", "can_read", "m")`. The writer counts itself as waiting **before** its own `while` loop, and stops counting after it.
--- hint
When a writer leaves, another waiting writer goes first (`signal` on `"can_write"`); only when none is waiting does it wake every reader with `broadcast`, since any number of them can read together.
--- check case | Two readers and a writer: safe, writers first, and everyone finishes
explore(wp_setup(2, 1), 400, lambda m: m["writing"] > 1 or (m["writing"] and m["readers"] > 0) or not prefers_writers(m["log"]), lambda m: len(m["log"]) == 4 and m["readers"] == 0 and m["waiting_writers"] == 0)
=> None
--- check case | One reader and two writers
explore(wp_setup(1, 2), 400, lambda m: m["writing"] > 1 or (m["writing"] and m["readers"] > 0) or not prefers_writers(m["log"]), lambda m: len(m["log"]) == 5)
=> None
--- check test | Readers can still read together when no writer is around
(lambda r: r is not None and r[0] == "bad")(explore(wp_setup(2, 0), 100, lambda m: m["readers"] >= 2))
--- check test | A reader who arrives while a writer waits must wait too
(lambda k: ([step(k, i) for i in [0, 0, 2, 2, 1, 1]], k["status"], k["cv_q"])[1:])(wp_setup(2, 1)()) == (["ready", "blocked", "blocked"], {"can_read": [(1, "m")], "can_write": [(2, "m")]})
?? Reader 0 is reading. The writer arrived and waits for it. Reader 1 must now wait on can_read, even though only readers are inside.
--- check test | The same schedule runs to the end: the writer writes before the late reader reads
(lambda k: ([step(k, i) for i in [0, 0, 2, 2, 1, 1]], run(k, []), [e for e in k["mem"]["log"] if e[0] != "arrive"], k["status"])[2:])(wp_setup(2, 1)()) == ([("read", 0), ("write", 2), ("read", 1)], ["done", "done", "done"])
=== os-09 | Deadlock: the four conditions, resource-allocation graphs and the banker's algorithm
--- teach
In the last two lessons deadlocks kept appearing: a producer that took the lock before waiting for a slot, five philosophers each holding one fork. Each time we found a fix for that one problem. This lesson steps back and studies deadlock itself: exactly when it can happen, how to see it in a picture, and the three strategies an operating system can use against it. The main algorithm is Dijkstra's **banker's algorithm**, which refuses any request that could lead to a deadlock later.

### What a deadlock is

A **deadlock** is a set of threads in which every thread is waiting for something that only another thread in the set can provide. None of them can move, so none of them ever will.

In everyday words: four cars arrive at a four-way junction at once, and each driver waits for the car on their right to go first. Nobody is broken, and everybody is waiting politely, forever.

The "something" is usually a **resource**: a lock, a semaphore's token, a printer, a block of memory, a slot in a buffer. A resource **type** can have several identical **instances** (three printers, twelve antennas). A thread uses a resource in three steps: **request** it (waiting if none is free), **use** it, **release** it.

### The four conditions

In 1971 Coffman, Elphick and Shoshani showed that a deadlock can only happen when **all four** of these hold at the same time:

1. **Mutual exclusion.** At least one resource can be held by only one thread at a time.
2. **Hold and wait.** A thread holds at least one resource while waiting for another.
3. **No preemption.** A resource cannot be taken away from a thread; the thread must release it itself.
4. **Circular wait.** There is a cycle of threads T₁, T₂, …, Tₖ where each one waits for a resource held by the next, and Tₖ waits for one held by T₁.

Each condition is **necessary**: take any one away and deadlock is impossible. If resources could be shared, nobody would wait. If nobody waited while holding, every waiting thread would hold nothing, so it could not be blocking anyone. If resources could be taken away, the system could break the standstill by force. And without a cycle, the chain of "waits for" ends at a thread that is not waiting, which will finish and release what it holds.

That is the whole theory of deadlock **prevention**: design the system so that one of the four can never hold.

### Resource-allocation graphs

A **resource-allocation graph** draws the state of the system:

- a circle for each thread, and a box for each resource type, with a dot per instance;
- a **request edge** T → R when thread T is waiting for resource R;
- an **assignment edge** R → T when an instance of R is held by T.

```text
  T1 ──request──▶ [R1] ──assigned──▶ T2
   ▲                                  │
assigned                           request
   │                                  ▼
  [R2] ◀──────────────────────────────┘
```

Here T1 waits for R1, which T2 holds, and T2 waits for R2, which T1 holds: a cycle.

**When every resource has one instance, a cycle means deadlock, and deadlock means a cycle.**

- *Cycle ⇒ deadlock.* Each thread on the cycle waits for a resource held by the next one. With one instance, that resource can only be freed by its holder, and its holder is also waiting, for the next one, and so on round the cycle. Nobody on it can move.
- *Deadlock ⇒ cycle.* Every thread in a deadlocked set waits for a resource held by another thread in the set. Start anywhere and follow "waits for a resource held by": every step lands on another thread of the set. The set is finite, so you must come back to a thread you have already seen, and the loop you walked is a cycle.

With one instance per resource, you can simplify the picture to a **wait-for graph**: drop the boxes and draw T → U when T waits for something U holds. Deadlock detection is then cycle detection, which depth-first search from DSA I does in O(threads + edges).

**With several instances, a cycle is necessary but not enough.** Say R has two instances, one held by T1 and one by T3. T2 holds S and waits for R; T1 waits for S. There is a cycle T1 → S → T2 → R → T1. But T3 is not waiting for anything: it finishes, frees its instance of R, T2 takes it, finishes, frees S, and T1 goes on. No deadlock.

### Four ways to deal with deadlock

1. **Prevention:** make one of the four conditions impossible, by design.
2. **Avoidance:** know in advance what each thread might ask for, and refuse any request that could lead to a deadlock later.
3. **Detection and recovery:** let deadlocks happen, notice them, and break them.
4. **Ignore it:** most general-purpose operating systems do this for user programs, and leave it to programmers. It is jokingly called the [[ostrich algorithm|ostrich]].

### Prevention: break one condition

- **Mutual exclusion** is usually unavoidable: a lock exists to exclude. But read-only data can be shared, and a printer can be **spooled**: jobs go into a queue that one printer thread owns, so no user thread ever holds the printer.
- **Hold and wait:** make a thread ask for everything it needs **at once**, and give it all or nothing. It is safe, but resources sit idle while held "just in case", and a thread that needs many popular resources can starve.
- **No preemption:** if a thread cannot get the next lock, it releases the ones it has and tries again later. C++'s `std::scoped_lock` locks several mutexes this way. The danger is **livelock** (below).
- **Circular wait:** give every resource a number and always request in increasing order. Last lesson proved there can then be no cycle: the numbers would have to climb all the way round. This is the method real systems use most.

```cpp fragment
#include <mutex>

std::mutex fuel, oxidiser;

void mix() {
    std::scoped_lock both(fuel, oxidiser);   // takes both without deadlock, in any order
    // ... use both tanks ...
}                                            // both released here
```

### Avoidance: safe states

Avoidance needs one more piece of information: each thread declares in advance the **maximum** number of each resource it may ever hold at once. The system then keeps itself in a **safe state**.

A state is **safe** if there is some order of all the threads, a **safe sequence** T₁, T₂, …, Tₙ, such that each Tᵢ could get **everything it might still ask for** from the resources that are free now plus those held by T₁ to Tᵢ₋₁. In words: even if every thread asked for its maximum right now, we could still let them finish one at a time, each one handing back its resources to the next.

Here is a station with 12 antennas and three tracking jobs:

| job | maximum | holds | may still ask for |
|---|---|---|---|
| A | 9 | 4 | 5 |
| B | 4 | 3 | 1 |
| C | 7 | 3 | 4 |

4 + 3 + 3 = 10 are held, so 2 are free. Is it safe? B may still ask for 1, and 2 are free, so B can finish and hand back its 3: now 5 are free. Then A may ask for 5: fine, and A hands back 9, making 9 free. Then C needs 4: fine. So B, A, C is a safe sequence, and the state is safe.

Now suppose C asks for 2 more antennas. Two are free, so a careless system would hand them over. Then 0 would be free, and every job may still ask for at least 1 more: if they all do, none can ever finish. That state is **unsafe**, so the banker makes C wait, even though the antennas are sitting right there. If C had asked for just 1, the state would stay safe (B, then C, then A can finish), and the banker would grant it.

**Safe means no deadlock can happen**, whatever the threads do: the system can always run them in the safe order. **Unsafe does not mean deadlocked**; it means the system can no longer promise. Threads might release early, or never ask for their maximum. Avoidance simply refuses to leave the safe states.

### The banker's algorithm

Dijkstra designed it in 1965, with a [[banker|banker]] in mind who never lends out so much cash that he could not meet every customer's full credit line in some order. With n threads and m resource types, the system keeps:

- `available[j]`: free instances of type j;
- `max_need[i][j]`: the most of type j thread i may ever hold;
- `allocation[i][j]`: how many thread i holds now;
- `need[i][j] = max_need[i][j] − allocation[i][j]`: what it may still ask for.

A vector is **at most** another when every entry is: (1, 0, 2) is at most (1, 2, 2), but (0, 3, 0) is not at most (1, 2, 2), because 3 is more than 2.

**The safety check.** Start with `work = available`, and nobody finished. Repeatedly find a thread that is not finished and whose `need` is at most `work`; pretend it runs to the end, so add its `allocation` to `work` and mark it finished. If everyone ends up finished, the order you found is a safe sequence. If at some point nobody unfinished fits, the state is unsafe.

**A request.** When thread i asks for the vector `req`:

1. If `req` is more than `need[i]` in any entry, the thread broke its promise: an error.
2. If `req` is more than `available` in any entry, the thread must wait: the resources are not there.
3. Otherwise **pretend** to grant it: take `req` from `available` and add it to `allocation[i]`. Run the safety check. If the new state is safe, the grant stands. If not, undo the pretend grant and make the thread wait.

### Why the safety check is right

Two things need proving.

**A sequence it finds really is safe.** Each thread it marks had `need` at most `work`, and `work` was exactly the free resources plus everything held by the threads marked before it. That is the definition of a safe sequence, one thread at a time.

**If a safe sequence exists, the check never gets stuck, whichever fitting thread it picks at each step.** The key fact is that `work` only grows: finishing a thread adds its allocation and takes nothing away. So a thread that fits now still fits later. Suppose the check did get stuck, with some set U of threads unfinished and nobody in U fitting. Take any safe sequence S and let q be the **first** thread of S that is in U. Every thread before q in S is not in U, so it is already finished, and `work` includes all of their allocations, plus `available`. The safe sequence promises that this much covers q's need. So q fits, and the check was not stuck after all.

So the check may pick any thread that fits. This lesson always picks the **lowest-numbered** one, so that the answer is predictable.

### What it costs

The safety check makes up to n passes, each scanning up to n threads and comparing m numbers: **O(m · n²)** time, and O(m · n) space for the matrices. It runs on every request. Worse, it needs every thread's maximum in advance and a fixed set of threads, which general-purpose systems do not have. That is why the banker's algorithm is rare in desktop kernels, and lives on in systems that plan their resources ahead: some real-time and embedded schedulers, and admission control, which decides whether to accept a new job at all.

### Detection and recovery

Detection lets threads ask freely and checks now and then for a deadlock.

- **One instance per resource:** build the wait-for graph and look for a cycle.
- **Several instances:** run the safety check, with each thread's **current request** in place of its need. Threads that can never be marked finished are deadlocked.

Detection costs the same O(m · n²), so systems run it when something looks wrong (a thread has waited too long, or CPU use has dropped) rather than on every request. To **recover**, the system either **aborts** threads, all of the deadlocked ones or one at a time until the cycle breaks, or **preempts** a resource and rolls its thread back to an earlier checkpoint. Choosing the same **victim** every time starves it, so real systems count how often a thread has been chosen.

Databases work exactly like this. PostgreSQL waits one second (its `deadlock_timeout`), then searches the wait-for graph, aborts one transaction with the error "deadlock detected", and lets the rest go on.

### Livelock

Two people meet in a corridor. Both step left to let the other pass, then both step right, then both step left again. They are both moving, and neither gets anywhere. That is **livelock**: threads keep changing state in response to each other without making progress. It is the risk of "release everything and try again": two threads can grab and release in lockstep forever. The standard cure is to wait a **random** time before retrying, growing each time ([[exponential backoff|backoff]]).

### Where it is used

- **Lock ordering** is how the Linux kernel, databases and flight software prevent deadlock among their own locks, checked by tools such as lockdep.
- **Detection and abort** is how databases handle deadlocks between transactions, which they cannot order in advance.
- **The Go runtime** stops a program with "fatal error: all goroutines are asleep - deadlock!" when every goroutine is blocked, exactly the check your explorers make.
- **Avoidance** appears in admission control: a flight scheduler that accepts a new task only if every accepted task can still meet its needs.

**Watch out:**

- **A cycle is not always a deadlock.** With several instances of a resource, a thread outside the cycle may release one and break it.
- **Unsafe is not deadlocked.** It only means the system can no longer promise that no deadlock will happen.
- **Compare every entry.** "At most" for vectors means at most in **every** entry; comparing sums, or only the first entry, is a classic bug.
- **Undo the pretend grant.** A request that fails the safety check must leave `available` and `allocation` exactly as they were.
- **`<` instead of `<=`.** A thread whose need exactly equals what is free **can** finish. Using `<` calls safe states unsafe.

::: context ostrich Burying your head in the sand
The name comes from the myth that an ostrich hides its head in the sand when danger comes (it does not). An operating system that "uses" it simply does nothing about deadlock among user programs: if your two threads lock mutexes in opposite orders, they hang, and it is your bug. This is a sensible engineering choice, not laziness: deadlocks between user programs are rare, and prevention or avoidance would slow down every program, all the time, to guard against them.
:::

::: context banker Why a banker?
Dijkstra described the algorithm in 1965 with the image of a small-town banker. Each customer has a credit limit (their maximum), has borrowed some of it (their allocation), and may ask for more at any time. The banker has a limited amount of cash (available). He grants a loan only if, afterwards, he could still satisfy every customer's full remaining credit in some order, as each customer who is fully paid out eventually repays everything. Customers who ask at a bad moment are told to wait, never refused for good.
:::

::: context backoff Waiting a random, growing time
Classic Ethernet solved a livelock just like the corridor. Two computers that transmit at the same moment collide, and if both retried at once they would collide forever. So after the k-th collision in a row, each waits a random number of time slots between 0 and 2ᵏ − 1. The chance of colliding again halves each time. The same trick, called exponential backoff, is used by network clients retrying a busy server and by locks that retry instead of sleeping.
:::
--- task
Write the banker's algorithm. Vectors are lists with one number per resource type; `max_need` and `allocation` are lists of such vectors, one per thread.

1. `is_safe(available, max_need, allocation)`: return a safe sequence as a list of thread numbers, or `None` if the state is unsafe. Work out each thread's need as `max_need[i][j] - allocation[i][j]`. Start with `work` equal to `available` and repeat: find the **lowest-numbered** unfinished thread whose need is at most `work` in every entry, add its allocation to `work`, mark it finished and add it to the sequence. If no unfinished thread fits, the state is unsafe. Do not change the lists you are given.
2. `request(available, max_need, allocation, i, req)`: thread `i` asks for the vector `req`. Return `"error"` if `req` is more than thread `i`'s need in any entry. Otherwise return `"wait"` if it is more than `available` in any entry. Otherwise pretend to grant it (take it from `available` and add it to `allocation[i]`, changing those lists in place) and run `is_safe`: if the result is a safe sequence, keep the grant and return `"granted"`; if not, put both lists back exactly as they were and return `"wait"`.

For example, with one resource type, `is_safe([1], [[2], [3]], [[1], [1]])` is `[0, 1]`: thread 0 needs 1 more, gets it, and hands back 2, which covers thread 1's need of 2.
--- starter
def is_safe(available, max_need, allocation):
    return list(range(len(allocation)))


def request(available, max_need, allocation, i, req):
    return "granted"
--- solution
def fits(small, big):
    return all(s <= b for s, b in zip(small, big))


def is_safe(available, max_need, allocation):
    n = len(allocation)
    need = [[m - a for m, a in zip(max_need[i], allocation[i])] for i in range(n)]
    work = list(available)
    finished = [False] * n
    order = []
    while len(order) < n:
        for i in range(n):
            if not finished[i] and fits(need[i], work):
                work = [w + a for w, a in zip(work, allocation[i])]
                finished[i] = True
                order.append(i)
                break
        else:
            return None
    return order


def request(available, max_need, allocation, i, req):
    need = [m - a for m, a in zip(max_need[i], allocation[i])]
    if not fits(req, need):
        return "error"
    if not fits(req, available):
        return "wait"
    for j, r in enumerate(req):
        available[j] -= r
        allocation[i][j] += r
    if is_safe(available, max_need, allocation) is not None:
        return "granted"
    for j, r in enumerate(req):
        available[j] += r
        allocation[i][j] -= r
    return "wait"
--- hint
Write a small helper that says whether one vector is at most another in every entry. You need it three times: need against work, the request against the need, and the request against what is available.
--- hint
For `is_safe`, loop while the sequence is shorter than the number of threads. Inside, scan the threads from 0 upwards and take the first unfinished one that fits; if the scan finds none, return `None`. A `for` loop with an `else` block, or a flag, says "the scan found nothing".
--- hint
In `request`, change `available` and `allocation[i]` entry by entry, then call `is_safe`. If it returns `None`, undo exactly the same changes before returning `"wait"`.
--- check case | The example from the task
is_safe([1], [[2], [3]], [[1], [1]])
=> [0, 1]
--- check case | The classic five threads and three resource types
is_safe([3, 3, 2], [[7, 5, 3], [3, 2, 2], [9, 0, 2], [2, 2, 2], [4, 3, 3]], [[0, 1, 0], [2, 0, 0], [3, 0, 2], [2, 1, 1], [0, 0, 2]])
=> [1, 3, 0, 2, 4]
?? Always scan from thread 0 again after each thread finishes: thread 0 fits once threads 1 and 3 have handed back their resources.
--- check case | An unsafe state
is_safe([1], [[10], [4], [9]], [[5], [2], [3]])
=> None
--- check case | A need exactly equal to what is free can finish
is_safe([2, 1], [[3, 1], [2, 2]], [[1, 0], [0, 1]])
=> [0, 1]
--- check case | No threads is safe; threads that need nothing more finish in order
(is_safe([0], [], []), is_safe([0, 0], [[1, 1], [2, 0]], [[1, 1], [2, 0]]))
=> ([], [0, 1])
