A TLB holds from a few dozen to a couple of thousand entries, and it still hits over 99% of the time, because programs have [[locality|locality]]: they use the same few pages again and again. The memory a TLB covers is its **reach**: 64 entries × 4 KiB = 256 KiB. To cover more, systems use [[huge pages|huge-pages]].

Two things the kernel must keep right:

- **When a mapping changes**, the old translation may still sit in the TLB. The kernel must remove it, or the process keeps using a frame it no longer owns. On a multicore machine every core's TLB must be cleaned: a [[TLB shootdown|shootdown]].
- **On a context switch**, the TLB holds the old process's translations. Either the kernel flushes the whole TLB, or each entry is tagged with an [[address-space identifier|asid]] (ASID; PCID on x86) so that entries of different processes can live side by side.

### Effective access time

How much does translation cost on average? Say a memory read takes m nanoseconds, a TLB lookup t, a walk has L levels, and a fraction h of accesses hit the TLB. A hit costs t + m. A miss costs t + L·m for the walk, plus m for the access itself. So the **effective access time** is:

EAT = h · (t + m) + (1 − h) · (t + (L + 1) · m)

With m = 80 ns, t = 1 ns, L = 3 and h = 0.98: EAT = 0.98 × 81 + 0.02 × 321 = 79.38 + 6.42 = 85.8 ns. The walk costs four times a normal read, yet the average is only 7% above a plain read, because misses are rare. That is why multi-level tables are affordable: the TLB hides them. Caches for the page tables themselves make real misses cheaper still.

### Sharing and copy-on-write

Paging makes two useful tricks cheap:

- **Shared pages.** The code of a library such as the C library is mapped read-only into every process that uses it, all pointing to the same frames. One copy in memory serves hundreds of processes.
- **Copy-on-write.** In lesson 2, `fork` copied the whole address space. Real kernels copy only the page table. Every writable page is marked read-only in both parent and child. The first time either one writes to a page, the protection fault lands in the kernel, which copies just that page into a new frame, maps the copy writable, and returns. Pages that are never written are never copied, which is why `fork` followed by `exec` is cheap.

### Where it is used

- **Every general-purpose CPU and OS** since the 1980s: Linux, Windows, macOS, iOS and Android all run every process on paging.
- **Memory-mapped files:** `mmap` maps a file's pages into the address space, and a page is read from disk only when first touched (next lesson).
- **Guard pages:** an unmapped page below each thread's stack turns a stack overflow into an immediate fault instead of silent corruption.
- **Flight computers** often have a simpler memory protection unit instead of full paging, which still gives each task its own regions with permission bits.

**Watch out:**

- **The mask is one less than a power of two.** The offset of a 2ᵏ-byte page is `vaddr & ((1 << k) - 1)`, not `vaddr & (1 << k)`, which keeps a single bit.
- **Page number versus frame number.** Translation replaces the **page** number with the **frame** number; the offset never changes.
- **A stale TLB entry.** Unmapping a page without removing its TLB entry leaves a working translation to a frame the process no longer owns: a security hole.
- **Counting a walk.** A TLB miss with L levels costs L reads for the walk **plus** one for the access itself, and a walk that finds an invalid entry stops at that level.
- **Virtual is not physical.** A 64-bit process can have a huge virtual address space on a machine with little memory; only the pages it uses need frames, and only the tables it uses need to exist.

::: context locality Why caches work at all
Locality is the habit programs have of using the same memory again soon (temporal locality) and memory near what they just used (spatial locality). A loop runs the same instructions over and over, and walks through an array page by page. You met it in Computer Organization as the reason CPU caches work; the TLB relies on it in the same way. A program that jumps randomly across gigabytes, such as some database index lookups, has little locality and misses the TLB often.
:::

::: context huge-pages Bigger pages, fewer entries
Most CPUs also offer huge pages, for example 2 MiB and 1 GiB on x86-64, made by stopping the page walk one or two levels early. One TLB entry then covers 512 or 262,144 times more memory, so programs with big data sets, like databases and virtual machines, miss the TLB far less. The cost is internal fragmentation, and finding a 2 MiB run of free physical memory, which the buddy allocator from last lesson provides. Linux can hand them out automatically ("transparent huge pages").
:::

::: context shootdown Cleaning every core's TLB
Each core has its own TLB. When the kernel unmaps or changes a page that several cores might have cached, it sends an inter-processor interrupt to each of them, and each core removes the entry and reports back. Only then is the old frame safe to reuse. This "TLB shootdown" can cost several microseconds, which is one reason programs that constantly map and unmap memory on many threads can be surprisingly slow.
:::

::: context asid Tagging translations with their owner
An address-space identifier is a small number, often 12 to 16 bits, that the kernel gives each process and loads into a CPU register on every context switch. Each TLB entry records the identifier it was filled under, and a lookup only matches entries with the current one. So the TLB can keep entries from several processes at once, and a switch no longer throws them all away. x86 calls it a PCID; RISC-V keeps it in the `satp` register next to the page table's address.
:::
--- task
Write a class `MMU`, the translation unit of a 32-bit machine with 4 KiB pages and a two-level page table. A virtual address splits into a 10-bit top index (`vaddr >> 22`), a 10-bit second-level index (`(vaddr >> 12) & 0x3FF`) and a 12-bit offset (`vaddr & 0xFFF`); the virtual page number is `vaddr >> 12`.

- `MMU(tlb_size)`: a top-level table of 1,024 entries, all `None`; a TLB that holds at most `tlb_size` translations (0 means no TLB); and three counters, the attributes `hits`, `misses` and `reads`, all 0.
- `map(vaddr, frame, writable=True)`: make the page containing `vaddr` map to `frame`. If the top-level entry is `None`, first create a second-level table of 1,024 entries, all `None`. The page table entry is a dict `{"frame": frame, "writable": writable, "accessed": False, "dirty": False}`. Remove that page from the TLB if it is there.
- `unmap(vaddr)`: remove the page's entry (set it back to `None`) and its TLB entry. Return `True` if it was mapped, `False` otherwise.
- `pte(vaddr)`: the page's entry, or `None`. `tables()`: how many page tables exist, the top-level one included.
- `translate(vaddr, write=False)`: if the page is in the TLB, that is a hit (add 1 to `hits`, and mark it most recently used). Otherwise it is a miss (add 1 to `misses`) and a walk: read the top-level entry (add 1 to `reads`); if it is `None`, return `"page fault"`. Otherwise read the second-level entry (add 1 to `reads`); if it is `None`, return `"page fault"`. Put the translation in the TLB, first removing the least recently used one if the TLB is full. Then, hit or miss: if `write` is true and the page is not writable, return `"protection fault"`. Otherwise set the entry's `"accessed"` to `True`, and `"dirty"` to `True` for a write, and return the physical address `(frame << 12) | offset`.

For example, after `map(0x00403000, 7)`, translating `0x00403ABC` is a miss with 2 reads and returns `0x7ABC`; translating it again is a hit.
--- starter
class MMU:
    def __init__(self, tlb_size):
        self.tlb_size = tlb_size
        self.hits = 0
        self.misses = 0
        self.reads = 0

    def map(self, vaddr, frame, writable=True):
        pass

    def unmap(self, vaddr):
        return False

    def pte(self, vaddr):
        return None

    def tables(self):
        return 1

    def translate(self, vaddr, write=False):
        return "page fault"
--- solution
class MMU:
    def __init__(self, tlb_size):
        self.tlb_size = tlb_size
        self.top = [None] * 1024
        self.tlb = {}
        self.hits = 0
        self.misses = 0
        self.reads = 0

    def map(self, vaddr, frame, writable=True):
        t, s = vaddr >> 22, (vaddr >> 12) & 0x3FF
        if self.top[t] is None:
            self.top[t] = [None] * 1024
        self.top[t][s] = {"frame": frame, "writable": writable, "accessed": False, "dirty": False}
        self.tlb.pop(vaddr >> 12, None)

    def unmap(self, vaddr):
        t, s = vaddr >> 22, (vaddr >> 12) & 0x3FF
        self.tlb.pop(vaddr >> 12, None)
        if self.top[t] is None or self.top[t][s] is None:
            return False
        self.top[t][s] = None
        return True

    def pte(self, vaddr):
        second = self.top[vaddr >> 22]
        return None if second is None else second[(vaddr >> 12) & 0x3FF]

    def tables(self):
        return 1 + sum(1 for second in self.top if second is not None)

    def translate(self, vaddr, write=False):
        vpn = vaddr >> 12
        if vpn in self.tlb:
            self.hits += 1
            entry = self.tlb.pop(vpn)
            self.tlb[vpn] = entry
        else:
            self.misses += 1
            self.reads += 1
            second = self.top[vaddr >> 22]
            if second is None:
                return "page fault"
            self.reads += 1
            entry = second[vpn & 0x3FF]
            if entry is None:
                return "page fault"
            if self.tlb_size > 0:
                if len(self.tlb) == self.tlb_size:
                    del self.tlb[next(iter(self.tlb))]
                self.tlb[vpn] = entry
        if write and not entry["writable"]:
            return "protection fault"
        entry["accessed"] = True
        if write:
            entry["dirty"] = True
        return (entry["frame"] << 12) | (vaddr & 0xFFF)
--- hint
Keep the TLB as a dict from page number to the page's entry. Python dicts remember insertion order, so the least recently used page is the first key: `next(iter(self.tlb))`. On a hit, `pop` the page and put it back, which moves it to the end.
--- hint
Count exactly as the task says: a miss adds 1 to `reads` for the top-level entry, and 1 more only if there is a second-level table to read. A page fault puts nothing in the TLB.
--- hint
Storing the entry dict itself in the TLB means setting its `"accessed"` or `"dirty"` bit through the TLB changes the page table too. That only stays correct because `map` and `unmap` remove the page's TLB entry.
--- check test | The example from the task: a miss, then a hit
(lambda m: (m.map(0x00403000, 7), m.translate(0x00403ABC), (m.hits, m.misses, m.reads), m.translate(0x00403ABC), (m.hits, m.misses, m.reads))[1:] == (0x7ABC, (0, 1, 2), 0x7ABC, (1, 1, 2)))(MMU(4))
--- check test | The offset is kept and the frame number replaces the page number
(lambda m: (m.map(0xFFFFF000, 0x12345), m.map(0x00000000, 1), m.translate(0xFFFFFFFF), m.translate(0x00000FFF), m.translate(0))[2:] == (0x12345FFF, 0x1FFF, 0x1000))(MMU(8))
--- check test | An unmapped region stops the walk after one read
(lambda m: (m.translate(0x80000000), m.reads, m.misses) == ("page fault", 1, 1))(MMU(4))
--- check test | A missing page in an existing second-level table costs two reads
(lambda m: (m.map(0x00400000, 3), m.translate(0x00401000), m.reads)[1:] == ("page fault", 2))(MMU(4))
--- check test | A page fault puts nothing in the TLB
(lambda m: (m.translate(0x1000), m.translate(0x1000), m.hits, m.misses)[2:] == (0, 2))(MMU(4))
--- check test | Writing to a read-only page is a protection fault, and the dirty bit stays off
(lambda m: (m.map(0x5000, 9, False), m.translate(0x5010), m.translate(0x5010, True), m.pte(0x5000))[1:] == (0x9010, "protection fault", {"frame": 9, "writable": False, "accessed": True, "dirty": False}))(MMU(4))
--- check test | Reads set accessed; writes set accessed and dirty
(lambda m: (m.map(0x2000, 4), m.map(0x3000, 5), m.translate(0x2004), m.translate(0x3008, True), m.pte(0x2000)["dirty"], m.pte(0x3000)["dirty"], m.pte(0x3000)["accessed"])[4:] == (False, True, True))(MMU(4))
--- check test | The TLB throws out the least recently used page
(lambda m: ([m.map(p << 12, p) for p in range(1, 5)], [m.translate(p << 12) for p in [1, 2, 1, 3, 2, 1]], (m.hits, m.misses))[2] == (1, 5))(MMU(2))
?? With room for 2: 1 miss, 2 miss, 1 hit, 3 miss (throws out 2, not 1), 2 miss (throws out 1), 1 miss.
--- check test | unmap removes the page and its TLB entry
(lambda m: (m.map(0x7000, 2), m.translate(0x7000), m.unmap(0x7000), m.translate(0x7000), m.unmap(0x7000), m.hits)[2:] == (True, "page fault", False, 0))(MMU(4))
?? After unmap, a translation must walk the table and fault. A stale TLB entry would still translate the address.
--- check test | Remapping a page changes the translation at once
(lambda m: (m.map(0x7000, 2), m.translate(0x7123), m.map(0x7000, 6), m.translate(0x7123))[1:] == (0x2123, None, 0x6123))(MMU(4))
--- check test | Second-level tables are created only where needed
(lambda m: (m.tables(), [m.map(a, 1) for a in [0x0, 0x1000, 0x3FF000, 0x400000, 0xFFC00000]], m.tables())[::2] == (1, 4))(MMU(4))
--- check test | With no TLB, every access walks
(lambda m: (m.map(0x1000, 1), [m.translate(0x1000) for _ in range(5)], (m.hits, m.misses, m.reads))[2] == (0, 5, 10))(MMU(0))
--- check test | A hit also checks permission
(lambda m: (m.map(0x9000, 3, False), m.translate(0x9000), m.translate(0x9000, True), (m.hits, m.misses))[2:] == ("protection fault", (1, 1)))(MMU(4))

+++ question | The size of a flat table
--- ask
A machine has 32-bit virtual addresses, 4 KiB pages and 4-byte page table entries. How many bytes does one flat (single-level) page table take? Answer with a number.
--- answer
4194304
4,194,304
4 MiB
4MiB
--- why
There are 2³² / 2¹² = 2²⁰ = 1,048,576 pages, one entry each, and 2²⁰ × 4 bytes = 2²² = 4,194,304 bytes, 4 MiB, per process, whether the pages are used or not. Multi-level tables avoid most of it.

+++ question | Why the TLB makes multi-level tables affordable
--- ask
A four-level page walk costs five memory reads per access. Why do real systems still perform well?
--- choice
The page tables are stored in the CPU, not in memory.
--- choice correct
The TLB hits almost every time, because programs reuse the same few pages, so walks are rare.
--- choice
Only the kernel's accesses are translated.
--- choice
The four levels are read in parallel, costing one read.
--- why
With a hit ratio of 99%, the walk's cost is paid on 1 access in 100, so the average is close to one read. That is locality at work. Page tables live in ordinary memory, and the levels must be read one after another, because each entry says where the next table is.

+++ practice | Fields of an address, and the cost of a flat table
--- task
Write two functions.

- `split(vaddr, bits)`: `bits` lists the widths of an address's fields from the top level down, ending with the offset, for example `[10, 10, 12]`. Return a tuple of the field values, top level first. Only the lowest `sum(bits)` bits of `vaddr` count.
- `flat_table_bytes(va_bits, page_bytes, pte_bytes)`: the size in bytes of a single-level page table for addresses of `va_bits` bits, pages of `page_bytes` bytes (a power of two) and entries of `pte_bytes` bytes.

For example, `split(0x00403ABC, [10, 10, 12])` is `(1, 3, 2748)`, and `flat_table_bytes(32, 4096, 4)` is `4194304`.
--- starter
def split(vaddr, bits):
    return ()


def flat_table_bytes(va_bits, page_bytes, pte_bytes):
    return 0
--- solution
def split(vaddr, bits):
    fields = []
    for width in reversed(bits):
        fields.append(vaddr & ((1 << width) - 1))
        vaddr >>= width
    return tuple(reversed(fields))


def flat_table_bytes(va_bits, page_bytes, pte_bytes):
    offset_bits = page_bytes.bit_length() - 1
    return (1 << (va_bits - offset_bits)) * pte_bytes
--- hint
Take the fields from the bottom up: the lowest field is `vaddr & ((1 << width) - 1)`, and `vaddr >>= width` moves the next one down. Reverse at the end so the top level comes first.
--- hint
The table has one entry per page, and there are 2 to the power (address bits − offset bits) pages. For a power of two, `page_bytes.bit_length() - 1` is its exponent.
--- check case | The examples from the task
(split(0x00403ABC, [10, 10, 12]), flat_table_bytes(32, 4096, 4))
=> ((1, 3, 2748), 4194304)
--- check case | x86-64: four 9-bit levels over a 12-bit offset
split(0x00007F1234567890, [9, 9, 9, 9, 12])
=> (254, 72, 418, 359, 2192)
--- check case | Bits above the address width are ignored
split(0xFF0000ABCD, [4, 4, 8])
=> (10, 11, 205)
--- check case | Zero and the largest address
(split(0, [10, 10, 12]), split(2 ** 32 - 1, [10, 10, 12]))
=> ((0, 0, 0), (1023, 1023, 4095))
--- check case | Flat tables for 48-bit and 16-bit machines
(flat_table_bytes(48, 4096, 8), flat_table_bytes(16, 256, 2))
=> (549755813888, 512)

+++ practice | Effective access time, both ways
--- task
Write two functions for a machine where a memory read takes `mem_ns` nanoseconds, a TLB lookup takes `tlb_ns`, and a page walk reads `levels` page table entries.

- `eat(mem_ns, tlb_ns, levels, hit)`: the effective access time when a fraction `hit` of accesses hit the TLB. A hit costs `tlb_ns + mem_ns`; a miss costs `tlb_ns + (levels + 1) * mem_ns`. Return it rounded to 2 decimal places.
- `min_hit(mem_ns, tlb_ns, levels, target_ns)`: the smallest hit ratio that keeps the effective access time at or below `target_ns`, rounded to 4 decimal places. Return `0.0` if even a hit ratio of 0 meets the target, and `None` if even a hit ratio of 1 does not.

For example, `eat(80, 1, 3, 0.98)` is `85.8`.
--- starter
def eat(mem_ns, tlb_ns, levels, hit):
    return mem_ns


def min_hit(mem_ns, tlb_ns, levels, target_ns):
    return 1.0
--- solution
def eat(mem_ns, tlb_ns, levels, hit):
    hit_cost = tlb_ns + mem_ns
    miss_cost = tlb_ns + (levels + 1) * mem_ns
    return round(hit * hit_cost + (1 - hit) * miss_cost, 2)


def min_hit(mem_ns, tlb_ns, levels, target_ns):
    hit_cost = tlb_ns + mem_ns
    miss_cost = tlb_ns + (levels + 1) * mem_ns
    if miss_cost <= target_ns:
        return 0.0
    if hit_cost > target_ns:
        return None
    return round((miss_cost - target_ns) / (miss_cost - hit_cost), 4)
--- hint
The effective access time is a weighted average: `hit * hit_cost + (1 - hit) * miss_cost`.
--- hint
For `min_hit`, set that average equal to the target and solve for `hit`: `hit = (miss_cost - target) / (miss_cost - hit_cost)`. Handle the two ends first: if a miss is already fast enough, any ratio works; if even a hit is too slow, none does.
--- check case | The example from the task
eat(80, 1, 3, 0.98)
=> 85.8
--- check case | Four levels, 100 ns memory, a 99% hit ratio
eat(100, 1, 4, 0.99)
=> 105.0
--- check case | All hits and all misses
(eat(100, 2, 2, 1), eat(100, 2, 2, 0))
=> (102.0, 302.0)
--- check case | The hit ratio needed to stay within 10% of a plain read
min_hit(100, 1, 4, 110)
=> 0.9775
--- check case | A target that any ratio meets, and one that none can
(min_hit(100, 1, 1, 500), min_hit(100, 1, 4, 100))
=> (0.0, None)
--- check case | Exactly the cost of a hit needs every access to hit
min_hit(50, 5, 3, 55)
=> 1.0

+++ practice | Copy-on-write fork
--- task
Model copy-on-write with a class `System` of `nframes` physical frames. Frame contents are in `self.frames` (frame number to value), each frame's number of mappings is in `self.refs`, and each process's page table is in `self.tables[pid]`, a dict from page number to an entry `{"frame": f, "writable": bool, "cow": bool}`. A new frame is always the lowest-numbered frame not in use.

- `create(pid, pages)`: `pages` maps page numbers to values. Give each page a new frame, in increasing page order, holding its value, writable and not copy-on-write, with a reference count of 1.
- `fork(parent, child)`: the child gets a copy of every entry of the parent's table, pointing to the **same** frames, and each of those frames' counts goes up by 1. Every page that was writable becomes read-only and copy-on-write, in **both** tables.
- `read(pid, page)`: the value, or `"page fault"` if the page is not mapped.
- `write(pid, page, value)`: return `"page fault"` if not mapped. If writable, store the value and return `"ok"`. If read-only and not copy-on-write, return `"protection fault"`. If copy-on-write and the frame's count is 1, nobody else uses it: make the entry writable, not copy-on-write, store the value and return `"owned"`. Otherwise copy: take a new frame (return `"out of memory"`, changing nothing, if none is free), put the value there, point the entry to it, writable and not copy-on-write, with a count of 1, take 1 from the old frame's count, and return `"copied"`.
- `exit(pid)`: remove the table and take 1 from each of its frames' counts; a frame whose count reaches 0 is freed (removed from `frames` and `refs`).
--- starter
class System:
    def __init__(self, nframes):
        self.nframes = nframes
        self.frames = {}
        self.refs = {}
        self.tables = {}

    def create(self, pid, pages):
        pass

    def fork(self, parent, child):
        pass

    def read(self, pid, page):
        return "page fault"

    def write(self, pid, page, value):
        return "page fault"

    def exit(self, pid):
        pass
--- solution
class System:
    def __init__(self, nframes):
        self.nframes = nframes
        self.frames = {}
        self.refs = {}
        self.tables = {}

    def new_frame(self):
        for f in range(self.nframes):
            if f not in self.refs:
                return f
        return None

    def create(self, pid, pages):
        table = {}
        for page in sorted(pages):
            f = self.new_frame()
            self.frames[f] = pages[page]
            self.refs[f] = 1
            table[page] = {"frame": f, "writable": True, "cow": False}
        self.tables[pid] = table

    def fork(self, parent, child):
        table = {}
        for page, entry in self.tables[parent].items():
            if entry["writable"]:
                entry["writable"] = False
                entry["cow"] = True
            table[page] = dict(entry)
            self.refs[entry["frame"]] += 1
        self.tables[child] = table

    def read(self, pid, page):
        entry = self.tables[pid].get(page)
        if entry is None:
            return "page fault"
        return self.frames[entry["frame"]]

    def write(self, pid, page, value):
        entry = self.tables[pid].get(page)
        if entry is None:
            return "page fault"
        if entry["writable"]:
            self.frames[entry["frame"]] = value
            return "ok"
        if not entry["cow"]:
            return "protection fault"
        if self.refs[entry["frame"]] == 1:
            entry["writable"], entry["cow"] = True, False
            self.frames[entry["frame"]] = value
            return "owned"
        f = self.new_frame()
        if f is None:
            return "out of memory"
        self.refs[entry["frame"]] -= 1
        self.frames[f] = value
        self.refs[f] = 1
        entry["frame"], entry["writable"], entry["cow"] = f, True, False
        return "copied"

    def exit(self, pid):
        for entry in self.tables.pop(pid).values():
            f = entry["frame"]
            self.refs[f] -= 1
            if self.refs[f] == 0:
                del self.refs[f]
                del self.frames[f]
--- hint
A frame is in use exactly when it is a key of `self.refs`, so the lowest free frame is the first number from 0 that is not a key there.
--- hint
In `fork`, change the parent's entry first (writable to read-only and copy-on-write), then copy it with `dict(entry)` for the child, so both tables agree and neither shares the same dict.
--- hint
`write` has five outcomes. Test them in the order the task lists them. Only the `"copied"` case takes a new frame and changes two reference counts.
--- check test | fork shares every frame and marks writable pages copy-on-write
(lambda s: (s.create("P", {0: "code", 1: "data"}), s.fork("P", "C"), s.refs, s.tables["C"][1], s.tables["P"][1])[2:] == ({0: 2, 1: 2}, {"frame": 1, "writable": False, "cow": True}, {"frame": 1, "writable": False, "cow": True}))(System(8))
--- check test | The child's write copies the page; the parent still sees the old value
(lambda s: (s.create("P", {0: "a", 1: "b"}), s.fork("P", "C"), s.write("C", 1, "B"), s.read("C", 1), s.read("P", 1), s.refs, s.tables["C"][1])[2:] == ("copied", "B", "b", {0: 2, 1: 1, 2: 1}, {"frame": 2, "writable": True, "cow": False}))(System(8))
--- check test | After the copy, the other side owns its page without copying
(lambda s: (s.create("P", {5: "x"}), s.fork("P", "C"), s.write("C", 5, "y"), s.write("P", 5, "z"), s.write("P", 5, "w"), s.read("P", 5), s.read("C", 5), len(s.frames))[2:] == ("copied", "owned", "ok", "w", "y", 2))(System(4))
?? After the child's copy, the parent's frame has a count of 1: the parent's next write just takes it back, with no copy.
--- check test | Pages never written are never copied
(lambda s: (s.create("P", {p: p * 10 for p in range(6)}), s.fork("P", "C"), s.write("C", 4, 99), len(s.frames), s.read("C", 3))[3:] == (7, 30))(System(16))
--- check test | Reading or writing an unmapped page
(lambda s: (s.create("P", {0: 1}), s.read("P", 9), s.write("P", 9, 0))[1:] == ("page fault", "page fault"))(System(4))
--- check test | A read-only page that is not copy-on-write refuses writes
(lambda s: (s.create("P", {0: "rom"}), s.tables["P"][0].update(writable=False), s.fork("P", "C"), s.write("C", 0, "x"), s.write("P", 0, "x"), s.refs)[3:] == ("protection fault", "protection fault", {0: 2}))(System(4))
--- check test | No free frame for the copy: nothing changes
(lambda s: (s.create("P", {0: "a", 1: "b"}), s.fork("P", "C"), s.write("C", 0, "z"), s.read("C", 0), s.refs)[2:] == ("out of memory", "a", {0: 2, 1: 2}))(System(2))
--- check test | exit frees frames nobody else maps, and new frames reuse the lowest numbers
(lambda s: (s.create("P", {0: "a", 1: "b"}), s.fork("P", "C"), s.write("C", 1, "B"), s.exit("P"), sorted(s.frames), dict(s.refs), s.create("Q", {7: "q"}), s.tables["Q"][7]["frame"])[4:] == ([0, 2], {0: 1, 2: 1}, None, 1))(System(8))

+++ practice | How many page tables does a sparse process need?
--- task
In a multi-level page table, a table exists only if some mapped page needs it. Write `tables_needed(addresses, bits)`. `addresses` is a list of virtual addresses that are mapped (one per page, possibly with repeats), and `bits` gives the field widths from the top level down, ending with the offset, as in `[10, 10, 12]`.

Return a list with the number of tables at each level, top level first. There is always exactly one top-level table, even when nothing is mapped. A table at level `i` (counting the top level as 0) is needed for each different value of the first `i` index fields among the addresses.

For example, `tables_needed([0x00000000, 0x00001000, 0xFFC00000], [10, 10, 12])` is `[1, 2]`: one top-level table, and second-level tables for top indexes 0 and 1023.
--- starter
def tables_needed(addresses, bits):
    return [1] * (len(bits) - 1)
--- solution
def tables_needed(addresses, bits):
    levels = len(bits) - 1
    counts = [1]
    for i in range(1, levels):
        shift = sum(bits[i:])
        counts.append(len({a >> shift for a in addresses}))
    return counts
--- hint
The table at level `i` that a given address uses is identified by the address's first `i` index fields, which is the address shifted right by the widths of all the fields below them.
--- hint
Put those shifted values in a set: its size is the number of tables at that level. Level 0 is always 1.
--- check case | The example from the task
tables_needed([0x00000000, 0x00001000, 0xFFC00000], [10, 10, 12])
=> [1, 2]
--- check case | Nothing mapped still needs the top-level table
(tables_needed([], [10, 10, 12]), tables_needed([], [9, 9, 9, 9, 12]))
=> ([1, 0], [1, 0, 0, 0])
--- check case | Code at the bottom and a stack at the top, four levels
tables_needed([0x400000, 0x401000, 0x7FFFFFFFE000, 0x7FFFFFFFF000], [9, 9, 9, 9, 12])
=> [1, 2, 2, 2]
--- check case | Neighbouring pages across a table boundary need two tables
tables_needed([0x003FF000, 0x00400000], [10, 10, 12])
=> [1, 2]
--- check case | Repeats count once
tables_needed([0x5000, 0x5000, 0x5FFF], [10, 10, 12])
=> [1, 1]
--- check case | One page in each of three far-apart places, three levels
tables_needed([0x0, 0x40000000, 0x7FFFF000], [2, 9, 9, 12])
=> [1, 2, 3]

+++ practice | Debug: translations that land in the wrong place
--- task
`translate(table, vaddr)` translates an address on a small machine with 16-bit addresses and 512-byte pages, so the offset is the low 9 bits and the page number is the high 7 bits. `table` is a list of 128 entries, each a frame number or `None`. It should return `(frame << 9) | offset`, or `"page fault"` when the entry is `None`.

Almost every translation comes out wrong. Find the bug and fix it.
--- starter
OFFSET_BITS = 9


def translate(table, vaddr):
    page = vaddr >> OFFSET_BITS
    offset = vaddr & (1 << OFFSET_BITS)
    frame = table[page]
    if frame is None:
        return "page fault"
    return (frame << OFFSET_BITS) | offset
--- solution
OFFSET_BITS = 9


def translate(table, vaddr):
    page = vaddr >> OFFSET_BITS
    offset = vaddr & ((1 << OFFSET_BITS) - 1)
    frame = table[page]
    if frame is None:
        return "page fault"
    return (frame << OFFSET_BITS) | offset
--- hint
Write `1 << OFFSET_BITS` in binary. How many 1 bits does it have, and which bits of the address does `&` with it keep?
--- hint
The mask for the low 9 bits has nine 1s: one less than `1 << 9`.
--- check case | An address in the middle of a page
translate([None, 5] + [None] * 126, 0x0345)
=> 0x0B45
--- check case | The first and last byte of a page
(translate([3] + [None] * 127, 0x0000), translate([3] + [None] * 127, 0x01FF))
=> (0x0600, 0x07FF)
--- check case | The last page of the address space
translate([None] * 127 + [0], 0xFFFF)
=> 0x01FF
--- check case | A missing page
translate([None] * 128, 0x1234)
=> "page fault"

+++ practice | ASIDs against flushing the TLB
--- task
On a context switch, a kernel either **flushes** the TLB, or keeps it and tags each entry with the process it belongs to (an **ASID**). Write `tlb_misses(events, size)` to compare the two on the same run.

`events` is a list of `("switch", pid)` and `("ref", page)` events, and the process running at the start is `0`. The TLB holds at most `size` translations and throws out the least recently used one when it needs room; a hit makes an entry most recently used. A reference is a hit if the running process's translation for that page is in the TLB.

- In **flush** mode, the TLB holds pages; a switch to a **different** process empties it.
- In **ASID** mode, the TLB holds `(pid, page)` pairs, and a switch changes nothing but the running process.

A switch to the process that is already running does nothing in either mode. Return a pair: the number of misses with flushing, then with ASIDs.
--- starter
def tlb_misses(events, size):
    return (0, 0)
--- solution
def lru_misses(keys, size, flush_points):
    tlb = {}
    misses = 0
    for i, key in enumerate(keys):
        if i in flush_points:
            tlb.clear()
        if key in tlb:
            tlb[key] = tlb.pop(key)
        else:
            misses += 1
            if len(tlb) == size:
                del tlb[next(iter(tlb))]
            tlb[key] = True
    return misses


def tlb_misses(events, size):
    running = 0
    pages = []
    tagged = []
    flush_points = set()
    for kind, value in events:
        if kind == "switch":
            if value != running:
                running = value
                flush_points.add(len(pages))
        else:
            pages.append(value)
            tagged.append((running, value))
    return (lru_misses(pages, size, flush_points), lru_misses(tagged, size, set()))
--- hint
Both modes are the same LRU cache; only the keys and the flushes differ. Write one helper that counts misses for a list of keys, emptying the cache at given positions.
--- hint
Walk the events once, keeping the running process. Each reference adds its page to one list and `(pid, page)` to another; each real switch records that the flush-mode cache must be emptied before the next reference.
--- check case | Two processes taking turns, with room for both
tlb_misses([("ref", 1), ("ref", 2), ("switch", 1), ("ref", 1), ("ref", 2), ("switch", 0), ("ref", 1), ("ref", 2)], 4)
=> (6, 4)
?? With flushing, every reference after a switch misses. With ASIDs, process 0's pages are still there when it comes back.
--- check case | The same page number means different things in different processes
tlb_misses([("ref", 7), ("switch", 2), ("ref", 7), ("switch", 0), ("ref", 7)], 2)
=> (3, 2)
--- check case | A switch to the running process flushes nothing
tlb_misses([("ref", 3), ("switch", 0), ("ref", 3)], 2)
=> (1, 1)
--- check case | A small TLB: ASIDs help less when the processes do not fit together
tlb_misses([("ref", 1), ("ref", 2), ("switch", 1), ("ref", 1), ("ref", 2), ("switch", 0), ("ref", 1), ("ref", 2)], 2)
=> (6, 6)
--- check case | No switches: both modes agree
tlb_misses([("ref", p) for p in [1, 2, 3, 1, 2, 4, 1, 2]], 3)
=> (4, 4)
--- check case | No events
tlb_misses([], 4)
=> (0, 0)
=== os-12 | Page replacement: FIFO, LRU, Clock and OPT, and Belady's anomaly
--- teach
Last lesson, a page table entry had a **valid** bit, and touching an invalid page caused a page fault. So far a page fault meant a bug. This lesson makes it a feature: the kernel keeps only **some** of a process's pages in memory and loads the others from disk when they are touched. Memory then acts as a cache for the disk, and the kernel must keep deciding which page to throw out to make room. That choice is **page replacement**. You will build a simulator for the four classic policies, prove which one is optimal, and meet a policy that gets **worse** when you give it more memory.

### Demand paging

With **demand paging**, a process starts with none of its pages in memory. Each page is loaded only when the process first touches it. The pages that are not in memory live on disk: code pages in the program file, and data pages in a **swap** area.

When the process touches a page that is not there, the page fault handler:

1. checks that the address is legal (if not, the process is killed);
2. finds a free frame, or chooses a **victim** page to evict, writing it to disk first if it is **dirty** (changed since it was loaded);
3. reads the page from disk into the frame, while the process sleeps and another runs;
4. fills in the page table entry, marks it valid, and restarts the instruction that faulted.

Most programs never touch large parts of their code (error handlers, unused features), so demand paging loads less, starts faster, and lets many more processes share memory.

### What a fault costs

A memory access takes about 100 ns. A page fault that must read from a hard disk takes about 8 ms: **80,000 times** longer. With a fault rate p (the fraction of accesses that fault):

effective access time = (1 − p) · 100 ns + p · 8,000,000 ns

To be at most 10% slower than memory, that has to stay under 110 ns, so p must be below 10 / 7,999,900: about **one fault in 800,000 accesses**. An SSD, at roughly 0.1 ms per fault, allows about one in 10,000. Faults must be very rare, so the choice of victim matters enormously.

### The replacement problem

Study it with a **reference string**: the sequence of page numbers a program touches, such as `7 0 1 2 0 3`. Given a number of frames, a policy decides which page to evict on each fault, and the score is the **number of faults**. Every page's first use is a fault whatever the policy: those are [[compulsory faults|three-cs]].

### FIFO

**First in, first out** evicts the page that has been in memory longest, like the oldest carton of milk in the fridge. It needs only a queue.

Its weakness: the oldest page may be the busiest one, like the page holding the main loop, loaded at the start and used ever since. FIFO throws it out anyway, and it faults straight back in.

### Belady's anomaly

In 1969, [[László Bélády|belady]] and colleagues found something shocking. On this reference string:

`1 2 3 4 1 2 5 1 2 3 4 5`

FIFO makes **9** faults with 3 frames and **10** faults with 4 frames. More memory, more faults. This is **Belady's anomaly**. It means that with FIFO you cannot even count on memory upgrades helping.

### OPT: the best possible policy

The **optimal** policy (OPT, or Belady's MIN, 1966) evicts the page whose **next use is farthest in the future**; a page never used again is the perfect victim. On the string above it makes 7 faults with 3 frames.

**Why it is optimal**, in outline. Take any policy A, and the first fault where A evicts a page x while keeping a page y whose next use comes later (or never). Build a policy A′ that evicts y there instead, and afterwards copies A's choices, except that A′ has x where A has y. Since y is not used before x's next use, A′ has no fault that A does not have until then; and at x's next use, A faults while A′ hits. After that, A′ can pay back at most that one saved fault, at y's next use, before its memory becomes the same as A's. (The full proof follows the one page on which the two memories differ.) So A′ makes no more faults than A, and it makes the farthest-next-use choice at one more fault. Repeating this, fault after fault, turns any policy into OPT without ever adding a fault: no policy can have fewer faults than OPT.

OPT needs to know the future, so no kernel can run it. It is the **yardstick**: to judge a real policy, compare it with OPT on recorded reference strings.

### LRU: the past as a guide to the future

**Least recently used** evicts the page whose last use is longest ago. It bets on **locality**: a page not used for a long time will probably not be needed soon. That is OPT looking backwards instead of forwards, and on real programs it comes close to OPT.

**LRU never suffers Belady's anomaly.** With n frames, LRU always holds exactly the n most recently used distinct pages (or all of them, if fewer have been used). The n most recently used pages are always among the n + 1 most recently used. So at every moment, what LRU holds with n frames is a subset of what it holds with n + 1 frames. Any reference that hits with n frames therefore also hits with n + 1, so more frames can never add a fault. Policies with this **inclusion property** are called **stack algorithms**. OPT is one too; FIFO is not.

### Implementing LRU

Exact LRU has to note the time of **every** memory access, not just every fault: billions per second. With a dict and a doubly linked list (from DSA I) each update is O(1), which is fine for a software cache, but no CPU records a timestamp in every page table entry on every access. Real kernels **approximate** LRU with the **accessed bit** from last lesson, which the hardware sets for free.

### Clock: second chance

The **Clock** algorithm arranges the frames in a circle, with a **hand** pointing at one of them. Each frame has a reference bit, set to 1 whenever its page is used. When a victim is needed:

- look at the frame under the hand;
- if its bit is 1, the page was used recently: give it a **second chance** by clearing the bit to 0, and move the hand on;
- if its bit is 0, evict that page, put the new page there with its bit set, and move the hand on.

Here is Clock with 3 frames on `1 2 3 4 2 5 1`. Frames fill in order, each new page with its bit set; the hand starts at the first frame:

| use | frames | bits | hand after | what happened |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1, 2, 3 | 1 2 3 | 1 1 1 | 0 | three compulsory faults |
| 4 | 4 2 3 | 1 0 0 | 1 | every bit was 1: cleared all the way round, evicted 1 |
| 2 | 4 2 3 | 1 1 0 | 1 | hit: 2's bit set again |
| 5 | 4 2 5 | 1 0 1 | 0 | 2 had its bit set: second chance; 3 evicted |
| 1 | 4 1 5 | 0 1 1 | 2 | 4 gets a second chance; 2 evicted |

FIFO on the same string would evict 2 at the use of 5, just after it was used. If every bit is 1, the hand clears them all and comes back to where it started, and Clock behaves like FIFO for that eviction.

Kernels refine this. A **dirty** page costs a disk write to evict, so the **enhanced** Clock prefers pages that are both unused and clean.

### Thrashing and the working set

If the processes in memory together need more frames than exist, every process faults constantly, each fault evicts a page another process is about to use, and the CPU sits idle waiting for the disk. This collapse is **thrashing**. Adding more processes, the naive way to use an idle CPU, makes it worse.

Peter Denning's **[[working set|working-set]]** model (1968) describes what a process needs: its working set at time t is the set of distinct pages it used in the last Δ references. If the kernel gives every process at least its working set, faults stay rare; if the working sets do not all fit, it should **suspend** some processes entirely rather than let all of them thrash.

### What it costs

- **FIFO:** O(1) per reference with a queue.
- **LRU:** O(1) per reference with a hash map and a doubly linked list, in software. The simple version, a list where each use moves the page to the end, costs O(frames) per reference.
- **Clock:** O(1) per hit; an eviction may sweep the whole circle, but each sweep clears bits that must be set again by real uses, so the cost is O(1) on average.
- **OPT:** needs the whole future reference string.

### Where it is used

- **Linux** approximates LRU with an active and an inactive list of pages, using accessed bits; since version 6.1 it can use a newer design called [[MGLRU|mglru]].
- **Databases** keep a **buffer pool** of disk pages in memory and use Clock or LRU variants to choose victims.
- **CPU caches** use cheap approximations of LRU inside each set, as you saw in Computer Organization.
- **Software caches**, from web caches to Python's `functools.lru_cache`, face the same question: when full, what goes?

**Watch out:**

- **Hits must count for LRU.** An "LRU" that only updates on faults is really FIFO.
- **Compulsory faults count.** The first use of every page is a fault, even with plenty of frames.
- **More frames can hurt FIFO.** Only stack algorithms like LRU and OPT promise that more memory never adds faults.
- **Clock's hand moves on after placing a page.** Forgetting that makes the next search start on the page just loaded.
- **OPT's ties.** When several pages are never used again, any of them is a perfect victim; a simulator must still pick one by a fixed rule so that its results can be checked.

::: context three-cs Three kinds of miss
Cache designers sort misses into three kinds, the "three Cs". Compulsory misses are first uses, which no policy can avoid. Capacity misses happen because the working data does not fit, and would happen even with a perfect policy. Conflict misses come from the policy or the cache's layout choosing badly. OPT removes all but the first two kinds. The same words apply to CPU caches, TLBs and page replacement.
:::

::: context belady The man behind MIN and the anomaly
László Bélády, a Hungarian-born computer scientist, worked at IBM on the virtual-memory system of an experimental machine, the M44/44X, in the 1960s. His 1966 paper described the optimal policy MIN, now called OPT or Belady's algorithm, as a benchmark no real policy could beat. In 1969 he, Robert Nelson and Gerald Shedler published the FIFO anomaly that carries his name.
:::

::: context working-set What a process is using right now
The working set W(t, Δ) is the set of distinct pages a process referenced in its last Δ references before time t. If Δ is too small, it misses pages the process will soon use again; if too large, it includes pages from an old phase of the program. Denning showed that giving each process its working set keeps page faults low, and a practice problem in this lesson computes working-set sizes. Windows, for one, calls the memory it lets each process keep its "working set".
:::

::: context mglru A newer Linux page reclaimer
Multi-generational LRU (MGLRU) was written at Google and merged in Linux 6.1 in 2022. Instead of two lists, active and inactive, it sorts pages into several generations by how recently their accessed bits were seen set, and scans page tables in bulk to collect those bits cheaply. Google reported fewer out-of-memory kills and lower CPU use for reclaiming memory on Android phones and Chromebooks, which is why it was adopted.
:::
--- task
Write `simulate(policy, refs, frames)`, a page-replacement simulator. `refs` is a reference string (a list of page numbers), `frames` (at least 1) is how many pages fit in memory, and memory starts empty. Every reference to a page not in memory is a fault. If a frame is free, the page goes in; otherwise a victim is evicted first. Return a tuple: the number of faults, and the list of evicted pages in the order they were evicted.

The policy is one of:

- `"fifo"`: evict the page that was loaded earliest.
- `"lru"`: evict the page whose most recent use is longest ago.
- `"opt"`: evict the page whose next use comes latest; a page never used again counts as latest of all. If several pages tie (all never used again), evict the smallest page number.
- `"clock"`: the frames form a circle, each with a reference bit, and a hand that starts at frame 0. A hit sets that page's bit to 1. A fault with a free frame puts the page in the lowest-numbered free frame with its bit set to 1, and the hand does not move. A fault with memory full: while the frame under the hand has its bit set, clear it and move the hand to the next frame (after the last frame comes frame 0); then evict the page under the hand, put the new page there with its bit set to 1, and move the hand to the next frame.

For example, `simulate("fifo", [1, 2, 1, 3, 4], 2)` is `(4, [1, 2])`.
--- starter
import random


def simulate(policy, refs, frames):
    return (0, [])
--- solution
import random


def simulate(policy, refs, frames):
    faults = 0
    evicted = []
    if policy == "clock":
        slots = []
        bits = []
        hand = 0
        for page in refs:
            if page in slots:
                bits[slots.index(page)] = 1
                continue
            faults += 1
            if len(slots) < frames:
                slots.append(page)
                bits.append(1)
                continue
            while bits[hand] == 1:
                bits[hand] = 0
                hand = (hand + 1) % frames
            evicted.append(slots[hand])
            slots[hand] = page
            bits[hand] = 1
            hand = (hand + 1) % frames
        return faults, evicted

    memory = []
    for t, page in enumerate(refs):
        if page in memory:
            if policy == "lru":
                memory.remove(page)
                memory.append(page)
            continue
        faults += 1
        if len(memory) == frames:
            if policy == "opt":
                def next_use(p):
                    for u in range(t + 1, len(refs)):
                        if refs[u] == p:
                            return u
                    return len(refs)
                victim = max(memory, key=lambda p: (next_use(p), -p))
            else:
                victim = memory[0]
            memory.remove(victim)
            evicted.append(victim)
        memory.append(page)
    return faults, evicted
--- hint
FIFO and LRU can share one list of the pages in memory, oldest first, and evict `memory[0]`. The only difference: on a hit, LRU moves the page to the end of the list, and FIFO leaves it where it is.
--- hint
For OPT, write a helper that finds the next position after the current one where a page is used, or `len(refs)` if never. Then `max` over the pages in memory with the key `(next use, -page)`, which picks the latest next use and, on a tie, the smallest page.
--- hint
For Clock, keep two lists by frame, the pages and their bits, plus the hand. Only a fault with memory full moves the hand: clear bits while they are 1, evict at the hand, then move the hand once more past the new page.
--- check case | The example from the task
simulate("fifo", [1, 2, 1, 3, 4], 2)
=> (4, [1, 2])
--- check case | Belady's anomaly: FIFO gets worse with more frames
(simulate("fifo", [3, 5, 7, 9, 3, 5, 1, 3, 5, 7, 9, 1], 3)[0], simulate("fifo", [3, 5, 7, 9, 3, 5, 1, 3, 5, 7, 9, 1], 4)[0])
=> (9, 10)
--- check case | LRU and OPT get better with more frames on the same string
[simulate(p, [3, 5, 7, 9, 3, 5, 1, 3, 5, 7, 9, 1], f)[0] for p in ["lru", "opt"] for f in [3, 4]]
=> [10, 8, 7, 6]
--- check case | FIFO on a longer string, with the evictions
simulate("fifo", [7, 0, 1, 2, 0, 3, 0, 4, 2, 3, 0, 3, 2, 1, 2, 0, 1, 7, 0, 1], 3)
=> (15, [7, 0, 1, 2, 3, 0, 4, 2, 3, 0, 1, 2])
--- check case | LRU on the same string
simulate("lru", [7, 0, 1, 2, 0, 3, 0, 4, 2, 3, 0, 3, 2, 1, 2, 0, 1, 7, 0, 1], 3)
=> (12, [7, 1, 2, 3, 0, 4, 0, 3, 2])
?? A hit must move the page to the most recently used end. Without that, LRU behaves like FIFO.
--- check case | OPT on the same string
simulate("opt", [7, 0, 1, 2, 0, 3, 0, 4, 2, 3, 0, 3, 2, 1, 2, 0, 1, 7, 0, 1], 3)
=> (9, [7, 1, 0, 4, 3, 2])
--- check case | Clock on the same string
simulate("clock", [7, 0, 1, 2, 0, 3, 0, 4, 2, 3, 0, 3, 2, 1, 2, 0, 1, 7, 0, 1], 3)
=> (14, [7, 1, 2, 0, 3, 4, 2, 0, 3, 1, 2])
--- check case | Clock gives a recently used page a second chance
simulate("clock", [2, 4, 6, 2, 8, 4, 10, 2], 3)
=> (6, [2, 6, 4])
?? At the use of 10, page 4 was used since the last sweep, so its bit is set: it is cleared and skipped, and 6 is evicted instead.
--- check case | OPT breaks ties by evicting the smallest page
simulate("opt", [4, 2, 9, 1, 2], 3)
=> (4, [4])
?? When 1 arrives, pages 4 and 9 are never used again, so either is a perfect victim; the rule says the smaller one, 4.
--- check case | Nothing to do, and enough frames for everything
[simulate(p, [], 3) for p in ["fifo", "lru", "opt", "clock"]] + [simulate(p, [1, 2, 1, 3, 2], 5) for p in ["fifo", "lru", "opt", "clock"]]
=> [(0, []), (0, []), (0, []), (0, []), (3, []), (3, []), (3, []), (3, [])]
--- check case | One frame: every change of page is a fault
[simulate(p, [1, 1, 2, 2, 1], 1) for p in ["fifo", "lru", "opt", "clock"]]
=> [(3, [1, 2]), (3, [1, 2]), (3, [1, 2]), (3, [1, 2])]
--- check test | On 100 random strings, OPT is never beaten, and every policy faults at least once per distinct page
all((lambda r: all(len(set(r)) <= simulate("opt", r, f)[0] <= min(simulate(p, r, f)[0] for p in ["fifo", "lru", "clock"]) for f in [2, 3, 4]))((lambda g: [g.randint(0, 6) for _ in range(30)])(random.Random(s))) for s in range(100))
--- check test | On 100 random strings, LRU never faults more with more frames
all((lambda r: all(simulate("lru", r, f)[0] >= simulate("lru", r, f + 1)[0] for f in range(1, 7)))((lambda g: [g.randint(0, 7) for _ in range(40)])(random.Random(s))) for s in range(100))

+++ question | The fault rate that keeps up
--- ask
Memory takes 200 ns and a page fault takes 10 ms (10,000,000 ns). About how rare must faults be for the effective access time to stay at or below 220 ns?
--- choice
About one fault in 1,000 accesses.
--- choice
About one fault in 50,000 accesses.
--- choice correct
About one fault in 500,000 accesses.
--- choice
About one fault in 50,000,000 accesses.
--- why
Solve (1 − p) · 200 + p · 10,000,000 ≤ 220. That is 200 + p · 9,999,800 ≤ 220, so p ≤ 20 / 9,999,800, about 2 × 10⁻⁶: one fault in about 500,000 accesses. A fault costs 50,000 memory accesses, so even rare faults dominate.

+++ question | Why LRU cannot show Belady's anomaly
--- ask
Which fact proves that giving LRU more frames never adds a fault?
--- choice
LRU evicts the page with the farthest next use.
--- choice correct
With n frames LRU holds the n most recently used pages, which are always among the n + 1 most recently used, so every hit with n frames is also a hit with n + 1.
--- choice
LRU uses a queue, like FIFO, but longer.
--- choice
LRU never evicts a dirty page.
--- why
That is the inclusion property of stack algorithms: memory with n frames is always a subset of memory with n + 1 frames. FIFO lacks it, because which pages it holds depends on when they were loaded, and a bigger memory can load and evict at different moments.

+++ question | Clock when every bit is set
--- ask
Every frame's reference bit is 1 when a fault needs a victim under Clock. Which page is evicted?
--- choice
The page with the most uses.
--- choice correct
The page under the hand when the search started: the hand clears every bit going round once and evicts it, just as FIFO would.
--- choice
None: the fault fails because every page is in use.
--- choice
The page loaded most recently.
--- why
The hand clears each bit it passes, so after one full turn it is back at its starting frame, whose bit it has just cleared. That page is evicted. With all bits set, Clock has no information about recency and degenerates to FIFO for that eviction.

+++ practice | Hunting for Belady's anomaly
--- task
Write two functions.

- `fifo_faults(refs, frames)`: the number of page faults FIFO makes on the reference string `refs` with `frames` frames, starting from empty memory.
- `belady(refs, max_frames)`: the list of frame counts `f`, from 1 up to `max_frames - 1`, for which FIFO makes **more** faults with `f + 1` frames than with `f` frames. An empty list means no anomaly in that range.

For example, `belady([1, 2, 3, 4, 1, 2, 5, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5], 5)` is `[3]`: 9 faults with 3 frames, 10 with 4.
--- starter
def fifo_faults(refs, frames):
    return len(refs)


def belady(refs, max_frames):
    return []
--- solution
def fifo_faults(refs, frames):
    memory = []
    faults = 0
    for page in refs:
        if page not in memory:
            faults += 1
            if len(memory) == frames:
                memory.pop(0)
            memory.append(page)
    return faults


def belady(refs, max_frames):
    counts = [fifo_faults(refs, f) for f in range(1, max_frames + 1)]
    return [f for f in range(1, max_frames) if counts[f] > counts[f - 1]]
--- hint
FIFO only needs a list of the pages in memory, oldest first: on a fault with memory full, drop the first one.
--- hint
Compute the fault count for every frame count from 1 to `max_frames` once, then compare each count with the next.
--- check case | The example from the task
belady([1, 2, 3, 4, 1, 2, 5, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5], 5)
=> [3]
--- check case | FIFO fault counts on the classic string
[fifo_faults([1, 2, 3, 4, 1, 2, 5, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5], f) for f in range(1, 6)]
=> [12, 12, 9, 10, 5]
--- check case | A string with locality shows no anomaly
belady([1, 2, 1, 3, 1, 2, 1, 4, 1, 2], 4)
=> []
--- check case | The classic string twice over: still one anomaly
belady([1, 2, 3, 4, 1, 2, 5, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5] * 2, 6)
=> [3]
--- check case | No references, and one frame only
(belady([], 4), fifo_faults([6, 6, 7, 6], 1), belady([1, 2], 1))
=> ([], 3, [])

+++ practice | LRU for every memory size at once
--- task
Because LRU is a stack algorithm, one pass over the reference string gives its fault count for **every** number of frames. Keep a list of the pages seen so far, most recently used first (the **LRU stack**). For each reference, its **stack distance** is its position in that list counting from 1 (the page used just before has distance 1), or infinity if it has never been used. Then move the page to the front.

With f frames, LRU holds the top f pages of the stack, so a reference hits exactly when its stack distance is at most f.

Write `lru_all(refs, max_frames)`, which returns a list of LRU fault counts for 1, 2, …, `max_frames` frames, computed from the stack distances in a single pass (do not run a separate simulation per frame count).

For example, `lru_all([1, 2, 1, 3, 2], 3)` is `[5, 4, 3]`.
--- starter
import random


def lru_all(refs, max_frames):
    return [len(refs)] * max_frames
--- solution
import random


def lru_all(refs, max_frames):
    stack = []
    hits_at = [0] * (max_frames + 1)
    for page in refs:
        if page in stack:
            d = stack.index(page) + 1
            if d <= max_frames:
                hits_at[d] += 1
            stack.remove(page)
        stack.insert(0, page)
    faults = []
    hits = 0
    for f in range(1, max_frames + 1):
        hits += hits_at[f]
        faults.append(len(refs) - hits)
    return faults
--- hint
Count how many references have each stack distance. A reference with distance d is a hit for every frame count of d or more.
--- hint
So the hits with f frames are the references with distance 1, 2, …, f: a running total over the distance counts. Faults are the length of the string minus the hits.
--- check case | The example from the task
lru_all([1, 2, 1, 3, 2], 3)
=> [5, 4, 3]
?? Distances: 1 and 2 are new, then 1 has distance 2, 3 is new, 2 has distance 3.
--- check case | The classic string: no anomaly for LRU
lru_all([1, 2, 3, 4, 1, 2, 5, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5], 5)
=> [12, 12, 10, 8, 5]
--- check case | Repeating one page hits at distance 1
lru_all([4, 4, 4, 4], 2)
=> [1, 1]
--- check case | A cycle one page bigger than memory faults every time
lru_all([1, 2, 3, 4] * 3, 4)
=> [12, 12, 12, 4]
--- check case | No references
lru_all([], 3)
=> [0, 0, 0]
--- check case | Three random strings, checked against a direct simulation
[lru_all((lambda g: [g.randint(0, 8) for _ in range(40)])(random.Random(s)), 6) for s in range(3)]
=> [[37, 33, 29, 23, 21, 16], [33, 28, 24, 19, 18, 12], [32, 25, 23, 22, 19, 15]]

+++ practice | Faults and write-backs
--- task
Evicting a **dirty** page costs a disk write before the frame can be reused; a clean page can simply be dropped. Write `lru_writes(refs, frames)` for LRU replacement where each reference is a pair `(page, op)`, with `op` either `"r"` (read) or `"w"` (write).

A page becomes dirty when it is written, and stays dirty until it is evicted. When it is loaded again later, it starts clean. Return a tuple: the number of faults, and the number of **write-backs** (evictions of dirty pages). Pages still in memory at the end are not written back.

For example, `lru_writes([(1, "w"), (2, "r"), (3, "r")], 2)` is `(3, 1)`: page 1 is dirty when 3 evicts it.
--- starter
def lru_writes(refs, frames):
    return (0, 0)
--- solution
def lru_writes(refs, frames):
    memory = []
    dirty = set()
    faults = 0
    writebacks = 0
    for page, op in refs:
        if page in memory:
            memory.remove(page)
        else:
            faults += 1
            if len(memory) == frames:
                victim = memory.pop(0)
                if victim in dirty:
                    writebacks += 1
                    dirty.discard(victim)
        memory.append(page)
        if op == "w":
            dirty.add(page)
    return faults, writebacks
--- hint
Keep the LRU list as in the lesson's task, plus a set of dirty pages. A write, hit or fault, adds the page to the set.
--- hint
When a victim is evicted, count a write-back only if it is in the dirty set, and remove it from the set, so that it starts clean if it comes back.
--- check case | The example from the task
lru_writes([(1, "w"), (2, "r"), (3, "r")], 2)
=> (3, 1)
--- check case | Reads alone never cause write-backs
lru_writes([(p, "r") for p in [1, 2, 3, 1, 4, 2]], 2)
=> (6, 0)
--- check case | A page written on a hit is dirty when it is evicted
lru_writes([(1, "r"), (2, "r"), (1, "w"), (3, "r"), (4, "r")], 2)
=> (4, 1)
?? 1 is written while in memory. 3 evicts 2 (clean), then 4 evicts 1, which is dirty.
--- check case | A reloaded page starts clean
lru_writes([(1, "w"), (2, "r"), (1, "r"), (2, "r")], 1)
=> (4, 1)
--- check case | Dirty pages left in memory at the end are not counted
lru_writes([(1, "w"), (2, "w")], 2)
=> (2, 0)
--- check case | No references
lru_writes([], 3)
=> (0, 0)

+++ practice | Working-set sizes
--- task
The working set of a process at time `t` is the set of distinct pages it used in its last `delta` references, up to and including reference `t` (counting from 0). Near the start, there are fewer than `delta` earlier references: use all of them.

Write `working_sets(refs, delta)`, which returns the list of working-set **sizes**, one for each time `t` from 0 to `len(refs) - 1`, and `peak(refs, delta)`, the largest of them (0 for an empty reference string). `delta` is at least 1.

For example, `working_sets([1, 2, 1, 3, 3, 3], 3)` is `[1, 2, 2, 3, 2, 1]`.
--- starter
def working_sets(refs, delta):
    return [len(set(refs))] * len(refs)


def peak(refs, delta):
    return len(set(refs))
--- solution
def working_sets(refs, delta):
    sizes = []
    for t in range(len(refs)):
        window = refs[max(0, t - delta + 1): t + 1]
        sizes.append(len(set(window)))
    return sizes


def peak(refs, delta):
    return max(working_sets(refs, delta), default=0)
--- hint
The window at time `t` is the slice from `t - delta + 1` to `t`, inclusive. Near the start, `t - delta + 1` is negative: clamp it at 0, because a negative slice start would count from the end of the list.
--- hint
The size is `len(set(window))`. For `peak`, `max` has a `default` for the empty case.
--- check case | The example from the task
working_sets([1, 2, 1, 3, 3, 3], 3)
=> [1, 2, 2, 3, 2, 1]
--- check case | A window of 1 always holds one page
working_sets([5, 6, 5, 7], 1)
=> [1, 1, 1, 1]
--- check case | A window longer than the string counts everything so far
working_sets([1, 2, 1, 3], 10)
=> [1, 2, 2, 3]
?? At time 1 with a window of 10, the slice must start at 0, not at 1 - 10 + 1 = -8, which would wrap around to the end of the list.
--- check case | Two phases of a program: the working set shrinks, then changes
working_sets([1, 2, 3, 1, 2, 3, 9, 9, 9, 9], 4)
=> [1, 2, 3, 3, 3, 3, 4, 3, 2, 1]
--- check case | Peaks, and an empty string
(peak([1, 2, 3, 1, 2, 3, 9, 9, 9, 9], 4), peak([4, 4, 4], 2), peak([], 5), working_sets([], 5))
=> (4, 1, 0, [])

+++ practice | Debug: an LRU that forgets its hits
--- task
`lru_faults(refs, frames)` is meant to count LRU page faults. It keeps the pages in a list, least recently used first, and evicts the first one. But on the string `[1, 2, 3, 1, 4, 1, 2, 5, 1, 2, 6, 1, 2]` with 3 frames it reports 10 faults, when LRU makes only 7: it keeps evicting pages 1 and 2, which are used all the time. Find the bug and fix it.
--- starter
def lru_faults(refs, frames):
    memory = []
    faults = 0
    for page in refs:
        if page in memory:
            continue
        faults += 1
        if len(memory) == frames:
            memory.pop(0)
        memory.append(page)
    return faults
--- solution
def lru_faults(refs, frames):
    memory = []
    faults = 0
    for page in refs:
        if page in memory:
            memory.remove(page)
            memory.append(page)
            continue
        faults += 1
        if len(memory) == frames:
            memory.pop(0)
        memory.append(page)
    return faults
--- hint
What happens to the order of the list when a page is used but is already in memory? Is this LRU, or another policy you have met?
--- hint
On a hit, move the page to the most recently used end of the list before going on.
--- check case | The string from the task
lru_faults([1, 2, 3, 1, 4, 1, 2, 5, 1, 2, 6, 1, 2], 3)
=> 7
--- check case | A longer string with 3 frames
lru_faults([7, 0, 1, 2, 0, 3, 0, 4, 2, 3, 0, 3, 2, 1, 2, 0, 1, 7, 0, 1], 3)
=> 12
--- check case | Plenty of frames: only the first uses fault
lru_faults([3, 1, 3, 2, 1], 3)
=> 3
--- check case | One frame
lru_faults([2, 2, 3, 2], 1)
=> 3

+++ practice | The fewest frames for a fault budget
--- task
A ground-station process replays a recorded reference string, and the operators want it to make at most `budget` page faults under LRU. Write `min_frames(refs, budget)`, which returns the smallest number of frames (at least 1) for which LRU makes at most `budget` faults, or `None` if even enough frames for every distinct page is not enough.

Because LRU is a stack algorithm, its fault count never goes up as frames are added, so the answer can be found by **binary search** on the number of frames, as in DSA I. The checks include a reference string of 60,000 pages over 2,000 distinct pages, so trying every frame count one by one is far too slow, and each LRU simulation must take constant time per reference: keep the pages in a dict, which remembers insertion order, and move a page to the end on each use.
--- starter
def lru_count(refs, frames):
    return 0


def min_frames(refs, budget):
    return None
--- solution
def lru_count(refs, frames):
    memory = {}
    faults = 0
    for page in refs:
        if page in memory:
            del memory[page]
        else:
            faults += 1
            if len(memory) == frames:
                del memory[next(iter(memory))]
        memory[page] = True
    return faults


def min_frames(refs, budget):
    distinct = len(set(refs))
    if lru_count(refs, max(distinct, 1)) > budget:
        return None
    low, high = 1, max(distinct, 1)
    while low < high:
        mid = (low + high) // 2
        if lru_count(refs, mid) <= budget:
            high = mid
        else:
            low = mid + 1
    return low
--- hint
Two separate speed-ups are needed. First, make one simulation O(1) per reference: with a dict, `del memory[page]` and `memory[page] = True` move a page to the end, and `next(iter(memory))` is the least recently used page.
--- hint
Second, binary search the smallest `f` in `1 .. distinct` with `lru_count(refs, f) <= budget`. The predicate is monotone because LRU faults never increase with more frames. If it is false even at `distinct`, return `None`.
--- hint
With enough frames for every distinct page, only the compulsory faults remain, so a budget below the number of distinct pages is impossible.
--- check case | Small strings
(min_frames([1, 2, 1, 3, 1, 2], 4), min_frames([1, 2, 1, 3, 1, 2], 3), min_frames([1, 2, 1, 3, 1, 2], 6))
=> (2, 3, 1)
--- check case | A budget below the compulsory faults is impossible
min_frames([4, 5, 6, 4], 2)
=> None
--- check case | An empty string needs one frame and makes no faults
min_frames([], 0)
=> 1
--- check case | 60,000 references: a sweep over 2,000 pages mixed with a hot loop of 40
min_frames([(i * 7919) % 2000 if i % 3 else i % 40 for i in range(60000)], 30000)
=> 1346
--- check test | The answer is really the smallest
(lambda r: lru_count(r, 1346) <= 30000 < lru_count(r, 1345))([(i * 7919) % 2000 if i % 3 else i % 40 for i in range(60000)])

=== os-13 | File systems: inodes, directories and block allocation
--- teach
Last lesson kept the busiest pages in RAM and pushed the rest out to disk. RAM forgets everything when the power goes; the disk does not. But a disk knows nothing about files or folders. It is a long row of numbered blocks, and all it can do is read block 7 or write block 7. This lesson builds the layer that turns that row of blocks into named files in folders: the **file system**. By the end you will have a working one inside a Python `bytearray`.

### What a file system is

A **file** is a named sequence of bytes that stays after the program that wrote it has ended. A **file system** is the data structure on a disk, plus the kernel code that runs it, that stores many files in fixed-size blocks and finds them again by name.

In everyday words: the disk is a warehouse of identical numbered shelves. The file system is the warehouse's record-keeping: a card for every item saying which shelves hold its pieces, a ledger of which shelves are empty, and a catalogue from names to cards.

The disk reads and writes whole **blocks**, never single bytes. A block is usually 4,096 bytes (4 KiB), made of eight 512-byte [[sectors|sector]]. Our model uses 64-byte blocks, so a small file already needs several of them.

### The layout of a disk

A simple Unix-style file system divides its blocks into regions, in this order:

| region | holds |
|---|---|
| superblock | the sizes of everything below, so the kernel can find it |
| block bitmap | one bit per block: 1 in use, 0 free |
| inode table | a fixed number of inodes, one per file |
| data region | the contents of files and folders |

The superblock, bitmap and inode table are **metadata**: data about the data. The data region is where file contents live. In our model the disk has 64 blocks; block 1 is the bitmap, blocks 2 to 9 hold 16 inodes, and blocks 10 to 63 are data.

### The inode

An **inode** (index node) is the fixed-size record that describes one file. It holds:

- the file's **type** (a regular file or a directory);
- its **size** in bytes;
- its **link count**: how many names point to it (more on this below);
- owner, permissions and times, which our model leaves out;
- and **block pointers**: the numbers of the data blocks that hold the contents.

Each inode has a number, its position in the inode table. The inode does **not** hold the file's name. Names live in directories, which map names to inode numbers. That split is what lets one file have two names.

Python can show you a real file's inode through `os.stat`:

```python fragment
import os

st = os.stat("telemetry.log")
st.st_ino      # the inode number, for example 1311
st.st_nlink    # the link count
st.st_size     # the size in bytes
st.st_blocks   # space really used, counted in 512-byte units
```

### From a byte offset to a block

A program asks for byte `offset` of a file. The file system splits the offset into two parts with integer division, exactly as a page table splits a virtual address (lesson 11):

- `k = offset // BLOCK` is the **file block index**: which block of the file, counting from 0;
- `offset % BLOCK` is the position inside that block.

Then it needs the disk block that holds file block `k`. With 64-byte blocks, byte 200 is in file block 3, at position 8:

```python
BLOCK = 64
for offset in [0, 63, 64, 200]:
    print(offset, divmod(offset, BLOCK))
```

It prints `(0, 0)`, `(0, 63)`, `(1, 0)` and `(3, 8)`. `divmod(a, b)` gives the pair `(a // b, a % b)` in one call.

### Direct and indirect pointers

The inode has room for only a few pointers. Big files need more, so the pointers come in levels:

- The first pointers are **direct**: pointer `k` holds the disk block of file block `k`. Our model has 5.
- The next one is **single indirect**: it holds the number of a block that is full of pointers. With 64-byte blocks and 4-byte pointers, that block holds 16 of them, for file blocks 5 to 20.
- Real systems add a **double indirect** pointer (a block of pointers to blocks of pointers) and a **triple indirect** one.

So file block `k` is found like this. If `k` is below the number of direct pointers, use `direct[k]`. Otherwise subtract that number: what is left, `k - NDIRECT`, is the slot inside the indirect block. Block 5 is slot 0 of the indirect block, not slot 5.

Why levels? Most files are small, and small files need no extra reads: their pointers are in the inode itself. A huge file pays one extra read per level, and gets an enormous range. Here is how many blocks each level adds in ext2, with 4,096-byte blocks, 4-byte pointers and 12 direct pointers:

```python
per_block = 4096 // 4
reach = [12, per_block, per_block ** 2, per_block ** 3]
print(reach)
print(sum(reach) * 4096 // 2 ** 40, "TiB, roughly")
```

It prints `[12, 1024, 1048576, 1073741824]`, then `4 TiB, roughly`. Each level multiplies the reach by 1,024.

### Holes

A pointer that holds 0 means "no block yet". Block 0 is never a data block, so 0 is safe to use as "none". If a program writes one byte at offset 1,000,000 of an empty file, the file system allocates only the block that byte lands in (plus any indirect blocks on the way). Every file block before it stays a **hole**: reading it gives zero bytes, and it uses no disk space. Such a file is a [[sparse file|sparse]].

That is why `st_size` and `st_blocks` can disagree. The size is where the last byte is; the blocks are what is really stored.

### Allocating blocks with a bitmap

Where does a new block come from? The **block bitmap** has one bit per block. To allocate, find a 0 bit, set it to 1, and use that block. To free, set it back to 0.

Bit `b` lives in byte `b // 8`, at bit position `b % 8` inside it (lowest bit first, in our model). Here is a test of one bit, on a two-byte bitmap:

```python
bitmap = bytes([0b11111111, 0b00000101])
for b in [3, 8, 9, 10]:
    print(b, (bitmap[b // 8] >> (b % 8)) & 1)
```

It prints `3 1`, `8 1`, `9 0` and `10 1`. Shifting right by `b % 8` brings the wanted bit to the bottom, and `& 1` keeps only it.

Scanning for the first free bit costs O(number of blocks) in the worst case. Real file systems keep a count of free blocks per group of blocks, so they skip full groups, and they search near the file's last block, so a file's blocks end up next to each other on disk (lesson 15 shows why that matters).

Other ways to allocate:

- **Contiguous**: each file is one run of blocks. Reading is fast, but files cannot grow, and free space breaks into holes exactly like the memory allocators of lesson 10.
- **Linked**: each block holds the number of the next one. The [[FAT|fat]] file system keeps all those "next" numbers in one table. Seeking to the middle of a file means following the chain from the start.
- **Indexed**: the inode holds the pointers, as above.
- **Extents**: the inode holds runs, "8,000 blocks starting at block 34,000", instead of one pointer per block. ext4 does this.

### Directories

A **directory** is a file whose contents are a list of **entries**, each mapping a name to an inode number. Its inode says "directory" in the type field. In old Unix each entry was exactly 16 bytes: a 2-byte inode number and a 14-byte name, padded with zero bytes. An entry with inode number 0 was an empty slot.

Every directory holds two special entries: `.` (itself) and `..` (its parent). The root directory is its own parent.

To open `/data/today/log`, the kernel does **path resolution**:

1. Start at the root directory's inode, a number the kernel knows (2 on ext4).
2. Read that directory's entries and find `data`. That gives an inode number.
3. Read that inode. It must be a directory. Find `today` in it.
4. Repeat for `log`. Its inode is the file.

A relative path such as `today/log` starts at the process's current directory instead of the root.

**Cost:** a path with d parts reads d directories. With directories stored as plain lists, each lookup scans its directory, so the cost is the total size of the directories on the path. That is why ext4 indexes large directories with a hash tree, and why the kernel keeps recently used names in memory, in the [[dentry cache|dentry]].

### Links

Because names are separate from inodes, two directory entries can hold the same inode number. Each entry is a **hard link**: a name for the file. Neither name is the "real" one.

- The inode's **link count** is the number of entries that point to it.
- `unlink` removes one entry and lowers the count. The file's inode and blocks are freed only when the count reaches 0 **and** no process still has the file open. That is why a log file you delete while a program writes to it keeps using space until the program closes it.
- Hard links to directories are forbidden (apart from `.` and `..`), so the directory tree can never contain a loop.

A **symbolic link** is different: a small file whose contents are a path. Opening it makes the kernel resolve that path instead. It can point anywhere, even across file systems, and it can **dangle**, pointing at a name that no longer exists.

```python fragment
import os

os.link("run.log", "run-backup.log")      # a second name: st_nlink is now 2
os.symlink("run.log", "latest.log")        # a file holding the text "run.log"
os.unlink("run.log")                       # run-backup.log still works; latest.log dangles
```

### Why it stays correct

A file system is correct when its metadata agrees with itself. Three rules, which the next lesson's recovery tools check:

1. **Every block is used once or free.** A data block is marked in the bitmap if and only if exactly one inode points to it (directly or through an indirect block). Indirect blocks count as used too.
2. **Link counts are true.** Each inode's link count equals the number of directory entries holding its number.
3. **Sizes fit.** Bytes past the size of a file are never returned by a read.

Each operation keeps these rules if it runs to the end. Look at what writing into a hole does: it marks a free block in the bitmap (rule 1 is now briefly broken: a marked block nobody points to), then stores its number in the inode (rule 1 holds again), then grows the size (rule 3). Before and after, all three rules hold. In between, they do not. If the power fails in between, the disk is left in a broken state. That problem is the whole of the next lesson.

### What it costs

Reading file block `k` costs one inode read (usually already in memory), plus one read per indirect level on the way, plus the data block. With our model: 1 read for blocks 0 to 4, 2 reads for blocks 5 to 20. Allocation with a bitmap scan is O(blocks) at worst. Path resolution costs one directory lookup per part of the path.

### Where it is used

- **ext4**, the usual Linux file system, uses inodes, a bitmap per group of blocks, extents, and hashed directories.
- **FAT** and **exFAT** are on almost every SD card and USB stick, because every operating system can read them. Small satellites and cameras often log to such cards.
- Flight computers with raw flash chips use file systems made for flash, such as LittleFS, which never overwrite a block in place (lesson 15 explains why).

**Watch out:**

- **The boundary between direct and indirect.** File block `NDIRECT` is slot 0 of the indirect block. Using `k` instead of `k - NDIRECT`, or testing `k <= NDIRECT`, is the classic off-by-one.
- **The indirect block is a block too.** Allocate it (and mark it in the bitmap) before storing pointers in it, and free it when the file is deleted.
- **A hole is not an error.** A zero pointer inside the file's size reads as zero bytes.
- **Deleting a name is not deleting the file.** The data goes only when the link count is 0 and nobody has it open.

::: context sector The smallest piece a disk writes
A sector is the unit a disk drive itself reads and writes. For decades it was 512 bytes; since about 2010 most hard drives use 4,096-byte sectors inside, called Advanced Format, while many still pretend to have 512-byte ones for old software. A file system block is one or more sectors. A write that is smaller than a sector forces the drive to read the whole sector, change part of it, and write it back. That is one reason block sizes are chosen to match the hardware.
:::

::: context sparse Files with holes
A sparse file has regions that were never written and take no disk space. Virtual machine disk images are the classic use: a 100 GB virtual disk that holds 3 GB of data takes about 3 GB on the host, and grows as the guest writes. Databases and core dumps use holes too. Copying a sparse file with a tool that does not know about holes (an old `cp`, or a simple read-and-write loop) writes every zero out, and the copy suddenly takes its full size.
:::

::: context fat A table of next pointers
FAT stands for File Allocation Table, the file system of MS-DOS from the late 1970s. It keeps one table entry per cluster (a group of blocks).
