A file's directory entry holds its first cluster; that cluster's table entry holds the next cluster, and so on, until a special end-of-chain value. It has no inodes: size and first cluster sit in the directory entry, so FAT cannot have hard links. FAT32 and its successor exFAT remain the common format for SD cards and USB sticks.
:::

::: context dentry Remembering names you just looked up
The dentry (directory entry) cache is a hash table inside the Linux kernel that maps a directory and a name to the inode it refers to. The first time you open `/home/ada/notes.txt`, the kernel reads three directories from disk. After that, the same lookups are answered from memory in well under a microsecond. It also remembers names that do not exist ("negative dentries"), so searching the same missing file again, as compilers do with include paths, is fast too.
:::
--- task
Write the core of a tiny Unix-style file system that lives in a `bytearray`. The starter gives the disk model; leave it as it is:

- 64 blocks of `BLOCK = 64` bytes. Block 1 is the bitmap (bit `b` is in byte `b // 8` of that block, lowest bit first). Blocks 2 to 9 are the inode table; data blocks are 10 to 63.
- An `Inode` has `kind`, `nlink`, `size`, a list `direct` of `NDIRECT = 5` block numbers and one `indirect` block number. 0 means "no block". An indirect block holds `PTRS = 16` pointers of 4 bytes each.
- Helpers: `mkfs()` makes an empty disk; `new_inode(disk, kind)` returns a fresh inode number; `read_inode(disk, n)` and `write_inode(disk, n, ino)` load and store inode `n`; `alloc_block(disk)` marks the lowest free data block in the bitmap, fills it with zeros and returns its number, or returns 0 when the disk is full; `read_ptr(disk, b, i)` and `write_ptr(disk, b, i, value)` read and write pointer `i` of block `b`.

Write three functions:

1. `bmap(disk, ino, k, alloc)` returns the disk block holding file block `k` of the inode object `ino`, or 0 if there is none. Blocks 0 to 4 are direct; block `k` from 5 to 20 is slot `k - 5` of the indirect block; anything from 21 on is beyond the largest file, so return 0. When `alloc` is true, allocate what is missing with `alloc_block`: first the indirect block if the inode has none, then the data block, and store the new numbers (in `ino` for direct and indirect pointers, in the indirect block for the others). If the disk fills up, return 0. `bmap` does not write the inode back; its caller does.
2. `read_at(disk, inum, offset, count)` returns the bytes of file `inum` from `offset`, at most `count` of them, and never past the file's size (so `b""` when `offset` is at or past the end). Holes read as zero bytes.
3. `write_at(disk, inum, offset, data)` writes `data` into file `inum` starting at `offset`, allocating blocks as it goes, and returns how many bytes it wrote. If a block cannot be had (the file would pass its largest size, or the disk is full), stop there and return the count so far. Afterwards the size is the larger of the old size and `offset` plus the bytes written, and the inode is written back.
--- starter
import struct

BLOCK = 64
NBLOCKS = 64
NINODES = 16
INODE_SIZE = 32
NDIRECT = 5
PTRS = BLOCK // 4
BITMAP_BLOCK = 1
INODE_START = 2
DATA_START = INODE_START + NINODES * INODE_SIZE // BLOCK

FREE, FILE, DIR = 0, 1, 2
INODE_FORMAT = "<HHI5II"


class Inode:
    def __init__(self, kind=FREE, nlink=0, size=0, direct=None, indirect=0):
        self.kind = kind
        self.nlink = nlink
        self.size = size
        self.direct = list(direct) if direct else [0] * NDIRECT
        self.indirect = indirect


def get_bit(disk, b):
    return (disk[BITMAP_BLOCK * BLOCK + b // 8] >> (b % 8)) & 1


def set_bit(disk, b, v):
    at = BITMAP_BLOCK * BLOCK + b // 8
    if v:
        disk[at] |= 1 << (b % 8)
    else:
        disk[at] &= ~(1 << (b % 8)) & 0xFF


def mkfs():
    disk = bytearray(BLOCK * NBLOCKS)
    for b in range(DATA_START):
        set_bit(disk, b, 1)
    return disk


def alloc_block(disk):
    for b in range(DATA_START, NBLOCKS):
        if not get_bit(disk, b):
            set_bit(disk, b, 1)
            disk[b * BLOCK:(b + 1) * BLOCK] = bytes(BLOCK)
            return b
    return 0


def read_ptr(disk, b, i):
    return struct.unpack_from("<I", disk, b * BLOCK + 4 * i)[0]


def write_ptr(disk, b, i, value):
    struct.pack_into("<I", disk, b * BLOCK + 4 * i, value)


def read_inode(disk, n):
    kind, nlink, size, *rest = struct.unpack_from(INODE_FORMAT, disk, INODE_START * BLOCK + n * INODE_SIZE)
    return Inode(kind, nlink, size, rest[:NDIRECT], rest[NDIRECT])


def write_inode(disk, n, ino):
    struct.pack_into(INODE_FORMAT, disk, INODE_START * BLOCK + n * INODE_SIZE,
                     ino.kind, ino.nlink, ino.size, *ino.direct, ino.indirect)


def new_inode(disk, kind):
    for n in range(1, NINODES):
        if read_inode(disk, n).kind == FREE:
            write_inode(disk, n, Inode(kind, 1))
            return n
    return 0


def bmap(disk, ino, k, alloc):
    raise NotImplementedError("map file block k to a disk block")


def read_at(disk, inum, offset, count):
    raise NotImplementedError("read bytes from the file")


def write_at(disk, inum, offset, data):
    raise NotImplementedError("write bytes into the file")
--- solution
import struct

BLOCK = 64
NBLOCKS = 64
NINODES = 16
INODE_SIZE = 32
NDIRECT = 5
PTRS = BLOCK // 4
BITMAP_BLOCK = 1
INODE_START = 2
DATA_START = INODE_START + NINODES * INODE_SIZE // BLOCK

FREE, FILE, DIR = 0, 1, 2
INODE_FORMAT = "<HHI5II"


class Inode:
    def __init__(self, kind=FREE, nlink=0, size=0, direct=None, indirect=0):
        self.kind = kind
        self.nlink = nlink
        self.size = size
        self.direct = list(direct) if direct else [0] * NDIRECT
        self.indirect = indirect


def get_bit(disk, b):
    return (disk[BITMAP_BLOCK * BLOCK + b // 8] >> (b % 8)) & 1


def set_bit(disk, b, v):
    at = BITMAP_BLOCK * BLOCK + b // 8
    if v:
        disk[at] |= 1 << (b % 8)
    else:
        disk[at] &= ~(1 << (b % 8)) & 0xFF


def mkfs():
    disk = bytearray(BLOCK * NBLOCKS)
    for b in range(DATA_START):
        set_bit(disk, b, 1)
    return disk


def alloc_block(disk):
    for b in range(DATA_START, NBLOCKS):
        if not get_bit(disk, b):
            set_bit(disk, b, 1)
            disk[b * BLOCK:(b + 1) * BLOCK] = bytes(BLOCK)
            return b
    return 0


def read_ptr(disk, b, i):
    return struct.unpack_from("<I", disk, b * BLOCK + 4 * i)[0]


def write_ptr(disk, b, i, value):
    struct.pack_into("<I", disk, b * BLOCK + 4 * i, value)


def read_inode(disk, n):
    kind, nlink, size, *rest = struct.unpack_from(INODE_FORMAT, disk, INODE_START * BLOCK + n * INODE_SIZE)
    return Inode(kind, nlink, size, rest[:NDIRECT], rest[NDIRECT])


def write_inode(disk, n, ino):
    struct.pack_into(INODE_FORMAT, disk, INODE_START * BLOCK + n * INODE_SIZE,
                     ino.kind, ino.nlink, ino.size, *ino.direct, ino.indirect)


def new_inode(disk, kind):
    for n in range(1, NINODES):
        if read_inode(disk, n).kind == FREE:
            write_inode(disk, n, Inode(kind, 1))
            return n
    return 0


def bmap(disk, ino, k, alloc):
    if k < NDIRECT:
        if ino.direct[k] == 0 and alloc:
            ino.direct[k] = alloc_block(disk)
        return ino.direct[k]
    slot = k - NDIRECT
    if slot >= PTRS:
        return 0
    if ino.indirect == 0:
        if not alloc:
            return 0
        ino.indirect = alloc_block(disk)
        if ino.indirect == 0:
            return 0
    b = read_ptr(disk, ino.indirect, slot)
    if b == 0 and alloc:
        b = alloc_block(disk)
        write_ptr(disk, ino.indirect, slot, b)
    return b


def read_at(disk, inum, offset, count):
    ino = read_inode(disk, inum)
    end = min(offset + count, ino.size)
    out = bytearray()
    pos = offset
    while pos < end:
        k, within = divmod(pos, BLOCK)
        n = min(BLOCK - within, end - pos)
        b = bmap(disk, ino, k, False)
        if b:
            out += disk[b * BLOCK + within:b * BLOCK + within + n]
        else:
            out += bytes(n)
        pos += n
    return bytes(out)


def write_at(disk, inum, offset, data):
    ino = read_inode(disk, inum)
    done = 0
    while done < len(data):
        k, within = divmod(offset + done, BLOCK)
        b = bmap(disk, ino, k, True)
        if b == 0:
            break
        n = min(BLOCK - within, len(data) - done)
        disk[b * BLOCK + within:b * BLOCK + within + n] = data[done:done + n]
        done += n
    if done:
        ino.size = max(ino.size, offset + done)
    write_inode(disk, inum, ino)
    return done
--- hint
`bmap` has two cases. Below `NDIRECT`, the pointer is `ino.direct[k]`. From `NDIRECT` on, the slot in the indirect block is `k - NDIRECT`, and you may need to allocate the indirect block before you can read or write that slot.
--- hint
Both `read_at` and `write_at` walk the range piece by piece: `k, within = divmod(pos, BLOCK)` gives the file block and the position in it, and the piece is `min(BLOCK - within, bytes left)` long. A piece can start in the middle of a block and end in the middle of the next.
--- hint
In `read_at`, first cut the end down to the size: `end = min(offset + count, size)`. A zero from `bmap(..., False)` inside the file is a hole: add `bytes(n)`. In `write_at`, stop the loop when `bmap(..., True)` returns 0, then set the size and call `write_inode` once at the end.
--- check test | A short write lands in the first data block and reads back

+++ question | Where file block 5 lives
--- ask
An inode has 5 direct pointers and one single indirect pointer. Which pointer leads to file block 5 (the sixth block)?
--- choice
Direct pointer 5.
--- choice correct
Slot 0 of the block the indirect pointer names.
--- choice
Slot 5 of the block the indirect pointer names.
--- choice
Slot 4 of the block the indirect pointer names.
--- why
Direct pointers cover file blocks 0 to 4. The indirect block covers the blocks after them, starting from its own slot 0, so file block k is slot k − 5. There is no direct pointer 5: the list has 5 entries, numbered 0 to 4.

+++ question | When the blocks are freed
--- ask
`report.txt` has link count 2 (a second hard link is `backup.txt`). A program has `report.txt` open. You `unlink("report.txt")`, then `unlink("backup.txt")`. When are the file's data blocks freed?
--- choice
At the first unlink, because the original name is gone.
--- choice
At the second unlink, because the link count reaches 0.
--- choice correct
When the program closes the file, after the second unlink.
--- choice
Never: the blocks leak until the disk is checked.
--- why
An inode and its blocks are freed only when two things are both true: no directory entry names it (link count 0) and no process has it open. After the second unlink the count is 0, but the open file still reaches the inode, so the data must stay readable. The close frees it.

+++ question | Reads for a big file
--- ask
ext2 has 12 direct pointers, one single, one double and one triple indirect pointer, with 1,024 pointers per block. The inode is already in memory, and nothing else is cached. How many disk reads does it take to read file block 2,000? Type a whole number.
--- answer
3
--- why
Blocks 0 to 11 are direct and 12 to 1,035 are single indirect. Block 2,000 is past 1,035 and well below 1,036 + 1,024², so it is in the double indirect range. That takes one read for the double indirect block, one for the block of pointers it leads to, and one for the data: 3.

+++ practice | Where block k lives
--- task
An inode has `ndirect` direct pointers, then one single, one double and one triple indirect pointer. Every indirect block holds `per_block` pointers.

Write `locate(k, ndirect, per_block)` that says where file block `k` is found, as a tuple:

- `("direct", i)` if it is direct pointer `i`;
- `("single", i)` if it is slot `i` of the single indirect block;
- `("double", i, j)` if it is slot `j` of the block found in slot `i` of the double indirect block;
- `("triple", i, j, m)` in the same way, three levels down;
- `None` if `k` is negative or past the largest file.

Then write `reads(k, ndirect, per_block)`: how many disk reads it takes to get the data block of `k` when the inode is already in memory (one per indirect block on the way, plus the data block itself), or `None` when `locate` gives `None`.

For example, with 12 direct pointers and 1,024 per block, `locate(12, 12, 1024)` is `("single", 0)` and `reads(12, 12, 1024)` is 2.
--- starter
def locate(k, ndirect, per_block):
    return ("direct", k)


def reads(k, ndirect, per_block):
    return 1
--- solution
def locate(k, ndirect, per_block):
    if k < 0:
        return None
    if k < ndirect:
        return ("direct", k)
    k -= ndirect
    if k < per_block:
        return ("single", k)
    k -= per_block
    if k < per_block ** 2:
        return ("double", k // per_block, k % per_block)
    k -= per_block ** 2
    if k < per_block ** 3:
        return ("triple", k // per_block ** 2, k // per_block % per_block, k % per_block)
    return None


def reads(k, ndirect, per_block):
    where = locate(k, ndirect, per_block)
    if where is None:
        return None
    return {"direct": 1, "single": 2, "double": 3, "triple": 4}[where[0]]
--- hint
Peel the levels off one at a time. If `k` is not direct, subtract `ndirect`; if what is left is not in the single range, subtract `per_block`; then `per_block ** 2`, and so on. What is left is the position inside that level.
--- hint
Inside the double range, position `p` is slot `p // per_block` of the top block and slot `p % per_block` below it. The triple range has three digits in base `per_block`. The number of reads depends only on the level: 1 for direct, 2 for single, and one more for each level below that.
--- check case | The example from the task
(locate(12, 12, 1024), reads(12, 12, 1024))
=> (("single", 0), 2)
--- check case | The last direct and the first single block
[locate(k, 5, 16) for k in (0, 4, 5, 20)]
=> [("direct", 0), ("direct", 4), ("single", 0), ("single", 15)]
--- check case | The double range starts at ndirect + per_block
[locate(k, 5, 16) for k in (21, 36, 37, 276)]
=> [("double", 0, 0), ("double", 0, 15), ("double", 1, 0), ("double", 15, 15)]
--- check case | The triple range and past the end
[locate(k, 5, 16) for k in (277, 277 + 16 * 16 * 16 - 1, 277 + 16 ** 3)]
=> [("triple", 0, 0, 0), ("triple", 15, 15, 15), None]
--- check case | A block in the middle of the triple range
locate(2 + 4 + 16 + 1 * 16 + 3 * 4 + 1, 2, 4)
=> ("triple", 1, 3, 1)
--- check case | Reads grow by one per level, and a negative block is None
[reads(k, 12, 1024) for k in (0, 11, 1035, 1036, 2000, 1036 + 1024 ** 2, -1)]
=> [1, 1, 2, 3, 3, 4, None]
--- check case | No direct pointers at all
(locate(0, 0, 8), locate(8, 0, 8), reads(8 + 64, 0, 8))
=> (("single", 0), ("double", 0, 0), 4)

+++ practice | Resolving a path
--- task
Model path resolution. `dirs` is a dict from the inode number of each **directory** to its entries: a dict from name to inode number. Every directory has `"."` (itself) and `".."` (its parent) among its entries; the root is inode 1 and its `".."` is 1. An inode number that is not a key of `dirs` is a regular file.

Write `resolve(dirs, path, cwd)` that returns the inode number `path` names, or an error string:

- A path starting with `/` starts at the root; any other path starts at `cwd`.
- Split it at `/`. Empty parts (from `//` or a slash at either end) are skipped.
- Each part is looked up in the current directory. If the current inode is a regular file, the answer is `"ENOTDIR"`. If the name is missing, it is `"ENOENT"`.
- A path that ends with `/` must name a directory, or the answer is `"ENOTDIR"`.
- The empty path `""` is `"ENOENT"`.

For example, with the tree in the checks, `resolve(dirs, "/data/today/log", 1)` is 7.
--- starter
def resolve(dirs, path, cwd):
    current = 1
    for part in path.split("/"):
        current = dirs[current][part]
    return current
--- solution
def resolve(dirs, path, cwd):
    if path == "":
        return "ENOENT"
    current = 1 if path.startswith("/") else cwd
    for part in path.split("/"):
        if part == "":
            continue
        if current not in dirs:
            return "ENOTDIR"
        if part not in dirs[current]:
            return "ENOENT"
        current = dirs[current][part]
    if path.endswith("/") and current not in dirs:
        return "ENOTDIR"
    return current
--- hint
Pick the starting inode first: 1 for an absolute path, `cwd` otherwise. Then walk the parts, skipping the empty ones.
--- hint
Before each lookup, check that the current inode is a directory (`current in dirs`), then that the name is in it. `"."` and `".."` need no special code: they are ordinary entries. Check the trailing slash after the loop.
--- check case | The example: an absolute path to a file
resolve({1: {".": 1, "..": 1, "data": 2, "bin": 3}, 2: {".": 2, "..": 1, "today": 4, "old": 5}, 3: {".": 3, "..": 1, "ls": 6}, 4: {".": 4, "..": 2, "log": 7}, 5: {".": 5, "..": 2}}, "/data/today/log", 1)
=> 7
--- check case | Relative paths start at cwd, and .. climbs
[resolve({1: {".": 1, "..": 1, "data": 2, "bin": 3}, 2: {".": 2, "..": 1, "today": 4, "old": 5}, 3: {".": 3, "..": 1, "ls": 6}, 4: {".": 4, "..": 2, "log": 7}, 5: {".": 5, "..": 2}}, p, 4) for p in ("log", "../old", "../../bin/ls", ".", "./log")]
=> [7, 5, 6, 4, 7]
--- check case | The root's parent is the root; slashes pile up harmlessly
[resolve({1: {".": 1, "..": 1, "data": 2}, 2: {".": 2, "..": 1, "f": 3}}, p, 2) for p in ("/..", "/../../data", "//data///f", "/", "..")]
=> [1, 2, 3, 1, 1]
--- check case | A missing name, and a file used as a directory
[resolve({1: {".": 1, "..": 1, "data": 2}, 2: {".": 2, "..": 1, "f": 3}}, p, 1) for p in ("/nope", "/data/nope", "/data/f/x", "data/f/..")]
=> ["ENOENT", "ENOENT", "ENOTDIR", "ENOTDIR"]
--- check case | A trailing slash needs a directory
[resolve({1: {".": 1, "..": 1, "data": 2}, 2: {".": 2, "..": 1, "f": 3}}, p, 1) for p in ("/data/", "/data/f/", "data/f")]
=> [2, "ENOTDIR", 3]
--- check case | The empty path
resolve({1: {".": 1, "..": 1}}, "", 1)
=> "ENOENT"

+++ practice | Three tables: descriptors, open files, inodes
--- task
A Unix kernel keeps three levels of tables. Each process has a **descriptor table** from fd numbers to entries in the kernel's **open-file table**. An open-file entry holds an inode number, a current **offset** and a reference count. Two descriptors can share one entry: `dup` does it inside a process, and `fork` does it between parent and child, which is why they share one offset.

Write a class `Kernel`. `Kernel(files)` gets a dict from inode number to the file's contents (`bytes`). Its methods:

- `open(pid, inum)`: makes a **new** open-file entry at offset 0 and gives it the lowest free fd of process `pid` (a process with no table yet starts with an empty one). Returns the fd.
- `read(pid, fd, n)`: returns up to `n` bytes of the file from the entry's offset and moves the offset on by the number returned. An fd that is not open gives `None`.
- `lseek(pid, fd, offset)`: sets the entry's offset and returns it (`None` for a bad fd).
- `dup(pid, fd)`: the lowest free fd now points to the **same** entry. Returns the new fd (`None` for a bad fd).
- `fork(parent, child)`: the child gets a copy of the parent's descriptor table, pointing to the same entries.
- `close(pid, fd)`: removes the fd and returns 0 (`None` for a bad fd). An entry that no descriptor points to any more is freed.
- `open_entries()`: how many open-file entries exist.
--- starter
class Kernel:
    def __init__(self, files):
        self.files = files
        self.fds = {}

    def open(self, pid, inum):
        return 0

    def read(self, pid, fd, n):
        return b""

    def lseek(self, pid, fd, offset):
        return None

    def dup(self, pid, fd):
        return None

    def fork(self, parent, child):
        pass

    def close(self, pid, fd):
        return None

    def open_entries(self):
        return 0
--- solution
class OpenFile:
    def __init__(self, inum):
        self.inum = inum
        self.offset = 0
        self.refs = 1


class Kernel:
    def __init__(self, files):
        self.files = files
        self.fds = {}
        self.entries = 0

    def table(self, pid):
        return self.fds.setdefault(pid, {})

    def lowest_free(self, pid):
        fd = 0
        while fd in self.table(pid):
            fd += 1
        return fd

    def open(self, pid, inum):
        fd = self.lowest_free(pid)
        self.table(pid)[fd] = OpenFile(inum)
        self.entries += 1
        return fd

    def read(self, pid, fd, n):
        entry = self.table(pid).get(fd)
        if entry is None:
            return None
        data = self.files[entry.inum][entry.offset:entry.offset + n]
        entry.offset += len(data)
        return data

    def lseek(self, pid, fd, offset):
        entry = self.table(pid).get(fd)
        if entry is None:
            return None
        entry.offset = offset
        return offset

    def dup(self, pid, fd):
        entry = self.table(pid).get(fd)
        if entry is None:
            return None
        new = self.lowest_free(pid)
        self.table(pid)[new] = entry
        entry.refs += 1
        return new

    def fork(self, parent, child):
        copy = dict(self.table(parent))
        for entry in copy.values():
            entry.refs += 1
        self.fds[child] = copy

    def close(self, pid, fd):
        entry = self.table(pid).pop(fd, None)
        if entry is None:
            return None
        entry.refs -= 1
        if entry.refs == 0:
            self.entries -= 1
        return 0

    def open_entries(self):
        return self.entries
--- hint
Make a small class for an open-file entry with `inum`, `offset` and a reference count. A descriptor table is a dict from fd to one of these objects, so two fds can hold the very same object.
--- hint
`dup` and `fork` copy references to entries, never the entries themselves, and add one to each entry's count. `close` takes one away; when it reaches 0 the entry is gone.
--- check test | Reading moves the offset on
(lambda k: (k.open(1, 5), k.read(1, 0, 4), k.read(1, 0, 4), k.read(1, 0, 99), k.read(1, 0, 3)) == (0, b"GYRO", b" OK ", b"T+12", b""))(Kernel({5: b"GYRO OK T+12"}))
--- check test | Two opens of one file have separate offsets
(lambda k: (k.open(1, 5), k.open(1, 5), k.read(1, 0, 4), k.read(1, 1, 2), k.open_entries()) == (0, 1, b"GYRO", b"GY", 2))(Kernel({5: b"GYRO OK"}))
--- check test | dup shares the offset
(lambda k: (k.open(1, 5), k.dup(1, 0), k.read(1, 0, 5), k.read(1, 1, 2), k.open_entries()) == (0, 1, b"GYRO ", b"OK", 1))(Kernel({5: b"GYRO OK"}))
--- check test | After fork, parent and child share the offset
(lambda k: (k.open(10, 5), k.fork(10, 11), k.read(11, 0, 3), k.read(10, 0, 3), k.read(11, 0, 3)) == (0, None, b"aaa", b"bbb", b"ccc"))(Kernel({5: b"aaabbbccc"}))
--- check test | lseek on one descriptor moves the shared offset
(lambda k: (k.open(1, 5), k.dup(1, 0), k.lseek(1, 1, 6), k.read(1, 0, 10), k.lseek(1, 9, 0)) == (0, 1, 6, b"cd", None))(Kernel({5: b"abcdefcd"}))
--- check test | The entry lives until its last descriptor closes
(lambda k: (k.open(1, 5), k.fork(1, 2), k.close(1, 0), k.open_entries(), k.read(2, 0, 2), k.close(2, 0), k.open_entries(), k.close(2, 0)) == (0, None, 0, 1, b"ab", 0, 0, None))(Kernel({5: b"abc"}))
--- check test | Closed fds are reused lowest first, and a bad fd gives None
(lambda k: (k.open(1, 5), k.open(1, 6), k.open(1, 5), k.close(1, 1), k.open(1, 6), k.read(1, 7, 1), k.dup(1, 9), k.open_entries()) == (0, 1, 2, 0, 1, None, None, 3))(Kernel({5: b"x", 6: b"y"}))

+++ practice | Links, unlinks and open files
--- task
Model hard links in one directory. Write a class `Dir` for a root directory (inode 1) that holds names directly (no slashes). Inode numbers for new files and directories are the lowest free number from 2 up; a freed number can be used again. Each method returns 0 on success or an error string:

- `create(name)`: a new regular file with link count 1. `"EEXIST"` if the name is taken.
- `mkdir(name)`: a new directory with link count 2 (its entry, and its own `.`). The root's link count, which starts at 2, goes up by 1 (the new directory's `..`). `"EEXIST"` if the name is taken.
- `link(old, new)`: a second name for the file `old`, adding 1 to its link count. `"ENOENT"` if `old` is missing, `"EEXIST"` if `new` is taken, `"EPERM"` if `old` is a directory.
- `unlink(name)`: removes a file's name and takes 1 from its link count. `"ENOENT"` if missing, `"EISDIR"` if it is a directory.
- `rmdir(name)`: removes a directory (they are always empty here), frees its inode, and takes 1 from the root's link count. `"ENOENT"` if missing, `"ENOTDIR"` if it is a file.
- `open(name)`: returns the file's inode number (not 0) and counts one more opener; `"ENOENT"` if missing. `close(inum)` counts one fewer.

A file's inode is freed as soon as its link count is 0 **and** it has no openers, whichever happens last. `freed` is the list of inode numbers freed so far, in order, and `stat(name)` returns `(inum, link count)` or `"ENOENT"`. `stat(".")` gives the root.
--- starter
class Dir:
    def __init__(self):
        self.names = {}
        self.freed = []

    def create(self, name):
        self.names[name] = len(self.names) + 2
        return 0

    def mkdir(self, name):
        return self.create(name)

    def link(self, old, new):
        self.names[new] = self.names[old]
        return 0

    def unlink(self, name):
        del self.names[name]
        return 0

    def rmdir(self, name):
        return self.unlink(name)

    def open(self, name):
        return self.names[name]

    def close(self, inum):
        pass

    def stat(self, name):
        return (self.names[name], 1)
--- solution
class Dir:
    def __init__(self):
        self.names = {".": 1}
        self.nlink = {1: 2}
        self.is_dir = {1: True}
        self.opens = {}
        self.freed = []

    def new_inum(self):
        n = 2
        while n in self.nlink:
            n += 1
        return n

    def free(self, inum):
        del self.nlink[inum]
        del self.is_dir[inum]
        self.opens.pop(inum, None)
        self.freed.append(inum)

    def maybe_free(self, inum):
        if self.nlink[inum] == 0 and self.opens.get(inum, 0) == 0:
            self.free(inum)

    def create(self, name):
        if name in self.names:
            return "EEXIST"
        n = self.new_inum()
        self.names[name] = n
        self.nlink[n] = 1
        self.is_dir[n] = False
        return 0

    def mkdir(self, name):
        if name in self.names:
            return "EEXIST"
        n = self.new_inum()
        self.names[name] = n
        self.nlink[n] = 2
        self.is_dir[n] = True
        self.nlink[1] += 1
        return 0

    def link(self, old, new):
        if old not in self.names:
            return "ENOENT"
        if new in self.names:
            return "EEXIST"
        n = self.names[old]
        if self.is_dir[n]:
            return "EPERM"
        self.names[new] = n
        self.nlink[n] += 1
        return 0

    def unlink(self, name):
        if name not in self.names:
            return "ENOENT"
        n = self.names[name]
        if self.is_dir[n]:
            return "EISDIR"
        del self.names[name]
        self.nlink[n] -= 1
        self.maybe_free(n)
        return 0

    def rmdir(self, name):
        if name not in self.names:
            return "ENOENT"
        n = self.names[name]
        if not self.is_dir[n]:
            return "ENOTDIR"
        del self.names[name]
        self.free(n)
        self.nlink[1] -= 1
        return 0

    def open(self, name):
        if name not in self.names:
            return "ENOENT"
        n = self.names[name]
        self.opens[n] = self.opens.get(n, 0) + 1
        return n

    def close(self, inum):
        self.opens[inum] -= 1
        self.maybe_free(inum)

    def stat(self, name):
        if name not in self.names:
            return "ENOENT"
        n = self.names[name]
        return (n, self.nlink[n])
--- hint
Keep the names separate from the inodes: one dict from name to inode number, and per inode its link count, whether it is a directory, and how many openers it has. That is exactly the split a real file system makes.
--- hint
Write one helper that frees an inode when its link count is 0 and its opener count is 0, and call it from both `unlink` and `close`. Freeing must make the number available again for the next `create`.
--- check test | create, link and unlink move the link count
(lambda d: (d.create("a"), d.link("a", "b"), d.stat("a"), d.unlink("a"), d.stat("b"), d.stat("a"), d.freed) == (0, 0, (2, 2), 0, (2, 1), "ENOENT", []))(Dir())
--- check test | The last unlink frees the inode, and its number is reused
(lambda d: (d.create("a"), d.create("b"), d.unlink("a"), d.freed, d.create("c"), d.stat("c")) == (0, 0, 0, [2], 0, (2, 1)))(Dir())
--- check test | An open file outlives its last name
(lambda d: (d.create("log"), d.open("log"), d.unlink("log"), list(d.freed), d.create("new"), d.stat("new"), d.close(2), d.freed) == (0, 2, 0, [], 0, (3, 1), None, [2]))(Dir())
?? After unlink the count is 0 but the file is open, so inode 2 is still taken: the next file gets 3. Closing frees 2.
--- check test | Two openers: freed at the second close
(lambda d: (d.create("f"), d.open("f"), d.open("f"), d.unlink("f"), d.close(2), list(d.freed), d.close(2), d.freed) == (0, 2, 2, 0, None, [], None, [2]))(Dir())
--- check test | Closing while names remain frees nothing
(lambda d: (d.create("f"), d.open("f"), d.close(2), d.freed, d.stat("f")) == (0, 2, None, [], (2, 1)))(Dir())
--- check test | Directories: link counts, no hard links, rmdir
(lambda d: (d.mkdir("etc"), d.mkdir("var"), d.stat("."), d.stat("etc"), d.link("etc", "e2"), d.unlink("etc"), d.rmdir("etc"), d.stat("."), d.freed) == (0, 0, (1, 4), (2, 2), "EPERM", "EISDIR", 0, (1, 3), [2]))(Dir())
--- check test | Error cases
(lambda d: (d.create("a"), d.create("a"), d.mkdir("a"), d.link("zz", "b"), d.link("a", "a"), d.unlink("zz"), d.rmdir("a"), d.rmdir("zz"), d.open("zz"), d.stat("a")) == (0, "EEXIST", "EEXIST", "ENOENT", "EEXIST", "ENOENT", "ENOTDIR", "ENOENT", "ENOENT", (2, 1)))(Dir())

+++ practice | Debug: the sixth block comes from the wrong slot
--- task
`file_blocks(direct, indirect, size)` should list, in order, the disk block of every file block of a file of `size` bytes, with 64-byte blocks. `direct` is the inode's list of 4 direct pointers; `indirect` is the list of pointers stored in its indirect block, or `None` if the file has no indirect block. A pointer of 0 is a hole and is listed as 0; a block past the end of `indirect`, or any block beyond the direct ones when `indirect` is `None`, is listed as 0 too.

It has a bug. For a file of 6 blocks, the fifth block (file block 4) is listed as the disk block in slot 1 of the indirect block, and the sixth as slot 2, so every block behind the direct pointers is shifted by one and the very last one may be read from past the end. Find the bug and fix it.
--- starter
NDIRECT = 4
BLOCK = 64


def block_of(direct, indirect, k):
    if k < NDIRECT:
        return direct[k]
    if indirect is None:
        return 0
    slot = k - NDIRECT + 1
    if slot >= len(indirect):
        return 0
    return indirect[slot]


def file_blocks(direct, indirect, size):
    count = (size + BLOCK - 1) // BLOCK
    return [block_of(direct, indirect, k) for k in range(count)]
--- solution
NDIRECT = 4
BLOCK = 64


def block_of(direct, indirect, k):
    if k < NDIRECT:
        return direct[k]
    if indirect is None:
        return 0
    slot = k - NDIRECT
    if slot >= len(indirect):
        return 0
    return indirect[slot]


def file_blocks(direct, indirect, size):
    count = (size + BLOCK - 1) // BLOCK
    return [block_of(direct, indirect, k) for k in range(count)]
--- hint
Which slot of the indirect block holds file block 4, the first block that is not direct?
--- hint
File block `NDIRECT` is slot 0. The slot is `k - NDIRECT`, with nothing added.
--- check case | Six blocks: the two behind the direct pointers come from slots 0 and 1
file_blocks([10, 11, 12, 13], [20, 21, 22], 6 * 64)
=> [10, 11, 12, 13, 20, 21]
--- check case | A size that is not a whole number of blocks rounds up
file_blocks([10, 11, 12, 13], [20, 21, 22], 4 * 64 + 1)
=> [10, 11, 12, 13, 20]
--- check case | The last slot of the indirect block
file_blocks([1, 2, 3, 4], [5, 6], 6 * 64)
=> [1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6]
--- check case | Holes, and no indirect block
(file_blocks([10, 0, 12, 0], [0, 30], 6 * 64), file_blocks([7, 8, 0, 0], None, 5 * 64))
=> ([10, 0, 12, 0, 0, 30], [7, 8, 0, 0, 0])
--- check case | An empty file has no blocks
file_blocks([0, 0, 0, 0], None, 0)
=> []

+++ practice | Symbolic links and loops
--- task
Extend path resolution with symbolic links. `fs` is a dict with two keys: `"dirs"` maps each directory's inode number to its entries (name to inode number, with `"."` and `".."`, root 1, as usual), and `"links"` maps the inode number of each **symbolic link** to the path it holds (a string). Any other inode number is a regular file.

Write `resolve(fs, path, cwd)` that returns the inode number `path` names, following symbolic links, or an error string:

- Absolute paths start at 1, relative ones at `cwd`; empty parts are skipped; `"."` and `".."` are ordinary entries.
- When a part names a symbolic link, resolve the link's path in its place: an absolute link path starts at the root, a relative one starts at the directory that **contains** the link. Then carry on with the remaining parts from wherever that leads. Follow links in the last part too.
- Count every link followed during the whole call. Following more than 8 gives `"ELOOP"`.
- A part looked up in a regular file gives `"ENOTDIR"`; a missing name gives `"ENOENT"` (so a dangling link gives `"ENOENT"`). The empty path is `"ENOENT"`.
--- starter
def resolve(fs, path, cwd):
    dirs = fs["dirs"]
    current = 1 if path.startswith("/") else cwd
    for part in path.split("/"):
        if part:
            current = dirs[current][part]
    return current
--- solution
MAX_LINKS = 8


def resolve(fs, path, cwd):
    if path == "":
        return "ENOENT"
    followed = [0]

    def walk(path, start):
        dirs, links = fs["dirs"], fs["links"]
        current = 1 if path.startswith("/") else start
        for part in path.split("/"):
            if part == "":
                continue
            if current not in dirs:
                return "ENOTDIR"
            if part not in dirs[current]:
                return "ENOENT"
            parent = current
            current = dirs[current][part]
            if current in links:
                followed[0] += 1
                if followed[0] > MAX_LINKS:
                    return "ELOOP"
                current = walk(links[current], parent)
                if isinstance(current, str):
                    return current
        return current

    return walk(path, cwd)
--- hint
Write the walk as a function of a path and a starting directory, so a link can call it again on its own path, starting from the directory that held it. Keep that directory before you step into the part.
--- hint
Keep one counter for the whole call (a one-item list or an attribute works from inside a nested function). Add 1 every time you follow a link, and give `"ELOOP"` as soon as it passes 8. When a nested walk returns an error string, pass it straight up.
--- check case | A relative link resolves from the directory that holds it
resolve({"dirs": {1: {".": 1, "..": 1, "logs": 2, "latest": 5}, 2: {".": 2, "..": 1, "run1": 3, "cur": 4}}, "links": {4: "run1", 5: "logs/cur"}}, "/logs/cur", 1)
=> 3
--- check case | A link to a link, in the middle and at the end
[resolve({"dirs": {1: {".": 1, "..": 1, "logs": 2, "latest": 5}, 2: {".": 2, "..": 1, "run1": 3, "cur": 4}}, "links": {4: "run1", 5: "logs/cur"}}, p, 2) for p in ("/latest", "cur", "../latest")]
=> [3, 3, 3]
--- check case | A link to a directory, then more parts after it
resolve({"dirs": {1: {".": 1, "..": 1, "data": 2, "d": 6}, 2: {".": 2, "..": 1, "today": 3}, 3: {".": 3, "..": 2, "log": 4}}, "links": {6: "/data/today"}}, "/d/log", 1)
=> 4
--- check case | A link whose path uses ..
resolve({"dirs": {1: {".": 1, "..": 1, "a": 2, "f": 5}, 2: {".": 2, "..": 1, "up": 3}}, "links": {3: "../f"}}, "a/up", 1)
=> 5
--- check case | A dangling link, and a link used as a directory when it leads to a file
[resolve({"dirs": {1: {".": 1, "..": 1, "gone": 2, "f": 3, "tof": 4}}, "links": {2: "/missing", 4: "f"}}, p, 1) for p in ("/gone", "/tof", "/tof/x")]
=> ["ENOENT", 3, "ENOTDIR"]
--- check case | Two links pointing at each other loop
resolve({"dirs": {1: {".": 1, "..": 1, "a": 2, "b": 3}}, "links": {2: "b", 3: "a"}}, "/a", 1)
=> "ELOOP"
--- check case | A chain of 8 links is fine; 9 is too many
(resolve({"dirs": {1: dict([(".", 1), ("..", 1), ("f", 2)] + [(f"l{i}", 10 + i) for i in range(9)])}, "links": {10 + i: ("f" if i == 0 else f"l{i - 1}") for i in range(9)}}, "l7", 1), resolve({"dirs": {1: dict([(".", 1), ("..", 1), ("f", 2)] + [(f"l{i}", 10 + i) for i in range(9)])}, "links": {10 + i: ("f" if i == 0 else f"l{i - 1}") for i in range(9)}}, "l8", 1))
=> (2, "ELOOP")
--- check case | The empty path
resolve({"dirs": {1: {".": 1, "..": 1}}, "links": {}}, "", 1)
=> "ENOENT"

=== os-14 | Journaling and crash consistency
--- teach
Last lesson, writing into a hole changed three things on disk: a bit in the bitmap, a pointer in the inode (and the size), and the data block itself. Each of those is a separate block write. The disk does them one at a time, and the power can fail between any two. This lesson is about what a crash leaves behind, and about the trick nearly every modern file system and database uses to make a group of writes happen all together or not at all: the **journal**.

### The crash consistency problem

A disk promises only this: a single sector write either happens completely or not at all. Anything bigger is a sequence of writes, and a crash can stop it in the middle.

**Crash consistency** is the property that, after a crash at any moment, the file system can get back to a state that obeys its rules (lesson 13: every block used once or free, true link counts, sizes that fit), and that each operation either happened or did not.

In everyday words: you move house and must tell the bank and the post office. If you are interrupted after telling only one of them, your records disagree. Nothing is wrong with either record on its own; the problem is that they no longer match.

### What one append can leave behind

Take the append from lesson 13. It writes the bitmap (block B is now used), the inode (it points to B, and the size grew), and B itself (the new bytes). If the crash lets only some of them reach the disk:

| reached the disk | what you find afterwards |
|---|---|
| only B | Nothing points to B. The append is lost, but everything is consistent. |
| only the bitmap | B is marked used but nobody points to it: a **leak**. |
| only the inode | The inode points to B, which the bitmap calls free. The file reads garbage, and B may later be given to another file too. |
| bitmap and inode | The metadata agree, but B holds garbage: the file shows old bytes from some deleted file. |
| inode and B | The data is right, but the bitmap calls B free: another file can be given B. |
| bitmap and B | A leak again. |

Only "all three" and "none" are right. And the order you issue the writes does not decide which ones land.

### Disks reorder writes

A disk has a [[write cache|wcache]] of its own. It says "done" as soon as the data is in its cache, and writes the cache to the platter or the flash later, in whatever order is fastest. So the operating system cannot rely on the order it sent the writes in.

What it can rely on is a **barrier** (also called a flush): a command that waits until everything written so far is really durable. Writes sent before a barrier are on the disk before any write sent after it. Between two barriers, any subset of the writes may survive a crash, in any combination.

Programs reach the barrier through `fsync`:

```python fragment
import os

fd = os.open("config.json", os.O_WRONLY | os.O_CREAT | os.O_TRUNC, 0o644)
os.write(fd, b'{"mode": "safe"}')
os.fsync(fd)       # returns only when these bytes (and the file's inode) are durable
os.close(fd)
```

### Fix one: check everything after a crash

The old answer was to let crashes happen and repair the damage afterwards. At boot, a program called [[fsck|fsck]] (file system check) scans the whole disk and enforces the rules of lesson 13:

- Rebuild the bitmap from the inodes: a block is used exactly when some inode points to it. That fixes leaks and blocks wrongly marked free.
- Count the directory entries for every inode and correct the link counts. An inode with links but no entry is put in `lost+found`.
- Find blocks that two inodes both claim, and copy or remove one.

It works, but it has two flaws. It is **slow**: it reads every inode and every directory, O(size of the disk), which took hours on large disks. And it **cannot see everything**: in the "bitmap and inode" case above, the metadata agree perfectly, so fsck finds nothing wrong, yet the file holds garbage.

### Fix two: write it down first

**Write-ahead logging**, called **journaling** in file systems, is the idea behind almost every modern file system and database: before changing anything in place, write a description of the whole change to a separate area, the **journal** (or log). Only when that description is complete and durable, change the real blocks.

In everyday words: a surgeon's checklist. Write down every step of the operation first. If you are interrupted halfway through the operation, the list says exactly what still has to be done.

The protocol, for one **transaction** (a group of block writes that must happen together):

1. **Journal write.** Write a begin record (with the transaction's id), then a copy of every new block, to the journal.
2. **Barrier.** Wait until all of those are durable.
3. **Commit.** Write one commit record, holding the same transaction id. It fits in one sector, so it is written completely or not at all.
4. **Barrier.** From this moment the transaction is **committed**: it will happen, crash or no crash.
5. **Checkpoint.** Write the new blocks to their real places on disk (their **home** locations). Barrier.
6. **Free.** Mark the journal space free again, so the next transaction can use it. Barrier.

**Recovery** after a crash reads the journal. A transaction with a matching commit record is **replayed**: its blocks are written to their homes again. A transaction without one is ignored. Then the journal is marked free.

### Why the journal is correct

The claim: **after a crash at any moment and a recovery, each transaction has either fully happened or not happened at all.** Split on when the crash came.

- **Before the commit record was durable** (steps 1 to 3). No home block has been touched yet: checkpointing starts only after the barrier in step 4. Recovery finds no valid commit, so it ignores the transaction, and the disk holds the complete old state.
- **After the commit record was durable** (steps 4 to 6). The barrier in step 2 guarantees every journal copy was durable before the commit record was even written. So the journal holds the complete new contents, and recovery writes them all to their homes. Whatever part of step 5 had already happened is simply written again.
- **During recovery itself.** Recovery only copies journal blocks to their homes, and frees the journal last. A crash in the middle leaves the journal intact, and the next recovery does the same copying again from the start.

The last two cases rest on one property: replay is [[idempotent|idem]]. The journal stores the new **contents** of each block, so writing them once or five times gives the same disk:

```python
disk = {"inode 12": "size 0", "bitmap 3": "free"}
journal = [("inode 12", "size 4096"), ("bitmap 3", "used")]
for replay in range(2):              # a crash during recovery: replay again
    for block, contents in journal:
        disk[block] = contents
print(disk)
```

It prints the same dictionary however many times you replay. Had the journal said "add 4,096 to the size" instead, replaying twice would add it twice. That is why file system journals log physical block contents, not operations.

Take away either barrier and the argument breaks. Without the first, the commit record can reach the disk before the copies it vouches for, and recovery replays garbage. Without the second, checkpoint writes can reach the disk before the commit record, and a crash leaves half the home blocks new and half old with nothing to redo.

### Stale records and checksums

The journal is reused, so it is full of records from earlier transactions. Suppose transaction 7 left its commit record in slot 5. Transaction 8, with the same number of blocks, is being written when the power fails, before its own commit record is written. Slot 5 still says "commit". If recovery only asks "is there a commit record where I expect one?", it replays half-written transaction 8. That is why every record carries the **transaction id**, and recovery demands that the commit's id match the begin's id.

ext4 goes one step further. Its commit record can hold a **checksum** of the transaction's blocks: a short number computed from their bytes. Recovery computes the checksum again, and replays only if the two match. Then the first barrier is no longer needed, because a commit record that arrived before its data is caught by the checksum:

```python
import zlib

blocks = [b"inode 12: size 4096", b"bitmap 3: used"]
stored = zlib.crc32(b"".join(blocks))
torn = [b"inode 12: size 4096", b"\x00" * 14]     # the second copy never arrived
print(zlib.crc32(b"".join(torn)) == stored)       # False: do not replay
```

`zlib.crc32` computes a 32-bit cyclic redundancy check: any change to a few bytes changes the result.

### What it costs

Journaling writes every block **twice**: once to the journal, once home. Three things make that affordable.

- **Metadata journaling.** ext4's default mode, called **ordered**, journals only metadata (inodes, bitmaps, directories). The data blocks go straight to their homes, **before** the metadata transaction commits. So an inode can never point at a block that has not been written yet: the "garbage" row of the table cannot happen. Only the small metadata blocks are written twice.
- **Sequential writes.** The journal is one contiguous region written front to back, which is the fastest thing a hard disk does (lesson 15).
- **[[Group commit|groupcommit]].** Many operations are batched into one transaction. ext4 commits every 5 seconds by default, so a thousand small file updates share one journal write and one pair of barriers.

Recovery costs O(size of the journal), a few megabytes, instead of O(size of the disk). That is why a journaled file system mounts in seconds after a crash.

### Other ways to stay consistent

- **Redo and undo logs.** A file system journal is a **redo log**: it holds new contents, and recovery redoes committed work. A database's **undo log** holds old contents, and recovery rolls unfinished transactions back. SQLite's default "rollback journal" is an undo log; its WAL mode is a redo log.
- **[[Copy-on-write|cow]].** ZFS, btrfs and Apple's APFS never overwrite a live block. They write new copies elsewhere, then switch one root pointer to the new tree in a single sector write. The old tree stays intact until that one write lands.

### What applications must do

The file system protects its own metadata, not your file's contents. To replace a file safely, the standard recipe is: write the new version to a temporary file, `fsync` it, `rename` it over the old name (rename is atomic: the name points to the old inode or the new one, never neither), then `fsync` the directory so the rename itself is durable. Skipping the first `fsync` is exactly what produced a famous wave of [[zero-length files|zero-length]] in 2009.

### Where it is used

- **ext4** (its journal layer is called JBD2), **XFS** and **NTFS** all journal metadata.
- **Databases** such as PostgreSQL and SQLite rely on write-ahead logs.
- **Flight software** stores configuration and event logs with the same pattern, so a reset during a write never leaves the spacecraft with a half-written parameter table.

**Watch out:**

- **Committing before the data is durable.** Without a barrier between the journal copies and the commit record, the commit can land first.
- **Matching only the record type.** A commit record must carry the transaction id (or a checksum), or an old transaction's commit makes a half-written new one look complete.
- **Freeing the journal too early.** The journal may be freed only after the checkpoint writes are durable.
- **Logging operations instead of contents.** "Add 64 to the size" is not idempotent; "the size is 128" is.

::: context wcache The disk's own memory
Hard disks and SSDs have tens to hundreds of megabytes of RAM used as a write cache. A write is acknowledged when it reaches that RAM, which makes writes look fast. If the power fails, anything still in the cache is lost, unless the drive has capacitors to finish writing (common in data-centre SSDs). The operating system asks the drive to empty its cache with a flush command, or marks a single write "force unit access" so it bypasses the cache. `fsync` on Linux ends with such a flush.
:::

::: context fsck Scanning the whole disk
`fsck` (file system check) is a program run at boot, before the file system is mounted, when the file system was not shut down cleanly. It reads every inode, follows every block pointer, walks every directory, and repairs what disagrees. On the large disks of the 2000s this took hours, during which the machine could not boot. With a journal, `fsck` is still kept around for real damage, such as a failing disk or a kernel bug, but it is no longer needed after an ordinary crash.
:::

::: context idem Doing it twice is the same as once
An operation is idempotent when doing it twice has the same effect as doing it once. "Set the light to off" is idempotent; "flip the light switch" is not. Recovery code may itself be interrupted by another crash and then run again, so every step it takes must be safe to repeat. The same idea appears in networking, where a lost reply makes a client send its request again, and in distributed systems, where a message may be delivered twice.
:::

::: context groupcommit Many updates, one journal write
Group commit means gathering many operations into one transaction before committing it. Each commit costs two barriers, and a barrier can take milliseconds on a hard disk. If a thousand small updates arrive within a few milliseconds, committing them together costs about the same as committing one. The price is latency: an update may wait up to the commit interval (5 seconds by default on ext4) before it is durable, unless the program calls `fsync`, which forces a commit at once.
:::

::: context cow Never overwrite, switch a pointer
Copy-on-write file systems treat the disk as a tree of blocks hanging from one root. To change a block, they write a new copy of it somewhere free, then new copies of every block on the path up to the root, then finally the root pointer. Until that last single write, the old tree is untouched, so a crash leaves the old version complete. The same trick gives cheap snapshots: keep an old root, and you keep the whole old file system.
:::

::: context zero-length Empty files after a crash
In 2009, users of the new ext4 file system found configuration files emptied after crashes. Programs rewrote a file by truncating it and writing new contents, without `fsync`. ext4 delays choosing blocks for new data (delayed allocation) for up to about 30 seconds, while it commits the metadata (the truncation) within 5. A crash in between left the file truncated with no new data. ext4 added a workaround that forces the data out early in exactly this pattern, but the lesson stands: only `fsync` promises durability.
:::
--- task
Write a journaled update and its crash recovery, for a model disk. The starter gives you the model; leave it as it is:

- `Disk(home, journal_size)` has two areas: `home`, the list of real blocks, and `journal`, a list of `journal_size` slots, all `None` at first. `disk.write(area, i, value)` sends a write (`area` is `"home"` or `"journal"`), `disk.read(area, i)` reads the latest value, and `disk.flush()` is a barrier: writes sent before it are durable after it. At a crash, writes sent since the last barrier may or may not survive, in any combination.
- `crash_test(make_disk, tx, updates)` makes the power fail at every possible point of your `commit`, with several survival patterns, then runs your `recover` (and makes it crash at every point too, then recover again). It returns a list of the cases where the home blocks ended up neither all old nor all new, or the journal was not left free. An empty list means your code is crash-safe.

Write two functions:

1. `commit(disk, tx, updates)`: `updates` is a dict from home block number to its new value, and `tx` is the transaction id. If `len(updates) + 2` is more than the journal's size, return `False` and write nothing. Otherwise use exactly this layout: slot 0 holds `("begin", tx, n)` with `n = len(updates)`, slots 1 to `n` hold `("data", block, value)` in the dict's order, and slot `n + 1` holds `("commit", tx)`. Follow the protocol from the lesson: journal records, barrier, commit record, barrier, home writes, barrier, then free the journal by writing `None` to slot 0, and a final barrier. Return `True`.
2. `recover(disk)`: if slot 0 is `None`, return `None`. If it is a begin record `("begin", tx, n)` and slot `n + 1` exists and is exactly `("commit", tx)`, replay: write each data record's value to its home block, then a barrier, and return `tx`. In every other case replay nothing and return `None`. Either way (unless slot 0 was already `None`), finish by writing `None` to slot 0, then a barrier.
--- starter
class Crash(Exception):
    pass


class Disk:
    def __init__(self, home, journal_size):
        self.home = list(home)
        self.journal = [None] * journal_size
        self.pending = []
        self.budget = None
        self.keep = "all"

    def tick(self):
        if self.budget is not None:
            if self.budget == 0:
                raise Crash()
            self.budget -= 1

    def write(self, area, i, value):
        self.tick()
        self.pending.append((area, i, value))

    def flush(self):
        self.tick()
        for area, i, value in self.pending:
            getattr(self, area)[i] = value
        self.pending = []

    def read(self, area, i):
        for a, j, value in reversed(self.pending):
            if (a, j) == (area, i):
                return value
        return getattr(self, area)[i]

    def crash(self):
        if self.keep == "all":
            survivors = self.pending
        elif self.keep == "last":
            survivors = self.pending[-1:]
        elif self.keep == "ends":
            survivors = self.pending[:1] + self.pending[1:][-1:]
        else:
            survivors = []
        for area, i, value in survivors:
            getattr(self, area)[i] = value
        self.pending = []
        self.budget = None


def copy_disk(disk):
    other = Disk(disk.home, len(disk.journal))
    other.journal = list(disk.journal)
    return other


def crash_test(make_disk, tx, updates):
    old = list(make_disk().home)
    new = list(old)
    for block, value in updates.items():
        new[block] = value
    bad = []
    budget = 0
    while True:
        finished = False
        for keep in ("none", "last", "ends", "all"):
            disk = make_disk()
            disk.budget, disk.keep = budget, keep
            try:
                commit(disk, tx, updates)
                finished = True
                if disk.home != new or disk.journal[0] is not None:
                    bad.append((budget, keep, "no crash", disk.home))
                continue
            except Crash:
                disk.crash()
            rbudget = 0
            while True:
                again = copy_disk(disk)
                again.budget, again.keep = rbudget, keep
                try:
                    recover(again)
                    done = True
                except Crash:
                    again.crash()
                    done = False
                except Exception as e:
                    bad.append((budget, keep, rbudget, type(e).__name__))
                    break
                if not done:
                    try:
                        recover(again)
                    except Exception as e:
                        bad.append((budget, keep, rbudget, type(e).__name__))
                        break
                if again.home not in (old, new) or again.journal[0] is not None:
                    bad.append((budget, keep, rbudget, again.home))
                if done:
                    break
                rbudget += 1
        if finished:
            return bad
        budget += 1


def commit(disk, tx, updates):
    raise NotImplementedError("write the transaction through the journal")


def recover(disk):
    raise NotImplementedError("replay committed transactions")
--- solution
class Crash(Exception):
    pass


class Disk:
    def __init__(self, home, journal_size):
        self.home = list(home)
        self.journal = [None] * journal_size
        self.pending = []
        self.budget = None
        self.keep = "all"

    def tick(self):
        if self.budget is not None:
            if self.budget == 0:
                raise Crash()
            self.budget -= 1

    def write(self, area, i, value):
        self.tick()
        self.pending.append((area, i, value))

    def flush(self):
        self.tick()
        for area, i, value in self.pending:
            getattr(self, area)[i] = value
        self.pending = []

    def read(self, area, i):
        for a, j, value in reversed(self.pending):
            if (a, j) == (area, i):
                return value
        return getattr(self, area)[i]

    def crash(self):
        if self.keep == "all":
            survivors = self.pending
        elif self.keep == "last":
            survivors = self.pending[-1:]
        elif self.keep == "ends":
            survivors = self.pending[:1] + self.pending[1:][-1:]
        else:
            survivors = []
        for area, i, value in survivors:
            getattr(self, area)[i] = value
        self.pending = []
        self.budget = None


def copy_disk(disk):
    other = Disk(disk.home, len(disk.journal))
    other.journal = list(disk.journal)
    return other


def crash_test(make_disk, tx, updates):
    old = list(make_disk().home)
    new = list(old)
    for block, value in updates.items():
        new[block] = value
    bad = []
    budget = 0
    while True:
        finished = False
        for keep in ("none", "last", "ends", "all"):
            disk = make_disk()
            disk.budget, disk.keep = budget, keep
            try:
                commit(disk, tx, updates)
                finished = True
                if disk.home != new or disk.journal[0] is not None:
                    bad.append((budget, keep, "no crash", disk.home))
                continue
            except Crash:
                disk.crash()
            rbudget = 0
            while True:
                again = copy_disk(disk)
                again.budget, again.keep = rbudget, keep
                try:
                    recover(again)
                    done = True
                except Crash:
                    again.crash()
                    done = False
                except Exception as e:
                    bad.append((budget, keep, rbudget, type(e).__name__))
                    break
                if not done:
                    try:
                        recover(again)
                    except Exception as e:
                        bad.append((budget, keep, rbudget, type(e).__name__))
                        break
                if again.home not in (old, new) or again.journal[0] is not None:
                    bad.append((budget, keep, rbudget, again.home))
                if done:
                    break
                rbudget += 1
        if finished:
            return bad
        budget += 1


def commit(disk, tx, updates):
    n = len(updates)
    if n + 2 > len(disk.journal):
        return False
    disk.write("journal", 0, ("begin", tx, n))
    for slot, (block, value) in enumerate(updates.items(), start=1):
        disk.write("journal", slot, ("data", block, value))
    disk.flush()
    disk.write("journal", n + 1, ("commit", tx))
    disk.flush()
    for block, value in updates.items():
        disk.write("home", block, value)
    disk.flush()
    disk.write("journal", 0, None)
    disk.flush()
    return True


def recover(disk):
    head = disk.read("journal", 0)
    if head is None:
        return None
    replayed = None
    if head[0] == "begin" and head[2] + 2 <= len(disk.journal):
        tx, n = head[1], head[2]
        if disk.read("journal", n + 1) == ("commit", tx):
            for slot in range(1, n + 1):
                _, block, value = disk.read("journal", slot)
                disk.write("home", block, value)
            disk.flush()
            replayed = tx
    disk.write("journal", 0, None)
    disk.flush()
    return replayed
--- hint
`commit` is the six steps of the protocol in order, with `disk.flush()` as every barrier: four flushes in all. The home writes must come after the barrier that follows the commit record, and the journal is freed only after the home writes have had their own barrier.
--- hint
In `recover`, read slot 0 first. For a begin record, check that slot `n + 1` is inside the journal before you read it, and compare it with the whole tuple `("commit", tx)`, so a commit record left behind by an older transaction does not count.
--- hint
Replaying is a loop over slots 1 to `n`: unpack each `("data", block, value)` and `disk.write("home", block, value)`. Then a flush, then `None` into slot 0 and a final flush. Because the journal holds whole values, replaying twice after a second crash does no harm.
--- check test | A commit with no crash updates the home blocks and frees the journal
(lambda d: (commit(d, 1, {2: "inode v2", 5: "bitmap v2"}), d.home, d.journal[0], d.pending) == (True, [0, 0, "inode v2", 0, 0, "bitmap v2", 0, 0], None, []))(Disk([0] * 8, 6))
--- check test | The journal layout: begin, data in order, commit
(lambda d: (commit(d, 4, {6: "b", 1: "a"}), d.journal[1:4]) == (True, [("data", 6, "b"), ("data", 1, "a"), ("commit", 4)]))(Disk([0] * 8, 6))
--- check test | Too big for the journal: False, and nothing written
(lambda d: (commit(d, 1, {i: "x" for i in range(5)}), d.home, d.journal, d.pending) == (False, [0] * 8, [None] * 6, []))(Disk([0] * 8, 6))
--- check test | Exactly filling the journal is allowed
(lambda d: (commit(d, 1, {i: "x" for i in range(4)}), d.home[:5]) == (True, ["x", "x", "x", "x", 0]))(Disk([0] * 8, 6))
--- check test | Crash-safe at every point, three blocks
crash_test(lambda: Disk([0] * 8, 6), 1, {1: "a", 2: "b", 3: "c"}) == []
?? Every case listed is a crash point where the home blocks were half old, half new. Check your four barriers.
--- check test | Crash-safe with one block and with a transaction that fills the journal
(crash_test(lambda: Disk(["old"] * 4, 3), 9, {3: "new"}), crash_test(lambda: Disk(list("abcd"), 6), 2, {0: "A", 1: "B", 2: "C", 3: "D"})) == ([], [])
--- check test | Crash-safe when the journal still holds an old transaction's records
crash_test(lambda: (lambda d: (setattr(d, "journal", [None, ("data", 1, "OLD1"), ("data", 2, "OLD2"), ("commit", 7), ("commit", 7), None]), d)[1])(Disk([0] * 8, 6)), 8, {1: "a", 2: "b"}) == []
?? A crash while transaction 8 is written can leave transaction 7's commit record where 8's belongs. Compare the whole record, id included.
--- check test | recover on a free journal does nothing
(lambda d: (recover(d), d.home, d.pending) == (None, [1, 2, 3], []))(Disk([1, 2, 3], 5))
--- check test | recover replays a committed transaction that was never checkpointed
(lambda d: (setattr(d, "journal", [("begin", 3, 2), ("data", 0, "x"), ("data", 2, "z"), ("commit", 3), None]), recover(d), d.home, d.journal[0])[1:] == (3, ["x", 1, "z"], None))(Disk([0, 1, 2], 5))
--- check test | recover ignores a transaction without its commit, and frees the journal
(lambda d: (setattr(d, "journal", [("begin", 3, 2), ("data", 0, "x"), ("data", 2, "z"), None, None]), recover(d), d.home, d.journal[0])[1:] == (None, [0, 1, 2], None))(Disk([0, 1, 2], 5))
--- check test | recover ignores a commit with the wrong id, and a begin that claims too much
(lambda d, e: (setattr(d, "journal", [("begin", 5, 1), ("data", 0, "x"), ("commit", 4)]), setattr(e, "journal", [("begin", 5, 9), ("data", 0, "x"), ("commit", 5)]), recover(d), recover(e), d.home, e.home, d.journal[0], e.journal[0])[2:] == (None, None, [0, 1], [0, 1], None, None))(Disk([0, 1], 3), Disk([0, 1], 3))
--- check test | Replaying twice gives the same disk
(lambda d: (setattr(d, "journal", [("begin", 1, 1), ("data", 1, "v"), ("commit", 1)]), recover(d), setattr(d, "journal", [("begin", 1, 1), ("data", 1, "v"), ("commit", 1)]), recover(d), d.home)[4] == [0, "v"])(Disk([0, 0], 3))
--- check test | An empty transaction is fine too
(crash_test(lambda: Disk([0, 0], 2), 1, {}), (lambda d: (commit(d, 1, {}), d.journal[0], d.home))(Disk([5], 2))) == ([], (True, None, [5]))

+++ question | Which barrier matters
--- ask
A journal writes the begin record and block copies, then the commit record, then a barrier, then checkpoints. (The barrier between the copies and the commit record is missing.) What can go wrong?
--- choice
Nothing: the commit record is written last, so it reaches the disk last.
--- choice correct
The disk may make the commit record durable before some of the copies, so after a crash recovery replays a transaction whose copies are garbage.
--- choice
Checkpoint writes may land before the commit record, leaving home blocks half updated.
--- choice
Recovery may replay the transaction twice.
--- why
Between two barriers the disk may complete writes in any order. Without the first barrier, the commit record and the copies share one batch, so the commit can land while a copy does not. The third choice describes the missing second barrier, which this journal has. Replaying twice is harmless, because replay is idempotent.

+++ question | What fsck cannot catch
--- ask
An append wrote the bitmap and the inode, but the crash lost the data block. Why does fsck not repair this?
--- choice
fsck does not look at bitmaps.
--- choice correct
The bitmap and the inode agree, so every rule fsck checks holds; it has no way to know the block's contents are not the ones the program wrote.
--- choice
fsck only runs when the journal is full.
--- why
fsck checks that metadata agree with each other: blocks marked used are pointed to, link counts are true, and so on. Here they all agree. The file silently holds whatever bytes were in that block before. Ordered journaling prevents it by writing data blocks before committing the metadata that points to them.

+++ practice | What a crash leaves behind
--- task
Appending one block to a file writes three things: the bitmap (marking block B used), the inode (pointing to B, with the bigger size), and B itself (the new bytes). A crash may let any of them reach the disk.

Write `after_crash(bitmap, inode, data)`. Each argument is `True` if that write reached the disk. Return a tuple `(consistent, reads, fsck)`:

- `consistent`: `True` when the bitmap and the inode agree about B (both written, or neither).
- `reads`: what reading the file's new part gives: `"old size"` if the inode was not written (the file still ends where it did), `"new data"` if the inode and B were both written, `"garbage"` if the inode was written but B was not.
- `fsck`: what a file system check would do: `"free B"` if only the bitmap marks B, `"mark B used"` if only the inode points to it, `"nothing"` otherwise.

For example, `after_crash(True, True, False)` is `(True, "garbage", "nothing")`.
--- starter
def after_crash(bitmap, inode, data):
    return (True, "new data", "nothing")
--- solution
def after_crash(bitmap, inode, data):
    consistent = bitmap == inode
    if not inode:
        reads = "old size"
    elif data:
        reads = "new data"
    else:
        reads = "garbage"
    if bitmap and not inode:
        fsck = "free B"
    elif inode and not bitmap:
        fsck = "mark B used"
    else:
        fsck = "nothing"
    return (consistent, reads, fsck)
--- hint
Each part of the answer depends on only some of the writes. `consistent` depends on the bitmap and the inode alone; `reads` on the inode and the data; `fsck` on the bitmap and the inode.
--- hint
Consistency is just `bitmap == inode`. For `reads`, test the inode first: without it, the data block does not matter at all.
--- check case | The example: metadata agree, data lost
after_crash(True, True, False)
=> (True, "garbage", "nothing")
--- check case | Everything written, and nothing written
(after_crash(True, True, True), after_crash(False, False, False))
=> ((True, "new data", "nothing"), (True, "old size", "nothing"))
--- check case | Only the data block
after_crash(False, False, True)
=> (True, "old size", "nothing")
--- check case | A leak: the bitmap without the inode
(after_crash(True, False, False), after_crash(True, False, True))
=> ((False, "old size", "free B"), (False, "old size", "free B"))
--- check case | The inode without the bitmap
(after_crash(False, True, False), after_crash(False, True, True))
=> ((False, "garbage", "mark B used"), (False, "new data", "mark B used"))

+++ practice | Replaying a circular log
--- task
A real journal holds many transactions one after another, and wraps around to its start when it reaches its end. Each transaction has a **sequence number**, one more than the transaction before it.

Write `replay(home, log, start, seq)`. `home` is a dict from block number to value; `log` is a list of records; `start` is the slot where the oldest transaction not yet checkpointed begins; `seq` is the sequence number it must have. Records are `("begin", s)`, `("data", block, value)` and `("commit", s)`; a slot may also hold `None` or anything left from before.

Starting at `start` and moving forward (after the last slot comes slot 0), read transactions: a begin record with the expected sequence number, then any number of data records, then a commit record with the same number. Apply each complete transaction's data records to a copy of `home`, in order, and expect the next sequence number. Stop at the first record that does not fit (a wrong sequence number, a missing commit, anything unexpected), or once you have read `len(log)` slots in total. Return `(new_home, next_seq)`, where `next_seq` is the sequence number of the first transaction not applied. Do not change `home` itself.
--- starter
def replay(home, log, start, seq):
    new = dict(home)
    for record in log:
        if record and record[0] == "data":
            new[record[1]] = record[2]
    return new, seq
--- solution
def replay(home, log, start, seq):
    new = dict(home)
    n = len(log)
    read = 0
    while read < n:
        head = log[(start + read) % n]
        if head != ("begin", seq):
            break
        writes = []
        i = read + 1
        committed = False
        while i < n:
            record = log[(start + i) % n]
            if isinstance(record, tuple) and len(record) == 3 and record[0] == "data":
                writes.append(record)
                i += 1
            elif record == ("commit", seq):
                committed = True
                i += 1
                break
            else:
                break
        if not committed:
            break
        for _, block, value in writes:
            new[block] = value
        read = i
        seq += 1
    return new, seq
--- hint
Keep a count of slots read so far, and find slot `(start + count) % len(log)`. A transaction is applied only after you have seen its commit, so collect its data records in a list first.
--- hint
Compare whole records: the begin must equal `("begin", seq)` and the commit `("commit", seq)`. Any other record in the middle of a transaction ends the replay without applying it. Stop the outer loop when the count reaches `len(log)`.
--- check case | Two transactions, then leftovers from an old lap
replay({1: "a"}, [("begin", 5), ("data", 1, "b"), ("commit", 5), ("begin", 6), ("data", 2, "c"), ("data", 1, "d"), ("commit", 6), ("begin", 2), ("data", 1, "OLD"), ("commit", 2)], 0, 5)
=> ({1: "d", 2: "c"}, 7)
--- check case | Wrapping around the end of the log
replay({}, [("data", 4, "y"), ("commit", 9), None, ("begin", 8), ("data", 3, "x"), ("commit", 8), ("begin", 9)], 3, 8)
=> ({3: "x", 4: "y"}, 10)
--- check case | A transaction without its commit is not applied
replay({7: 0}, [("begin", 1), ("data", 7, 1), ("commit", 1), ("begin", 2), ("data", 7, 2), None, None], 0, 1)
=> ({7: 1}, 2)
--- check case | A commit with the wrong number stops the replay
replay({}, [("begin", 3), ("data", 0, "x"), ("commit", 2), None], 0, 3)
=> ({}, 3)
--- check case | Nothing to replay: a begin with the wrong number, or an empty log
(replay({1: 1}, [("begin", 4), ("commit", 4)], 0, 5), replay({}, [], 0, 1))
=> (({1: 1}, 5), ({}, 1))
--- check case | A transaction with no data records still counts
replay({}, [("begin", 1), ("commit", 1), ("begin", 2), ("data", 0, "z"), ("commit", 2)], 0, 1)
=> ({0: "z"}, 3)
--- check case | A full lap is read at most once
replay({}, [("begin", 1), ("commit", 1)], 0, 1)
=> ({}, 2)
--- check test | home itself is not changed
(lambda h: (replay(h, [("begin", 1), ("data", 0, "new"), ("commit", 1)], 0, 1), h)[1] == {0: "old"})({0: "old"})

+++ practice | A file system check
--- task
Write the core of `fsck` for a tiny file system. You get:

- `inodes`: a dict from inode number to `{"nlink": count, "blocks": [block numbers]}` for every allocated inode;
- `entries`: a list of directory entries `(dir_inum, name, inum)`, one per name in any directory (the root is inode 1, and its own entry is not listed);
- `bitmap`: the set of block numbers marked used.

Write `fsck(inodes, entries, bitmap)` returning a dict with these keys:

- `"bitmap"`: the correct set of used blocks, every block some inode points to;
- `"leaked"`: sorted list of blocks marked used that no inode points to;
- `"unmarked"`: sorted list of blocks some inode points to that are not marked;
- `"shared"`: sorted list of blocks that appear more than once across all inodes' block lists;
- `"nlink"`: a dict from inode number to its true link count (the number of entries naming it), only for inodes whose stored count is wrong;
- `"orphans"`: sorted list of inodes that no entry names;
- `"dangling"`: sorted list of `(dir_inum, name)` for entries that name an inode that is not allocated.

Leave the root (inode 1) out of `"nlink"` and `"orphans"`: its entry and its `..` links are not listed. Entries that dangle do not count towards any link count.
--- starter
def fsck(inodes, entries, bitmap):
    return {"bitmap": set(bitmap), "leaked": [], "unmarked": [], "shared": [], "nlink": {}, "orphans": [], "dangling": []}
--- solution
def fsck(inodes, entries, bitmap):
    seen = {}
    for info in inodes.values():
        for block in info["blocks"]:
            seen[block] = seen.get(block, 0) + 1
    used = set(seen)
    links = {inum: 0 for inum in inodes}
    dangling = []
    for directory, name, inum in entries:
        if inum in links:
            links[inum] += 1
        else:
            dangling.append((directory, name))
    return {
        "bitmap": used,
        "leaked": sorted(bitmap - used),
        "unmarked": sorted(used - bitmap),
        "shared": sorted(b for b, count in seen.items() if count > 1),
        "nlink": {inum: n for inum, n in links.items() if n != inodes[inum]["nlink"] and inum != 1},
        "orphans": sorted(inum for inum, n in links.items() if n == 0 and inum != 1),
        "dangling": sorted(dangling),
    }
--- hint
Walk every inode's block list once and count how many times each block appears. The keys are the true bitmap; counts above 1 are shared blocks. Set differences with the stored bitmap give the leaks and the unmarked blocks.
--- hint
Start a link count of 0 for every allocated inode, then walk the entries: an entry for an allocated inode adds one, an entry for any other number is dangling. Compare the counts with the stored `nlink`, and inodes still at 0 (apart from the root) are orphans.
--- check case | A clean file system: nothing to fix
fsck({1: {"nlink": 2, "blocks": [10]}, 2: {"nlink": 1, "blocks": [11, 12]}}, [(1, "log", 2)], {10, 11, 12})
=> {"bitmap": {10, 11, 12}, "leaked": [], "unmarked": [], "shared": [], "nlink": {}, "orphans": [], "dangling": []}
--- check case | A leak and a block marked free
fsck({2: {"nlink": 1, "blocks": [11, 13]}}, [(1, "a", 2)], {11, 12})
=> {"bitmap": {11, 13}, "leaked": [12], "unmarked": [13], "shared": [], "nlink": {}, "orphans": [], "dangling": []}
--- check case | Two inodes claim one block
fsck({2: {"nlink": 1, "blocks": [20, 21]}, 3: {"nlink": 1, "blocks": [21]}}, [(1, "a", 2), (1, "b", 3)], {20, 21})
=> {"bitmap": {20, 21}, "leaked": [], "unmarked": [], "shared": [21], "nlink": {}, "orphans": [], "dangling": []}
--- check case | Wrong link counts, an orphan and a dangling entry
fsck({2: {"nlink": 1, "blocks": []}, 3: {"nlink": 2, "blocks": []}, 4: {"nlink": 1, "blocks": []}}, [(1, "x", 2), (1, "y", 2), (1, "z", 3), (1, "ghost", 9)], set())
=> {"bitmap": set(), "leaked": [], "unmarked": [], "shared": [], "nlink": {2: 2, 3: 1, 4: 0}, "orphans": [4], "dangling": [(1, "ghost")]}
--- check case | The same block twice in one inode is shared too
fsck({2: {"nlink": 1, "blocks": [30, 30]}}, [(1, "a", 2)], {30})["shared"]
=> [30]
--- check case | Nothing at all
fsck({}, [], {5})
=> {"bitmap": set(), "leaked": [5], "unmarked": [], "shared": [], "nlink": {}, "orphans": [], "dangling": []}

+++ practice | Commit records with checksums
--- task
ext4 can put a checksum of a transaction's data into its commit record, so recovery can tell a complete transaction from a torn one without relying on a barrier before the commit.

The starter gives `checksum(records)`, which computes a CRC-32 of a list of data records. Write `to_replay(journal)`. `journal` is a list of slots. Slot 0 may hold `("begin", tx, n)`; slots 1 to `n` should hold `("data", block, value)`; slot `n + 1` should hold `("commit", tx, crc)`. Return the list of `(block, value)` pairs to write home, in order, when **all** of these hold:

- slot 0 is a begin record (a tuple whose first item is `"begin"`);
- slot `n + 1` exists in the journal;
- slot `n + 1` is a commit record with the same `tx`;
- its `crc` equals `checksum` of the list of slots 1 to `n` exactly as they are.

Otherwise return an empty list. A transaction with `n = 0` is valid if its commit matches (the checksum of an empty list).
--- starter
import zlib


def checksum(records):
    return zlib.crc32(repr(records).encode())


def to_replay(journal):
    head = journal[0]
    n = head[2]
    return [(r[1], r[2]) for r in journal[1:n + 1]]
--- solution
import zlib
