def checksum(records):
    return zlib.crc32(repr(records).encode())


def to_replay(journal):
    if not journal:
        return []
    head = journal[0]
    if not (isinstance(head, tuple) and len(head) == 3 and head[0] == "begin"):
        return []
    _, tx, n = head
    if n < 0 or n + 1 >= len(journal):
        return []
    records = journal[1:n + 1]
    if journal[n + 1] != ("commit", tx, checksum(records)):
        return []
    return [(block, value) for _, block, value in records]
--- hint
Check the cheap things first, each returning `[]` when it fails: is slot 0 a begin record, and is `n + 1` a valid index? Only then read the commit slot.
--- hint
Compare the commit slot with the whole tuple `("commit", tx, checksum(journal[1:n + 1]))`. If a data slot is torn or missing, its contents differ, so the checksum differs.
--- check case | A complete transaction
to_replay([("begin", 4, 2), ("data", 7, "inode v2"), ("data", 3, "bitmap v2"), ("commit", 4, checksum([("data", 7, "inode v2"), ("data", 3, "bitmap v2")]))])
=> [(7, "inode v2"), (3, "bitmap v2")]
--- check case | A torn data record: the checksum does not match
to_replay([("begin", 4, 2), ("data", 7, "inode v2"), None, ("commit", 4, checksum([("data", 7, "inode v2"), ("data", 3, "bitmap v2")]))])
=> []
--- check case | A commit from another transaction
to_replay([("begin", 5, 1), ("data", 1, "x"), ("commit", 4, checksum([("data", 1, "x")]))])
=> []
--- check case | The commit slot would be past the end
to_replay([("begin", 5, 3), ("data", 1, "x"), ("commit", 5, 0)])
=> []
--- check case | An empty transaction, an empty journal, a free journal
(to_replay([("begin", 2, 0), ("commit", 2, checksum([]))]), to_replay([]), to_replay([None, None]))
=> ([], [], [])
--- check case | A commit record with no checksum at all
to_replay([("begin", 1, 1), ("data", 0, "v"), ("commit", 1)])
=> []
--- check case | Garbage in slot 0
(to_replay([("data", 0, "v"), ("commit", 1, 0)]), to_replay([("begin",), None]))
=> ([], [])

+++ practice | Debug: recovery replays a stale commit
--- task
`recover(journal, home)` should replay the transaction in `journal` onto `home` (a list of blocks) and return its id, or return `None` and leave `home` alone if the transaction was not committed. The layout: slot 0 holds `("begin", tx, n)` or `None`, slots 1 to `n` hold `("data", block, value)`, and slot `n + 1` holds `("commit", tx)`.

It has a bug. The journal is reused. When the power fails while transaction 8 is being written, before its commit record, the slot where 8's commit belongs can still hold transaction 7's old commit record. Recovery then replays transaction 8 as if it had committed, including data slots that still hold transaction 7's old copies. Find the bug and fix it.
--- starter
def recover(journal, home):
    head = journal[0]
    if head is None or head[0] != "begin":
        return None
    _, tx, n = head
    if n + 1 >= len(journal):
        return None
    tail = journal[n + 1]
    if tail is None or tail[0] != "commit":
        return None
    for _, block, value in journal[1:n + 1]:
        home[block] = value
    return tx
--- solution
def recover(journal, home):
    head = journal[0]
    if head is None or head[0] != "begin":
        return None
    _, tx, n = head
    if n + 1 >= len(journal):
        return None
    tail = journal[n + 1]
    if tail != ("commit", tx):
        return None
    for _, block, value in journal[1:n + 1]:
        home[block] = value
    return tx
--- hint
What does the check on `tail` look at, and what does it ignore?
--- hint
A commit record only vouches for the transaction with the same id. Compare `tail` with `("commit", tx)`.
--- check test | Transaction 7's stale commit does not make transaction 8 committed
(lambda home: (recover([("begin", 8, 2), ("data", 1, "new"), ("data", 2, "OLD"), ("commit", 7)], home), home) == (None, ["a", "b", "c"]))(["a", "b", "c"])
--- check test | A real commit is replayed
(lambda home: (recover([("begin", 8, 2), ("data", 1, "x"), ("data", 2, "y"), ("commit", 8)], home), home) == (8, ["a", "x", "y"]))(["a", "b", "c"])
--- check test | A missing commit, a free journal, a begin that claims too much
(lambda home: (recover([("begin", 3, 1), ("data", 0, "z"), None], home), recover([None, None], home), recover([("begin", 3, 4), ("commit", 3)], home), home) == (None, None, None, ["a"]))(["a"])
--- check test | An empty transaction that committed
(lambda home: (recover([("begin", 5, 0), ("commit", 5)], home), home) == (5, [1]))([1])

+++ practice | Exploring every crash
--- task
Build a small crash explorer, the file-system cousin of the interleaving explorer from lesson 5.

A **protocol** is a list of operations: `("write", block, value)` or `("flush",)`. The disk starts as the list `initial`. A crash can happen after any number of operations, from none to all of them. At a crash, every write followed by a flush in the operations done so far is durable. The writes after the last flush may each survive or not, independently: any subset of them survives, applied in the order they were issued.

Write `crash_states(ops, initial)` that returns the sorted list of every disk state a crash can leave, each as a tuple. Then write `is_safe(ops, initial, ok)`: `True` if `ok(state)` is true for every state `crash_states` returns.

For example, `crash_states([("write", 0, "x"), ("write", 1, "y")], ["a", "b"])` is `[("a", "b"), ("a", "y"), ("x", "b"), ("x", "y")]`: with no flush, either write may be lost.
--- starter
def crash_states(ops, initial):
    state = list(initial)
    for op in ops:
        if op[0] == "write":
            state[op[1]] = op[2]
    return [tuple(initial), tuple(state)]


def is_safe(ops, initial, ok):
    return True
--- solution
from itertools import combinations


def crash_states(ops, initial):
    states = set()
    for cut in range(len(ops) + 1):
        durable = list(initial)
        pending = []
        for op in ops[:cut]:
            if op[0] == "flush":
                for _, block, value in pending:
                    durable[block] = value
                pending = []
            else:
                pending.append(op)
        for size in range(len(pending) + 1):
            for chosen in combinations(pending, size):
                state = list(durable)
                for _, block, value in chosen:
                    state[block] = value
                states.add(tuple(state))
    return sorted(states)


def is_safe(ops, initial, ok):
    return all(ok(state) for state in crash_states(ops, initial))
--- hint
Try every crash point: for each `cut` from 0 to `len(ops)`, run the first `cut` operations, keeping a durable list and a list of pending writes that a flush moves into it.
--- hint
At the crash point, every subset of the pending writes can survive. `itertools.combinations(pending, size)` for every size from 0 to `len(pending)` gives each subset, with the writes still in their original order. Collect the states in a set of tuples, then sort.
--- check case | The example: no flush, either write may be lost
crash_states([("write", 0, "x"), ("write", 1, "y")], ["a", "b"])
=> [("a", "b"), ("a", "y"), ("x", "b"), ("x", "y")]
--- check case | A flush between them: y cannot survive without x
crash_states([("write", 0, "x"), ("flush",), ("write", 1, "y")], ["a", "b"])
=> [("a", "b"), ("x", "b"), ("x", "y")]
--- check case | Two writes to one block: the later one wins when both survive
crash_states([("write", 0, "p"), ("write", 0, "q")], ["o"])
=> [("o",), ("p",), ("q",)]
--- check case | No operations
crash_states([], [1, 2])
=> [(1, 2)]
--- check test | A journal with its barrier is safe; without it, it is not
(lambda ok: (is_safe([("write", 1, "copy"), ("flush",), ("write", 2, "commit"), ("flush",), ("write", 0, "new"), ("flush",)], ["old", "", ""], ok), is_safe([("write", 1, "copy"), ("write", 2, "commit"), ("flush",), ("write", 0, "new"), ("flush",)], ["old", "", ""], ok)))(lambda s: s[2] != "commit" or s[1] == "copy") == (True, False)
--- check test | Checkpointing before the commit is durable is unsafe
is_safe([("write", 1, "copy"), ("flush",), ("write", 2, "commit"), ("write", 0, "new"), ("flush",)], ["old", "", ""], lambda s: s[0] == "old" or s[2] == "commit") == False
--- check test | Three unflushed writes give 8 states
len(crash_states([("write", 0, 1), ("write", 1, 1), ("write", 2, 1)], [0, 0, 0])) == 8

=== os-15 | I/O and disks: disk scheduling, SSDs and buffering
--- teach
The last two lessons counted block writes: how many an append needs, and in which order they must reach the disk. They never asked how long one takes. On a hard disk, the answer depends enormously on **where** the block is: reading the block next to the last one costs a few microseconds, while reading one on the other side of the disk costs about ten milliseconds, a thousand times more. This lesson measures that cost, builds the algorithms that order disk requests to save it, then looks at flash storage (where the rules are completely different) and at the buffers the kernel puts in between.

### How the kernel talks to a device

You met the hardware side in Computer Organization. A device has a **controller** with a few registers. The kernel writes a command into them ("read 8 sectors starting at sector 90,112 into this memory address"), and the controller does the work while the CPU does something else. Three ways to know it has finished:

- **Polling:** the CPU keeps reading a status register. Fine for a device that answers within nanoseconds; wasteful for a disk.
- **Interrupts:** the device raises an interrupt when it is done (lesson 1: one of the three ways into the kernel).
- **DMA** (direct memory access): the controller copies the data between the device and RAM itself, then interrupts once for the whole transfer.

The code that knows one device's registers is its **device driver**. Above the drivers, the kernel keeps a **queue** of pending requests for each disk. Which request to send next is the kernel's choice, and on a hard disk it matters a great deal.

### What a hard disk read costs

A **hard disk drive** (HDD) stores data on spinning platters. Each surface has thousands of concentric rings, called **tracks**, each divided into sectors. One arm carries the read/write heads across all surfaces together. The set of tracks under the heads at one arm position is a **cylinder**. The kernel sees only sector numbers, but neighbouring numbers sit next to each other on a track, so "cylinder" is a good model of where a block is.

Reading a block takes three steps, one after another:

1. **Seek:** move the arm to the right cylinder. A few milliseconds; a short hop is faster than a long one.
2. **Rotation:** wait for the sector to come round under the head. On average half a turn.
3. **Transfer:** read the bytes as they pass under the head.

So **access time = seek time + rotational latency + transfer time**. For a laptop disk spinning at 5,400 revolutions per minute (rpm), with a 12 ms average seek and 100 MB/s transfer (1 MB is 10⁶ bytes here, as disk makers count), a 4 KiB read costs:

```python
rpm, seek_ms, mb_per_s = 5400, 12, 100
turn_ms = 60_000 / rpm                          # one revolution
transfer_ms = 4096 / (mb_per_s * 10**6) * 1000
print(round(turn_ms, 2), round(seek_ms + turn_ms / 2 + transfer_ms, 2))
```

It prints `11.11` and `17.6`. One turn takes 11.11 ms, and one random 4 KiB read about 17.6 ms, of which the transfer is only 0.04 ms. The disk spends 99.8% of the time moving and waiting.

That gives the most important fact about hard disks: **sequential access is hundreds of times faster than random access.** Reading 1,000 consecutive blocks costs one seek and one rotation, then pure transfer. Reading 1,000 scattered blocks costs 1,000 seeks. That is why lesson 13's allocator keeps a file's blocks together, and why lesson 14's journal is one contiguous region.

### Disk scheduling

The queue holds several requests, each for some cylinder. Serving them in a clever order cuts the total seek distance. Every policy below is judged by the **total head movement** (the sum of the distances the arm travels, in cylinders) and by **fairness** (whether any request can wait for ever).

The classic textbook example: cylinders 0 to 199, the head at 53, and the queue 98, 183, 37, 122, 14, 124, 65, 67 (in arrival order).

- **FCFS** (first come, first served) serves them in arrival order: 53 → 98 → 183 → 37 → 122 → 14 → 124 → 65 → 67. Fair, but the arm swings back and forth: 640 cylinders.
- **SSTF** (shortest seek time first) always serves the pending request nearest the head: 65, 67, 37, 14, 98, 122, 124, 183. Total 236.
- **SCAN**, the **elevator**: sweep in one direction serving everything on the way, all the way to the last cylinder, then reverse. Moving down from 53: 37, 14, then on to cylinder 0, then up through 65, 67, 98, 122, 124, 183. Total 53 + 183 = 236.
- **LOOK**: SCAN, but reverse at the last request instead of the edge. Moving down: 37, 14, then 65 … 183. Total 39 + 169 = 208.
- **C-SCAN** (circular SCAN): sweep up only. At the top edge, jump straight back to cylinder 0 and sweep up again. Every request then waits about the same, whatever its cylinder.
- **C-LOOK**: C-SCAN, but turn at the last request and jump back to the lowest pending one.

**Conventions matter.** Whether the return jump of C-SCAN and C-LOOK counts as head movement varies between books. The arm really does move, so in this course it counts.

### Why LOOK is the best order for a fixed batch

Suppose every request is already in the queue and no more will come. What is the least possible total movement?

Let the lowest request be at `a`, the highest at `b`, and the head at `h`, somewhere between them.

- **A lower bound.** The arm must visit both `a` and `b`. Whichever it reaches first, it must first travel from `h` to that end, and then cross the whole range `b − a` to reach the other. So any order moves at least **min(h − a, b − h) + (b − a)**.
- **LOOK reaches it.** LOOK heading towards the nearer end travels `h − a` (or `b − h`) to reach it, serving everything on the way, then turns and travels `b − a` to the other end, serving the rest. That is exactly the bound.

So for a static batch, LOOK in the better direction is optimal. In the example, the bound is min(53 − 14, 183 − 53) + (183 − 14) = 39 + 169 = 208, which is what LOOK moving down gave. (If all requests are on one side of the head, the bound is just the distance to the farthest one, and LOOK towards them reaches it.)

**SSTF is greedy, and greedy is not optimal.** With the head at 14 and requests 12, 15, 20 and 25, SSTF goes to 15 (distance 1), back to 12 (3), then up to 20 (8) and 25 (5): 17 in all. Going down to 12 first and then up costs 2 + 13 = 15.

### Starvation, and why SCAN bounds the wait

In real life requests keep arriving. SSTF can then **starve** a request: if new requests keep arriving near the head, a far one is never the nearest, and it waits for ever.

SCAN cannot starve anyone. Take a request for cylinder `c` that arrives at any moment. Within one sweep, the arm reaches an end and turns; on its next sweep it crosses the whole disk, so it passes `c`. So every request is served within two sweeps: its wait is bounded by about twice the time to cross the disk, however many other requests arrive.

### What real systems do

Linux's block layer offers several schedulers per disk. **mq-deadline** sorts requests by position like LOOK, but gives each one a [[deadline|deadline]] (500 ms for reads by default) and serves any request whose deadline has passed first, so nothing starves. **BFQ** shares the disk fairly between processes. Modern drives also reorder requests themselves with [[NCQ|ncq]], since only the drive knows its exact geometry. For flash drives, Linux usually picks **none**: no reordering at all, for reasons the next section explains.

### Flash memory and SSDs

A **solid-state drive** (SSD) has no moving parts. Seek time and rotation are gone: a random read costs about the same as a sequential one, roughly 50 to 100 microseconds. But flash has three strange rules:

1. Flash is written in **pages** (typically 4 to 16 KiB).
2. A page cannot be overwritten. It must be **erased** first, and erasing works only on a whole **erase block** of many pages (typically hundreds).
3. Each block survives only a limited number of [[erase cycles|pe]].

So the SSD never updates a page in place. A small computer inside it, running the **flash translation layer** (FTL), keeps a map from each logical page number (what the kernel asks for) to a physical page. An update is written to a fresh page, the map is changed, and the old page is marked **invalid**. This is the same idea as the page table of lesson 11, and the copy-on-write of lesson 14.

Invalid pages pile up. To get erased blocks back, the FTL runs **garbage collection**: it picks a **victim** block, copies the pages in it that are still valid to a fresh block, and erases the victim.

### Write amplification

Those copies are writes the host never asked for. **Write amplification** is

  write amplification = pages written to flash ÷ pages the host wrote

and it is 1 at best. Here is why it grows as the drive fills up. Say every erase block has P pages, and the victim still holds v valid ones. Collecting it copies v pages and frees P − v pages for new data. So each new page the host writes costs, on average, its own write plus v / (P − v) copies:

  write amplification = 1 + v / (P − v) = P / (P − v)

A victim that is half valid doubles every write; one that is 90% valid multiplies it by 10. A fuller drive leaves fuller victims, which is why SSDs keep hidden spare space (**over-provisioning**), and why the kernel tells the SSD which pages a deleted file used, with [[TRIM|trim]], so they stop counting as valid. Choosing the block with the fewest valid pages as the victim is called **greedy** collection.

**Wear levelling** is the FTL's other job: it spreads erases evenly over all blocks, so no block wears out long before the rest.

### Buffering

A **buffer** is memory that holds data on its way between two parties that do not work at the same speed or in the same sizes. You met one in lesson 1 (`printf`'s buffer) and built one in lesson 7 (the bounded buffer). The kernel uses buffers everywhere in I/O:

- **The page cache** keeps recently used file blocks in RAM (lesson 12's replacement policies decide what stays). A second read of a file costs no disk access at all.
- **[[Read-ahead|readahead]]**: when a program reads a file sequentially, the kernel fetches the next blocks before they are asked for, in one large request.
- **Write-back**: a `write` only copies into the page cache and marks the page **dirty**. Background threads write dirty pages out later (Linux writes pages that have been dirty for about 30 seconds), merging many small writes into a few large ones. `fsync` forces them out now.
- **Double buffering**: a device fills one buffer while the program empties the other, then they swap. A spacecraft's radio can downlink one telemetry frame while the next one is being assembled, and neither waits.

### Many disks as one: RAID

**RAID** (redundant array of independent disks) makes several disks act as one.

- **RAID 0** stripes blocks across the disks: fast, but losing any disk loses everything.
- **RAID 1** mirrors: every block on two disks.
- **RAID 5** stores, in every stripe of blocks across the disks, one **parity** block that is the bitwise XOR of the others. XOR has the property `x ^ x = 0`, so any one missing block equals the XOR of all the rest:

```python
d0, d1, d2 = 0b1100, 0b1010, 0b0111
p = d0 ^ d1 ^ d2
print(bin(p), bin(d0 ^ d2 ^ p) == bin(d1))
```

It prints `0b1` and `True`: the disk holding `d1` died, and XOR of the survivors gives it back. RAID 5 survives the loss of any one disk, at the cost of one disk's worth of space. Updating one data block also means updating the parity, and a crash between the two leaves them disagreeing: the [[RAID write hole|writehole]].

### Where it is used

- **Every operating system** schedules disk requests, keeps a page cache and does read-ahead and write-back.
- **Data centres** use RAID or its big relatives (erasure codes, which survive several lost disks), and choose SSD over-provisioning by workload.
- **Spacecraft** use flash for mass storage, where radiation and limited erase cycles make the FTL's wear levelling and error correction essential.

**Watch out:**

- **SSTF looks optimal and is not.** It is greedy, it can starve far requests, and it can move farther than LOOK even on a fixed batch.
- **Count the first move.** Total head movement includes the trip from the starting position to the first request, and (in this course) the return jumps of C-SCAN and C-LOOK.
- **Requests at the head's own cylinder** must be served, at distance 0. An "ahead" test with `>` instead of `>=` loses them.
- **An SSD does not overwrite in place.** Its cost is in erases and copies, so its numbers (write amplification, wear) are not a hard disk's numbers (seeks, rotation).

::: context deadline Sorting, with a time limit
The deadline scheduler keeps two views of the queue: one sorted by sector, for efficient sweeps, and one by arrival time. It normally serves requests in sector order, in batches. Before each batch it checks the oldest request, and if that one has waited past its deadline (by default 500 ms for reads and 5 seconds for writes), it jumps there first. Reads get the shorter deadline because a program usually waits for a read, while most writes go to the page cache and nobody waits for them.
:::

::: context ncq The drive reorders too
Native Command Queuing lets the kernel send up to 32 requests to a SATA drive at once. The drive's own firmware then chooses the order, and it knows things the kernel does not: exactly where each sector sits on the track and where the head is in its rotation. It can serve a request that is slightly farther away but about to pass under the head. NVMe flash drives go much further, with up to 65,535 queues of up to 65,536 commands each, so every CPU core can have its own.
:::

::: context pe How long flash lasts
Every program and erase pushes electrons through a thin insulating layer, which slowly wears out. A block is rated for a number of program/erase (P/E) cycles: roughly 100,000 for old single-level cells, 3,000 or so for common triple-level cells that store three bits per cell, and around 1,000 for four-bit cells. Drives are sold with an endurance rating, such as 600 terabytes written, and write amplification eats directly into it.
:::

::: context trim Telling the drive what is free
When you delete a file, the file system only updates its own bitmap. The SSD does not know that those pages hold nothing useful, so it keeps copying them during garbage collection. The TRIM command (called discard on Linux, deallocate in NVMe) tells the drive which logical pages are no longer in use, so it can mark them invalid at once. Linux can send it at every delete, or in batches from a weekly `fstrim` job.
:::

::: context readahead Fetching blocks before they are asked for
Linux watches each open file's reads. When they look sequential, it reads ahead: first a small window, then doubling up to a maximum (128 KiB by default on many systems), and it starts the next read-ahead when the program reaches a marked page inside the current window, so the disk is already busy before the data runs out. Random reads turn it off, since fetching neighbours would only waste memory and disk time.
:::

::: context writehole When data and parity disagree
Updating one block of a RAID 5 stripe takes two writes to two disks: the new data and the new parity. If the power fails between them, the stripe's parity no longer matches its data, and nothing on disk says so. Everything seems fine until a disk dies: then its blocks are rebuilt from the wrong parity, and come back corrupted. Hardware RAID cards avoid this with battery-backed memory; ZFS's RAID-Z avoids it by never overwriting a stripe in place.
:::
--- task
Write a disk scheduler simulator. `schedule(policy, head, requests, lo, hi, direction)` gets:

- `policy`: one of `"FCFS"`, `"SSTF"`, `"SCAN"`, `"LOOK"`, `"C-SCAN"`, `"C-LOOK"`;
- `head`: the cylinder the head starts on;
- `requests`: the list of requested cylinders, in arrival order (all arrive at once; duplicates are allowed and each is served);
- `lo` and `hi`: the first and last cylinders of the disk;
- `direction`: `"up"` (towards `hi`) or `"down"` (towards `lo`), the way the arm is moving at the start. FCFS and SSTF ignore it.

Return a pair `(order, moved)`: the cylinders in the order they are served, and the total head movement.

- **FCFS** serves in arrival order.
- **SSTF** always serves the nearest pending request; on a tie, the lower cylinder.
- For the sweeping policies, requests **ahead** are those at the head's cylinder or beyond it in `direction`; the rest are **behind**. First serve everything ahead, nearest first. If nothing is behind, stop there. Otherwise:
  - **SCAN** carries on to the edge (`hi` going up, `lo` going down), then reverses and serves the rest, nearest first.
  - **LOOK** reverses at once and serves the rest, nearest first.
  - **C-SCAN** carries on to the edge, jumps to the opposite edge (the jump counts as movement), then sweeps in the original direction again and serves the rest.
  - **C-LOOK** jumps straight to the farthest request behind (the one a fresh sweep in the original direction meets first, and the jump counts as movement), then sweeps in the original direction serving the rest.
--- starter
def schedule(policy, head, requests, lo, hi, direction):
    raise NotImplementedError("serve the requests in the policy's order")
--- solution
def schedule(policy, head, requests, lo, hi, direction):
    pos = head
    moved = 0
    order = []

    def go(to):
        nonlocal pos, moved
        moved += abs(to - pos)
        pos = to

    def serve(cylinders):
        for c in cylinders:
            go(c)
            order.append(c)

    if policy == "FCFS":
        serve(requests)
        return order, moved
    if policy == "SSTF":
        pending = list(requests)
        while pending:
            nearest = min(pending, key=lambda c: (abs(c - pos), c))
            pending.remove(nearest)
            serve([nearest])
        return order, moved
    if direction == "up":
        ahead = sorted(c for c in requests if c >= head)
        behind = sorted(c for c in requests if c < head)
        edge, far = hi, lo
    else:
        ahead = sorted((c for c in requests if c <= head), reverse=True)
        behind = sorted((c for c in requests if c > head), reverse=True)
        edge, far = lo, hi
    serve(ahead)
    if not behind:
        return order, moved
    if policy == "SCAN":
        go(edge)
        serve(reversed(behind))
    elif policy == "LOOK":
        serve(reversed(behind))
    elif policy == "C-SCAN":
        go(edge)
        go(far)
        serve(behind)
    elif policy == "C-LOOK":
        serve(behind)
    return order, moved
--- hint
Write one small helper that moves the head to a cylinder and adds the distance to the total, and use it for every move, including the trips to an edge and the jumps back. Then each policy is just a list of places to go.
--- hint
For the sweeping policies, split the requests into two sorted lists. Going up, "ahead" is every cylinder `>= head` in increasing order, and "behind" is the rest. Going down, it is the mirror image: `<= head` in decreasing order.
--- hint
After the ahead list, SCAN and LOOK serve the behind list from nearest to farthest (the reverse of a fresh sweep), while C-SCAN and C-LOOK serve it in the same direction as the first sweep, starting from the far end. Only SCAN and C-SCAN visit the edges.
--- check case | FCFS on the classic queue
schedule("FCFS", 53, [98, 183, 37, 122, 14, 124, 65, 67], 0, 199, "up")
=> ([98, 183, 37, 122, 14, 124, 65, 67], 640)
--- check case | SSTF on the classic queue
schedule("SSTF", 53, [98, 183, 37, 122, 14, 124, 65, 67], 0, 199, "up")
=> ([65, 67, 37, 14, 98, 122, 124, 183], 236)
--- check case | SCAN moving down goes to cylinder 0 before turning
schedule("SCAN", 53, [98, 183, 37, 122, 14, 124, 65, 67], 0, 199, "down")
=> ([37, 14, 65, 67, 98, 122, 124, 183], 236)
--- check case | LOOK moving down turns at the last request
schedule("LOOK", 53, [98, 183, 37, 122, 14, 124, 65, 67], 0, 199, "down")
=> ([37, 14, 65, 67, 98, 122, 124, 183], 208)
--- check case | C-SCAN moving up: to 199, back to 0, then up again
schedule("C-SCAN", 53, [98, 183, 37, 122, 14, 124, 65, 67], 0, 199, "up")
=> ([65, 67, 98, 122, 124, 183, 14, 37], 382)
--- check case | C-LOOK moving up jumps from 183 straight to 14
schedule("C-LOOK", 53, [98, 183, 37, 122, 14, 124, 65, 67], 0, 199, "up")
=> ([65, 67, 98, 122, 124, 183, 14, 37], 322)
--- check case | All six policies on a new queue, moving up
[schedule(p, 40, [10, 70, 45, 20, 90], 0, 99, "up") for p in ("FCFS", "SSTF", "SCAN", "LOOK", "C-SCAN", "C-LOOK")]
=> [([10, 70, 45, 20, 90], 210), ([45, 20, 10, 70, 90], 120), ([45, 70, 90, 20, 10], 148), ([45, 70, 90, 20, 10], 130), ([45, 70, 90, 10, 20], 178), ([45, 70, 90, 10, 20], 140)]
--- check case | The sweeping policies moving down
[schedule(p, 40, [10, 70, 45, 20, 90], 0, 99, "down") for p in ("SCAN", "LOOK", "C-SCAN", "C-LOOK")]
=> [([20, 10, 45, 70, 90], 130), ([20, 10, 45, 70, 90], 110), ([20, 10, 90, 70, 45], 193), ([20, 10, 90, 70, 45], 155)]
--- check case | A request at the head's cylinder is served first, at distance 0
(schedule("LOOK", 50, [60, 50, 40], 0, 99, "up"), schedule("C-SCAN", 50, [50, 40], 0, 99, "down"))
=> (([50, 60, 40], 30), ([50, 40], 10))
--- check case | Nothing behind: SCAN and C-SCAN do not visit the edge
[schedule(p, 30, [35, 80, 31], 0, 99, "up") for p in ("SCAN", "C-SCAN", "LOOK", "C-LOOK")]
=> [([31, 35, 80], 50), ([31, 35, 80], 50), ([31, 35, 80], 50), ([31, 35, 80], 50)]
--- check case | SSTF ties go to the lower cylinder, and duplicates are each served
(schedule("SSTF", 50, [55, 45, 45], 0, 99, "up"), schedule("LOOK", 50, [70, 70, 20], 0, 99, "up"))
=> (([45, 45, 55], 15), ([70, 70, 20], 70))
--- check case | No requests at all
[schedule(p, 12, [], 0, 99, "up") for p in ("FCFS", "SSTF", "SCAN", "C-LOOK")]
=> [([], 0), ([], 0), ([], 0), ([], 0)]
--- check case | SSTF is not optimal: LOOK down beats it here
(schedule("SSTF", 14, [15, 20, 12, 25], 0, 99, "up")[1], schedule("LOOK", 14, [15, 20, 12, 25], 0, 99, "down")[1])
=> (17, 15)

+++ question | The lower bound
--- ask
Requests wait at cylinders 20, 35, 60 and 90, and the head is at 50. No more requests will arrive. What is the smallest total head movement any order can achieve? Type a whole number.
--- answer
100
--- why
The arm must visit both 20 and 90. Whichever end it reaches first, it must first travel there and then cross the whole range of 70 cylinders. Going down first costs 30 + 70 = 100; going up first costs 40 + 70 = 110. LOOK moving down achieves the 100.

+++ question | Write amplification
--- ask
An SSD's garbage collector always picks victim blocks that still hold 75% valid pages. About what is its write amplification?
--- choice
1.33
--- choice
1.75
--- choice correct
4
--- choice
0.25
--- why
Collecting a block with P pages, of which v = 0.75P are valid, copies v pages and frees P − v = 0.25P pages for new data. So the amplification is P / (P − v) = 1 / 0.25 = 4: every page the host writes costs four flash writes on average.

+++ question | Why SCAN cannot starve
--- ask
Why can a request never wait for ever under SCAN, even if new requests keep arriving?
--- choice
SCAN serves requests in arrival order.
--- choice correct
Within one sweep the arm reaches an edge and turns, and the next sweep crosses every cylinder, so it passes the request's cylinder within two sweeps.
--- choice
SCAN always serves the oldest request next.
--- choice
New requests are not allowed to join the queue during a sweep.
--- why
SCAN's movement does not depend on which requests arrive: it goes edge to edge. So the time until the arm passes any cylinder is bounded by about two crossings of the disk. SSTF has no such bound: a stream of nearby requests can keep the arm away from a far one for ever.

+++ practice | Seeks, rotation and transfer
--- task
Write three functions for a hard disk. Throughout, 1 MB is 10⁶ bytes and 1 KiB is 1,024 bytes; all times are in milliseconds, and all results are floats (do not round them).

- `rotation_ms(rpm)`: the time of one full revolution at `rpm` revolutions per minute.
- `access_ms(rpm, seek_ms, mb_per_s, kib)`: the average time to read `kib` KiB from a random place: the seek time, plus half a revolution, plus the transfer time at `mb_per_s` MB per second.
- `random_mb_per_s(rpm, seek_ms, mb_per_s, kib)`: the throughput, in MB per second, of reading `kib`-KiB pieces from random places one after another.

For example, `rotation_ms(7200)` is about 8.333, and `access_ms(7200, 9, 150, 4)` is about 13.194.
--- starter
def rotation_ms(rpm):
    return 0.0


def access_ms(rpm, seek_ms, mb_per_s, kib):
    return seek_ms


def random_mb_per_s(rpm, seek_ms, mb_per_s, kib):
    return float(mb_per_s)
--- solution
def rotation_ms(rpm):
    return 60_000 / rpm


def transfer_ms(mb_per_s, kib):
    return kib * 1024 / (mb_per_s * 10**6) * 1000


def access_ms(rpm, seek_ms, mb_per_s, kib):
    return seek_ms + rotation_ms(rpm) / 2 + transfer_ms(mb_per_s, kib)


def random_mb_per_s(rpm, seek_ms, mb_per_s, kib):
    bytes_per_read = kib * 1024
    reads_per_second = 1000 / access_ms(rpm, seek_ms, mb_per_s, kib)
    return bytes_per_read * reads_per_second / 10**6
--- hint
A minute is 60,000 ms, so one revolution takes `60000 / rpm` ms. The transfer time is the number of bytes divided by the bytes per millisecond.
--- hint
For the throughput, one read moves `kib * 1024` bytes and takes `access_ms(...)` milliseconds, so a second holds `1000 / access_ms(...)` reads. Multiply, then divide by 10⁶ to get MB.
--- check test | One revolution at common speeds
all(abs(rotation_ms(r) - t) < 1e-9 for r, t in [(7200, 60000 / 7200), (5400, 60000 / 5400), (15000, 4.0)])
--- check test | The example: a random 4 KiB read on a 7,200 rpm disk
abs(access_ms(7200, 9, 150, 4) - (9 + 60000 / 7200 / 2 + 4096 / 150000)) < 1e-9
--- check test | Random 4 KiB reads get about 0.31 MB/s from a disk that streams 150
abs(random_mb_per_s(7200, 9, 150, 4) - 4096 / (9 + 60000 / 14400 + 4096 / 150000) / 1000) < 1e-9 and 0.3 < random_mb_per_s(7200, 9, 150, 4) < 0.32
--- check test | Big pieces get close to the streaming rate
abs(access_ms(15000, 3.5, 250, 10240) - (3.5 + 2.0 + 10485760 / 250000)) < 1e-9 and 215 < random_mb_per_s(15000, 3.5, 250, 10240) < 225
--- check test | A read of 0 KiB still costs the seek and half a turn
abs(access_ms(5400, 12, 100, 0) - (12 + 60000 / 5400 / 2)) < 1e-9 and random_mb_per_s(5400, 12, 100, 0) == 0

+++ practice | Requests that keep arriving
--- task
Simulate a disk queue where requests arrive over time. The head starts at cylinder `head` at time 0 and moves one cylinder per millisecond; serving a request takes no extra time. `arrivals` is a list of `(time, cylinder)` pairs.

Write `simulate(policy, head, arrivals)`. Whenever the head is free, it picks one request among those that have arrived by the current time and are not yet served:

- `"FCFS"`: the one that arrived first (for equal times, the one earlier in the list);
- `"SSTF"`: the nearest to the head; on a tie, the lower cylinder; then the earlier arrival.

It then travels there (arrivals during the trip wait until it gets there). If nothing is waiting, the head stays where it is until the next arrival. Return a list with one `(cylinder, arrival_time, finish_time)` tuple per request, in the order they were served.
--- starter
def simulate(policy, head, arrivals):
    return [(c, t, t) for t, c in arrivals]
--- solution
def simulate(policy, head, arrivals):
    future = sorted(arrivals, key=lambda r: r[0])
    waiting = []
    served = []
    now, pos, i = 0, head, 0
    while i < len(future) or waiting:
        while i < len(future) and future[i][0] <= now:
            waiting.append(future[i])
            i += 1
        if not waiting:
            now = future[i][0]
            continue
        if policy == "FCFS":
            pick = waiting[0]
        else:
            pick = min(waiting, key=lambda r: (abs(r[1] - pos), r[1], r[0]))
        waiting.remove(pick)
        now += abs(pick[1] - pos)
        pos = pick[1]
        served.append((pick[1], pick[0], now))
    return served
--- hint
Keep the current time, the head's position, and a list of waiting requests. Each round, first move every request whose arrival time is at most the current time into the waiting list. If it is empty, jump the clock to the next arrival.
--- hint
Sort the arrivals by time with a stable sort (`sorted(..., key=lambda r: r[0])`), so equal times keep their list order. For SSTF, choose with `min` and a key tuple: distance, then cylinder, then arrival time.
--- check case | FCFS: each request in turn
simulate("FCFS", 0, [(0, 10), (0, 5), (3, 20)])
=> [(10, 0, 10), (5, 0, 15), (20, 3, 30)]
--- check case | SSTF: nearest first among those that have arrived
simulate("SSTF", 0, [(0, 10), (0, 5), (3, 20)])
=> [(5, 0, 5), (10, 0, 10), (20, 3, 20)]
--- check case | The head waits when nothing has arrived
simulate("SSTF", 50, [(10, 55), (100, 40)])
=> [(55, 10, 15), (40, 100, 115)]
--- check test | SSTF starves a far request while near ones keep coming; FCFS does not
(lambda arr: (simulate("SSTF", 50, arr)[-1], simulate("FCFS", 50, arr)[0]))([(0, 90)] + [(2 * k, 48 + 4 * (k % 2)) for k in range(30)]) == ((90, 0, 100), (90, 0, 40))
--- check case | SSTF ties: lower cylinder, then earlier arrival
simulate("SSTF", 50, [(0, 55), (0, 45), (0, 45)])
=> [(45, 0, 5), (45, 0, 5), (55, 0, 15)]
--- check case | Nothing to do
simulate("FCFS", 7, [])
=> []
--- check case | Arrivals given out of time order
simulate("FCFS", 0, [(5, 1), (0, 3)])
=> [(3, 0, 3), (1, 5, 7)]

+++ practice | A page cache with read-ahead
--- task
Model a page cache that reads ahead. `PageCache(capacity, window)` holds at most `capacity` pages and evicts the **least recently used** one when it needs room (as in lesson 12).

`read(p)` reads page `p` of a file:

- If `p` is cached, count a hit, make `p` the most recently used page, and return `True`.
- Otherwise it is a miss: make **one** disk request for the run of pages `p`, `p + 1`, … that stops before the first page already cached, and holds at most `min(window, capacity)` pages. Put those pages into the cache in increasing order, each as the most recently used (evicting as needed), then make `p` the most recently used page. Return `False`.

Keep three counters: `hits`, `requests` (disk requests made) and `pages_read` (pages fetched from disk in total).
--- starter
class PageCache:
    def __init__(self, capacity, window):
        self.capacity = capacity
        self.window = window
        self.cached = []
        self.hits = 0
        self.requests = 0
        self.pages_read = 0

    def read(self, p):
        if p in self.cached:
            self.hits += 1
            return True
        self.requests += 1
        self.pages_read += 1
        self.cached.append(p)
        return False
--- solution
class PageCache:
    def __init__(self, capacity, window):
        self.capacity = capacity
        self.window = window
        self.cached = {}
        self.hits = 0
        self.requests = 0
        self.pages_read = 0

    def touch(self, p):
        self.cached.pop(p, None)
        self.cached[p] = True

    def insert(self, p):
        if p not in self.cached and len(self.cached) == self.capacity:
            oldest = next(iter(self.cached))
            del self.cached[oldest]
        self.touch(p)

    def read(self, p):
        if p in self.cached:
            self.hits += 1
            self.touch(p)
            return True
        run = []
        q = p
        while q not in self.cached and len(run) < min(self.window, self.capacity):
            run.append(q)
            q += 1
        self.requests += 1
        self.pages_read += len(run)
        for q in run:
            self.insert(q)
        self.touch(p)
        return False
--- hint
A dict remembers insertion order, so it makes an LRU list: to mark a page as most recently used, delete it and insert it again; the least recently used page is `next(iter(d))`.
--- hint
On a miss, build the run first: start at `p` and keep adding the next page while it is not cached and the run is shorter than `min(window, capacity)`. That whole run is one request.
--- check test | Reading 96 pages in order: one request per 8 pages
(lambda c: ([c.read(p) for p in range(96)], c.requests, c.pages_read, c.hits)[1:] == (12, 96, 84))(PageCache(16, 8))
--- check test | Without read-ahead every page is a request
(lambda c: ([c.read(p) for p in range(20)], c.requests, c.hits)[1:] == (20, 0))(PageCache(16, 1))
--- check test | Read-ahead stops before a page that is already cached
(lambda c: (c.read(5), c.read(2), c.requests, c.pages_read, [c.read(p) for p in (2, 3, 4, 5, 6)])[2:] == (2, 7, [True, True, True, True, True]))(PageCache(10, 4))
--- check test | LRU eviction: the least recently used page goes first
(lambda c: (c.read(0), c.read(10), c.read(0), c.read(20), c.read(0), c.read(10), c.requests)[5:] == (False, 4))(PageCache(2, 1))
--- check test | A window bigger than the cache is cut down to the capacity
(lambda c: (c.read(0), c.pages_read, c.read(2), c.read(0))[1:] == (3, True, True))(PageCache(3, 8))
--- check test | The page asked for stays cached even when the run fills the cache
(lambda c: (c.read(0), c.read(4), c.read(4), c.read(0))[2:] == (True, False))(PageCache(4, 4))

+++ practice | RAID 5 parity
--- task
Write the arithmetic of RAID 5. Blocks are `bytes` objects of equal length.

- `parity(blocks)`: the bytewise XOR of a non-empty list of blocks. For one block, it is a copy of that block.
- `rebuild(stripe)`: `stripe` is the list of every block in one stripe, data and parity together, with the block from a dead disk replaced by `None`. Return the missing block. If no block is missing, return `None`; if more than one is missing, return `None` as well (RAID 5 cannot recover that).
- `new_parity(old_data, new_data, old_parity)`: the parity after one data block changes from `old_data` to `new_data`, computed **without** reading the other data blocks.
--- starter
def parity(blocks):
    return blocks[0]


def rebuild(stripe):
    return parity([b for b in stripe if b is not None])


def new_parity(old_data, new_data, old_parity):
    return new_data
--- solution
def xor(a, b):
    return bytes(x ^ y for x, y in zip(a, b))


def parity(blocks):
    result = bytes(blocks[0])
    for block in blocks[1:]:
        result = xor(result, block)
    return result


def rebuild(stripe):
    missing = [i for i, block in enumerate(stripe) if block is None]
    if len(missing) != 1:
        return None
    return parity([block for block in stripe if block is not None])


def new_parity(old_data, new_data, old_parity):
    return xor(xor(old_parity, old_data), new_data)
--- hint
XOR two blocks byte by byte with `zip`, then build `bytes` from the results. Parity folds that over the list.
--- hint
Any one block of a stripe is the XOR of all the others, parity included. For the update: XOR the old data out of the parity (XOR with it again cancels it), then XOR the new data in.
--- check case | Parity of three blocks
parity([b"\x0f\xf0", b"\xff\x00", b"\x01\x01"])
=> b"\xf1\xf1"
--- check case | Rebuilding a lost data block and a lost parity block
(rebuild([b"\x0f\xf0", None, b"\x01\x01", b"\xf1\xf1"]), rebuild([b"\x0f\xf0", b"\xff\x00", b"\x01\x01", None]))
=> (b"\xff\x00", b"\xf1\xf1")
--- check case | Two lost disks, or none, cannot be rebuilt
(rebuild([None, None, b"\x00"]), rebuild([b"a", b"a", b"\x00"]))
=> (None, None)
--- check case | One data disk: parity is a mirror
(parity([b"ORBIT"]), rebuild([None, b"ORBIT"]))
=> (b"ORBIT", b"ORBIT")
--- check test | The small-write update matches recomputing from scratch
(lambda d: new_parity(d[1], b"\x42\x42\x42", parity(d)) == parity([d[0], b"\x42\x42\x42", d[2], d[3]]))([b"\x10\x20\x30", b"\x01\x02\x03", b"\xaa\xbb\xcc", b"\x00\xff\x00"])
--- check case | Empty blocks
(parity([b"", b""]), rebuild([b"", None, b""]))
=> (b"", b"")
--- check test | parity returns bytes, and does not change its input
(lambda bs: (type(parity(bs)) is bytes, bs) == (True, [b"\x01", b"\x02"]))([b"\x01", b"\x02"])

+++ practice | Debug: LOOK loses a request
--- task
`look(head, requests, direction)` should serve disk requests with LOOK: first every request ahead (at the head's cylinder or beyond it in `direction`, `"up"` or `"down"`), nearest first, then turn and serve the rest, nearest first. It returns `(order, moved)`, the served cylinders and the total head movement.

It has a bug. A request for the cylinder the head is already on is never served: it is missing from the order, and so are its duplicates. Find the bug and fix it.
--- starter
def look(head, requests, direction):
    if direction == "up":
        ahead = sorted(c for c in requests if c > head)
        behind = sorted((c for c in requests if c < head), reverse=True)
    else:
        ahead = sorted((c for c in requests if c < head), reverse=True)
        behind = sorted(c for c in requests if c > head)
    order = ahead + behind
    moved = 0
    pos = head
    for c in order:
        moved += abs(c - pos)
        pos = c
    return order, moved
--- solution
def look(head, requests, direction):
    if direction == "up":
        ahead = sorted(c for c in requests if c >= head)
        behind = sorted((c for c in requests if c < head), reverse=True)
    else:
        ahead = sorted((c for c in requests if c <= head), reverse=True)
        behind = sorted(c for c in requests if c > head)
    order = ahead + behind
    moved = 0
    pos = head
    for c in order:
        moved += abs(c - pos)
        pos = c
    return order, moved
--- hint
Every request must land in exactly one of the two lists. Which list does a request equal to `head` land in?
--- hint
"Ahead" includes the head's own cylinder: use `>=` going up and `<=` going down.
--- check case | A request at the head, going up
look(50, [60, 50, 40], "up")
=> ([50, 60, 40], 30)
--- check case | Requests at the head, going down, with a duplicate
look(30, [30, 10, 30, 70], "down")
=> ([30, 30, 10, 70], 80)
--- check case | Nothing at the head: unchanged behaviour
look(40, [10, 70, 45, 20, 90], "up")
=> ([45, 70, 90, 20, 10], 130)
--- check case | Only a request at the head
look(8, [8], "up")
=> ([8], 0)

+++ practice | An SSD's flash translation layer
--- task
Model a flash translation layer with greedy garbage collection. `FTL(blocks, pages)` manages `blocks` erase blocks of `pages` pages each, all erased at the start. Block 0 is the first **open** block (the one being written); the other blocks are **free** (erased), kept in a list in increasing order at first.

`write(lpn)` writes logical page `lpn`:

1. Count a host write. If `lpn` is already stored somewhere, mark that physical page invalid.
2. Store `lpn` in the open block's next page (pages are filled in order) and count a flash write. Record where it is.

Before storing a page into a **full** open block, get a new open block:

- If more than one free block remains, take the first one from the free list.
- Otherwise collect garbage: among all blocks that are not in the free list (the full open block included), the **victim** is the one with the fewest valid pages (on a tie, the lowest number). Take the last free block as the new open block, erase the victim (count an erase, and append it to the end of the free list), then store the victim's valid pages, in page order, into the new open block (each is a flash write, not a host write). Then continue storing.

Also write `read(lpn)`, which returns `(block, page)` where `lpn` is stored, or `None`, and `write_amplification()`, flash writes divided by host writes. Keep the counters `host_writes`, `flash_writes` and `erases`. The checks never write more distinct logical pages than fit in `blocks - 2` blocks. The starter's `lcg(n, m)` makes a fixed pseudo-random list of `n` page numbers below `m`, for the checks; leave it as it is.
--- starter
def lcg(n, m, seed=7):
    x = seed
    out = []
    for _ in range(n):
        x = (x * 1103515245 + 12345) % 2 ** 31
        out.append((x >> 16) % m)
    return out


class FTL:
    def __init__(self, blocks, pages):
        self.host_writes = 0
        self.flash_writes = 0
        self.erases = 0

    def write(self, lpn):
        self.host_writes += 1
        self.flash_writes += 1

    def read(self, lpn):
        return None

    def write_amplification(self):
        return 1.0
--- solution
def lcg(n, m, seed=7):
    x = seed
    out = []
    for _ in range(n):
        x = (x * 1103515245 + 12345) % 2 ** 31
        out.append((x >> 16) % m)
    return out


class FTL:
    def __init__(self, blocks, pages):
        self.pages = pages
        self.valid = [[None] * pages for _ in range(blocks)]
        self.free = list(range(1, blocks))
        self.open = 0
        self.next = 0
        self.where = {}
        self.host_writes = 0
        self.flash_writes = 0
        self.erases = 0

    def live(self, b):
        return sum(lpn is not None for lpn in self.valid[b])

    def store(self, lpn):
        if self.next == self.pages:
            self.open_new()
        self.valid[self.open][self.next] = lpn
        self.where[lpn] = (self.open, self.next)
        self.next += 1
        self.flash_writes += 1

    def open_new(self):
        if len(self.free) > 1:
            self.open = self.free.pop(0)
            self.next = 0
            return
        used = [b for b in range(len(self.valid)) if b not in self.free]
        victim = min(used, key=lambda b: (self.live(b), b))
        self.open = self.free.pop()
        self.next = 0
        survivors = [lpn for lpn in self.valid[victim] if lpn is not None]
        self.valid[victim] = [None] * self.pages
        self.erases += 1
        self.free.append(victim)
        for lpn in survivors:
            self.store(lpn)

    def write(self, lpn):
        self.host_writes += 1
        if lpn in self.where:
            b, p = self.where[lpn]
            self.valid[b][p] = None
        self.store(lpn)

    def read(self, lpn):
        return self.where.get(lpn)

    def write_amplification(self):
        return self.flash_writes / self.host_writes
--- hint
Keep, for every block, a list of its pages holding the logical page number stored there or `None`, plus a dict from logical page to `(block, page)`. Invalidating a page is setting its slot to `None`; a block's valid count is how many slots are not `None`.
--- hint
Put the "get a new open block" step inside the function that stores a page, and let garbage collection store the survivors with that same function. The survivors of one victim always fit, because the victim had at least one invalid page (or the device would be full).
--- check test | Filling two blocks: pages go in order, no copies
(lambda f: ([f.write(i) for i in range(8)], f.read(0), f.read(5), f.free, f.flash_writes, f.erases)[1:] == ((0, 0), (1, 1), [2, 3], 8, 0))(FTL(4, 4))
--- check test | An overwrite invalidates the old copy and moves the page
(lambda f: (f.write(3), f.write(3), f.read(3), f.host_writes, f.flash_writes)[2:] == ((0, 1), 2, 2))(FTL(4, 4))
--- check test | Rewriting everything in order: victims are empty, amplification stays 1
(lambda f: ([f.write(i) for _ in range(4) for i in range(8)], f.host_writes, f.flash_writes, f.erases, f.write_amplification())[1:] == (32, 32, 5, 1.0))(FTL(4, 4))
--- check test | Greedy collection copies the valid pages of the emptiest block
(lambda f: ([f.write(p) for p in [0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 0, 1, 2, 8, 9]], f.erases, f.flash_writes, f.read(3), f.read(9), f.free)[1:] == (1, 14, (3, 0), (3, 1), [0]))(FTL(4, 4))
?? Before page 9 is written, block 0 holds only page 3, so it is the victim: page 3 is copied into block 3, then page 9 goes after it.
--- check test | Random overwrites on a half-full device
(lambda f: ([f.write(i) for i in range(24)], [f.write(x) for x in lcg(3000, 24)], f.host_writes, f.flash_writes, f.erases)[2:] == (3024, 3368, 414))(FTL(8, 8))
--- check test | The fuller the device, the worse the amplification
(lambda a, b: (round(a.write_amplification(), 4), round(b.write_amplification(), 4)))(*[(lambda f: ([f.write(i) for i in range(n)], [f.write(x) for x in lcg(3000, n)], f)[2])(FTL(8, 8)) for n in (32, 48)]) == (1.3377, 2.9498)
--- check test | Every logical page can still be found after all that collecting
(lambda f: ([f.write(i) for i in range(40)], [f.write(x) for x in lcg(2000, 40)], len({f.read(i) for i in range(40)}) == 40 and None not in {f.read(i) for i in range(40)}, f.read(41))[2:] == (True, None))(FTL(8, 8))

=== os-16 | Virtualisation and containers
--- teach
This whole course has been about illusions. The scheduler gives every process its own CPU; paging gives it its own memory; the file system gives it named files instead of numbered blocks. This lesson pushes the idea two steps further. A **virtual machine** gives a whole operating system the illusion of its own computer. A **container** gives a group of ordinary processes the illusion of their own operating system, while they share the real one. Both run most of the cloud, and both are built from mechanisms you already know: traps, page tables, process trees and file systems.

### What a virtual machine is

A **virtual machine** (VM) is a software copy of a computer, good enough that an unmodified operating system runs on it. The program that creates and runs VMs is the **hypervisor**, also called a virtual machine monitor (VMM). The OS inside a VM is the **guest**; the machine underneath is the **host**.

In everyday words: a flight simulator. The pilot works real controls and sees a real-looking cockpit, and nothing in front of them says "simulator". Behind the panels, a computer decides what every instrument shows.

There are two kinds of hypervisor:

- A **type 1** hypervisor runs directly on the hardware, in place of an OS: Xen, VMware ESXi, Microsoft Hyper-V. KVM turns the Linux kernel itself into one.
- A **type 2** hypervisor is a program running on an ordinary OS: VirtualBox, VMware Workstation.

### The rules a hypervisor must meet

In 1974, Gerald Popek and Robert Goldberg wrote down what a hypervisor must achieve:

1. **Equivalence.** A program in a VM behaves exactly as it would on the real machine, apart from timing and the amount of resources.
2. **Resource control.** The hypervisor stays in charge of the real hardware. A guest cannot touch memory or devices it was not given.
3. **Efficiency.** Almost all instructions run directly on the real CPU, at full speed. (An interpreter that simulates every instruction meets the first two rules, but it is 10 to 100 times too slow.)

### Trap and emulate

The obvious design follows lesson 1. Run the guest kernel in **user mode**. Its ordinary instructions (adding, loading, branching) run directly on the CPU. When it tries a privileged instruction, such as turning interrupts off or loading a new page table, the CPU traps, because it is in user mode. The trap lands in the hypervisor, which **emulates** the instruction: it changes the **virtual CPU**, a record of what the guest believes its CPU state is (its interrupt flag, its page-table register, its mode), then returns to the guest after the instruction. The guest never notices.

This is **trap and emulate**. Each trap from guest to hypervisor is called a **VM exit**, and costs on the order of a microsecond, so a good hypervisor keeps them rare.

### When trap and emulate works

Popek and Goldberg sorted instructions into two kinds:

- A **privileged** instruction traps when run in user mode (lesson 1).
- A **sensitive** instruction reads or changes the machine's control state: the mode, the interrupt flag, the memory map, the devices.

Their theorem: **a machine can be virtualised by trap and emulate if every sensitive instruction is privileged.** Here is the argument in plain steps.

- An instruction that is not sensitive neither reads nor changes control state, so it behaves the same whatever that state is. Running it directly on the real CPU gives the guest exactly the result it would get on its own machine.
- A sensitive instruction is privileged, by assumption. The guest runs in user mode, so it traps, every single time. The hypervisor therefore sees it, and carries it out on the virtual CPU instead of the real one.
- So everything the guest can observe comes either from direct execution that does not depend on control state, or from the hypervisor's faithful emulation: equivalence holds. And the guest can never change real control state without trapping first: resource control holds. Most instructions run directly: efficiency holds.

The weak link is the middle step. If a sensitive instruction does **not** trap, it runs directly, sees or changes the real state instead of the virtual one, and the illusion breaks without anyone noticing.

### x86 broke the rule

The classic x86 processor had [[17 sensitive instructions that do not trap|x86-17]] in user mode. The best-known is `POPF`, which loads the flags register. In kernel mode it can switch interrupts on or off. In user mode it silently ignores the interrupt bit: no trap, no error. A guest kernel that disables interrupts with `POPF` believes they are off, the hypervisor never hears about it, and they stay on.

Three answers followed:

- **Binary translation** (VMware, 1999): before running a block of guest kernel code, the hypervisor scans it and rewrites each problem instruction into a call to itself.
- **Paravirtualisation** (Xen, 2003): change the guest OS's source code so it calls the hypervisor directly (a **hypercall**) instead of using those instructions.
- **Hardware support** (Intel VT-x in 2005, AMD-V in 2006): a new CPU mode for guests, in which the troublesome instructions do trap. Today every mainstream hypervisor uses it.

### Virtualising memory

A guest OS manages what it thinks is physical memory with its own page tables. But that memory is really a slice of the host's. So there are two translations:

- **guest virtual → guest physical**, by the guest's page tables;
- **guest physical → host physical**, by the hypervisor's tables.

Early hypervisors built **shadow page tables**: one table mapping guest virtual pages straight to host physical pages, kept in step with every change the guest makes to its own tables. Composing two mappings is just looking up the result of one in the other:

```python
guest = {0: 7, 1: 3, 2: 9}          # guest virtual page -> guest physical page
host = {3: 40, 7: 12}               # guest physical page -> host physical page
shadow = {v: host[g] for v, g in guest.items() if g in host}
print(shadow)                       # {0: 12, 1: 40}
```

Guest page 2 is missing from the shadow table: its guest physical page 9 has no host page yet, so touching it will trap to the hypervisor.

Keeping shadows in step costs a VM exit on every guest page-table change. Modern CPUs instead walk both tables in hardware, a feature called **nested paging** (Intel's version is **EPT**, extended page tables). It costs no exits, but a TLB miss becomes expensive. Each of the guest's 4 page-table levels holds a guest physical address, and reading it needs a full 4-level host walk first, plus the read itself: 4 × (4 + 1) = 20 memory references. The final guest physical address needs one more host walk of 4. That is **24 memory references** for a single TLB miss, against 4 without virtualisation. That is why TLBs and large pages matter even more inside VMs.

### Virtualising devices

A guest's disk and network card are usually not real. The simplest way is to **emulate** a well-known device in software: the guest's driver pokes at registers, each access traps, and the hypervisor acts it out. It works with any guest, but each access is an exit. **Paravirtual devices** such as **virtio** define an interface made for VMs: the guest puts whole batches of requests in a shared ring buffer (lesson 7's bounded buffer) and notifies the hypervisor once. For full speed, a real device can be handed to a guest directly (**passthrough**, or **SR-IOV**, where one network card appears as many).

### Containers: processes with a restricted view

A VM brings a whole guest kernel. Often you want much less: run a program with its own files, its own process list and its own network, with limits on how much CPU and memory it uses, but on the host's own kernel. That is a **container**: a group of ordinary processes that the kernel shows a restricted view of the system, and holds to resource limits.

In everyday words: VMs are separate houses, each with its own plumbing and wiring. Containers are flats in one building: separate front doors and separate keys, but one foundation, one water main, and one building manager.

A container is built from three kernel features.

### Namespaces: what a process can see

A **namespace** wraps one kind of global resource so that the processes inside see their own copy. Linux has eight kinds:

| namespace | what the processes inside get |
|---|---|
| pid | their own process ids, starting from 1 |
| mount | their own tree of mounted file systems |
| net | their own network interfaces, addresses and ports |
| uts | their own host name |
| ipc | their own shared memory and message queues |
| user | their own user ids: root inside can be an ordinary user outside |
| cgroup | their own view of the cgroup tree |
| time | their own boot clock |

The rule that makes a namespace protect is simple: **a process can only act on what it can name.** A process in a pid namespace cannot even write down the pid of a process outside it, so it cannot signal or trace that process.

pid namespaces nest. A process has a pid in its own namespace **and** in every namespace above it. The first process in a new namespace gets pid 1 there, and plays init for it: if it exits, the kernel kills every other process in that namespace.

```python fragment
import os

os.unshare(os.CLONE_NEWUTS)       # Linux, as root: a private copy of the host name
os.sethostname("groundstation")   # changes it only inside the new namespace
```

### cgroups: how much a process may use

A **control group** (cgroup) is a set of processes that the kernel accounts for together and holds to limits. cgroups form a tree of folders under `/sys/fs/cgroup`, and each folder has files for its limits:

- `memory.max`: the most memory the group may use. Going over it makes the kernel reclaim memory from the group, and if that fails, kill one of its processes (the out-of-memory killer).
- `cpu.max`: a **quota** and a **period** in microseconds. `50000 100000` means the group may run for 50 ms in every 100 ms: half a CPU. Once its quota is used up, all its threads wait for the next period. This is **throttling**.
- `pids.max`: the most processes the group may have, which stops a fork bomb.

A limit applies to the whole subtree below its folder. So the effective limit of a group is the tightest one on the path from the root to it, and a child can never escape its parent's limit.

### Layered images and the overlay file system

A container's files come from an **image**: a stack of read-only **layers**. The bottom layer might be a Linux distribution; the next adds Python; the next adds the application. A thousand containers started from one image share those layers: they are stored once. Each container adds a thin writable **upper layer** of its own on top.

The kernel's **overlay file system** merges the stack into one view:

- **Lookup** goes from the top layer down; the first layer that has the path decides. `collections.ChainMap` does exactly this for dictionaries:

```python
from collections import ChainMap

base = {"/etc/motd": "hello", "/usr/bin/python3": "ELF"}
image = {"/srv/app.py": "run()", "/etc/motd": "welcome to the ground station"}
container = {}
view = ChainMap(container, image, base)
print(view["/etc/motd"])             # welcome to the ground station
print(sorted(view))                  # all three paths, merged
```

- **Copy-up.** The lower layers are shared, so they are never changed. The first time a container modifies a file that lives in a lower layer, the whole file is copied into its upper layer, and changed there. From then on the upper copy hides the lower one.
- **Whiteouts.** Deleting a file that lives in a lower layer cannot remove it from that layer. Instead, the overlay puts a [[whiteout|whiteout]] in the upper layer: a marker that means "this path does not exist". Lookup that meets a whiteout stops and reports "no such file". A file that exists only in the upper layer is simply removed.

**Why the view is right.** Treat the layers as a list of changes applied bottom to top: each entry sets a path, and each whiteout deletes it. The merged view must equal the result of applying them all in order. Lookup from the top returns the last change made to that path, which is exactly what applying them in order would leave. Copy-up keeps this true because it adds a top-most entry equal to what the path already showed, before changing it. A whiteout is a top-most "delete". And no operation ever touches a lower layer, so every other container sharing it keeps its own correct view.

### Virtual machines against containers

| | virtual machine | container |
|---|---|---|
| what is virtualised | the hardware | the OS's view: names and limits |
| kernel | its own guest kernel | the host's kernel, shared |
| start-up | seconds (boot a kernel) | milliseconds (start a process) |
| overhead | exits, nested paging | almost none: a system call is a system call |
| isolation | strong: a narrow hardware interface | weaker: the whole system-call interface |
| can run | any OS | only programs for the host's kernel |

The isolation row is the important one. A container's processes make system calls straight into the shared kernel, all 300 and more of them, so one kernel bug can let a process escape its container. A guest can reach the host only through the hypervisor's much smaller interface. That is why cloud providers put each customer's containers inside VMs, and why middle grounds exist: [[microVMs|microvm]] that boot in about a tenth of a second, and user-space kernels such as gVisor that answer a container's system calls themselves.

### Where it is used

- **The cloud.** Every virtual server you rent is a VM, usually on KVM or Xen. Serverless functions run in microVMs.
- **Software delivery.** Docker packages programs as images; Kubernetes runs millions of containers on fleets of machines. The Docker module shows the tools; this lesson showed what they ask the kernel to do.
- **Spacecraft.** Partitioning hypervisors such as XtratuM, supported by the European Space Agency, let flight software of different criticality share one computer, each application in its own partition, so a fault in a payload cannot touch attitude control.

**Watch out:**

- **A container is not a VM.** Its processes share the host kernel. Root in a container is root on the host unless a user namespace maps it to an ordinary user.
- **Sensitive is not the same as privileged.** An instruction that reads or changes control state but does not trap breaks trap and emulate silently.
- **Limits nest.** A container's `memory.max` cannot exceed its parent group's; the tightest limit on the path wins.
- **Deleting a lower file needs a whiteout.** Removing only the upper copy makes the lower file reappear.
- **CPU quotas throttle.** A service with a quota of 20 ms per 100 ms that needs 30 ms of CPU for one request takes over 100 ms to answer it, even on an idle machine.

::: context x86-17 The instructions that broke the rule
In 2000, John Robin and Cynthia Irvine listed 17 instructions of the Intel Pentium that are sensitive but not privileged. Besides `POPF`, they include `PUSHF` (it shows the real interrupt flag), `SGDT` and `SIDT` (they reveal where the real descriptor tables are), `SMSW` (it shows machine status bits), and instructions that expose the current privilege level through the code segment register. A guest kernel running any of them in user mode sees the host's state or has its change ignored, and nothing traps to tell the hypervisor.
:::

::: context whiteout Marking a file as deleted
Linux's overlay file system stores a whiteout as a character device file with device number 0/0 in the upper directory, under the deleted file's name. Container image formats store it as an empty file named with the prefix `.wh.`, such as `.wh.passwd`. A whole directory can be marked **opaque**, meaning "hide everything below me in the lower layers", which is how deleting a directory and creating a new one with the same name works. Whiteouts are why deleting a large file in a later image layer does not make the image smaller: the lower layer still holds it.
:::

::: context microvm Small virtual machines, fast
A microVM is a virtual machine with almost nothing in it: no emulated graphics card, no USB, only a few paravirtual devices for disk and network. Amazon's Firecracker, released in 2018, starts one in roughly 125 milliseconds and uses a few megabytes of memory for itself, so AWS Lambda can give every customer's function its own VM, with a VM's isolation, at nearly a container's speed. It is written in Rust and runs on top of KVM.
:::
--- task
Write an overlay file system, the way a container sees its image. Files are paths (strings such as `"/etc/hosts"`) with string contents; directories are implied by the paths. The starter defines `WHITEOUT`, the marker stored in a layer to mean "this path is deleted".

Write a class `Overlay`:

- `Overlay(lowers)`: `lowers` is the list of read-only image layers, **bottom first**. Each layer is a dict from path to contents or `WHITEOUT` (a lower layer may delete a file of the layers beneath it). Never change any lower layer. The container's own writable layer is the dict `upper`, empty at first. Keep a counter `copied_up`, starting at 0.
- `read(path)`: look in `upper` first, then the lower layers from the top down. The first layer that holds the path decides: its contents, or `None` if it holds `WHITEOUT`. `None` if no layer holds it.
- `write(path, data)`: the file's contents become `data` (a new file if it did not exist). This goes into `upper` and copies nothing.
- `append(path, data)`: add `data` to the end of an existing file. If the file is visible only from a lower layer, first **copy it up**: put its contents into `upper` and add their length to `copied_up`. Return 0, or `"ENOENT"` if the file does not exist.
- `delete(path)`: `"ENOENT"` if the file does not exist. Otherwise, if deleting only the upper copy would uncover a file in a lower layer, store `WHITEOUT` in `upper`; if not, just remove the path from `upper`. Return 0.
- `listdir(directory)`: the sorted list of names directly inside `directory` (for example `"/etc"` or `"/"`) in the merged view: a name counts if some visible file's path lies under it. Deleted files do not count.
--- starter
WHITEOUT = ("whiteout",)


class Overlay:
    def __init__(self, lowers):
        self.lowers = lowers
        self.upper = {}
        self.copied_up = 0

    def read(self, path):
        raise NotImplementedError("look the path up, top layer first")

    def write(self, path, data):
        raise NotImplementedError

    def append(self, path, data):
        raise NotImplementedError

    def delete(self, path):
        raise NotImplementedError

    def listdir(self, directory):
        raise NotImplementedError
--- solution
WHITEOUT = ("whiteout",)


class Overlay:
    def __init__(self, lowers):
        self.lowers = lowers
        self.upper = {}
        self.copied_up = 0

    def layers(self):
        return [self.upper] + self.lowers[::-1]

    def read(self, path):
        for layer in self.layers():
            if path in layer:
                value = layer[path]
                return None if value == WHITEOUT else value
        return None

    def in_lower(self, path):
        for layer in self.lowers[::-1]:
            if path in layer:
                return layer[path] != WHITEOUT
        return False

    def write(self, path, data):
        self.upper[path] = data

    def append(self, path, data):
        current = self.read(path)
        if current is None:
            return "ENOENT"
        if path not in self.upper:
            self.copied_up += len(current)
            self.upper[path] = current
        self.upper[path] += data
        return 0

    def delete(self, path):
        if self.read(path) is None:
            return "ENOENT"
        if self.in_lower(path):
            self.upper[path] = WHITEOUT
        else:
            del self.upper[path]
        return 0

    def listdir(self, directory):
        prefix = directory.rstrip("/") + "/"
        names = set()
        seen = set()
        for layer in self.layers():
            for path in layer:
                if path in seen:
                    continue
                seen.add(path)
                if layer[path] != WHITEOUT and path.startswith(prefix):
                    names.add(path[len(prefix):].split("/")[0])
        return sorted(names)
--- hint
Make one list of layers in lookup order: `upper` first, then the lower layers reversed (they are given bottom first). `read` walks it and stops at the first layer that has the path.
--- hint
For `delete`, you need to know what would show through if the upper layer had no entry for this path: walk only the lower layers, top down, and see whether the first one that has the path holds a real file or a whiteout. A real file there means you must write `WHITEOUT` into `upper`.
--- hint
For `listdir`, walk the layers in lookup order and remember which paths you have already decided, so a lower layer cannot bring back a path a higher layer deleted or replaced. For each visible path under `directory.rstrip("/") + "/"`, keep the first part of the rest of the path.
--- check test | Lookup goes from the top layer down
(lambda o: (o.read("/etc/os"), o.read("/bin/sh"), o.read("/app/main.py"), o.read("/nope")) == ("debian+app", "ELF", "print(1)", None))(Overlay([{"/etc/hosts": "localhost\n", "/bin/sh": "ELF", "/etc/os": "debian"}, {"/app/main.py": "print(1)", "/etc/os": "debian+app"}]))

(lambda base, o: (o.append("/etc/hosts", "db\n"), o.copied_up, o.append("/etc/hosts", "cache\n"), o.copied_up, o.read("/etc/hosts"), base["/etc/hosts"]) == (0, 10, 0, 10, "localhost\ndb\ncache\n", "localhost\n"))(*(lambda b: (b, Overlay([b])))({"/etc/hosts": "localhost\n"}))
