(lambda o: (o.delete("/opt/tool"), o.listdir("/"), o.listdir("/opt")) == (0, ["etc"], []))(Overlay([{"/opt/tool": "ELF", "/etc/os": "x"}]))
--- check test | "/etc" does not list "/etcetera"
Overlay([{"/etc/os": "x", "/etcetera/y": "z"}]).listdir("/etc") == ["os"]
--- check test | The overlay matches a plain dict over a long mixed run
(lambda lowers, ref, paths: (lambda o: all([(o.write(p, d), ref.__setitem__(p, d)) if k == "w" else ((o.delete(p), ref.pop(p, None)) if k == "d" else ((o.append(p, d), ref.__setitem__(p, ref[p] + d)) if p in ref else (o.append(p, d),))) for k, p, d in [("wda"[(i * 7) % 3], paths[(i * 5 + i // 3) % len(paths)], str(i)) for i in range(60)]]) and all(o.read(p) == ref.get(p) for p in paths) and all(l == c for l, c in zip(lowers, [{"/a": "A", "/b/c": "C", "/d": "D"}, {"/a": "A2", "/d": WHITEOUT, "/e": "E"}])) and o.listdir("/") == sorted({p.split("/")[1] for p in ref}))(Overlay(lowers)))([{"/a": "A", "/b/c": "C", "/d": "D"}, {"/a": "A2", "/d": WHITEOUT, "/e": "E"}], {"/a": "A2", "/b/c": "C", "/e": "E"}, ["/a", "/b/c", "/d", "/e", "/f"])

+++ question | Where trap and emulate fails
--- ask
A guest kernel runs in user mode under a trap-and-emulate hypervisor on classic x86. It executes `POPF` to turn interrupts off. What happens?
--- choice
The CPU traps, and the hypervisor turns off the guest's virtual interrupt flag.
--- choice correct
Nothing traps; in user mode `POPF` silently ignores the interrupt bit, so the guest believes interrupts are off while they stay on.
--- choice
The CPU raises an illegal-instruction fault and the guest crashes.
--- choice
Interrupts are turned off on the real CPU, freezing the host.
--- why
`POPF` is sensitive (it can change the interrupt flag) but not privileged (it does not trap in user mode). Popek and Goldberg's condition, "every sensitive instruction is privileged", fails, so the hypervisor never sees the instruction. Binary translation, paravirtualisation or VT-x were needed to fix it.

+++ question | A TLB miss inside a VM
--- ask
With nested paging, the guest and the host both use 4-level page tables. How many memory references can one TLB miss take in the worst case? Type a whole number.
--- answer
24
--- why
Each of the 4 guest page-table entries is at a guest physical address, which needs a 4-level host walk before it can be read: 4 × (4 + 1) = 20 references. The guest physical address of the data then needs one more host walk of 4: 24 in all.

+++ question | Two containers, one kernel
--- ask
Why is a container's isolation usually considered weaker than a virtual machine's?
--- choice
Containers cannot have memory limits.
--- choice
Containers run their processes in kernel mode.
--- choice correct
Its processes call the shared host kernel directly through hundreds of system calls, so one kernel bug can break every container on the machine.
--- choice
Namespaces let any process see every other process on the host.
--- why
Namespaces and cgroups restrict what a process can name and how much it can use, but the kernel doing the restricting is shared, and its whole system-call interface is exposed. A VM's guest reaches the host only through the hypervisor's much narrower interface. Containers do have memory limits (cgroups), and their processes run in user mode.

+++ practice | Throttled by a CPU quota
--- task
A cgroup's `cpu.max` gives a quota and a period in microseconds: in every period, starting at time 0, the group may run for at most `quota` microseconds; then it waits for the next period.

A single-threaded task in that group needs `work` microseconds of CPU and runs whenever it is allowed to. Write `finish_time(quota, period, work)`: the time at which it finishes. `quota` may be `None`, meaning no limit. A quota of at least `period` never throttles a single thread. `work` of 0 finishes at 0.

Then write `throttled_time(quota, period, work)`: how long the task spent waiting because of the quota, which is `finish_time` minus `work`.

For example, `finish_time(50000, 100000, 120000)` is 220000: 50,000 µs in the first period, 50,000 in the second, and the last 20,000 at the start of the third.
--- starter
def finish_time(quota, period, work):
    return work


def throttled_time(quota, period, work):
    return 0
--- solution
def finish_time(quota, period, work):
    if work == 0:
        return 0
    if quota is None or quota >= period:
        return work
    full, rest = divmod(work, quota)
    if rest == 0:
        return (full - 1) * period + quota
    return full * period + rest


def throttled_time(quota, period, work):
    return finish_time(quota, period, work) - work
--- hint
Count how many whole quotas the work fills with `divmod(work, quota)`. Each whole quota except the last costs a full period of wall-clock time.
--- hint
If the work is an exact multiple of the quota, the task finishes `quota` microseconds into its last period, not at the end of it. Otherwise the leftover runs at the start of one more period.
--- check case | The example from the task
finish_time(50000, 100000, 120000)
=> 220000
--- check case | Work that fits in the first quota is never throttled
(finish_time(20000, 100000, 15000), throttled_time(20000, 100000, 15000))
=> (15000, 0)
--- check case | An exact multiple ends inside the last period
(finish_time(50000, 100000, 100000), finish_time(25000, 100000, 100000))
=> (150000, 325000)
--- check case | A request needing 30 ms under a 20 ms quota takes over 100 ms
(finish_time(20000, 100000, 30000), throttled_time(20000, 100000, 30000))
=> (110000, 80000)
--- check case | No limit, or a quota of a whole CPU or more
(finish_time(None, 100000, 777), finish_time(100000, 100000, 350000), finish_time(200000, 100000, 350000))
=> (777, 350000, 350000)
--- check case | No work
(finish_time(10, 100, 0), throttled_time(10, 100, 0))
=> (0, 0)

+++ practice | Memory limits that nest
--- task
cgroups form a tree of paths: `"/"` is the root, `"/a"` its child, `"/a/b"` a grandchild. Memory used by a group is charged to it **and** to every group above it.

Write a class `MemCgroups`. `MemCgroups(limits)` gets a dict from path to limit in bytes; a path that is not in it, or has `None`, has no limit of its own. Every group starts with 0 bytes used. Methods:

- `chain(path)`: the list of paths from `path` up to the root, `path` first. For example `chain("/a/b")` is `["/a/b", "/a", "/"]`.
- `effective_limit(path)`: the smallest limit on that chain, or `None` if none has a limit.
- `charge(path, n)`: charge `n` bytes to `path`. If that would put any group on the chain above its limit, charge nothing and return the path of the **nearest** such group (the first one in `chain(path)`). Otherwise add `n` to every group on the chain and return `None`.
- `uncharge(path, n)`: take `n` bytes off every group on the chain.
- `usage(path)`: the bytes charged to that group (its own and all its descendants').
--- starter
class MemCgroups:
    def __init__(self, limits):
        self.limits = limits
        self.used = {}

    def chain(self, path):
        return [path]

    def effective_limit(self, path):
        return self.limits.get(path)

    def charge(self, path, n):
        self.used[path] = self.used.get(path, 0) + n
        return None

    def uncharge(self, path, n):
        self.used[path] -= n

    def usage(self, path):
        return self.used.get(path, 0)
--- solution
class MemCgroups:
    def __init__(self, limits):
        self.limits = limits
        self.used = {}

    def chain(self, path):
        out = [path]
        while path != "/":
            path = path.rsplit("/", 1)[0] or "/"
            out.append(path)
        return out

    def effective_limit(self, path):
        limits = [self.limits[p] for p in self.chain(path) if self.limits.get(p) is not None]
        return min(limits) if limits else None

    def charge(self, path, n):
        for p in self.chain(path):
            limit = self.limits.get(p)
            if limit is not None and self.usage(p) + n > limit:
                return p
        for p in self.chain(path):
            self.used[p] = self.usage(p) + n
        return None

    def uncharge(self, path, n):
        for p in self.chain(path):
            self.used[p] = self.usage(p) - n

    def usage(self, path):
        return self.used.get(path, 0)
--- hint
The parent of `"/a/b"` is everything before its last slash, `"/a"`; the parent of `"/a"` is the empty string, which stands for the root `"/"`. `path.rsplit("/", 1)[0]` gives that part.
--- hint
`charge` works in two passes: first check every group on the chain without changing anything, returning the first one that would go over; only if none would, add the bytes to all of them.
--- check case | The chain up to the root
(MemCgroups({}).chain("/a/b/c"), MemCgroups({}).chain("/x"), MemCgroups({}).chain("/"))
=> (["/a/b/c", "/a/b", "/a", "/"], ["/x", "/"], ["/"])
--- check case | The tightest limit on the path wins
(lambda m: (m.effective_limit("/web/api"), m.effective_limit("/web"), m.effective_limit("/batch"), m.effective_limit("/")))(MemCgroups({"/web": 800, "/web/api": 1000, "/": 5000, "/batch": None}))
=> (800, 800, 5000, 5000)
--- check test | Charges add up the tree
(lambda m: (m.charge("/web/api", 300), m.charge("/web/ui", 200), m.usage("/web"), m.usage("/"), m.usage("/web/api")) == (None, None, 500, 500, 300))(MemCgroups({"/web": 800}))
--- check test | The parent's limit stops a child that is still under its own
(lambda m: (m.charge("/web/api", 600), m.charge("/web/ui", 300), m.usage("/web/ui"), m.usage("/web")) == (None, "/web", 0, 600))(MemCgroups({"/web": 800, "/web/ui": 1000}))
--- check test | The nearest group over its limit is the one reported
(lambda m: m.charge("/a/b", 150) == "/a/b")(MemCgroups({"/a": 100, "/a/b": 120}))
--- check test | Reaching a limit exactly is allowed; one byte more is not
(lambda m: (m.charge("/j", 64), m.charge("/j", 1), m.uncharge("/j", 4), m.charge("/j", 4), m.usage("/")) == (None, "/j", None, None, 64))(MemCgroups({"/j": 64}))
--- check test | No limits anywhere
(lambda m: (m.effective_limit("/q/r"), m.charge("/q/r", 10 ** 9), m.usage("/q")) == (None, None, 10 ** 9))(MemCgroups({}))

+++ practice | Process ids in nested namespaces
--- task
Model pid namespaces together with the process tree of lesson 2. Write a class `PidNamespaces`:

- A new object has one namespace, number 0, and one process, init, with global pid 1. Namespace 0 is the host's: a process's pid there is its **global pid**.
- `spawn(parent, new_ns=False)`: `parent` is a global pid. Make a child process. With `new_ns` false, the child lives in its parent's namespace; with it true, the child is the first process of a brand-new namespace (numbered 1, 2, … in order of creation) whose parent namespace is the parent process's. The child gets a pid in its own namespace **and in every namespace above it**: in each, the next unused number there (a new namespace starts from 1; namespace 0 continues from 2). Return the child's global pid.
- `pid_in(gpid, ns)`: the pid of process `gpid` as seen from namespace `ns`, or `None` if the process is not visible there (it is visible only in its own namespace and the ones above it).
- `getpid(gpid)`: its pid in its own namespace.
- `getppid(gpid)`: its parent's pid as seen from the child's own namespace, or 0 if the parent is not visible there (as for the first process of a new namespace). Init's is 0.
- `exit(gpid)`: the process ends. If it was pid 1 of a namespace other than 0, every process in that namespace and in the namespaces below it ends too. Return the sorted list of global pids that ended.
--- starter
class PidNamespaces:
    def __init__(self):
        self.next = 2

    def spawn(self, parent, new_ns=False):
        self.next += 1
        return self.next - 1

    def pid_in(self, gpid, ns):
        return gpid

    def getpid(self, gpid):
        return gpid

    def getppid(self, gpid):
        return 0

    def exit(self, gpid):
        return [gpid]
--- solution
class PidNamespaces:
    def __init__(self):
        self.ns_parent = {0: None}
        self.next_pid = {0: 2}
        self.procs = {1: {"ns": 0, "parent": None, "ids": {0: 1}}}

    def ancestors(self, ns):
        out = []
        while ns is not None:
            out.append(ns)
            ns = self.ns_parent[ns]
        return out

    def spawn(self, parent, new_ns=False):
        ns = self.procs[parent]["ns"]
        if new_ns:
            child_ns = len(self.ns_parent)
            self.ns_parent[child_ns] = ns
            self.next_pid[child_ns] = 1
            ns = child_ns
        ids = {}
        for level in self.ancestors(ns):
            ids[level] = self.next_pid[level]
            self.next_pid[level] += 1
        self.procs[ids[0]] = {"ns": ns, "parent": parent, "ids": ids}
        return ids[0]

    def pid_in(self, gpid, ns):
        return self.procs[gpid]["ids"].get(ns)

    def getpid(self, gpid):
        return self.pid_in(gpid, self.procs[gpid]["ns"])

    def getppid(self, gpid):
        parent = self.procs[gpid]["parent"]
        if parent is None:
            return 0
        return self.pid_in(parent, self.procs[gpid]["ns"]) or 0

    def exit(self, gpid):
        proc = self.procs[gpid]
        ended = [gpid]
        if proc["ns"] != 0 and self.getpid(gpid) == 1:
            ended = [g for g, p in self.procs.items() if proc["ns"] in self.ancestors(p["ns"])]
        for g in ended:
            del self.procs[g]
        return sorted(ended)
--- hint
Store, for every process, its namespace, its parent's global pid, and a dict from namespace number to its pid there. A namespace's ancestors are found by following parent links up to namespace 0.
--- hint
`pid_in` is then a single dict lookup: the process has an entry exactly for its own namespace and those above. For `exit` of a namespace's pid 1, the victims are the processes whose namespace has that namespace among its ancestors (itself included).
--- check test | Init, and a child in the same namespace
(lambda n: (n.spawn(1), n.getpid(2), n.getppid(2), n.getppid(1), n.pid_in(2, 0)) == (2, 2, 1, 0, 2))(PidNamespaces())
--- check test | The first process of a new namespace is pid 1 there
(lambda n: (n.spawn(1), n.spawn(2, True), n.getpid(3), n.pid_in(3, 0), n.pid_in(3, 1), n.getppid(3)) == (2, 3, 1, 3, 1, 0))(PidNamespaces())
--- check test | Inside the namespace, numbers count from 1; outside, globally
(lambda n: (n.spawn(1, True), n.spawn(2), n.spawn(3), n.spawn(1), [n.getpid(g) for g in (2, 3, 4, 5)], n.getppid(4)) == (2, 3, 4, 5, [1, 2, 3, 5], 2))(PidNamespaces())
--- check test | A process outside a namespace is invisible from inside it
(lambda n: (n.spawn(1), n.spawn(1, True), n.pid_in(2, 1), n.pid_in(1, 1), n.pid_in(3, 1)) == (2, 3, None, None, 1))(PidNamespaces())
--- check test | Nested namespaces: one pid per level
(lambda n: (n.spawn(1, True), n.spawn(2, True), n.spawn(3), [n.pid_in(4, k) for k in (0, 1, 2)], n.pid_in(2, 2)) == (2, 3, 4, [4, 3, 2], None))(PidNamespaces())
--- check test | When a namespace's init exits, everything in it and below it ends
(lambda n: (n.spawn(1), n.spawn(2, True), n.spawn(3), n.spawn(4, True), n.spawn(5), n.exit(3), n.pid_in(2, 0)) == (2, 3, 4, 5, 6, [3, 4, 5, 6], 2))(PidNamespaces())
--- check test | Other processes end alone, even pid 1's children
(lambda n: (n.spawn(1, True), n.spawn(2), n.exit(3), n.getpid(2), n.exit(2)) == (2, 3, [3], 1, [2]))(PidNamespaces())

+++ practice | Two translations for every address
--- task
Inside a virtual machine, an address goes through two page tables. Model both with one level each. Pages are `page` bytes.

- `guest_pt` maps guest virtual page numbers to guest physical page numbers (the guest OS's table).
- `host_pt` maps guest physical page numbers to host physical page numbers (the hypervisor's table).

Write `translate(gva, guest_pt, host_pt, page)`: split the guest virtual address into page number and offset, translate the page through both tables, and return the host physical address. If the guest table has no entry, return `"guest page fault"` (the guest OS handles it); if the host table has no entry for the guest physical page, return `"EPT violation"` (the hypervisor handles it).

Then write `walk_refs(n, m)`: the worst-case number of memory references for one TLB miss with nested paging, when the guest's table has `n` levels and the host's has `m`. Each guest level's entry is at a guest physical address that needs a full host walk before it can be read, and so does the final guest physical address. With no guest levels (`n = 0`) the answer is just `m`.
--- starter
def translate(gva, guest_pt, host_pt, page):
    return gva


def walk_refs(n, m):
    return n + m
--- solution
def translate(gva, guest_pt, host_pt, page):
    vpn, offset = divmod(gva, page)
    if vpn not in guest_pt:
        return "guest page fault"
    gpn = guest_pt[vpn]
    if gpn not in host_pt:
        return "EPT violation"
    return host_pt[gpn] * page + offset


def walk_refs(n, m):
    return n * (m + 1) + m
--- hint
`divmod(gva, page)` gives the virtual page number and the offset. The offset passes through both translations unchanged.
--- hint
Each of the `n` guest levels costs a host walk of `m` references plus the read of the guest entry itself; then the final address costs one more walk of `m`. Check your formula against 4 and 4, which should give 24.
--- check case | An address translated through both tables
translate(4096 * 2 + 17, {2: 5}, {5: 90}, 4096)
=> 90 * 4096 + 17
--- check case | The first and last byte of a page
(translate(0, {0: 1}, {1: 3}, 64), translate(63, {0: 1}, {1: 3}, 64), translate(64, {0: 1, 1: 2}, {1: 3, 2: 8}, 64))
=> (192, 255, 512)
--- check case | A missing guest entry is the guest's fault; a missing host entry is the hypervisor's
(translate(4096 * 3, {2: 5}, {5: 90}, 4096), translate(4096 * 2, {2: 6}, {5: 90}, 4096))
=> ("guest page fault", "EPT violation")
--- check case | The guest fault wins when both entries are missing
translate(100, {}, {}, 64)
=> "guest page fault"
--- check case | Walk lengths
[walk_refs(n, m) for n, m in [(4, 4), (0, 4), (4, 0), (2, 3), (5, 5), (1, 1)]]
=> [24, 4, 4, 11, 35, 3]

+++ practice | Debug: the deleted file comes back
--- task
`Layers` is a two-layer overlay: `lower` is a read-only image layer (a dict from path to contents) and `upper` the container's writable layer. `read(path)` looks in `upper` first, then `lower`; `WHITEOUT` in `upper` means the file is deleted. `delete(path)` should make the file unreadable: return `"ENOENT"` if it is not visible, otherwise remove it and return 0, never changing `lower`.

It has a bug. If the container has changed a file (so it is in both layers) and then deletes it, the image's old version shows through again. Find the bug and fix it.
--- starter
WHITEOUT = ("whiteout",)


class Layers:
    def __init__(self, lower):
        self.lower = lower
        self.upper = {}

    def read(self, path):
        if path in self.upper:
            value = self.upper[path]
            return None if value == WHITEOUT else value
        return self.lower.get(path)

    def write(self, path, data):
        self.upper[path] = data

    def delete(self, path):
        if self.read(path) is None:
            return "ENOENT"
        if path in self.upper:
            del self.upper[path]
        else:
            self.upper[path] = WHITEOUT
        return 0
--- solution
WHITEOUT = ("whiteout",)


class Layers:
    def __init__(self, lower):
        self.lower = lower
        self.upper = {}

    def read(self, path):
        if path in self.upper:
            value = self.upper[path]
            return None if value == WHITEOUT else value
        return self.lower.get(path)

    def write(self, path, data):
        self.upper[path] = data

    def delete(self, path):
        if self.read(path) is None:
            return "ENOENT"
        if path not in self.lower:
            del self.upper[path]
        else:
            self.upper[path] = WHITEOUT
        return 0
--- hint
After `del self.upper[path]`, what does `read(path)` return if the path is also in `lower`?
--- hint
Whether a whiteout is needed depends on the lower layer, not the upper one: remove the upper entry only when the lower layer does not have the path.
--- check test | A changed-then-deleted file stays deleted
(lambda l: (l.write("/etc/os", "custom"), l.delete("/etc/os"), l.read("/etc/os"), l.lower) == (None, 0, None, {"/etc/os": "debian"}))(Layers({"/etc/os": "debian"}))
--- check test | Deleting an untouched lower file writes a whiteout
(lambda l: (l.delete("/bin/sh"), l.read("/bin/sh"), l.upper) == (0, None, {"/bin/sh": WHITEOUT}))(Layers({"/bin/sh": "ELF"}))
--- check test | A file only in the upper layer is simply removed
(lambda l: (l.write("/tmp/x", "1"), l.delete("/tmp/x"), l.upper, l.read("/tmp/x")) == (None, 0, {}, None))(Layers({}))
--- check test | Deleting twice, or deleting nothing, is ENOENT
(lambda l: (l.delete("/a"), l.delete("/a"), l.delete("/zzz")) == (0, "ENOENT", "ENOENT"))(Layers({"/a": "A"}))

+++ practice | Trap, emulate and translate
--- task
Model why classic x86 could not be virtualised by trap and emulate, and how binary translation fixed it. The starter gives `new_state()`, a CPU state `{"regs": {}, "if": 1, "ptbr": 0, "out": []}`, and `apply(state, ins, mode)`, which carries out one instruction on a state as a CPU in `mode` (`"kernel"` or `"user"`) would. Instructions are tuples:

- `("add", r, v)` adds `v` to register `r`; `("cli",)` and `("sti",)` set the interrupt flag to 0 and 1; `("load_ptbr", v)` sets the page-table base; `("out", port, v)` sends `v` to a device;
- `("popf", v)` sets the interrupt flag to `v`, **but in user mode it silently does nothing**; `("read_mode",)` puts the current mode into register `"m"`.

`PRIVILEGED` is `{"cli", "sti", "load_ptbr", "out"}`: these trap in user mode. `SENSITIVE` adds `"popf"` and `"read_mode"`. Leave all of that as it is and write:

1. `run_native(program)`: run a guest kernel on bare hardware, in kernel mode, from `new_state()`. Return the final state.
2. `run_trap_and_emulate(program)`: the real CPU runs the guest in user mode. A privileged instruction traps: count one exit, and the hypervisor applies it to the guest's virtual state as kernel mode would. Every other instruction runs directly, as user mode. Return `(state, exits)`.
3. `run_binary_translation(program)`: the same, but the hypervisor first rewrites every **sensitive** instruction into a trap, so each of those is an exit emulated as kernel mode would, and only the rest run directly. Return `(state, exits)`.
4. `virtualisable(isa)`: `isa` maps each instruction name to a set of tags, which may include `"sensitive"` and `"privileged"`. Return `True` exactly when every sensitive instruction is also privileged (the Popek and Goldberg condition).
--- starter
PRIVILEGED = {"cli", "sti", "load_ptbr", "out"}
SENSITIVE = PRIVILEGED | {"popf", "read_mode"}


def new_state():
    return {"regs": {}, "if": 1, "ptbr": 0, "out": []}


def apply(state, ins, mode):
    op = ins[0]
    if op == "add":
        state["regs"][ins[1]] = state["regs"].get(ins[1], 0) + ins[2]
    elif op == "cli":
        state["if"] = 0
    elif op == "sti":
        state["if"] = 1
    elif op == "load_ptbr":
        state["ptbr"] = ins[1]
    elif op == "out":
        state["out"].append((ins[1], ins[2]))
    elif op == "popf":
        if mode == "kernel":
            state["if"] = ins[1]
    elif op == "read_mode":
        state["regs"]["m"] = mode


def run_native(program):
    return new_state()


def run_trap_and_emulate(program):
    return new_state(), 0


def run_binary_translation(program):
    return new_state(), 0


def virtualisable(isa):
    return True
--- solution
PRIVILEGED = {"cli", "sti", "load_ptbr", "out"}
SENSITIVE = PRIVILEGED | {"popf", "read_mode"}


def new_state():
    return {"regs": {}, "if": 1, "ptbr": 0, "out": []}


def apply(state, ins, mode):
    op = ins[0]
    if op == "add":
        state["regs"][ins[1]] = state["regs"].get(ins[1], 0) + ins[2]
    elif op == "cli":
        state["if"] = 0
    elif op == "sti":
        state["if"] = 1
    elif op == "load_ptbr":
        state["ptbr"] = ins[1]
    elif op == "out":
        state["out"].append((ins[1], ins[2]))
    elif op == "popf":
        if mode == "kernel":
            state["if"] = ins[1]
    elif op == "read_mode":
        state["regs"]["m"] = mode


def run_native(program):
    state = new_state()
    for ins in program:
        apply(state, ins, "kernel")
    return state


def run_virtual(program, trapping):
    state = new_state()
    exits = 0
    for ins in program:
        if ins[0] in trapping:
            exits += 1
            apply(state, ins, "kernel")
        else:
            apply(state, ins, "user")
    return state, exits


def run_trap_and_emulate(program):
    return run_virtual(program, PRIVILEGED)


def run_binary_translation(program):
    return run_virtual(program, SENSITIVE)


def virtualisable(isa):
    return all("privileged" in tags for tags in isa.values() if "sensitive" in tags)
--- hint
Both hypervisors are the same loop with a different set of instructions that trap: for each instruction, either count an exit and apply it as kernel mode (the hypervisor emulating it), or apply it as user mode (the real CPU running it directly).
--- hint
`virtualisable` is one `all(...)` over the tag sets: every set that contains `"sensitive"` must also contain `"privileged"`.
--- check test | Innocuous and privileged code: trap and emulate matches bare hardware
(lambda p: (run_trap_and_emulate(p)[0] == run_native(p), run_trap_and_emulate(p)[1]))([("add", "a", 5), ("cli",), ("load_ptbr", 0x80), ("out", 3, "ping"), ("add", "a", 1), ("sti",)]) == (True, 4)
--- check test | popf breaks trap and emulate silently
(lambda p: (run_native(p)["if"], run_trap_and_emulate(p)[0]["if"], run_trap_and_emulate(p)[1]))([("popf", 0), ("add", "x", 1)]) == (0, 1, 0)
--- check test | read_mode lets the guest kernel see it is really in user mode
(run_native([("read_mode",)])["regs"], run_trap_and_emulate([("read_mode",)])[0]["regs"]) == ({"m": "kernel"}, {"m": "user"})
--- check test | Binary translation restores equivalence, at the cost of more exits
(lambda p: (run_binary_translation(p)[0] == run_native(p), run_binary_translation(p)[1], run_trap_and_emulate(p)[1]))([("add", "a", 2), ("popf", 0), ("read_mode",), ("out", 1, 7), ("popf", 1), ("add", "a", 3)]) == (True, 4, 1)
--- check test | An empty program, and one with no sensitive instructions at all
(run_native([]), run_trap_and_emulate([]), run_binary_translation([("add", "r", 9)])) == (new_state(), (new_state(), 0), ({"regs": {"r": 9}, "if": 1, "ptbr": 0, "out": []}, 0))
--- check case | The Popek and Goldberg condition
(virtualisable({"add": set(), "cli": {"sensitive", "privileged"}, "hlt": {"privileged"}}), virtualisable({"add": set(), "popf": {"sensitive"}, "cli": {"sensitive", "privileged"}}), virtualisable({}))
=> (True, False, True)

=== os-17 | Real threads in C++: std::thread, std::mutex and std::condition_variable
--- teach
Since lesson 5, every thread in this course has been a Python generator, and every lock and condition variable a request to a simulated kernel. That let you try every schedule, which no real program can do. This lesson crosses over to real threads, in C++: how to start them, how to protect shared data with `std::mutex`, how to make them sleep and wake with `std::condition_variable`, and what the C++ memory model promises. The finished product is the workhorse of concurrent programs: a **thread-safe bounded queue**. You will write it against the same simulated kernel as lessons 7 and 8, so the explorer can prove it correct in every schedule, and then you will know exactly what the C++ version must do.

ORBIT runs C++ as WebAssembly on a single thread, so the C++ in this lesson is shown, not run. Everything it does maps one to one onto the model you already have.

### Starting a thread

`std::thread`, from the header `<thread>`, starts a new operating-system thread through the system's [[thread library|stdthread]] (on Linux, a `clone` system call that shares the address space, lesson 5). The constructor takes a function and its arguments, and the new thread starts running at once:

```cpp fragment
#include <cstdio>
#include <thread>
#include <vector>

void count_frames(int id, int frames) {
    std::printf("receiver %d: %d frames\n", id, frames);
}

int main() {
    std::vector<std::thread> workers;
    for (int id = 0; id < 4; id++)
        workers.emplace_back(count_frames, id, 100 * id);   // starts running now
    for (std::thread& t : workers)
        t.join();                                            // wait for each to finish
}
```

The four lines can come out in any order, and each run may differ: the scheduler decides.

- **`join()`** waits until the thread has finished: the `wait` of lesson 2, for threads.
- **`detach()`** lets it run on alone; nobody can wait for it any more.
- A `std::thread` object that is still **joinable** (started, and neither joined nor detached) when it is destroyed calls `std::terminate`, which ends the whole program. C++20's **`std::jthread`** joins automatically in its destructor, and can ask its thread to stop through a [[stop token|stoptoken]].
- Arguments are **copied** into the new thread. To pass a reference, wrap it in `std::ref(x)`, and make sure `x` outlives the thread, or it reads a dangling reference (the Systems Programming bug, now with threads).

A lambda is often simpler than a named function. `[&]` captures the surrounding variables by reference:

```cpp fragment
int received = 0;
std::thread radio([&] { received = 42; });
radio.join();              // after join, reading received is safe
```

### Data races are undefined behaviour

Lesson 5 showed `hits++` losing updates. In C++ it is worse than a wrong number. A **data race** is two threads accessing the same memory location at the same time, at least one of them writing, with nothing ordering the two accesses. The C++ standard says a program with a data race has **undefined behaviour**: the compiler may assume races never happen. It may keep a variable in a register for a whole loop, so a thread spinning on `while (!done)` never sees another thread set `done`, and spins for ever.

What orders two accesses is the **happens-before** relation. Within one thread, earlier statements happen before later ones. Between threads, the main sources are:

- starting a thread happens before its first statement, and its last statement happens before the `join()` that waits for it returns (that is why `received` above is safe to read);
- unlocking a mutex happens before the next thread's lock of that mutex succeeds;
- an atomic store with release order happens before an atomic load with acquire order that reads it.

If every pair of conflicting accesses is ordered by happens-before, there is no data race, and the program behaves as if the threads' steps were interleaved in some order: the interleaving model of lesson 5 is exactly right.

### std::atomic

For a single shared variable, **`std::atomic<T>`** makes each operation indivisible, like the test-and-set instruction of lesson 6:

```cpp fragment
#include <atomic>

std::atomic<long long> packets{0};

void receive() {
    for (int i = 0; i < 1000000; i++)
        packets.fetch_add(1);          // one indivisible read-modify-write
}
```

Two threads running `receive` always end at 2,000,000. But an atomic protects only one variable at a time. "Take the front item of a queue and shrink its size" touches several memory locations, and must look like one step to other threads. That needs a mutex.

### std::mutex and RAII locks

**`std::mutex`**, from `<mutex>`, is the lock of lesson 6, with a sleeping wait instead of spinning when it is contended. Calling `lock()` and `unlock()` by hand is fragile: an early `return` skips the unlock, and the next thread waits for ever. So C++ code uses **RAII** (resource acquisition is initialisation): an object that locks in its constructor and unlocks in its destructor, which runs on every path out of the scope.

```cpp fragment
#include <mutex>

std::mutex m;
long long total = 0;

void add(long long n) {
    std::lock_guard<std::mutex> guard(m);   // locks here
    total += n;
}                                           // unlocks here, whatever path leaves
```

- **`std::lock_guard`** locks for exactly its scope.
- **`std::unique_lock`** can also unlock and lock again in the middle. A condition variable needs this, because waiting must release the lock.
- **`std::scoped_lock`** locks several mutexes at once, with a deadlock-avoiding algorithm, so two threads locking the same two accounts in opposite orders cannot deadlock. Without it, the rule from lesson 9 applies: always lock in one global order.

```cpp fragment
struct Account { std::mutex m; long long balance = 0; };

void transfer(Account& from, Account& to, long long amount) {
    std::scoped_lock both(from.m, to.m);    // never deadlocks, whatever the order
    from.balance -= amount;
    to.balance += amount;
}
```

### std::condition_variable

**`std::condition_variable`**, from `<condition_variable>`, is the Mesa-style condition variable you added to the kernel in lesson 7:

- `cv.wait(lock)` atomically releases the mutex held by `lock` and puts the thread to sleep. When it wakes, it locks the mutex again before `wait` returns.
- `cv.notify_one()` wakes one waiting thread (the model's `signal`); `cv.notify_all()` wakes them all (`broadcast`). With nobody waiting, a notification is simply lost.

Two facts force every wait into a loop:

- **Stolen wake-ups.** A woken thread does not run at once; it must win the mutex back first. Another thread can get the mutex in between and use up whatever the waiter was woken for. You proved this with the `take_if` slot in lesson 7.
- **Spurious wake-ups.** The C++ standard allows `wait` to return even when nobody notified, because some systems cannot avoid it cheaply.

So the condition is re-checked after every wake-up. C++ builds the loop in: `cv.wait(lock, pred)` is exactly `while (!pred()) cv.wait(lock);`. Here it is in an **unbounded** queue of telemetry frames:

```cpp fragment
#include <condition_variable>
#include <deque>
#include <mutex>

class FrameQueue {
    std::mutex m;
    std::condition_variable ready;
    std::deque<int> frames;
public:
    void put(int f) {
        {
            std::lock_guard<std::mutex> guard(m);
            frames.push_back(f);
        }
        ready.notify_one();                 // after the change, lock released or not
    }
    int take() {
        std::unique_lock<std::mutex> lock(m);
        ready.wait(lock, [this] { return !frames.empty(); });
        int f = frames.front();
        frames.pop_front();
        return f;
    }
};
```

`put` changes the queue while holding the mutex, and only then notifies. A thread that changed the condition **without** the mutex could do so in the gap between a waiter's check and its `wait`, and the notification would be lost for ever: the **lost wake-up**. Notifying after unlocking, as here, is allowed and saves the woken thread from waking straight into a locked mutex.

### From C++ to the model

Every call above has a counterpart in the kernel of lesson 7, whose threads yield requests:

| C++ | the model's request |
|---|---|
| `m.lock()` (or constructing a lock guard) | `("acquire", "m")` |
| `m.unlock()` (or the guard's destructor) | `("release", "m")` |
| `cv.wait(lock)` | `("wait", "cv", "m")` |
| `cv.notify_one()` | `("signal", "cv")` |
| `cv.notify_all()` | `("broadcast", "cv")` |

Shared data lives in the dict `mem`, and a method becomes a generator function that the calling thread runs with `yield from`, which hands back its `return` value. Here is a thread that waits for a launch flag, written both ways. In C++:

```cpp fragment
void wait_for_launch() {
    std::unique_lock<std::mutex> lock(m);
    go_changed.wait(lock, [] { return go; });
    log_line("launching");
}
```

In the model:

```python fragment
def wait_for_launch(mem):
    yield ("acquire", "m")
    while not mem["go"]:
        yield ("wait", "go_changed", "m")
    mem["log"].append("launching")
    yield ("release", "m")
```

The explorer from lesson 7 runs such threads in every order that matters. It remembers each state it has seen (shared memory, the kernel's queues, and where every generator is paused, following `yield from` into the method it is running), so it checks every schedule of a small test in well under a second. It returns `None` when all is well, or `("bad", schedule)`, `("deadlock", schedule)` or `("wrong end", schedule)` with a schedule you can replay.

### The bounded queue: what it must do

A **thread-safe bounded queue** holds at most `capacity` items. Producers `push`, consumers `pop`, oldest first. `push` waits while the queue is full; `pop` waits while it is empty. And a real program must be able to shut it down: after **`close()`**, pushes are refused, and pops drain whatever is left and then report "no more items" instead of waiting for ever. In C++ that is usually `bool push(T)` and `std::optional<T> pop()`.

It needs one mutex and **two** condition variables, one per reason to wait: `not_empty` for consumers and `not_full` for producers. With a single condition variable, a `notify_one` meant for a consumer could wake another producer instead, and the consumer would sleep on.

### Why it is correct

Three arguments, one per property.

- **Safety: the invariant.** Whenever the mutex is free, the queue holds at most `capacity` items, and they are exactly the items pushed and not yet popped, in push order. Every operation reads and changes the items only while holding the mutex, so the steps of different operations never interleave inside it. A push appends only after its loop found fewer than `capacity` items **while holding the mutex**, and nothing can change in between. So the invariant holds after it. The same goes for pop.
- **No lost wake-ups.** A waiter checks its condition and goes to sleep in one step, holding the mutex until `wait` releases it. Every change that could make a waiter's condition true happens under the mutex and is followed by a notification. So a change cannot slip between the check and the sleep.
- **Close wakes everyone.** Closing changes the condition of **every** waiter, producers and consumers alike: they must all re-check and leave. So close must use `notify_all` on both condition variables. `notify_one` would leave the other waiters asleep for ever: a deadlock at shutdown.

### What it costs

Locking a free mutex costs one atomic instruction, a few nanoseconds to tens of nanoseconds. A contended one costs a system call and two context switches: microseconds. On Linux, `std::mutex` is built on a [[futex|futex]], which makes exactly that split. So the design rules are: hold a mutex for as short a time as possible (never while doing I/O or heavy work), and move work, not individual bytes, through a queue. Starting a thread costs tens of microseconds, which is why programs keep a **pool** of threads and hand them jobs through a queue, rather than start one thread per job.

### Where it is used

- **Every server and ground station**: a receiver thread pushes packets, a pool of decoder threads pops them. The bounded capacity is **back-pressure**: when decoders fall behind, the receiver blocks instead of eating all memory.
- **Logging libraries** hand log lines to a background writer thread through such a queue, so the threads doing real work never wait for the disk.
- **Flight software** frameworks, such as NASA's F Prime, connect components through message queues of fixed size, for the same two reasons: decoupling and bounded memory.

**Watch out:**

- **A joinable `std::thread` destroyed** ends the program with `std::terminate`. Join or detach every thread, or use `std::jthread`.
- **A plain `bool` flag shared between threads** is a data race: undefined behaviour. Use `std::atomic<bool>`, or read and write it under a mutex.
- **`if` instead of `while` around `wait`** breaks on stolen and spurious wake-ups. Use the predicate form of `wait`.
- **Changing the condition without the mutex** can lose a wake-up, even if the variable is atomic.
- **Holding a mutex while calling unknown code** (a callback, a slow write) invites deadlock and ruins parallelism.
- **Exceptions are off in ORBIT's C++** (`-fno-exceptions`). Constructing a `std::thread` can fail when the system runs out of threads, which normally throws `std::system_error`; with exceptions off, the program aborts instead.

::: context stdthread The thread libraries underneath
On Linux and macOS, `std::thread` is a thin layer over POSIX threads (`pthread_create`, `pthread_join`, `pthread_mutex_lock`), and on Windows over the Windows thread API. Before C++11, portable code had to use those C libraries directly, and the language itself did not even say what a data race meant. C++11 added threads and the memory model to the standard together, so that the compiler, the library and the hardware all agree on what a concurrent program may do.
:::

::: context stoptoken Asking a thread to stop, politely
C++ has no safe way to kill a thread from outside: it might be holding a lock, or be halfway through an update. C++20's `std::jthread` passes its function a `std::stop_token` if the function accepts one. The owner calls `request_stop()`, and the thread checks `stop.stop_requested()` at safe points, such as the top of its loop, and returns. The `jthread` destructor requests a stop and then joins, so forgetting to clean up no longer ends the program.
:::

::: context futex Fast when free, sleeping when not
A futex (fast user-space mutex) is a Linux system call added in 2003. The lock itself is an ordinary integer in the program's memory. Taking a free lock is a single atomic compare-and-swap in user mode, with no system call at all. Only when the lock is already taken does the thread call `futex(FUTEX_WAIT)` to sleep in the kernel, and the unlocking thread calls `futex(FUTEX_WAKE)` only if someone is sleeping. `std::mutex`, `pthread_mutex_t` and condition variables on Linux are all built on it.
:::

--- task
Write the thread-safe bounded queue as a model the explorer can check: the Python twin of a C++ class with a `std::mutex m`, two `std::condition_variable`s `not_empty` and `not_full`, a `std::deque` of items, a capacity and a `closed` flag.

The queue's state lives in the dict `mem`: `mem["items"]` (a list, oldest first), `mem["capacity"]` and `mem["closed"]`. The mutex is the lock `"m"`, and the condition variables are `"not_empty"` and `"not_full"`. Use only the requests from the lesson: `("acquire", "m")`, `("release", "m")`, `("wait", cv, "m")`, `("signal", cv)` and `("broadcast", cv)`. Write three generator functions:

- `push(mem, x)`: wait while the queue is full and not closed. If it is closed, return `False` without adding anything. Otherwise append `x`, wake one consumer, and return `True`.
- `pop(mem)`: wait while the queue is empty and not closed. If it is still empty (so it is closed), return `None`. Otherwise remove and return the oldest item, after waking one producer.
- `close(mem)`: set `mem["closed"]` to `True` and wake **every** waiting thread, consumers and producers.

Each function takes the mutex first and releases it before it returns, on every path. The starter gives the kernel and explorer from lesson 7 (leave them as they are), plus the test harness the checks use: `producer`, `consumer` and `closer` threads, `queue_setup`, and `check_queue(capacity, batches, consumers, closing=True, counts=None)`, which explores every schedule of producers pushing the given `batches` of items, `consumers` consumer threads (each taking items until `pop` returns `None`, or `counts[i]` items), and a closer thread if `closing` is true. It returns `None` when every schedule ends with every pushed item popped exactly once, in order for each producer, without ever passing the capacity or deadlocking.
--- starter
def make_monitor_kernel(mem, locks, cvs, threads):
    return {
        "mem": mem,
        "lock": {name: None for name in locks},
        "lock_q": {name: [] for name in locks},
        "cv_q": {name: [] for name in cvs},
        "threads": list(threads),
        "status": ["ready"] * len(threads),
    }


def release(k, lock):
    if k["lock_q"][lock]:
        j = k["lock_q"][lock].pop(0)
        k["lock"][lock] = j
        k["status"][j] = "ready"
    else:
        k["lock"][lock] = None


def wake(k, cv):
    j, lock = k["cv_q"][cv].pop(0)
    if k["lock"][lock] is None:
        k["lock"][lock] = j
        k["status"][j] = "ready"
    else:
        k["lock_q"][lock].append(j)


def step(k, i):
    try:
        request = next(k["threads"][i])
    except StopIteration:
        k["status"][i] = "done"
        return
    if request is None:
        return
    op = request[0]
    if op == "acquire":
        lock = request[1]
        if k["lock"][lock] is None:
            k["lock"][lock] = i
        else:
            k["status"][i] = "blocked"
            k["lock_q"][lock].append(i)
    elif op == "release":
        if k["lock"][request[1]] != i:
            raise RuntimeError(f"thread {i} released a lock it does not hold")
        release(k, request[1])
    elif op == "wait":
        cv, lock = request[1], request[2]
        if k["lock"][lock] != i:
            raise RuntimeError(f"thread {i} waited without holding the lock")
        release(k, lock)
        k["status"][i] = "blocked"
        k["cv_q"][cv].append((i, lock))
    elif op == "signal":
        if k["cv_q"][request[1]]:
            wake(k, request[1])
    elif op == "broadcast":
        while k["cv_q"][request[1]]:
            wake(k, request[1])


def where(k):
    spots = []
    for g in k["threads"]:
        frames = []
        while g is not None and getattr(g, "gi_frame", None) is not None:
            f = g.gi_frame
            frames.append((f.f_lasti, repr(sorted(f.f_locals.items()))))
            g = g.gi_yieldfrom
        spots.append(frames)
    return repr((k["mem"], k["lock"], k["lock_q"], k["cv_q"], k["status"], spots))


def explore(setup, max_steps, bad=None, final=None):
    seen = set()

    def search(prefix):
        k = setup()
        for i in prefix:
            step(k, i)
        key = where(k)
        if key in seen:
            return None
        seen.add(key)
        if bad is not None and bad(k["mem"]):
            return ("bad", prefix)
        ready = [i for i, s in enumerate(k["status"]) if s == "ready"]
        if not ready:
            if "blocked" in k["status"]:
                return ("deadlock", prefix)
            if final is not None and not final(k["mem"]):
                return ("wrong end", prefix)
            return None
        if len(prefix) == max_steps:
            return None
        for i in ready:
            found = search(prefix + [i])
            if found is not None:
                return found
        return None

    return search([])


def producer(mem, items):
    for x in items:
        ok = yield from push(mem, x)
        mem["log"].append(("push", x, ok))


def consumer(mem, me, count):
    taken = 0
    while count is None or taken < count:
        x = yield from pop(mem)
        if x is None:
            mem["log"].append(("end", me, mem["closed"]))
            return
        mem["log"].append(("pop", me, x))
        taken += 1


def closer(mem):
    yield from close(mem)


def queue_setup(capacity, batches, consumers, closing=True, counts=None):
    def setup():
        mem = {"items": [], "capacity": capacity, "closed": False, "log": []}
        threads = [producer(mem, batch) for batch in batches]
        for me in range(consumers):
            threads.append(consumer(mem, me, counts[me] if counts else None))
        if closing:
            threads.append(closer(mem))
        return make_monitor_kernel(mem, ["m"], ["not_empty", "not_full"], threads)
    return setup


def over_capacity(mem):
    return len(mem["items"]) > mem["capacity"]


def delivered(mem, batches):
    pushed = [e[1] for e in mem["log"] if e[0] == "push" and e[2]]
    popped = [e for e in mem["log"] if e[0] == "pop"]
    if sorted(pushed) != sorted(e[2] for e in popped) or mem["items"]:
        return False
    if any(e[0] == "end" and not e[2] for e in mem["log"]):
        return False
    for me in {e[1] for e in popped}:
        mine = [e[2] for e in popped if e[1] == me]
        for batch in batches:
            if [x for x in mine if x in batch] != [x for x in batch if x in mine]:
                return False
    return True


def check_queue(capacity, batches, consumers, closing=True, counts=None, max_steps=400):
    return explore(queue_setup(capacity, batches, consumers, closing, counts), max_steps,
                   over_capacity, lambda mem: delivered(mem, batches))


def push(mem, x):
    raise NotImplementedError("wait for room, append, wake a consumer")


def pop(mem):
    raise NotImplementedError("wait for an item, take the oldest, wake a producer")


def close(mem):
    raise NotImplementedError("mark closed and wake everyone")
--- solution
def make_monitor_kernel(mem, locks, cvs, threads):
    return {
        "mem": mem,
        "lock": {name: None for name in locks},
        "lock_q": {name: [] for name in locks},
        "cv_q": {name: [] for name in cvs},
        "threads": list(threads),
        "status": ["ready"] * len(threads),
    }


def release(k, lock):
    if k["lock_q"][lock]:
        j = k["lock_q"][lock].pop(0)
        k["lock"][lock] = j
        k["status"][j] = "ready"
    else:
        k["lock"][lock] = None


def wake(k, cv):
    j, lock = k["cv_q"][cv].pop(0)
    if k["lock"][lock] is None:
        k["lock"][lock] = j
        k["status"][j] = "ready"
    else:
        k["lock_q"][lock].append(j)


def step(k, i):
    try:
        request = next(k["threads"][i])
    except StopIteration:
        k["status"][i] = "done"
        return
    if request is None:
        return
    op = request[0]
    if op == "acquire":
        lock = request[1]
        if k["lock"][lock] is None:
            k["lock"][lock] = i
        else:
            k["status"][i] = "blocked"
            k["lock_q"][lock].append(i)
    elif op == "release":
        if k["lock"][request[1]] != i:
            raise RuntimeError(f"thread {i} released a lock it does not hold")
        release(k, request[1])
    elif op == "wait":
        cv, lock = request[1], request[2]
        if k["lock"][lock] != i:
            raise RuntimeError(f"thread {i} waited without holding the lock")
        release(k, lock)
        k["status"][i] = "blocked"
        k["cv_q"][cv].append((i, lock))
    elif op == "signal":
        if k["cv_q"][request[1]]:
            wake(k, request[1])
    elif op == "broadcast":
        while k["cv_q"][request[1]]:
            wake(k, request[1])

    for g in k["threads"]:

        while g is not None and getattr(g, "gi_frame", None) is not None:
            f = g.gi_frame
            frames.append((f.f_lasti, repr(sorted(f.f_locals.items()))))
            g = g.gi_yieldfrom

    return repr((k["mem"], k["lock"], k["lock_q"], k["cv_q"], k["status"], spots))


def explore(setup, max_steps, bad=None, final=None):

    def search(prefix):
        k = setup()
        for i in prefix:
            step(k, i)
        key = where(k)
        if key in seen:
            return None
        seen.add(key)
        if bad is not None and bad(k["mem"]):
            return ("bad", prefix)
        ready = [i for i, s in enumerate(k["status"]) if s == "ready"]
        if not ready:
            if "blocked" in k["status"]:
                return ("deadlock", prefix)
            if final is not None and not final(k["mem"]):
                return ("wrong end", prefix)
            return None
        if len(prefix) == max_steps:
            return None
        for i in ready:
            found = search(prefix + [i])
            if found is not None:
                return found
        return None

    return search([])


def producer(mem, items):
    for x in items:
        ok = yield from push(mem, x)
        mem["log"].append(("push", x, ok))

    taken = 0
    while count is None or taken < count:

        if x is None:
            mem["log"].append(("end", me, mem["closed"]))
            return

    yield from close(mem)


def queue_setup(capacity, batches, consumers, closing=True, counts=None):
    def setup():
        mem = {"items": [], "capacity": capacity, "closed": False, "log": []}
        threads = [producer(mem, batch) for batch in batches]
        for me in range(consumers):
            threads.append(consumer(mem, me, counts[me] if counts else None))
        if closing:
            threads.append(closer(mem))

def over_capacity(mem):
    return len(mem["items"]) > mem["capacity"]


def delivered(mem, batches):
    pushed = [e[1] for e in mem["log"] if e[0] == "push" and e[2]]
    popped = [e for e in mem["log"] if e[0] == "pop"]
    if sorted(pushed) != sorted(e[2] for e in popped) or mem["items"]:
        return False
    if any(e[0] == "end" and not e[2] for e in mem["log"]):
        return False
    for me in {e[1] for e in popped}:
        mine = [e[2] for e in popped if e[1] == me]
        for batch in batches:
            if [x for x in mine if x in batch] != [x for x in batch if x in mine]:
                return False
    return True


def check_queue(capacity, batches, consumers, closing=True, counts=None, max_steps=400):
    return explore(queue_setup(capacity, batches, consumers, closing, counts), max_steps,
                   over_capacity, lambda mem: delivered(mem, batches))


def push(mem, x):
    yield ("acquire", "m")
    while len(mem["items"]) == mem["capacity"] and not mem["closed"]:
        yield ("wait", "not_full", "m")
    if mem["closed"]:
        yield ("release", "m")
        return False
    mem["items"].append(x)
    yield ("signal", "not_empty")
    yield ("release", "m")
    return True


def pop(mem):
    yield ("acquire", "m")
    while not mem["items"] and not mem["closed"]:
        yield ("wait", "not_empty", "m")
    if not mem["items"]:
        yield ("release", "m")
        return None
    x = mem["items"].pop(0)
    yield ("signal", "not_full")
    yield ("release", "m")
    return x


def close(mem):
    yield ("acquire", "m")
    mem["closed"] = True
    yield ("broadcast", "not_empty")
    yield ("broadcast", "not_full")
    yield ("release", "m")
--- hint
Each function has the shape of `wait_for_launch` in the lesson: acquire, a `while` loop that waits while the thread cannot go on, the change, a wake-up for the other side, release. The waiting condition includes "and not closed", so closing ends every wait.
--- hint
After the loop, `push` and `pop` each have two ways out. For `push`: if closed, release and `return False`; otherwise append, signal `"not_empty"`, release, `return True`. For `pop`: if the list is empty, release and `return None`; otherwise `pop(0)`, signal `"not_full"`, release, and return the item.
--- hint
`close` needs `("broadcast", "not_empty")` and `("broadcast", "not_full")` between its acquire and release. With `signal`, the second of two waiting consumers would sleep for ever.
--- check test | Pushing into a roomy queue, one thread alone
(lambda k: ([step(k, 0) for _ in range(7)], k["mem"]["items"], k["mem"]["log"], k["status"], k["lock"])[1:])(queue_setup(5, [[7, 8]], 0, False)()) == ([7, 8], [("push", 7, True), ("push", 8, True)], ["done"], {"m": None})
--- check test | A push into a full queue waits on not_full, having released the mutex
(lambda k: ([step(k, 0) for _ in range(5)], k["mem"]["items"], k["status"], k["cv_q"]["not_full"], k["lock"])[1:])(queue_setup(1, [[7, 8]], 0, False)()) == ([7], ["blocked"], [(0, "m")], {"m": None})
--- check test | After close: push is refused and pop returns None at once
(lambda k: ([step(k, 2) for _ in range(5)], [step(k, 0) for _ in range(4)], [step(k, 1) for _ in range(4)], k["mem"]["log"], k["status"])[3:])(queue_setup(1, [[5]], 1)()) == ([("push", 5, False), ("end", 0, True)], ["done", "done", "done"])
--- check test | A closed queue still hands out what it holds, then None
(lambda k: ([step(k, 0) for _ in range(5)], [step(k, 2) for _ in range(5)], [step(k, 1) for _ in range(9)], k["mem"]["log"])[3])(queue_setup(1, [[5]], 1)()) == [("push", 5, True), ("pop", 0, 5), ("end", 0, True)]
--- check test | Items come out oldest first
(lambda k: ([step(k, 0) for _ in range(10)], [step(k, 1) for _ in range(13)], [e for e in k["mem"]["log"] if e[0] == "pop"])[2])(queue_setup(3, [[4, 5, 6]], 1, False, [3])()) == [("pop", 0, 4), ("pop", 0, 5), ("pop", 0, 6)]
--- check test | Every schedule: one producer, one consumer, capacity 1, closed at any moment
check_queue(1, [[1, 2, 3]], 1) is None
--- check test | Every schedule: two consumers, and close must wake them both
check_queue(1, [[1]], 2) is None
?? If this finds a deadlock, a consumer is still waiting after close: close must broadcast, not signal.
--- check test | Every schedule: two consumers taking one item each, no close
check_queue(1, [[1, 2]], 2, False, [1, 1]) is None
?? A "wrong end" here usually means a consumer woke, found the item already taken by the other one, and gave up. Re-check the condition in a while loop.
--- check test | Every schedule: two producers against a full buffer
check_queue(1, [[1, 2], [3]], 1, False, [3]) is None
?? "bad" means the queue went over its capacity: a producer must re-check that there is room every time it wakes.
--- check test | Every schedule: capacity 2
check_queue(2, [[1, 2, 3]], 1, False, [3]) is None
--- check test | Every schedule: producers stuck on a full queue are freed by close
explore(queue_setup(1, [[1, 2, 3]], 0), 300, over_capacity, lambda m: [e[2] for e in m["log"]] in ([True, False, False], [False, False, False]) and len(m["items"]) == sum(e[2] for e in m["log"])) is None

+++ question | The destructor of a running thread
--- ask
What happens when this function returns?

```cpp fragment
void start_logger() {
    std::thread t(write_logs);
}
```
--- choice
The thread keeps running in the background.
--- choice
The destructor waits for the thread to finish.
--- choice correct
The program ends with `std::terminate`, because `t` is still joinable when it is destroyed.
--- choice
It does not compile: a thread must be joined in the same scope.
--- why
A `std::thread` that was started and neither joined nor detached is joinable, and destroying a joinable `std::thread` calls `std::terminate`. The fix is `t.join()` (wait for it), `t.detach()` (let it run alone), or C++20's `std::jthread`, whose destructor requests a stop and joins.

+++ question | Why the predicate
--- ask
Why does `not_empty.wait(lock, [&] { return !items.empty() || closed; })` check its condition again after every wake-up?
--- choice
Because `notify_one` might wake the thread before the mutex is unlocked.
--- choice correct
Because between the notification and getting the mutex back, another thread may have taken the item, and because `wait` may also return spuriously, with no notification at all.
--- choice
Because the condition variable forgets which thread it woke.
--- choice
It does not need to; the predicate is only checked once, before the first wait.
--- why
`wait(lock, pred)` is `while (!pred()) wait(lock);`. A woken thread must reacquire the mutex, and another thread can get it first and use up the item (a stolen wake-up, as in lesson 7's `take_if`). The standard also permits spurious wake-ups. Re-checking under the mutex makes both harmless.

+++ question | A flag without a lock
--- ask
One thread runs `while (!done) {}` and another later sets `done = true;`, where `done` is a plain `bool`. What does the C++ standard say?
--- choice
The loop ends a few nanoseconds after the store, when the caches agree.
--- choice correct
It is a data race, so the behaviour is undefined; in practice the compiler may read `done` once and loop for ever.
--- choice
It is fine on x86, because stores to a `bool` are atomic there.
--- choice
The loop ends at the next context switch.
--- why
Unordered conflicting accesses where one is a write are a data race, which is undefined behaviour. The compiler may assume no other thread changes `done` inside the loop and hoist the read out of it. `std::atomic<bool> done` (or a mutex around both accesses) creates the happens-before edge that makes the store visible.

+++ practice | A latch: wait until N workers are done
--- task
C++20's `std::latch` is a counter that threads count down; other threads wait until it reaches zero. Model one with the lesson's kernel. The count is `mem["count"]`, the mutex is `"m"` and the condition variable is `"zero"`.

Write two generator functions:

- `count_down(mem)`: take 1 from the count. If that makes it 0, wake **every** waiting thread.
- `wait_zero(mem)`: return once the count is 0; if it is not, wait for it.

Both take the mutex first and release it before returning. The starter gives the kernel and explorer, plus `latch_setup(n, waiters)`, which runs `n` workers that each log `("work", i)` and then count down once, and `waiters` threads that wait and then log `("go", i)`; and `all_work_before_go(mem)`, which is true when every "go" in the log comes after all the "work" entries. The checks explore every schedule.
--- starter
def make_monitor_kernel(mem, locks, cvs, threads):
    return {
        "mem": mem,
        "lock": {name: None for name in locks},
        "lock_q": {name: [] for name in locks},
        "cv_q": {name: [] for name in cvs},
        "threads": list(threads),
        "status": ["ready"] * len(threads),
    }


def release(k, lock):
    if k["lock_q"][lock]:
        j = k["lock_q"][lock].pop(0)
        k["lock"][lock] = j
        k["status"][j] = "ready"
    else:
        k["lock"][lock] = None


def wake(k, cv):
    j, lock = k["cv_q"][cv].pop(0)
    if k["lock"][lock] is None:
        k["lock"][lock] = j
        k["status"][j] = "ready"
    else:
        k["lock_q"][lock].append(j)


def step(k, i):
    try:
        request = next(k["threads"][i])
    except StopIteration:
        k["status"][i] = "done"
        return
    if request is None:
        return
    op = request[0]
    if op == "acquire":
        lock = request[1]
        if k["lock"][lock] is None:
            k["lock"][lock] = i
        else:
            k["status"][i] = "blocked"
            k["lock_q"][lock].append(i)
    elif op == "release":
        if k["lock"][request[1]] != i:
            raise RuntimeError(f"thread {i} released a lock it does not hold")
        release(k, request[1])
    elif op == "wait":
        cv, lock = request[1], request[2]
        if k["lock"][lock] != i:
            raise RuntimeError(f"thread {i} waited without holding the lock")
        release(k, lock)
        k["status"][i] = "blocked"
        k["cv_q"][cv].append((i, lock))
    elif op == "signal":
        if k["cv_q"][request[1]]:
            wake(k, request[1])
    elif op == "broadcast":
        while k["cv_q"][request[1]]:
            wake(k, request[1])


def where(k):
    spots = []
    for g in k["threads"]:
        frames = []
        while g is not None and getattr(g, "gi_frame", None) is not None:
            f = g.gi_frame
            frames.append((f.f_lasti, repr(sorted(f.f_locals.items()))))
            g = g.gi_yieldfrom
        spots.append(frames)
    return repr((k["mem"], k["lock"], k["lock_q"], k["cv_q"], k["status"], spots))


def explore(setup, max_steps, bad=None, final=None):
    seen = set()

    def search(prefix):
        k = setup()
        for i in prefix:
            step(k, i)
        key = where(k)
        if key in seen:
            return None
        seen.add(key)
        if bad is not None and bad(k["mem"]):
            return ("bad", prefix)
        ready = [i for i, s in enumerate(k["status"]) if s == "ready"]
        if not ready:
            if "blocked" in k["status"]:
                return ("deadlock", prefix)
            if final is not None and not final(k["mem"]):
                return ("wrong end", prefix)
            return None
        if len(prefix) == max_steps:
            return None
        for i in ready:
            found = search(prefix + [i])
            if found is not None:
                return found
        return None

    return search([])


def worker(mem, me):
    mem["log"].append(("work", me))
    yield from count_down(mem)


def waiter(mem, me):
    yield from wait_zero(mem)
    mem["log"].append(("go", me))


def latch_setup(n, waiters):
    def setup():
        mem = {"count": n, "log": []}
        threads = [worker(mem, i) for i in range(n)] + [waiter(mem, i) for i in range(waiters)]
        return make_monitor_kernel(mem, ["m"], ["zero"], threads)
    return setup


def all_work_before_go(mem):
    work = sum(1 for e in mem["log"] if e[0] == "work")
    return all(sum(1 for e in mem["log"][:i] if e[0] == "work") == work
               for i, e in enumerate(mem["log"]) if e[0] == "go")


def count_down(mem):
    raise NotImplementedError


def wait_zero(mem):
    raise NotImplementedError
--- solution
def make_monitor_kernel(mem, locks, cvs, threads):
    return {
        "mem": mem,
        "lock": {name: None for name in locks},
        "lock_q": {name: [] for name in locks},
        "cv_q": {name: [] for name in cvs},
        "threads": list(threads),
        "status": ["ready"] * len(threads),
    }
